->WHICH BEGAN ANEW AT THE TIME OF ITS EMERGENCE.<-

->**A LITTLE BOOK** full of Astronomical, Physical, Metaphysical,<-
->Meteorological & Astrological Disputations,<-
->both esteemed and paradoxical.<-
<note>original Greek: "ἐνδόξοις & παραδόξοις" (endoxois & paradoxois). Kepler is signaling that he will discuss both widely accepted ideas and strange, counter-intuitive theories.</note>

->**THERE ARE ADDED:**<-

->I. ON THE UNKNOWN STAR IN THE SWAN:<-
->An Astronomical Account.<-
<note>This refers to the star P Cygni, which had suddenly brightened in 1600, appearing as a "new" star to observers of the time.</note>

->II. ON THE TRUE YEAR OF THE BIRTH OF<-
->**JESUS CHRIST THE SAVIOR**,<-
->a very recent consideration of the opinion of LAWRENCE SUSLYGA the Pole, who finds four years lacking in the commonly used Era.<-
<note>Kepler used astronomical data, such as the conjunction of planets, to argue that the Christian calendar was off by several years, placing the birth of Christ earlier than traditionally thought.</note>

->With the Privilege of His Sacred Imperial Majesty for 15 years.<-
<note>This "Privilege" acted as an early form of copyright, granted by Emperor Rudolf II to protect the author’s work from being pirated.</note>

-># TO LORD RUDOLF II<-
->## EMPEROR OF THE ROMANS<-
->## ALWAYS AUGUST<-
->### PIOUS, FORTUNATE, FATHER OF THE COUNTRY <note>original: "P. F. P. P." standing for "Pio, Felici, Patri Patriae," traditional honorary titles for Roman emperors</note><-

->### *ETC.*<-

**W**HY I have dedicated this little book as well, **MOST AUGUST CAESAR**, <note>A title used here for the Holy Roman Emperor, drawing a direct line back to the ancient Roman rulers</note> which I have written concerning the **NEW STAR**, <note>The "New Star" refers to the supernova of 1604, now known as Kepler's Supernova</note> especially to Your Sacred Majesty's Name: I do not believe the reason for this matter needs to be explained to Your Majesty at great length.

For besides the fact that, to Your Sacred Imperial Majesty <margin>)( 2</margin> from the stu- <note>The text cuts off mid-word ("studiis") and continues on the next page; it likely refers to "from the studies of my youth" or "from the pursuit of my duties."</note>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page contains the opening of the dedicatory epistle of Johannes Kepler's "De Stella Nova" (1606), addressed to Emperor Rudolf II.</meta>

-># LETTER<-

I serve the study of mathematics <note>original: "studijs Mathematicis"; Kepler held the position of Imperial Mathematician</note>, and for this reason, I enjoy a generous salary. In addition, there was the most merciful command of Your Majesty, that I should devote all effort, care, and diligence to observing and describing the circumstances of this stupendous miracle <note>Kepler is referring to the supernova of 1604, now known as "Kepler's Star"</note>.

What, therefore, was more appropriate for me to do than to bring this little book into the sight of **Your Sacred Imperial Majesty**? Although I am bashful and trembling with the knowledge of my own insignificance, I do so as befits a faithful and honest servant, waiting to hear whether Your Majesty judges that what was commanded has been properly executed.

Rather, I must explain the cause of such a long delay: why, although this star first shone in the month of October in the year 1604, this description only now comes into public view, after almost two full years have passed. Indeed, the explanation of this matter seems to contain a new argument for why this little book should be offered to **Your Sacred Imperial Majesty**—to you alone of all mortals—and to no one else.

I could recount the facts of the case: that the end of the star <note>The point at which the supernova faded from naked-eye visibility</note> was not certain before February of this year; and that the matters written and discussed in the first part of this book—from the perspectives of <term>astronomy</term>, <term>physics</term>, and <term>metaphysics</term>—were ready in such a way that they could have appeared at the past spring fairs in Frankfurt <note>The Frankfurt Book Fair, even then the most important event for the European book trade</note>, had not the hope of an arriving printer failed me.

Deſigni- <note>This is a "catchword," a 17th-century printing feature where the first syllable of the next page is printed at the bottom of the current one to ensure the pages are bound in the correct order.</note>

<summary>Johannes Kepler addresses Emperor Rudolf II, apologizing for the two-year delay in publishing his observations of the 1604 supernova and explaining his duty as Imperial Mathematician.</summary>
<keywords>Johannes Kepler, Rudolf II, Supernova of 1604, Astronomy, Imperial Mathematician, Frankfurt Book Fair, 17th-century science</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page begins the dedicatory epistle of Johannes Kepler's work on the "New Star" (the supernova of 1604). It establishes the parallel between astronomical study and the contemporary political turmoil in the Holy Roman Empire.</meta>

->E P I S T L E<-

This star has given birth to a new labor in Astronomy, a task that is inherently slow due to the star's long duration. Because of this, the investigation of the planetary motions has been greatly hindered until now. Furthermore, at the very time when Your Sacred Imperial Majesty <note>Kepler is addressing Holy Roman Emperor Rudolf II, his patron.</note> devoted effort to the settlement of Hungarian affairs—having arranged and weighed the means beneficial to this cause—in that nearly same stretch of time, astronomers sweated over the description of the Star, and I was most occupied in preparing and printing this little book. Indeed, so many specific details have aligned themselves thus far that nearly every time turbulent rumors reached us from Hungary, some new delay intervened in this little publication; yet when those affairs were calmed, these works also proceeded. Finally, there were frequent allusions to the people and the nature of these events.

It is not that I keep my mind tainted by this superstition, as to believe that these things are linked by some law of fate. I do not even concede that the events of human affairs are bound to the eternal courses of the heavens. <note>Kepler frequently walked a fine line between his duties as an Imperial Astrologer and his scientific belief that the stars do not "force" human events.</note> Rather, it is because—I know not how—just as we gaze with great joy at certain rudiments of perfect figures found in fossils, <term>fossils</term> <note>In the 17th century, "fossil" referred to anything dug out of the ground, including minerals and crystals, not just organic remains.</note> and preserve them among our treasures, assisting them with a chisel <note>original: "cælo," referring to an engraver's tool</note> and a brush so that they might more perfectly express that which they resemble from afar; so in these matters also, we uniquely love and praise whatever beauty and harmony chance provides.

When—

<summary>Kepler dedicates his study of the 1604 Supernova to Emperor Rudolf II, noting how the book's publication was delayed by the same political unrest in Hungary that occupied the Emperor's attention. He clarifies that while he sees a poetic harmony between celestial and earthly events, he does not believe the stars strictly dictate human fate.</summary>
<keywords>Johannes Kepler, Rudolf II, Supernova of 1604, Hungary, astrology vs astronomy, fossils, Imperial patronage</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page contains the dedication of Johannes Kepler's work on the "New Star" (the supernova of 1604) to the Holy Roman Emperor, Rudolf II. It establishes the relationship between the patron's political success and the scholar's scientific progress.</meta>

->DEDICATION<-

Although the order of nature dictates that all the successes and adversities of Princes are shared with their servants and those who cultivate letters and arts—and that the fates of the latter are governed in some way by the former—it is no disgrace to mention these things or to boast of them publicly. For such a shared fate <note>The author uses the word "conspiratio," implying a "breathing together" or harmony of purpose between the ruler and the scholar.</note> in both good and bad fortune, the more intimate it is, the more certain a testimony it offers of a subject’s loyalty.

Therefore, O great Omen of Peace and of the Arts which peace fosters, most August Emperor, graciously receive this little book, this final settlement <note>original: "transactionem." Kepler views this publication as his final accounting or "closing of the books" on the astronomical observations of the 1604 supernova.</note> regarding the **New Star**, through which I finally release myself from this extraordinary labor. Though it comes late, it arrives at an opportune moment: namely, at this very time when you, like a most gentle Father, have embraced your Hungary and welcomed it through the open doors of your grace <note>This likely refers to the Peace of Vienna in 1606, which settled a major uprising in Hungary during the Long Turkish War.</note>; and with that hope, you have filled the hearts and prayers of all.

I shall humbly pray to **GOD, THE BEST AND GREATEST**, that He may prolong the life, health, and happiness of your **Sacred Imperial Majesty** for a very long time, and that He may paternally guide all your actions and your Empire toward the glory of His Name, the safety of the human race, and the defense of the **Christian Commonwealth** <term>Reipublicae Christianae: a common term for the collective body of Christian nations in Europe</term>. May He grant such prosperity that, from it, astronomers may once again dare to find good omens for their own studies; and finally, when your **Sacred Imperial Majesty** concludes this long war...
[ornament: )(] 4 with

<summary>In this dedication to Emperor Rudolf II, Kepler presents his findings on the supernova of 1604 as a "settlement" of his labors, linking the success of his astronomical research to the political stability and peace brought by the Emperor.</summary>
<keywords>Johannes Kepler, Rudolf II, Dedication, New Star, Supernova of 1604, Hungary, Patronage, Peace of Vienna</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page concludes the dedicatory epistle of Johannes Kepler’s work, likely addressed to Emperor Rudolf II, and transitions into the table of contents.</meta>

# DEDICATORY EPISTLE

...having successfully finished with the infidels <note>original: "infidelibus"; this likely refers to the ongoing wars with the Ottoman Empire</note>; I myself also might finally be able to triumph in my astronomical work—to which I was appointed by Your Sacred Imperial Majesty—namely, concerning the restoration <note>Kepler uses "restoration" to mean the correction and calculation of planetary orbits to match observations</note> of the planets, assisted by the support of Your same Majesty.

->I commend myself most submissively to Your Sacred Imperial Majesty.<-

PRAGUE, the day after the Nones of September <note>The Nones of September fell on the 5th; this date is September 6th</note>, in the year from the birth of CHRIST (according to the common reckoning) one thousand, six hundred and six.

->*Your Sacred Imperial Majesty’s*<-

->*most submissive Mathematician*<-

->JOHANNES KEPLER.<-

### INDEX

<summary>In this concluding segment of his dedication, Johannes Kepler links his astronomical research to the Emperor's political triumphs and dates the completion of this preface to September 1606 in Prague.</summary>
<keywords>Johannes Kepler, Emperor Rudolf II, astronomy, planetary restoration, Prague, 1606, dedication</keywords>

# INDEX OF CHAPTERS, INTO
### WHICH THIS LITTLE BOOK IS DIS-
### tributed.

| CHAPTER. | PAGE. |
| :--- | :--- |
| I. **T**HE HISTORY *of the New Star, which shone in the constellation Ophiuchus <note>original Latin: "Serpentario," the Serpent-Bearer. This star is now known as Kepler's Supernova.</note> during the years 1604 and 1605, described through its circumstances.* | 1. |
| II. *Concerning the Fiery Trigon, <note>A "Trigon" or "Triplicity" is a group of three zodiac signs associated with an element (Fire, Earth, Air, or Water). The Fiery Trigon consists of Aries, Leo, and Sagittarius.</note> and beginning with the first meaning of the word* T R I G O N. | 10. |
| III. *Concerning the second meaning of the word Trigon, or the triangulation of the signs.* | 12. |
| IV. *Whether the division of the Zodiac into twelve equal parts is Natural—arguing against the astrologers.* | 14. |
| V. *Whether the names of the signs are arbitrary, or truly natural and meaningful—arguing against the astrologers.* | 18. |
| VI. *That the naming of the Trigons after the elements is not from the nature of things, but by human choice—arguing against the astrologers.* | 23. |
| VII. *What natural cause joins the signs of the Zodiac into one Triplicity at a distance of one-third of a circle? <note>A third of a circle (120 degrees) creates the "Trine" aspect, which Kepler explored through his theories of geometry and harmony.</note> Including the order of the periods of the Zodiac.* | 27. |
| VIII. *Concerning the natural powers of the Fiery Triplicity; and first, regarding the efficacy of Conjunctions—against Giovanni Pico, Count of Mirandola.* <note>Pico della Mirandola (1463–1494) was a famous philosopher who wrote a massive critique of astrology, which Kepler addresses here.</note> | 30. |
| IX. *Concerning the efficacy of the other Aspects, and likewise of the Trigon, against the same author; derived from the doctrine of harmony, the foundations of which are found here.* | 37. |
| X. *To what extent some importance is added to the locations of the zodiac <note>original Latin: "signiferi," the sign-bearer, a standard term for the zodiac belt.</note> by the meetings of the planets which occur within them: including more on the formative faculty of sublunary things.* <note>"Sublunary" refers to the Earth and its atmosphere—the realm "under the moon" that was thought to be influenced by the movements of the heavens.</note> | 42. |
| XI. *Concerning* | |

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the table of contents (Index) for Johannes Kepler’s work on the "New Star" (the supernova of 1604), following previous sections detailing its initial appearance and early observations.</meta>

-># I N D E X<-

XI. *Concerning the time of the **Great Conjunction**, which marked the beginning of the return of the **Fiery Trigon**; where observations of the three superior planets, made in the years 1603 and 1604, are recorded.* 44.
<note>The "Great Conjunction" refers to the rare alignment of Jupiter and Saturn. The "Fiery Trigon" <term>Triplicitas ignea</term> is an astrological grouping of the fire signs: Aries, Leo, and Sagittarius. Kepler was fascinated by how this new star appeared during such a significant alignment.</note>

XII. *Observations of the **New Star**, in the years 1604 and 1605.* 57.

XIII. *An investigation into the true position of the **New Star** beneath the Zodiac.* 60.

XIV. *Concerning the constellations of **Ophiuchus** (the Serpent Bearer), the **Serpent**, and the **Scorpion**.* 66.
<note>original: "Serpentarij, Serpentis & Scorpionis"</note>

->*A copperplate engraving, in which the visual summary of the book is presented.* 76.<-

XV. *Concerning the position of this star within the diameter of the Universe, or concerning its immense distance from the center of the Earth, particularly according to **Copernican hypotheses**.* 77.
<note>Kepler is testing whether this star fits better in the old Earth-centered system or the newer Sun-centered system of Copernicus.</note>

XVI. *A defense of the vastness of the **fixed stars** in the Copernican hypotheses; showing the balanced proportion of movements which is lacking in the Ptolemaic view; likewise, an exploration of the origin and beauty of the proportion of the fixed stars to the moving bodies; including a discussion on the size of the **New Star**.* 83.
<note>The "fixed stars" are what we now know as distant stars in our galaxy, which appeared to the ancients as if they were pinned to a rotating sphere.</note>

XVII. *It is proved by the quality of its light, and likewise by its lack of movement, within the framework of both the **Ptolemaic** and **Brahean** hypotheses, that the star stood among the fixed stars.* 89.
<note>Tycho Brahe, Kepler's mentor, proposed a system where the Sun orbits the Earth, but all other planets orbit the Sun. Kepler is proving the star is not a nearby phenomenon like a comet, but far away in the heavens.</note>

XVIII. *Concerning the purity of light, the colors, and the twinkling of the **New Star**, and their causes.* 92.

XIX. *Concerning the matter of the **New Star**.* 97.

XX. *Whether the matter and body of the **New Star** had already existed long ago.* 100.

XXI. *Whether this star was withdrawn into the depths of the ether by movement, and an astronomical proof that the sphere of the fixed stars is finite, even in the hypotheses of **Copernicus**.* 104.

XXII. *That the matter of the new star was celestial, and whether it originated from the **Milky Way**.* 110.

XXIII. *That the matter of the heavens is capable of change; five astronomical proofs, especially from the **Eclipse** of the year 1605.* 112.
<note>This was a revolutionary claim. Traditional Aristotelian science argued that the heavens were "incorruptible" and could never change. Kepler uses the new star to prove the universe is dynamic.</note>

XXIV. *Concerning the **efficient cause** of the New Star, where the Universe is likely endowed with some natural faculty.* 121.
<note>The "efficient cause" <term>efficiente</term> refers to the agency or force that actually creates the object.</note>

XXV. *A comparison of this **New Star** with the one from the year 1572 and with **Comets**; including whether it possessed a tail.* 124.
<note>The star of 1572 was "Tycho's Star," another supernova that had appeared decades earlier.</note>

XXVI. *Whether...*

<summary>This page lists the table of contents for Chapters XI through XXV of Kepler's treatise on the 1604 supernova. It covers the star's astronomical position, its physical properties, and its implications for the Copernican versus Ptolemaic models of the universe.</summary>
<keywords>Great Conjunction, Fiery Trigon, Ophiuchus, Copernicus, Ptolemy, Tycho Brahe, fixed stars, celestial matter, supernova of 1604, stellar twinkling</keywords>

<meta>This page serves as the Table of Contents and Errata for Johannes Kepler’s work on the "New Star" (the 1604 Supernova). It outlines the final chapters of the first part, the contents of the second part published in Frankfurt, and a significant chronological dissertation on the birth of Christ.</meta>

->CHAPTERS.<-

XXVI. *Whether this star coincided by chance with the time and place of the **Great Conjunction** <term>Great Conjunction: A rare astronomical event where Jupiter and Saturn appear very close together in the sky, once every 20 years. Kepler believed the 1604 supernova was significant because it appeared during a "Great Conjunction" in a "Fiery Triplicity."</term>, or by some design; four current opinions.* 129.
XXVII. *An examination of the stated opinions; concerning material necessity, concerning **Chance**, what it can do and what it cannot; and concerning the **Spirits** <note>original: "Spiritibus Mundo congenitis" — Kepler is discussing the "Soul of the World," a theory that the Earth or the universe has a natural animating force.</note> innate and natural to the World.* 136.
*The Account of the Star in the Swan <note>original: "Stella Cygni" — referring to P Cygni, a variable star discovered in 1600</note> follows, due to the similarity of the subject.* 149.
XXVIII. THE SECOND PART *which is published in Frankfurt, concerns* **Signi-**
XXIX. **fications**, *whether natural—in weather phenomena, in the powers of natural things, and even in Man; finally, in man himself as a reasoning being—or even arbitrary significations, if the star is presented to the World by the design of* **GOD** *or a high ministering creature.* 169.
XXX. *A dispute concerning the true year of the **Birth of Christ**, which is printed separately, arises from the discussion on page 135.* <note>Kepler famously used his astronomical observations to argue that Christ was actually born in 4 BC, linking the Star of Bethlehem to a previous Great Conjunction.</note>

[A rectangular decorative woodcut ornament featuring floral and geometric patterns.]

->THESE ERRORS WERE NOTICED IN PASSING.<-
Page 33. line 20. **feeling their way** <note>original Greek: ψηλαφῶντες (psēlaphōntes)</note>. P. 49. l. 34. 36½ Hours. P. 112. in the margin, 1596. P. 153. penultimate line, **legal pause** <note>original: "justitium" — usually referring to a cessation of public business.</note>. P. 164. l. 15. 1601. P. 168. l. 13. **to them**.

Perhaps other slight errors will occur, which the reader may easily correct for themselves.

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page serves as a poetic frontispiece or dedication, likely from Johannes Kepler’s work "De Stella Nova" (1606) regarding the supernova of 1604. It establishes a parallel between Kepler's astronomical discoveries and his personal life.</meta>

[Decorative border of leaf-like patterns]

->### EPIGRAM BY THE AUTHOR ON THE RISING OF THE NEW STAR.<-
<note>original: "Epigramma Authoris in Exortum Novae Stellae". This refers to the supernova of 1604, now known as Kepler's Supernova, which appeared in the constellation Ophiuchus.</note>

While I bring forth the orbits of **RED** MARS:
<note>Kepler was famously calculating the orbit of Mars at this time, leading to his discovery of elliptical orbits.</note>
Behold, Nature brings forth a rival **REDNESS**.
<note>The "rival redness" is the new star (supernova), which was noted for its bright, changing colors and proximity to Mars in the sky.</note>
While I bring forth the threats of the **RED** star:
Behold, my wife brings forth a **GOLDEN** little boy.
<note>Kepler’s son, Friedrich, was born on December 3, 1604, the same date recorded at the bottom of this poem.</note>
Go away, you sad things of **RED** light,
Stars pregnant with **WARLIKE** tumult:
<note>In astrology, Mars and the appearance of new stars were often interpreted as omens of war and disaster.</note>
Welcome instead, my **DARK-EYED**, yet
**GENTLE** stars of the **MILKY** boy.
<note>original: "Nigella... lactei puelli". Kepler tenderly compares the dark pupils of his newborn son's eyes to a new kind of "star" that brings him more joy than the astronomical ones.</note>
As for that radiant light which was born, let it set in its turn:
May the goodness of the <term>Thunderer</term> preserve you for me;
<note>term: Thunderer (Latin: Tonans). A poetic title for God, originally used for Jupiter in classical Latin, but adopted by Renaissance scholars to refer to the Christian God.</note>
Until the <term>Egg-shaped</term> Orbits of the Power-in-Arms,
<note>term: Egg-shaped (Latin: Oviformes). A direct reference to Kepler's revolutionary finding that planetary orbits are not perfect circles, but "ovoid" or elliptical.</note>
Which I reveal, you impress upon the mind.

->3 December, in the year 1604.<-

->J. K.<-

# HISTORY OF THE NEW STAR, WHICH SHONE IN THE YEARS 1604 AND 1605 IN OPHIUCHUS.

## CHAPTER ONE.

**A**NNO <note>The text begins with a large decorative initial A.</note> In the year 1604 from the Christian era: the beginning of which modern chronologists argue was actually several years after the birth of our Lord JESUS CHRIST, God and Man, from the Virgin Mary:

<margin>The circumstances of the time.</margin> In the first year of an eight-hundred-year astrological period, arising from the Fiery Trigon, the eighth such period since the foundation of the world: which the meeting of Saturn and Jupiter at the eighth degree of Sagittarius had already begun on December 17/27 of the previous year, 1603, though Mars was absent at that time: <term>Fiery Trigon: A rare celestial alignment where the "great conjunctions" of Jupiter and Saturn occur in the fire signs of the zodiac (Aries, Leo, and Sagittarius). It was thought to signal great historical shifts.</term>

Since various predictions from astrologers had existed for many years concerning the returning fiery trigon, and the massive portents <note>Events or signs believed to foreshadow the future.</note> and political upheavals expected to follow from this year 1604; and since, therefore, the minds of all people were alert for new developments:

And now also with Mars having passed Saturn at the 10th degree of Sagittarius on September 16/26, and from there hastening in the following days toward Jupiter (who had already moved a few degrees beyond Saturn); if Mars were to catch up to him, he would complete the "Great Conjunction" and fulfill the proper title of this alignment according to the doctrine of the astrologers: <term>Great Conjunction: The alignment of the planets Jupiter and Saturn; when Mars joins them, it is considered even more significant.</term>

Since the eyes of all astronomers who diligently perform their profession were fixed upon this spectacle of nature on the evenings of those consecutive days; some for the sake of correcting calculations of planetary movement; others for delight; others for the solemnity of an event returning after so many centuries; others out of interest in predictions; and some also to test the truth of their *prognoses* <note>original: "προγνωσιῶν" — forecasts or predictions</note>, specifically those who, following the teachings of the Arabs, had expressly predicted a comet would appear at this time:

Since I, in Prague, on September 17/27, during clear weather following the departure of Mars from Saturn, <note>The text cuts off here as it transitions to the next page.</note>

->### ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT-BEARER<- 3

...devoted solely to <term>horoscopy</term> <note>The practice of casting horoscopes and studying the positions of stars to predict earthly events.</note>, who long ago first gathered his knowledge of the celestial constellations from sailors in the Mediterranean Sea, and from that point increased it daily through the use of books and practice. This man, I say, intending to observe the positions of the planets following their <term>conjunction</term> <note>An astronomical event where two or more celestial bodies appear close together from Earth's perspective; in this case, the meeting of Jupiter and Mars.</note> from the previous day; at the very location of the conjunction, he noticed three very bright stars instead of two, just as clouds snatched the view away once more. 

But he, certain that he had seen something new, came to me the following morning and asked if I had seen anything "new." When I replied that the clouds had not granted me any view of the heavens, I warned him that he should not, through hasty belief and his own inexperience, ridiculously turn the bright planet of Jupiter <note>original: "clara Jovis stella"</note> into a "New Star." I admonished him to look again and again; he replied that he would not spread any rumors until the clearest experience itself could pronounce its verdict. Following this, through six continuous days of clouds, even the memory of this announcement was extinguished from my mind; <insert>Baron von</insert> Brunovvsky...

4 ->JOHANNES KEPLER<-

Roeslin in Alsace, while riding a horse; when he had found that first clear evening after long rains.
  On October 7/17 <note>Kepler provides dates in both the Julian "Old Style" and Gregorian "New Style" calendars, which were both in use across Europe at this time.</note> it was noticed everywhere in Württemberg.
  On that same October 7/17, David Fabricius in Frisia both saw it and recognized it, and observed it on this and the following day with his accurate instruments, built according to Tychonic <note>Referring to the methods and standards of Tycho Brahe, the famous Danish astronomer who had been Kepler's mentor.</note> standards.
  On October 8/18, its brightness shone through the clouds to Maestlin <note>Michael Maestlin, Kepler’s former professor and a renowned astronomer.</note>; but the novelty of it was not yet clear to him because of the density of the clouds.
  Finally here in Prague on October 8/18, the clouds were driven away, but only slightly; while Brunovvskius and my assistant Schuler were present—the former intent on confirming his report, the latter on refuting it, if possible. Having been warned by Schuler, while I was climbing to a suitable spot, that part of the sky had already passed out of view.
  On that same October 8/18, it was clearly seen by Maestlin in Tübingen.
  On the following day, October 9/19, under a very clear sky, I observed it as well as the location and instruments permitted.
  From that point, everyone was notified by letters everywhere: and Bartholomew Crestinus, Mathematician to the Prince of Savoy, reports that on November 7/17, letters written from Spain to his Prince had already arrived, in which the rising of the new star was celebrated.
  On October 11/21 it was observed by Jost Bürgi through an instrument he prepared himself, and on the same day by Frans Tengnagel and me, using the Tychonic sextant in the Imperial Garden <note>The Royal Garden at Prague Castle, where Kepler served as Imperial Mathematician to Holy Roman Emperor Rudolf II.</note>.

<margin>
**Venus mistaken for the new star.**
</margin>

  Meanwhile, while the common people were being stirred up everywhere by the news of the new star, the matter having been shared through frequent letters: the morning rising of Venus conveniently occurred. Since it was very close to the Earth—scarcely a fourth part of the distance of the Sun, and a sixth or seventh of the distance it reaches during its evening rising—it was fittingly many times brighter and larger in appearance. Thus it even cast shadows of objects and could be seen in a clear sky well into the day; even by me, though I have weaker eyesight <note>Kepler suffered from permanently impaired vision and double vision due to a childhood bout of smallpox.</note>.

<margin>
**The same happened this year, 1606, in the month of April with Venus; and in June with Mars at its acronychal rising, when the new star had long since disappeared.**
</margin>

  While the astronomers and those knowledgeable in these matters were looking toward the West in the evening, the common folk—both the busy and those curious about the heavens, and even the self-proclaimed wise—dragging one another along, flocked to the marketplaces in the morning hours everywhere, especially in Vienna. Astonished, they pointed their fingers at the star in the highest reaches of the air before the Sun, and like fools, they kept their minds and eyes occupied with the "foolish" Venus. This matter even crept into many of the ordinary news-sheets <note>original: *tabellas novorum*, referring to early printed news pamphlets or "broadsides" that preceded modern newspapers.</note>; and since it occasionally happened through the diligence of the common people,
**at its acronychal rising <term>acronychal rising: when a planet rises in the East just as the Sun sets in the West, making it visible all night long</term>, when the new star had long since disappeared.**

<meta>This page continues Kepler's detailed description of the supernova of 1604, moving from its astronomical position to its physical appearance and the way it compared to the brightness of Venus.</meta>

->**ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT BEARER, Page 5**<-

it happened that this brightest star, Venus, was seen emerging more and more from the rays of the Sun from the first sunrise, until she herself set a few hours before the Sun (for they were setting in the sign of short descents <note>This refers to the seasonal timing of the planets' setting in the zodiac</note>). This was carefully noted and celebrated as a primary omen.

As for the astronomers, I know of no one who saw this new star during the day. For it did not match Venus in size. Nevertheless, certain serious men told me of something wonderful, like a beam <note>original: *trabis*; a historical term for a long, rectangular atmospheric phenomenon or meteor</note>, seen in the sky during the day, which after some hours, following the daily motion of the heavens, sank behind the mountains.

Regarding the appearance <margin>Appearance of the star</margin> of the star, all who watched its first rising agree: it was exactly round, with no "hair," no "beard," or tail <note>Kepler is using classical terminology to distinguish the star from a comet</note> projected in any direction. For this reason, it was not to be numbered among any kind of "Hairy Stars" <term>Crinitarum: the classical category for comets</term>, neither the "Bearded Stars" <term>Pogoniae</term> nor the Comets; but it was most similar to the fixed stars, with rays flashing out from every side just like those of fixed stars. Its sparkling was most bright, its flashing or vibration so rapid, that some denied they had ever seen anything in the heavens in their lifetime with such swift motion—as they themselves put it. 

Different people expressed this with various comparisons; for since its color was as much a reddish-gold <note>original: *rutilo*</note> as it was near the horizon and deeper in the atmospheric vapors, some called it the appearance of a distant fire rising. Others more beautifully compared it to a torch tossed by the wind; because, just as in such a torch the continuity of the flames is interrupted by the force of the winds, and as soon as the first are barely pushed away or extinguished, new ones succeed them: so also this star now stretched out its rays very far, and quicker than a word could be said, sucked them back into itself, while simultaneously expanding them with a new start, in a restless back-and-forth. Our Austrians said, in an even more elegant local idiom, that the star was "laboring" <note>original: *laborare*; here meaning to be in distress or intense struggle</note> most vehemently: this word alludes to a feverish pulse, to the struggle of a fighter just about to be overcome, or to one fallen into the water, or someone gasping their last breath. For just as these people are accustomed to twitch with their arms and feet, so this star also shook its trembling point of light.

To me, who looked especially for the variety of colors <margin>Colors of the star</margin> of the rainbow, which it poured out according to one or another state of its sparkling; it pleased me to use the example of a multi-angled Diamond, which, while being turned, reflects the rays of the Sun to the eyes of the onlookers with a variable splendor. At the same time, I wanted to allude to the cause of sparkling that I brought forward in my *Optics* <note>Kepler refers to his 1604 work *Astronomiae Pars Optica*, where he discussed the physics of light and twinkling</note>.

Regarding the colors, Maestlin <note>Michael Maestlin, Kepler’s university professor and mentor</note> is clearly in agreement with me: that they vary from moment to moment, from yellow soon to saffron, immediately to purple and red, and appearing mostly white when it is raised a little higher out of the atmospheric vapors.

<summary>Kepler describes the visual characteristics of the 1604 supernova, noting its intense twinkling and color changes, while distinguishing it from a comet based on its lack of a "tail" or "hair." He compares its restless flashing to a wind-blown torch and a "laboring" pulse, and uses the optical properties of a diamond as a scientific analogy.</summary>

...remained.
Therefore, Crabb’s <note>Paul Krabbé, a contemporary observer whose data Johannes Kepler and others found highly suspect.</note> vanity must be immense; from the most flawed observations, which were deceitfully arranged into a specific order, he dared to attribute an extremely slow motion to our star <note>The "New Star" or Supernova of 1604, which appeared to be stationary to most observers.</note>. This alleged motion, over the entire duration of the star's appearance, did not even complete as large an arc <term>arc: the measurement of a path across the sky</term> as Crabb claimed it did on the very first day, the 4th of October.

<meta>This page continues Kepler’s analysis of the new star of 1604 (now known as Kepler's Supernova), specifically critiquing the observations of others and documenting the star's fading visibility as it approached the Sun.</meta>

->**ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT BEARER. 7**<-

<note>The text begins mid-sentence, continuing from the previous page's discussion of observation errors.</note> ...erred in his observation of October, when he reported it at 17° 0' of **Sagittarius**, whereas **Fabricius** <note>David Fabricius, a German astronomer and correspondent of Kepler</note> in East Frisia found it on the preceding day at 17° 37' or 17° 40' of **Sagittarius**. If he had only shared his observations with us, however imperfect, he would have earned our gratitude. But because he commends them so highly for their "certainty" that he relies on them to assign a minimal motion to the star, he shall rightly be criticized as a deceiver. For what do you think would become of Astronomy if—may God forbid—a wretched posterity were to place the same faith in his observations concerning the **Planets** as he himself affects? Therefore, I feel pity for **Herlicius** <note>David Herlicius, an astronomer and physician</note> who, even now while the author is still living, has allowed himself to be imposed upon, believing that this was a mobile **Comet**. If he built his prognostications <note>Astrological predictions based on the star's appearance</note> upon this motion, he has wasted both his oil and his labor <note>original: "oleum & operam perdidit"; a Latin idiom for wasting time and resources</note>, and he owes very little thanks to **Crabbus**.

When motion is removed, **parallax** <term>the apparent shift of an object against a distant background when viewed from different positions</term> is also taken away; for parallax, as the name indicates, is a certain apparent motion—an apparent shift of a celestial body that is, in itself, immobile. And once parallax is removed, no place is left for the star among the **Planets**; certainly none remains below the **Moon**, **Venus**, **Mercury**, the **Sun**, or **Mars**. 

Indeed, although the parallaxes of **Jupiter** and **Saturn** are imperceptible, it is proven by other arguments that the star did not even stand near them.

It was seen throughout the entire month of **October** with almost equal magnitude, except that as it approached the rays of the **Sun**, with **Jupiter** withdrawing somewhat and fleeing the **Sun**, the star's perceived size seemed to diminish when compared to **Jupiter**. In the month of **November**, it became known to many more people. Indeed, on October 30 or November 9 <note>Kepler provides dates in both the Julian (Old Style) and Gregorian (New Style) calendars</note>, a quarter hour before the fifth hour, I saw the new star after sunset, when I could not yet find **Jupiter**, so bright was it even then, although it was much closer to the rays of the **Sun** than **Jupiter** was.

On November 7/17, I saw it for the last time in the evening. For the following days were cloudy; however, **Brengger** <note>Johann Georg Brengger, a physician and astronomer</note> still observed it from **Jupiter** on November 8/18.

On November 12/22, when the location of the star was marked by the presence of the **Moon**, we were nevertheless unable to see the star itself here. Yet the **Moon** had not covered it, as the Moon's visible latitude <note>The angular distance north or south of the ecliptic</note> was three degrees and more, while the star did not have a latitude of even two degrees.

Therefore, in **Prague**, between November 7/17 and 12/22, it underwent its <margin>The heliacal / setting of / the star.</margin> **heliacal setting** <term>when a star becomes lost in the brightness of the sun's glare</term>. However, **Crestinus** asserts that in **Turin** in **Savoy**, where the elevation of the pole <note>The latitude of the observer</note> is lower, it could still be seen on November 13/23 by those who possessed especially sharp eyesight.

From November 26/December 6, at the beginning, until December 8/18, we observed **Mercury**; certainly on the 7/17th it was clearly visible. For its rising was further to the left,

8 &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;->**JOHANNES KEPLER**<-

later than the Sun; but within half an hour after its rising, it hid itself under the light of the twilight. 
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;On the day $\frac{8}{18}$ of December <note>Kepler uses dual dating for the Julian and Gregorian calendars, which were both in use across Europe at this time.</note>, it is uncertain whether I saw Mercury or the new star; except that if it had been that new star, Mercury—which is more evident and not far distant—ought to have met my eyes at the same time; and because a new star could hardly have risen so close to the Sun, and because the usual sparkling of new stars <note>original: "scintillatio"</note> did not alert me. Whichever it was, it chose a rising point further to the right than Mercury did on the $\frac{7}{17}$ day. After that, the days were cloudy until the Vigil of the Nativity of Christ, the $\frac{14}{24}$ of December: when, with Arcturus still appearing though the twilight was quite advanced, I noticed a sparkling star on that vertical line which marked the rising point of the Sun on the $\frac{7}{17}$ day. It sat at an altitude of several degrees, mostly hidden in the light of the twilight, yet with the flashes of its sparkling occasionally shining forth, it repeatedly struck my gaze, so much so that I could imagine a line from Venus through it, which seemed to lead to that place on the horizon where that doubtful star was seen to rise on the $\frac{8}{18}$ day.

<margin>*Heliacal rising of the star.*</margin> <note>A heliacal rising is the first time a celestial object becomes visible in the eastern sky at dawn after being obscured by the Sun's glare for a period of time.</note>

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;In the year 1605, on December 24th or January 3rd, after a long period of darkness, the clouds were driven away and the new star clearly appeared again beyond any doubt, sparkling very rapidly, but much diminished from that former magnitude. It was still larger than the heart of the Scorpion <note>The star Antares</note>, which is of the first magnitude <term>magnitude: a measurement of a star's brightness</term>, even though it was itself more overwhelmed by the rays of the Sun than the heart of the Scorpion was. I do not know if it was because it was outside the Sun's rays, but Arcturus seemed much larger. However, on the $\frac{3}{13}$ day of January, it was thought to be larger than Arcturus and Saturn, with no trace of twilight yet. Saturn had then risen heliacally three days prior. For ten days before, it had not yet come forth.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;From that time on, it was diminished more and more. For when I was intent on observation on the 20th day of March, the star seemed to be much smaller than Saturn, nor did it much exceed the magnitude of the stars in the knees of Ophiuchus <note>The "Serpent Bearer" constellation</note>, which are of the third rank <note>third magnitude</note>.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;On the 21st day of April, it seemed to equal the bright star in the right knee of Ophiuchus. From then on, this decrease of its brightness stayed somewhat consistent through May and June.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;On the 12th and 14th of August, having carefully contemplated the surrounding stars, I judged the new star to be equal to the star in the shin <note>original: "Tibia"</note> of Ophiuchus, which is very close; this star is also considered to be of the third rank, yet it is smaller than the star in the knee.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;The New Star still sparkled more clearly than the others.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;On the 29th of August, I indeed declared the New Star larger than the shin star...

->**ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT BEARER. 9**<-

...differentiate it <note>The OCR begins mid-sentence; Kepler is likely discussing the difficulty of distinguishing the star's exact magnitude</note> I could not, on account of the brightness of the summer nights; nevertheless, it could be seen better than the star in the Shin <note>original: "Tibia," referring to the star Eta Ophiuchi</note>, aided by its flickering.
   On September 13th, it appeared smaller than the one in the Shin.
   On October 8th, with a full year having now passed since its first appearance, it could only be perceived with difficulty even in the clearest sky; yet it was still better than the Shin—though not always, but only when it flashed. For by then, that region was whitening with the light of the evening twilight as the Sun approached the location. From this time, it again set heliacally <term>heliacal setting: the period when a star becomes lost in the Sun's glare as the Sun approaches its position in the sky</term>, forty days earlier than the year before. From this, one can understand how great its brightness and magnitude had been in the beginning.
   In the year 1606, in the month of January, there was rarely an opportunity for a clear morning sky, especially near the horizon, even though the winter was remarkable and the frost continuous.
   Nevertheless, on Saturday <note>original: "♄", the symbol for Saturn, used here for Saturday</note>, January 3rd, I happened to see the location. The right knee was visible, as was the curve of the serpent’s coil, and the bright star in the palm of the right foot of Ophiuchus <note>The "Serpent Bearer" constellation</note>; two of these were positioned just above the New Star, and the latter just below it. Yet I could not see the New Star at all, nor the one in the Shin nearest to the right of the New Star; at the same moment, the light of the morning twilight was kindled. Saturn had its heliacal rising <term>heliacal rising: the first day a planet or star becomes visible in the dawn after having been hidden by the Sun's light</term>.
   On January 25th (or February 4th) <note>Kepler often provides dates in both the Old Style/Julian and New Style/Gregorian calendars</note> in the morning, I saw the location of the New Star, but nothing of the New Star itself; however, the Moon was present, though not yet joined with Saturn.
   On January 27th (or February 6th), being uncertain whether I had seen a flickering trace of the New Star, I left the observatory. It appeared, therefore, to have become so small that it could not be seen in this brightness of dawn, even if it still remained. But in the month of March, after the location was so far removed from the Sun that it rose high enough above the horizon before the start of twilight, <margin>The disappearance of the star.</margin> no further trace of the star remained that could strike the eye. It is therefore uncertain on which day between October 1605 and February 1606 it was extinguished.
   It is memorable that it was visited by all the Planets. <margin>Configuration with the Planets.</margin>
   At its first emergence, it was joined as closely as possible to Jupiter and Mars, which were lower toward the south, forming the shape of an obtuse triangle; on October 12th and 13th, through the motion of the planets, this changed into a straight line. On October 25th, the Moon passed above it, and this was repeated in each of the following months. However, it never covered <note>original: "texit," meaning occulted</note> the location of the star. For at this time the ascending Node <term>Node: the point where the Moon's orbit crosses the ecliptic or the path of the Sun</term> was in Libra, moving further and further from the location of the star, so that each subsequent month showed the Moon further north in Sagittarius. These are the days of the conjunctions: in the year 1604, October 25th, November 22nd, December 20th. In the year 1605...

10 ->**JOHANNES KEPLER**<-
1605: January 16, February 12, March 11, April 8, May 5, June 2 & 29, July 26, August 23, September 19, October 16, November 13, December 10. In the year 1606: January 6, February 3.
     But let us also look at the other planets. The Sun, therefore, comes to this place annually on the tenth of December (or November 30 according to the Julian calendar). <note>original: "stylo Juliano"</note> Thus, in the year 1604 it found Saturn there, and in 1605 the Moon; and so the new moon of December 1605 was remarkable because it coincided with the position of the star. <note>Kepler is referring to the "New Star" or Supernova of 1604.</note>
     Saturn reached a point very close to the star on December 11, 1604, though passing somewhat below it; this was clearly visible to the eye on the following January 13. During the months of July, August, and September of 1605, retracing its previous steps, it completed its station <note>The "station" is the point where a planet appears to stand still before changing direction in the sky.</note> very near the star, being almost as far from the New Star to the left as the Shin of Ophiuchus <note>The constellation of the Serpent-Bearer.</note> was to the right in the same straight line. This was intersected crosswise by another straight line, drawn from above from the coil of the Serpent, which passed behind the right knee of Ophiuchus, almost through the New Star, to the palm of Ophiuchus’s foot. With these five stars, therefore, a cross was formed, whose longer beam, or base, was turned upward, so that the New Star was almost at the intersection of the lines, or at the center of the cross.
     After Saturn, Mercury passed this place on December 13, 1604, and exactly a year later on December 17.
     The last was Venus, which passed above the New Star on January 21, 1605; and since Saturn was found in the same place, it offered a beautiful spectacle to observers. In the year 1605, on November 12, it returned to the same spot, but then it passed below.
     This, then, is the true and certain history of this most celebrated star, to confirm and illustrate which I shall add the following few chapters.

->**On the Fiery Trigon and initially on the first meaning of the word Trigon.**<-

->**CHAPTER TWO**<-

**A**lthough astrology contains much vanity and is for the most part unworthy of having good hours spent upon it, yet because this "disease" <note>Kepler often expressed skepticism toward popular astrology while still practicing it for income and searching for deeper physical harmonies.</note> is not confined to a few, but pervades the greater part of the human race, it would seem that one who teaches the mathematical arts without mentioning it speaks very little for common use. I cannot, therefore, refrain from explaining...

<meta>This page continues the discussion from the previous section regarding the astronomical and astrological history of the "new star" (the supernova of 1604). The text begins mid-word with the conclusion of "applicationem" (application).</meta>

**ON THE FIERY TRIGON. 11**

...application of the history already handed down concerning the new star, I may begin from the commemoration of astrological traditions.

You may see a praiseworthy example in that most learned of Latin philosophers, Gaius Pliny <note>Pliny the Elder, author of *Natural History*.</note>; who, the more he is free from astrological superstition, the more he draws from the records of the Astrologers throughout his work.

For I must state from the beginning what sort of marvel the **Fiery Trigon** <term>Fiery Trigon: A grouping of three zodiac signs—Aries, Leo, and Sagittarius—which astrologers associate with the element of fire.</term> is, whose conjunction with the new star is celebrated everywhere as such a miracle. This must be deduced from the decrees of the Astrologers, so that those who are to read these things—statesmen and men of affairs—may not complain that they are left wanting in any part. It must also be examined by the level of truth, so that trifles may be separated from the nature of things. Let the six hundred astrologers who have written about the Fiery Trigon over these last four years—specifically that Herlicius <note>David Herlicius (1557–1636), a famous German physician and astrologer who wrote extensively on the 1604 supernova.</note>, the most eloquent and famous professor of Astrology—let them have, I say, what someone might judge of their writings who cannot be accused of ignorance of the art.

Indeed, if it is for the common people to judge who is the better astrologer, let them know I stand very well; but if they choose the judgment of the learned, they themselves have long since fallen. Whether they stand with me before the common people, or I lie with them before the learned: on both sides I am of their order, I am their equal, and I should not be rejected; certainly not by Herlicius, who has praised me publicly. Although the greatest consideration here must be given to most learned men, who, not being so well-versed in Mathematics, frequently ask: What is the Fiery Trigon naturally, or is it **something established by convention?** <note>original: *thetikón ti* (Greek: θετικόν τι). This refers to whether the Trigon is a physical reality or just a human-made classification.</note>

**Trigon** in Greek <note>original: *Trigonos*</note>; **Triangle** in Latin <note>original: *Triangulus*</note>, is a geometric term signifying a figure very well known to everyone. In Astrology, there are two or three other notions of this word. First, when the rays of two planets meet on earth in such a way that they intercept precisely one third part of a circle described from the point of meeting; this relationship of two rays is among the number of "aspects," and it is called a **Trigon** or **Trine aspect**. The reason for the name is that if a third planet were added at that same interval from the rays of the other two, casting its own ray to the common intersection, and if the points of the circle—divided by the three rays—were connected by straight lines, a genuine **equilateral Triangle** would be created in the geometric sense.

<margin>Diagram: A circle containing an inscribed equilateral triangle with a dot at its center. A vertical line extends from the top vertex of the triangle to a small star above the circle. A small asterisk is positioned to the left of the circle.</margin>

Here, however, the Astrologers—so they would not always be forced to describe a circle from the point where rays meet—used a shortcut. For the point of meeting, they use the center of the world and of the zodiac itself; for the circle to be described, they use the zodiac already described in the heavens, under which all the planets travel.

->**B 2**<- **elege-** <note>Catchword for the next page: "elegerunt" (they chose).</note>

<meta>This page continues Johannes Kepler’s discussion on the nature of astrological aspects, specifically the "Trine" (Trigonus), shifting from the geometric definition to how the term is applied to groupings of zodiac signs.</meta>

12 JOHANNES KEPLER

...they chose. Therefore, although the thing itself—namely, the <term>Trine</term> <note>A "Trine" or "Trigon" is an astrological aspect where two planets are 120 degrees apart, forming a third of a circle.</note>—exists at the point where the rays intersect at the center of the earth, its value is nevertheless realized in the highest ether. It is as if all the Planets were passing through that most exalted circle with their physical bodies, just as they appear to our sight.

Since, therefore, the name "Trine" was adopted for the radiation of the Planets (that is, for the positions of two Planets in the zodiac—the thing itself and the measure of that thing), this also seems to be the origin of the word **aspect**. This term is a figure of personification <note>original: "Prosopopoeia"</note>, in which "eyes" and "mutual sight" are attributed to parts of the zodiac that are separated by equal spaces around the circle. This occurs even if the Planets are merely imagined to be lifted from their lower orbits into these parts of the zodiac by the deception of our vision.

And this category of things—namely, the aspect of two stars where either both or at least one is moving—is essentially the only part of Astrology that I believe should be retained. I defend this point with great effort against those Philosophers who are entirely ignorant of the art, using philosophical arguments and the <term>doctrine of harmony</term> <note>Kepler’s "Harmonic" theory suggests that the universe is governed by the same geometric ratios that create musical scales.</note> (which is almost totally unknown to them). With that same confidence, I judge that almost all the rest of the Astrologers’ equipment should be discarded, a point I protest unceasingly in all my astrological writings.

### CHAPTER THREE. ###

From this astrological notion of the word "Triangle," which is its primary origin, the other meanings have flowed. For in the second place, this word was transferred from specific points in the zodiac to its entire sections, separated by that same interval. Since Astrologers divide the zodiac into twelve equal parts—starting from the intersection of the zodiac's center line with the line midway between the poles of the world <note>The celestial equator; the intersection refers to the vernal equinox.</note>, which the Sun passes through in the springtime—the names of these parts are:
->**Aries, Taurus, Gemini, Cancer, Leo, Virgo,**<-
->**Libra, Scorpio, Sagittarius, Capricorn, Aquarius, Pisces.**<-

Therefore, when these twelve are divided by three (because three points are required to form a triangle), the zodiac is said to have four **Trigons**, which they also call **Triplicities**.

<meta>This page continues a mathematical or astronomical treatise, specifically moving into the "Fire Triangle" (the first triplicity of the zodiac). The author transitions from naming conventions to a philosophical argument regarding the nature of astrological influences.</meta>

# **ON THE FIRE TRIANGLE.** **13**

The first is of Aries, Leo, Sagittarius;
The second of Taurus, Virgo, Capricorn;
The third of Gemini, Libra, Aquarius;
The fourth of Cancer, Scorpio, Pisces.
Here you see that between Aries and Leo there are three intervening parts: Taurus, Gemini, and Cancer; likewise between Leo and Sagittarius there are three: Virgo, Libra, and Scorpio; finally, there are just as many between Sagittarius and Aries, namely Capricorn, Aquarius, and Pisces—and so it is for all the rest. Therefore, if these parts are arranged in a circle and connected with straight lines, a perfect triangle will be created.

From the names of the individual parts comes the name "sign," common to all twelve parts. Therefore, that circle is called the "circle of signs," or in Greek, the *zodiac* <note>original: "ζωδιακός" (zōdiakos)</note>. Moreover, they distinguish the four "triplicities" <term>triplicity: a group of three zodiac signs belonging to the same element, forming a triangle on the zodiac wheel</term> by the names of the four elements. The first they call fiery; the second, earthy; the third, airy; the fourth, watery.

This entire system of division and naming should be retained in mathematics <note>The author uses "Mathematics" in the classical sense, which included the study of astronomy and the movements of the heavens</note> not only because it is very ancient, but also because it is necessary for memory and very convenient in practice. For even if these names were completely rooted out from human minds along with astronomy itself, those who came after—should they wish to restore astronomy among men—would be forced to use some similar system to distinguish the parts of the heavens by name.

Therefore, certain people are shamelessly foolish and foolishly shameless when they abuse their eloquence to summon the common people into the open air to see if they can find animals, fire, or water in the sky with their own eyes; or whether they wish to claim that astronomers and astrologers alike (making no distinction between the guilty and the innocent, or between necessary utility and superstition) are all insane to a man.

But when the question is asked whether, just like the names, a corresponding natural power <note>The author is referring to the "virtue" or physical influence of the stars</note> is also distributed among these parts of the zodiac—or whether nature or human choice invented these distinctions—here the Chaldeans, Indians, Egyptians, Arabs, Greeks, and Latin astrologers say "yes." However, the philosophers of all ages and times say "no," and I stand with them. Among the strongest reasons why I deny this is that those who affirm it rely solely on the persuasion of antiquity. Since they can find no logical reasons, they take refuge in the idea that it is unbelievable for this naming system to lack a natural foundation, since it is so ancient and has been celebrated as natural throughout all centuries. Therefore, so that the reason why I deny it, and the caution regarding how far I deny it,

->**Whether the division of the Zodiac into twelve equal parts is Natural.**<-

***

### CHAPTER FOUR.

In the beginning, I shall speak concerning the cause of the <term>duodecimal division</term> <note>A division into twelve parts.</note>: where I do not deny that several occasions were provided by the nature of things, by which the judgment of the Inventors was advised, and introduced this precise twelve-fold division. Of these occasions, I find three in particular.

The first is supplied by the proportion of the Sun's motion to the Moon's motion. For while the Sun traverses the zodiac once, the Moon returns to it nearly twelve times; yet in such a way that at the third revolution of the Sun, she adds nearly a thirty-seventh revolution.

Therefore, the zodiac is divided into twelve equal parts as closely as possible by the twelve meetings <note>original: "congressibus" — astronomical conjunctions where the Sun and Moon align.</note> of the Moon with the Sun.

Almost the same occasion existed for dividing any given sign into thirty degrees: for between two meetings of the Moon with the Sun—which, as already stated, are distant by nearly one sign—almost thirty days pass by.

Since, therefore, in former times after the distribution of the human race across the globe, few were learned in letters, and so they lacked the convenience of diaries <note>Kepler refers here to printed almanacs or calendars.</note>, which today even the lowest common people use—especially in the last hundred years—nor were holidays always announced from the pulpit, by whose annual recurrence the peasants might be advised of their rustic labors (which has been the practice in the Church for these thousand years), since they lived scattered and met together rather rarely: Since men lacked these conveniences, it is credible that the knowledge of the number of days in the natural year belonged to very few. And although there were always, and for all nations, their own holidays and solemnities; for the most part they were moveable, like our Easter, and were shifted back and forth with the <term>lunations</term> <note>The periods between new moons.</note>: therefore they were not sufficient for annual farming tasks.

For these reasons, farmers were necessarily driven to contemplate the rising of the fixed stars; and there existed among them precepts of this discipline handed down by tradition <note>original: "per manus tradita" — literally "passed through hands."</note>, long before they were recorded in writing by Hesiod <note>An early Greek poet whose work *Works and Days* provided agricultural advice based on the stars.</note> and others.

From this rustic discipline, by what reasoning the number of signs flowed, easily [it is shown...]

<summary>Kepler explains that the division of the zodiac into twelve signs and thirty degrees is based on the natural cycles of the Sun and Moon, providing a necessary calendar for ancient farmers who lacked modern almanacs.</summary>
<keywords>zodiac, lunar cycle, solar cycle, calendar history, farming, Hesiod, degrees, duodecimal division</keywords>

--- Page 31 ---
# ON THE FIERY TRIGON. 15

It is easy to observe. For whether they looked at the full Moon, the new Moon, the waning Moon, or finally—which is more convenient—the quarters, it was easy to see how, starting for example from the horns of Aries <note>The "horns of Aries" refers to the starting stars of the constellation Aries.</note>, the quarter of the second month stood near the horns or the eye of Taurus; that of the third near the stars of Gemini; that of the fourth near the faint stars of Cancer; and the rest stood near the remaining constellations. Thus, the entire circuit of the zodiac was marked in twelve places by the presence of these quarters within the span of a year.

But since it was clear that this recurrence of quarters did not establish exactly twelve parts (for as the year revolved, the locations of the lunar quarters changed, creating signs of a new division), the precise twelve-fold division was nevertheless retained, and its starting point remained the same and constant forever.

Alfraganus and Alcabitius <note>original: "Alfraganus, & Alcabitius" — refers to Ahmad ibn Muhammad ibn Kathir al-Farghani and Al-Qabisi, influential 9th and 10th-century astronomers whose works were translated into Latin.</note> cite this as the reason for this fact, which I mention as the second cause. The Sun, they say, distinguishes the circuit of the zodiac into four parts by four points, or notable boundaries, which are perpetually the same. For there is the solstitial point, where the Sun is at its highest; the winter point <note>original: "brumale"</note> where it is lowest; and the two equinoctial points, where it is midway between the poles of the world. At the first, it makes the days longest or shortest; at the second, it makes them equal to the nights. At these four points, it brings back the four great seasons of the year: Spring, Summer, Autumn, and Winter. All of these are natural and do not depend on human choice.

As to why each of these four quadrants of the zodiac should be further divided into three signs, so that there are twelve signs in total, Alfraganus gives this reason: that it is agreeable to nature and accepted by usage to distinguish any great thing into a beginning, a middle, and an end.

The third cause or occasion for the precise twelve-fold division is taken from a comparison of the Trine aspect <note>An astrological aspect where planets are 120 degrees apart.</note> with the Square aspect <note>An aspect where planets are 90 degrees apart.</note>.

For experience (which shows the effect) and harmonic ratio (which explains the cause) agree on this: that when two planets are seen to be apart by a third of the zodiac circle (of which we spoke above in Chapter 2), or a fourth, or a sixth, a great commotion occurs in sublunary nature <note>The "sublunary" realm refers to the Earth and its atmosphere, which medieval science believed was influenced by the movements of the stars and planets.</note>. Therefore, assuming Saturn to be at the start of Aries as if stationary, when the Sun has moved away from it so that it is first a sixth, then a fourth, and finally a third of the circle away, it will mark out the twelve parts of the circle at these three locations. For the difference between a sixth and a fourth part of the whole, as well as between a fourth and a third, is in both cases a twelfth part of the whole. In Gemini, the Sun will make a sextile <note>An angle of 60 degrees.</note> with Saturn; in Cancer, a square; in Leo, a trine. Thus, these two aspects provide an occasion for dividing the zodiac into twelve parts, though they do not show a certain starting point.

<margin>
[A geometric diagram depicting a circle with an inscribed triangle and square. The lines connect various points to illustrate the mathematical relationship between the 60, 90, and 120-degree angles discussed in the text.]
</margin>

<meta>This page continues Kepler’s critique of traditional astrological foundations, specifically refuting the arguments of the Persian astrologer Abu Ma'shar regarding the division of the zodiac.</meta>

16 **JOHANNES KEPLER**

->Albumasar<- <note>Abu Ma'shar (787–886 AD) was a leading Persian astrologer whose works were foundational to medieval European astrology.</note> alleges a fourth, though futile, reason: that the Ancients discovered forty-eight images in the sky, and since twelve of these fall within the zodiac, the zodiac was divided into that many parts. To begin my refutation from this last point: in ancient times, only eleven images fell within the zodiac. For those stars we now attribute to **Libra** <note>The Scales</note> were once counted as the **Claws** of the **Scorpion**. Therefore, let Albumasar hear this: the twelve parts of the zodiac were not established because twelve images of constellations had been found; rather, the stars in the vicinity of the zodiac were distributed into precisely twelve groups because the twelve-fold division of the zodiac had already been accepted for other reasons. Furthermore, the image of a constellation does not "make" a sign. For some are extended beyond a twelfth part of the circuit, and others are contracted. 

The <term>Signs</term> <note>In astrology, "Signs" are ideal 30-degree mathematical divisions, whereas "Constellations" are the actual groups of stars which vary in size.</note> of the astrologers are measured so minutely into twelve sections that if Jupiter is at 29 degrees, 59 minutes, and 59 seconds of **Gemini**, it is considered to be in its <term>detriment</term> <note>A position where a planet's influence is thought to be weakened.</note>, having earned five "black stones" <note>An ancient method of voting or scoring where black stones represented negative marks and white stones positive ones.</note>. Yet after a single minute of time, having made the transition into 0 degrees, 0 minutes, and 1 second of **Cancer**, it is suddenly happy, now in its <term>exaltation</term> <note>A position where a planet's influence is thought to be at its most powerful.</note>, enjoying four "white stones"—if it please the gods! <note>Kepler is being deeply sarcastic here about the arbitrary precision of astrological rules.</note> 

So much for this fourth reason of Albumasar’s. Let us also speak of the three previous ones, so it may be clear that they do not prove that the twelve signs are distinguished by true and natural boundaries or by various distinct qualities. Regarding the first and third reasons, it is not difficult to prove my point, since they do not establish constant starting points for this division. For example, if the 19th degree of **Sagittarius** were to obtain a power of "dryness" from a conjunction of the luminaries <note>The Sun and the Moon</note>, then after a few months, a full moon falling in that same 19th degree of Sagittarius would instead infuse it with a power of "moistening." The same can be said of the <term>aspects</term> <note>The geometric angles between planets.</note> of the planets, which they carry with their bodies across the entire circuit. 

Since the astrologers’ signs of the zodiac are said to have fixed starting points, these reasons contribute nothing to them—except for this one thing: they invited the first astronomers from afar toward the precise number twelve. Those astronomers then fully embraced that number, along with the thirty-fold subdivision <note>Dividing each of the 12 signs into 30 degrees to make 360.</note>, following not the nature of the motions (which differed somewhat), but their own choice and the magnitude of the number 360 arising from geometry, guided by a hidden instinct and manifest reason. Indeed, what I have said regarding the inconstant boundary of the division also applies to the second reason; for even the Sun shifts its own starting point—namely the first point of **Aries**—beneath the sphere of the fixed stars, which is the natural sphere. <note>Kepler is referring to the precession of the equinoxes, where the position of the stars slowly shifts relative to the Earth's seasonal calendar.</note> Thus, astrologers do not divide the exact circuit itself, but something slightly larger than the zodiac, into twelve parts; just as the quarters mentioned above do, dividing something shorter than the whole...

<meta>This page continues a discussion on the Fiery Trigon—a group of zodiac signs (Aries, Leo, and Sagittarius) associated with the element of fire. The author appears to be Johannes Kepler or a contemporary, debating whether the zodiac signs have inherent physical powers or if their effects are purely geometrical and harmonic.</meta>

## ON THE FIERY TRIGON. 17

...against all three causes, it can be said: that even if we were to concede that all lunations <note>The cycles of the moon from new moon to new moon.</note> return to the exact same spot after a full year—likewise all solstices and all aspects <note>The geometric angles between planets, such as a square or a trine.</note>—it would still be incredible to suggest that a power is infused into the specific location of the zodiac by whatever passes beneath it. Suppose the 19th degree of **Sagittarius** is "dry" when a New Moon occurs there, or the 4th degree of **Capricorn** is "humid" when the Full Moon is there. Let **Capricorn** be "wintry" when it holds the Sun, and **Cancer** "summery" from the presence of that same star. I will even concede more: let **Cancer** be more "prolific" when it hosts **Jupiter** as a guest than **Capricorn** is, thanks to the benefit of that same **Jupiter**. This suggests not only that the stars themselves strike more forcefully from on high than from a slope, but also that sublunary <term>sublunary: relating to the Earth and its atmosphere, which ancient science believed was subject to change, unlike the "unchanging" heavens</term> nature itself, out of a kind of reverence, shows itself more manageable and submissive to **Cancer**. Finally, let there be an effective conjunction at 0 degrees of **Aries** and 0 degrees of **Cancer** when **Saturn** is in the former and the Sun is in the latter. But when these guests depart from those places, what remains in those parts of the zodiac of these "guests" by which the signs might be understood as naturally distinct from one another—whether by their boundaries or by the qualities of the parts lying between those boundaries? Or if something does remain, is it not wiped out when another planet of an opposite quality follows it?

For one cannot entirely deny that for a little while—for example—the 8th degree of **Sagittarius** might retain some power from the meeting of **Jupiter** and **Saturn** which occurred in that degree in the year 1603. However, this must be explained by a most abstruse <note>original: "abstruſiſſima"</note> harmonic principle, a concept drawn down from the heavens and introduced to the earth. By this reasoning, no natural quality is left to the sign of **Sagittarius** itself, and even this retained power vanishes again over time.

I recognize the cardinal points of the Sun <note>The solstices and equinoxes.</note> as natural boundaries, and I confess that the power of the Sun is exerted while it dwells in them; but I do so for a far different reason than what is relevant here. For astrologers believe that at the start of **Capricorn**, the "hot" parts of the zodiac are suddenly exchanged for "cold" ones, right at the very point of division. In truth, the Sun’s summer heat (for we are not questioning what the elements contribute here) decreases until this point, and from this peak <note>The winter solstice, which is the "peak" of the sun's descent.</note> it begins to increase again; thus, at the point itself, the change is most gradual. So, what is a "metropolis of nature" <note>A central, fundamental turning point in the natural cycle.</note> becomes, for astrologers, a border between two hostile regions.

That argument concerning the beginning, middle, and end is also not aptly transferred to the natural qualities of the parts of the zodiac. Every quantity, every arc, has a beginning and an end: but those boundaries are points; they are not "parts" of the thing itself. According to natural qualities, however, which themselves possess dimensions, parts certainly differ from parts, the extremes from the mid—

<margin>C</margin> <note>This "C" is a signature mark used by early printers to keep the pages in the correct order for binding.</note>

<summary>The author argues that zodiac signs do not have inherent physical qualities (like heat or moisture) but instead act as geometric markers for planetary movements and harmonic ratios. He specifically challenges the astrological idea that qualities like "hot" or "cold" switch instantly at the boundaries of signs like Capricorn.</summary>
