On the previous page, he likely discussed why the standard starting points of the seasons (the equinoxes and solstices) are often questioned by those seeking more "natural" divisions based on physical qualities like heat or cold.</meta>

18 JOHANNES KEPLER

...from the middle; but not by the forms of opposing qualities, but rather by degrees of the same quality. In this way, if they began the summer quadrant at 15° of Taurus, the autumn at 15° of Leo, and the others accordingly, they could certainly philosophize with more justification regarding the three parts of the quadrant—where the ends are mild and the middle is intense heat; however, they would still be restricted by the arguments I made above.

Therefore, to conclude: these causes merely provided opportunities for human choice to adopt this form of division. They were not taken from the nature of the thing being divided, nor do they express a natural division, but a purely geometric or mathematical one. Go then, you Astrologers, and look for other explanations!

***

->**Whether the names of the signs are arbitrary, or truly natural and meaningful.**<-
### CHAPTER FIVE.

I will speak, therefore, in the second place about the origin of the names by which the stars are designated. Indeed, there are great men who believe that the names of the constellations were taken from a "hidden property" <note>original: *occulta proprietate*; a technical term in Renaissance science referring to powers that cannot be observed directly but are known by their effects</note> in which these stars excel over others, and which were discovered for the purpose of examining the fates <note>original: *genesibus*; literally "nativities" or birth charts</note> of men. Those who hold this view also call upon those many thousands of years of the Indians, Chaldeans, and Egyptians to defend this opinion, so as to grant the ancients enough time to investigate these things. 

Although I use this argument in a different way than Pico <note>Giovanni Pico della Mirandola (1463–1494), a famous philosopher who wrote a massive critique of astrology</note>, I do not intend to strip astrologers of all experience in all their teachings as he does. Rather, I will show that these specific names did not come to the stars from experience, unless it is established—guided by experience as in other arts—that some names were also corrected and changed for the better over the course of centuries. 

It is well established from Cicero, Ptolemy, Manilius, and Firmicus <note>Firmicus Maternus, a 4th-century Roman lawyer and astrologer</note> that this system of naming the signs was not merely imported into the Roman Empire under the Emperors Augustus and Tiberius, but was received from ancient times along with sound knowledge. It is also quite clear from the Prophet Isaiah that under the Babylonian Monarchs—when eclipses first began to be recorded—astrology was practiced even to the point of public madness <note>original: *delirium*</note>. It became the mother of a primary form of idolatry, while men served the fictional spirits of the Planets under the pretext of "elections" <note>the practice of picking the most "auspicious" time to start a project based on the stars</note>. 

What shall I say of Moses? Some who read that he was "learned in all the wisdom of the Egyptians" say he excelled in astrology; this is confirmed by Lucian. And he was no more than 800 years removed from the Great Flood. And yet, also, a little...

<summary>Kepler concludes that the division of the zodiac is a human geometric invention rather than a natural one, and begins a historical investigation into whether constellation names reflect actual physical powers or are merely arbitrary.</summary>
<keywords>Zodiac divisions, Constellation names, Astrology history, Pico della Mirandola, Babylonian astrology, Egyptian wisdom, Moses</keywords>

<meta>This page continues a discussion from page 18 regarding the antiquity of star names, specifically arguing that the names of the constellations predate the complex astrological theories often associated with them.</meta>

### ON THE FIERY TRIGON. 19
<note>The "Fiery Trigon" refers to the group of three zodiac signs associated with the element of fire: Aries, Leo, and Sagittarius.</note>

A little while before, in the time of Saint Job, the names of certain stars had already become widespread. Therefore, the time available for establishing this science of the "effects" of the zodiac signs—and from that, deriving the shapes of the constellations—was quite short. Conversely, the period during which the ancient method of naming the stars has endured until now without any change is shown to be quite long. For what novelty did the diligence of the Arabs bring into this art that cannot be reduced to, and derived from, the ancient principles known from Ptolemy and Manilius? <note>Ptolemy (2nd century AD) and Manilius (1st century AD) were the foundational authorities on astronomy and astrology in the classical world.</note> Thus, by reasoning from these old principles and the names of the stars, rather than by testing new experiments, they expanded the discipline.

But let us hear on this matter from a man of that Arabic era, steeped in an astronomy that had only just been reborn from Arabic manuscripts.

"Human persuasion," says **Johannes de Sacrobosco**, <note>Johannes de Sacrobosco (c. 1195–1256) was a scholar whose textbook 'The Sphere' was the standard astronomical text for centuries.</note> "gave numbers and names to the stars, not out of their own inherent nature, but as a classification suited to their meaning," whence **Bernardus Silvestris** <note>A 12th-century philosopher and poet.</note> says:

*Lest general things should wander without a common name,* <note>original: "Communi ne voce res generalis oberrent"</note>
*Man created the names which the stars now possess.* <note>original: "Quæ modò sunt stellis nomina, fecit homo."</note>

Likewise **Virgil** in Book 1 of the *Georgics*:

*While the sailor gave numbers and names to the stars:* <note>original: "Navita dum stellis numeros & nomina fecit:"</note>
*The Pleiades, the Hyades, and the bright Bear of Lycaon.* <note>original: "Pleiades Hyades, Claramq; Lycaonis Arcton."</note>

Nay, let us even hear the more sensible of the Arabs themselves, especially **Abraham Avenezra**, <note>Abraham Ibn Ezra (c. 1089–1167), a celebrated Jewish scholar from Spain who wrote extensively on astrology in Hebrew and Arabic.</note> whose words saying this very thing may be seen in the works of Pico della Mirandola, Book 8, Chapter 15.

It is altogether to be believed that there were two kinds of men who organized the stars into specific forms: **Farmers** and **Sailors**. Concerning the farmers, who seem to be the older group, I have spoken above. For how could they understand that the Sun, having finished its cycle, had returned to its original place, or that the seasons were returning to positions near the original ones, unless a distinction was made between the stars and names were assigned to them? Nature paved the way for those who would necessarily do this; the stars are scattered through the <term>ultimate sphere</term> <note>The "outermost sphere" of the heavens in the geocentric model, where the fixed stars reside.</note> in no permanent order—that is, confusedly—so that men would not be deceived by a similarity of arrangement in different places. From the farmers, therefore, we get **Aries** (the Ram), **Taurus** (the Bull), **Virgo** the grain-bearer, **Capricorn** (the Goat), **Boötes** (the Herdsman or Driver), **Heniochus** (the Charioteer), **Capella** (the Little Goat), the **Kids**, the **Wagon** or the **Bear** (names already known to Homer), and the like. From the sailors, truly, come the **Pleiades**, the **Ship** <note>The constellation Argo Navis.</note>—a constellation on the southern horizon, which is to say, touching the mountains or the sea whenever it is seen—and the like. From both groups, we get the **Hyades**. If any other names seem to depart further from things familiar to these two types of men, they were certainly taken from common daily use, from things striking the eyes, such as the **Hunter** (Orion), the **Wild Beast**, the **Hare**, the **Dog**, and the **Serpent**.

# 20 JOHANNES KEPLER

<margin>Causes for the naming of the stars.</margin>
<margin>The arrangement of the stars.</margin>
If I may be permitted to indulge in conjecture, there were two primary reasons why a specific choice of images and names was made—among the many that could have been imposed on a single constellation based on the nature of things: the physical arrangement of the stars, and the time of year that was opportune for either nautical or rustic labors. Indeed, anyone who gazes at the stars of the Bear, the Serpent-bearer <note>original: "Serpentarij," now commonly known by the Greek name Ophiuchus</note>, the Serpent, the Hydra, Orion, the Hare, the Dog, the Bull, the Twins, the Crab, the Lion, the wings in the Virgin, the Scorpion, the Arrow and the bow, the Hen <note>original: "Gallinæ," the constellation Cygnus (the Swan)</note>, the Raven, the Crown, the Triangle, and many other figures—especially those stars which in our own memory have been added from the southern sky through the industry of the Spaniards and the Dutch—sees a certain similarity in their arrangement to the limbs of the animals and the objects from which they take their names. He sees this more clearly in the actual sky than on a globe; for today these images are considered corrupted, shaped according to the whim of painters and engravers, and they differ greatly from the stars to which they gave names and from the intent of the ancient authors.
Hear the verses of Aratus <note>Aratus of Soli was a Greek poet (c. 315–240 BC) famous for his didactic poem "Phaenomena" about the stars.</note> concerning the Bull:

->The signs lie very much like it;<-
->so the head is distinguished: nor could anyone<-
->by any other sign recognize the head of an ox,<-
->as stars revolving on both sides shape it.<-
<note>original Greek: Τὰ δὲ οἱ μάλ’ ἐοικότα σήματα κεῖται / Τοίη ἡ κεφαλὴ διακέκριται : οὐδέ τις ἄλλῳ / Σήματι τεκμήραιτο κάρη βοός. οἷά μιν αὐτὸ / Ἀστέρες ἀμφοτέρωθεν ἑλισσόμενοι τυπόωσι.</note>

So indeed said Albumasar <note>An influential 9th-century Persian astronomer and astrologer.</note> above, seeking the cause of the images not from the inner depths of nature, but from the authority of Aratus.
Conversely, when one hears the names of the Pleiades, the Ear of Corn <note>original: "Aristam," referring to the star Spica in Virgo</note>, or the Vinedresser <note>original: "Vindemiatricem," the star Vindemiatrix in Virgo</note>, 
<margin>The time of the year.</margin>
and simultaneously considers that their rising served as a signal for the works of sailing, harvesting, and gathering the vintage; what "hidden philosophy," I ask, does he seek in the reasoning behind these names? For the cause of the name is plain to the eye, and therefore not even the arrangement of the stars needs to be sought to establish the name.
Pico <note>Giovanni Pico della Mirandola (1463–1494), an Italian Renaissance philosopher who wrote a famous critique of astrology.</note> extends this cause to all the images, with a conjecture about the thoughts of men that is no less ridiculous than the astrologers' theories about the natures of the signs, which he so greatly derides; for he says their plan was to express the seasons of the year through the twelve images of the Zodiac.
Let there be also a third cause, mixed with the first, which was also mentioned by Pico: 
<margin>Fables and histories.</margin>
that which brought it about that certain constellations representing a human likeness earned the names of specific individuals, namely from history, whether true or fabulous. In this category are Cepheus, Andromeda, Cassiopeia, Perseus, the Dragon, and others. Nor is it unlike the truth that, conversely, because the common folk of the farmers transcribed the names of animals onto certain stars,

**ON THE FIERY TRIGON. 21**
<note>The "Fiery Trigon" refers to the grouping of three zodiac signs associated with the element of fire: Aries, Leo, and Sagittarius. In traditional astrology, the movement of planets into these signs was thought to predict significant historical changes.</note>

...fables arose through poetic invention: to which seem to be referred the **Hydra**, the **Bowl**, the **Raven**, the **Bear**, the **Serpent-Bearer** with his snake, and the like. <note>These are the constellations Hydra, Crater, Corvus, Ursa Major, and Ophiuchus.</note>

Lucian, in his little book *On Astrology*, ridicules this human meddling <note>original: "periergiam," referring to excessive or superstitious curiosity</note> with such skill that you can hardly tell if he is playing or being serious, unless you know the man; whatever animals he found in the various rites and religions of nations, those he brought into the heavens; and whatever wanders in the heavens, he dragged down to human superstitions. <note>Lucian of Samosata was a 2nd-century satirist famous for mocking traditional beliefs and superstitions.</note>

Therefore, when all constellations have been examined through these causes, and it appears that none are left to us without a reason <note>original Greek: "anaition," meaning "causeless" or "without reason"</note>: will it not be clearly evident that there is no inherent power of signifying in these figures? Unless perhaps there can be many causes for a single heavenly figure; conspiring by mere chance and accident toward the same effect so many times, with such marvelous agreement. Whoever easily believes this suffers from a weakness of judgment, and is worthy of breaking his brain over the **Jewish Kabbalah**. For these people, in exactly the same way, neglecting the most evident causes of derivations and origins, claim their own language belongs to divine providence; they are persuaded that this was the purpose of God, the author of language: that from the transposition of letters, the nature of things might be depicted, and that in whole sentences, I know not what mysteries might be hidden away. But sober men, having confessed the power of divine providence with due veneration, understand languages—whether used by God or men—just as they sound; they leave to human will its own place among causes, and its own genuine effects. These same men also embrace the obvious causes of the figures in the sky as arising from human will, and they do not seek higher wisdom regarding the "hidden properties" of figures which might have been discovered through the contemplation of horoscopes <note>original: "genesium," referring to birth charts or the origins of things</note>.

But the students of this **Painted Philosophy** <note>A dismissive term for those who believe the "pictures" or shapes of the constellations have intrinsic mystical meaning.</note> will object that it is credible that <margin>Whether also</margin> God the Creator, whatever properties He bestowed upon the constellations, <margin>Creation.</margin> chose living creatures to share those same properties, whose shape He might express by the arrangement of the stars, so that the figure, reminding a person of a certain animal, might simultaneously warn them of that animal's property, which it shares with these stars.

Whoever asserts this uses both confidence and rashness. For not all names come from the arrangement of the stars: on the contrary, different names have quite often arisen from the same arrangement: even if these did not exist, another person might just as easily deny this "Theology" as such a person has affirmed it.

Come now, I will even grant something; let it be that the whole sublunary nature <note>"Sublunary" refers to the Earth and its atmosphere—the realm "below the moon" which was considered subject to change and decay in ancient science.</note>...

# 22 JOHANNES KEPLER

<margin>Sympathy with earthly things.</margin>
...although [nature] does not use reasoning, it nevertheless, for the same reasons as the human *phantasia* <note>imagination or the faculty of forming mental images</note>, imagines the same things about these stars as a man would. For this is a true doctrine concerning the color of the stars (and nothing argued by Pico <note>Giovanni Pico della Mirandola (1463–1494), a famous philosopher who wrote a massive critique of "divinatory" astrology</note> successfully contradicts it); as for their shape, I will not simply deny it, nor will I examine it more closely at present. Yet what does this matter to the star itself in the heavens? Or what new property can really be joined to the star itself from the imaginations of men and of their generative nature?

<margin>The stars have moved from their places.</margin>
What shall we say about the most tangled dispute currently being waged among modern Astrologers concerning this business of the "figures" <note>the shapes or constellations</note>? For two thousand years now, the stars have completely moved out of the <term>dodecatemoria</term> <note>the twelve equal 30-degree divisions of the zodiac</note> to which they originally gave their names, because of the "precession of the equinoxes" <note>a phenomenon where the positions of the stars slowly shift over centuries relative to the seasons</note>, which is where the beginning of the zodiac’s divisions is measured. To take an example from our own <term>theme</term> <note>a horoscope or specific astrological chart</note>: which sign of the zodiac shall we say provided a home for the meeting of Saturn and Jupiter, and for the New Star <note>Kepler’s Supernova of 1604</note>? Sagittarius or Scorpio? If you look at the equinoxes, the place of the conjunction is the eighth degree of Sagittarius, and the place of the New Star is the eighteenth degree of that same sign. But if you look at the stars themselves, the stars of Sagittarius—which gave the name "Sagittarius" to the third sign from the equinox—have now passed into the neighboring sign that begins at the winter solstice (named Capricorn). Conversely, not far from the place of the meeting, the star called the "Heart of the Scorpion" <note>Antares</note> is found. Therefore, if we look at the fixed stars, this conjunction of the higher planets and this New Star actually fell within the constellation of Scorpio.

<margin>The origin of the word Zodiac.</margin>
But enough regarding the origin of names. Let us conclude this section with an explanation of the word **Zodiac**. The Latins most accurately translated it as *Signifer* <note>Sign-bearer</note>. The Germans translated it unsuccessfully as *the Circle of Animals* <note>original: "den Thierkreyss"</note>; it is more authentically called *the Circle of Images* <note>original: "den Bilderkreyss"</note>. The Greek word **Zodion** <note>original: "Ζώδιον"</note> here does not mean a "little animal," but an **Idol** <note>a representation or figure</note> of any kind, even of a human, whether painted, sculpted, or cast in metal. It is in this sense that the "Zodia" <note>original: "τὰ Ζώδια"</note> are used by Hero <note>Hero of Alexandria, a mathematician and engineer</note> in his works on pneumatics; it is also in this sense that Cicero and other Latin writers use the word **Signs**. To Aratus <note>a Greek poet who wrote about the constellations</note>, the arrangements of the fixed stars are "signs of the limbs" <note>original: "σήματα" (semata)</note> in animals; but it is not likely that the person who first said "celestial Signs" was looking toward the word for "mark" rather than the word for "Image." However, whoever translated the twelve signs into German as *the twelve Signs* <note>original: "die zwölff Zeichen"</note> either did not pay attention to the original meaning of the word **Sign**, which in this context means the same as **Zodion** (in German, *Bild* or "Image"), or else the word *Zeichen* <note>Sign</note> was once broader than it is today, also encompassing the meaning of the word *Bild*. For today, the word *Zeichen*...

<meta>This page continues a technical discussion from the previous section regarding the definition of astrological "signs" and their classification into groups of three, known as Trigons. ->ON THE FIERY <term>TRIGON</term>.<- <note>A 'Trigon' is a group of three zodiac signs belonging to the same element; the Fiery Trigon consists of Aries, Leo, and Sagittarius.</note> 23

It signifies generally any kind of indicator, such as the buboes <note>Swollen lymph nodes</note> and rashes of the plague, or the sexual organs; or any kind of written mark, such as the written characters used for the Planets and the signs; or any sign placed for the sake of memory, whether in writing—such as <term>obelisks</term> <note>A mark (÷ or †) used in old manuscripts to indicate doubtful or superfluous text</note>, <term>asterisks</term> <note>A star-shaped mark (*) used to highlight text</note>, pointing hands, small indices, or the letters "N.B."; or outside of writing, such as heaps of stones, crosses, knots in the branches of trees, brandings, incisions, and the like. In this sense, the word "sign" hardly fits the celestial signs at all. More specifically, the word <note>original German: "Zeychen"</note> **Sign** sounds to the Germans like a celestial omen, a portent, or a miracle; yet again, this is not particularly appropriate for the signs of the Zodiac—though it is perhaps more tolerable when one considers how many portentous monsters we Astrologers have imagined in the heavens.

24 JOHANNES KEPLER

these lower things <note>The "sublunary" realm, or the Earthly world, as opposed to the celestial spheres.</note> do not produce cold, except by accident, after a certain certain degree of heating has already occurred. Much less do they cool of their own accord. Indeed, insofar as anything provides heat, it is itself hot, but I assert that the Sun is the most fervent: thus, in my view, there is no distinction of signs based on their qualities.

But why are three contiguous signs—Gemini, Cancer, Leo—not all fiery, because they are summer signs; and the autumnal signs earthy; the winter signs watery; and the spring signs airy?

The reason why this was omitted depends on the accepted number of the division. Since they had made twelve parts of the zodiac, it followed that two contiguous signs should be divided by contrary qualities, unless they wanted those parts—which they had divided into three for very good reason—to be considered as a single part.

Why then are the individual signs attributed those qualities which they have among Astrologers, and not the opposite ones?

Two causes are brought forward: the first is from the nature of the images <note>The constellations or figures associated with the zodiac signs.</note>: for Cancer, Scorpio, and Pisces are aquatic animals; therefore these signs were considered watery. Conversely, Aries, Leo, and Sagittarius (the latter being a horse <note>Referring to the Centaur figure of Sagittarius.</note>) are animals of a fiery nature; wherefore these signs are also fiery. Likewise, since these three, as well as Gemini and Aquarius, have a masculine appearance, and males have more abundant heat, therefore Gemini, Libra, and Aquarius participate in heat. However, this cause is not universal: for why is Aquarius not moist, or Taurus and Cancer not fiery? And why are Taurus and Capricorn feminine signs? Add to this that the images have departed from their signs <note>This refers to the "Precession of the Equinoxes." Over centuries, the physical constellations have shifted, so the sign of Aries no longer aligns with the stars of the constellation Aries. Kepler notes that astrologers kept the traditional qualities even though the stars moved.</note>; yet the qualities have remained according to their dogma.

Let us therefore hear Abu Ma'shar <note>original: "Albumasarem"; a Persian astrologer (787–886 AD) whose work was highly influential in the Middle Ages and Renaissance.</note>. First, he proposes a foundation for this distribution among the elements that is less worthy of trust: the two-fold division of signs into masculine and feminine. Why are they alternately masculine and feminine, beginning from Aries? It pleased the authors, as Ptolemy says, because of the perpetual alternation between day and night, so that the signs, which were twelve in equal number, should be distributed into six marriages, and each male should be joined to his female. For perhaps they would not seem to be twelve unless they were distinguished by the sex of those adjacent. Truly, I find no more solid reason.

Here then are the twelve signs, alternating masculine and feminine: and there are four elements, each of which, when the distribution is made equally, claims three signs for itself. Either, then, three contiguous signs must be hot, or, because this cannot be (lest contiguous signs merge), as compensation, it was necessary to arrange the three in a triangular figure <note>A "trigon," where signs of the same element are placed 120 degrees apart, forming a triangle in the circle of the zodiac.</note> through the zodiac. Since, therefore, Aries is the first and most important sign of the zodiac, and fire is the primary element, endowed with heat—which is the active quality for life—and therefore mascu-

## ON THE FIERY TRIANGLE. 25

...masculine. Therefore Aries, and those forming a triangle with it—Leo and Sagittarius—are fiery. In the first quadrant, the beginning is Aries; in the second, the middle is Leo; in the third, the end is Sagittarius. The fourth winter quadrant lacks a fiery sign.

->**THE FIERY TRIANGLE**<-
->[Diagram of a triangle connecting the zodiac signs Aries, Leo, and Sagittarius]<-
->Top Vertex (Aries): 1583, 1643, 1703, 1763 <note>Conjunction of Jupiter and Saturn</note> ☌ ♃ ♄<-
->Bottom Left (Leo): 1623, 1683, 1743<-
->Bottom Right (Sagittarius): 1599, 1659, 1719<-

But why are the three succeeding signs—Taurus, Virgo, and Capricorn—earthy rather than airy, since air immediately follows fire <note>in the natural order of elements: Fire, Air, Water, Earth</note>? Albumasar <note>original: "Albumasar"; Abu Ma'shar (787–886 AD), a Persian astrologer whose Latin translations were central to European astrology</note> provides this reason: that the action of heat is to give life, while the action of cold is likewise to perform an action, but one of mortifying and destroying. Indeed, every "active" quality is more noble than a "passive" one. Therefore, to Taurus, which follows immediately after Aries, the other active quality was assigned—namely cold—and among the elements, Earth, which is the seat of cold.

Thus, the same proportion that exists between life and death, day and night, or male and female, was also seen to exist between heat and cold. For the female also "acts" in some way during the act of generation—for poor Albumasar, laboring miserably <note>The author likely finds Albumasar’s logic strained here, as "cold" was often considered a privation of heat rather than a truly "active" force in the same way</note>, must be helped—and she does not behave in a purely passive manner.

Albumasar has explained why he thinks a fiery sign should be followed by a cold one; let him also explain why that sign is dry rather than moist.

First, he says, if Taurus had been established as moist, then Cancer would have had to be dry. But it was better for Taurus to be dry, so that two coupled signs might agree in one passive and material quality: Aries and Taurus in dryness; Gemini and Cancer in moisture; Leo and Virgo in dryness; and so on consequently.

<meta>This page continues Kepler's investigation into the origins of astrological systems, specifically addressing the "Triplicities" (groups of three signs associated with elements) and the theories of the Persian astrologer Abu Ma'shar.</meta>

26	JOHANNES KEPLER

	Here Abu Ma'shar <note>original: "Albumasar"; a 9th-century Persian astrologer whose works were foundational to European astrology.</note> seems to reveal some treasures from the <term>Kabbalah: a tradition of Jewish mysticism often used by Renaissance thinkers to find hidden symbolic meanings in nature.</term> For he explains the nature of things as if it were a game. It is altogether necessary that male and female agree in species so that they may propagate that species, but they are distinguished from one another by sex. To illustrate this, according to Abu Ma'shar’s opinion, those ancients took two adjacent signs, which they joined together in a "marriage," and said they agree in material quality but are distinguished by their active—or, so to speak, genital—qualities. And since there were only two passive qualities, the dry and the moist, they established two kinds of marriages, allotted to alternating places in the zodiac: just as in the nature of things there is one union between human male and female, and another between cattle. They perhaps also alluded to the mutual successions of summer and winter, and in both, the succession of day and night.

	However these things may be, and even though I am rightly delighted by this ingenious imitation of nature and this distribution of qualities into the signs, I do not value it more than is fair. Even if the first authors borrowed a certain law for their arbitrary reasonings from the nature of things, it does not mean that, in turn, the whole remaining nature of the heavens has subjected itself to these reasonings of theirs. It is like a gardener who, looking at the number five—which is familiar to flowers—builds pentagonal flowerbeds; he does not thereby cause the roses to grow more successfully in them. This is because the nature of the flower, although it reminds the reasoning gardener of the pentagon, does not receive a reciprocal benefit from the gardener’s actual pentagon.

	Rather, this becomes apparent from a careful weighing of these causes: that the signs of the zodiac were named after the elements by the mere whim of their inventors. In reality, they are connected by no close kinship with those elements from which each is named.

	Finally, these reasons for the names are so arranged that the names "Fiery," "Airy," "Watery," and "Earthy" remain, regardless of how much the fixed stars or the images of the constellations might move away. <note>Kepler refers to the "Precession of the Equinoxes," where the actual stars in the sky shift position over centuries relative to the calendar signs.</note>

	For if the person who first deliberated which signs should be named after which elements had looked at the fixed stars, he would have called Taurus "hot and moist." He would have called it "hot" from the fiery glow of Aldebaran <note>original: "Palilicij"; a traditional name for the bright star Aldebaran.</note> and "moist" from the Pleiades and Hyades, which were believed to generate rain.

	So too, he would have called Scorpio "fiery" from the fiery star Antares <note>original: "Antareos"; the prominent red star in the heart of the Scorpion.</note>, a star of the first magnitude, which was closest to the past conjunction of Saturn and Jupiter.

	Thus you see that these names, taken from the elements, did not come to the signs because of the fixed stars; therefore, they are not extinguished when the fixed stars depart from the signs.

								Which <note>This "Which" (Latin: "Quæ") is a catchword, a common printer's tool that repeats the first word of the next page to ensure the pages were bound in the correct order.</note>

->What natural cause links the signs of the Zodiac, separated by a third of the circle, into a single Triplicity?<-

### CHAPTER SEVEN.

It has been stated thus far what the **Fiery Trigon** is according to astrological definition; namely, that there are three twelfth-parts of the zodiac, arranged in the shape of a triangle across the zodiac, and named after the element of fire. However, since I have asserted that both the division of the zodiac into twelve parts and the naming of those parts—whether by sex, animals, or elements—is a matter of mere human choice rather than derived from the nature of things, you might rightly and deservedly ask further: is there no other natural reason available by which three signs might be linked in the form of a triangle?

Therefore, just as was said above regarding the division of the zodiac itself—that nature does not divide it into twelve precise parts, yet it provides the occasions for adopting this division (insofar as the Moon meets with the Sun in twelve locations of the zodiac each year)—likewise, although the nature of celestial motions does not trace these **Trigons** <term>Trigon: A group of three zodiac signs forming a triangle across the celestial circle, sharing the same "element" (fire, earth, air, or water).</term> to the exact nail as they were established by the authors, it nevertheless provides the occasions for observing them. For **Saturn** and **Jupiter**, the highest planets, arrange their two successive mutual meetings in such a way that they are distant by almost a third part of the zodiac. By this logic, it happens that in any given age, three signs of the zodiac, reduced under one trigon by the authors, obtain a special power from the conjunctions of these superior planets in stirring up (I do not say in compelling) the nature of sublunary things. This part of Astrology, **Giovanni Pico della Mirandola** <note>Pico della Mirandola (1463–1494) was a famous Italian philosopher who wrote a massive critique of astrology. Kepler often agreed with his scientific skepticism but defended the "natural" aspects of astrology.</note> has not yet snatched away from me; even if I subscribe to most of the arguments he disputed in his twelve books against the Astrologers, provided they are understood soberly and according to the value of the arguments employed.

The motion of the Moon also suggests something similar when compared with the motion of the Sun. Indeed, if the Moon holds Aries, after one year has revolved it occupies Leo, and in the next year Sagittarius, or the beginning of Capricorn.

Now, therefore, it is time for me to explain the second meaning of the term **Fiery Trigon**, <margin>What the Fiery Triplicity is.</margin> which is indeed specific to this topic. For Astrologers call the "Fiery Trigon" a period of nearly two hundred years, within which time the meetings of the superior planets, Saturn and Jupiter, are frequently repeated only in the signs of the **Fiery Triplicity**—Aries, Leo, and Sagittarius—as...

28 JOHANNES KEPLER

appear to us indeed living on Earth: where it often happens that before those slow moving planets exit these signs, all the remaining planets follow and are joined with those slow ones within the boundaries of these signs.

   How it could happen, however, that the meetings of the superior planets <note>The "superior planets" in traditional astronomy are those further from the Sun than Earth: Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn.</note> fall only into the signs of one Triplicity <note>A Triplicity (or Trigon) is a group of three Zodiac signs associated with the same element. For example, the Fiery Trigon consists of Aries, Leo, and Sagittarius.</note>, will be explained thus: Saturn traverses the zodiac in 30 years. Jupiter in 12 years. Therefore, the annual journey of Saturn is one-thirtieth, and of Jupiter one-twelfth of the entire circuit; subtract one-thirtieth from one-twelfth, and one-twentieth remains. Jupiter, therefore, overtakes Saturn annually by one-twentieth of the whole circuit: thus, in twenty years, it catches up to Saturn again. And yet, Jupiter himself measures out individual signs in individual years—that is, twelfths of the zodiac; therefore, in twenty years, he traverses twenty signs, which is to say, one full circle and eight signs besides. Let there be a conjunction of Saturn and Jupiter in Sagittarius in the year 1603. After 12 years have passed, Jupiter returns to Sagittarius, but Saturn stays in Taurus: after 8 more years, Saturn is moved forward from Taurus into Leo, and Jupiter likewise from Sagittarius into Leo, and there <margin>See the diagram on page 25.</margin> he catches up to Saturn. After another 20 years, the same happens in Aries; where you see that between Sagittarius, Leo, and Aries, a triangle is formed.

   By the same reasoning, it will also be clear how it happens that these meetings, after 200 years, pass from one Trigon into another. For what has already been said concerning the thirtieth and <margin>The duration of the fiery Trigon.</margin> twelfth part of the circuit; those things do not hold precisely so, nor does Jupiter terminate exactly with the year and the sign. It happens, therefore, that two consecutive meetings are not distant by exactly a third part of the circle, but by about three degrees less. Therefore, three degrees multiplied ten times make up one sign, whereby ten meetings, having started from the beginning of a sign, are shifted down to the end of the sign, and at the eleventh repetition they pass to the beginning of the following sign, which begins a new Triplicity. Therefore, the four Triplicities, by which the entire zodiac is contained, being extended over two hundred years each, create a period of a little less than eight hundred years; in which space of time the entire zodiac is divided by forty meetings into as many almost equal parts: and when that time has passed, it returns to the beginning. Therefore, I can say that in the place of the conjunction of Saturn and Jupiter which was in the year 1544 at 30 degrees of Scorpio, the nearest in both time and place is this one, which occurred in the year 1603 in December at 8 degrees of Sagittarius, such that within eight hundred years none will approach closer to 30 degrees of Scorpio.

<margin>Which number return of the fiery Trigon this is.</margin> From this place, therefore, a comparison having been made with the age of the world, the succession of the trigons and the repetition of the fiery one is evident. For since from the founding of all things <note>Kepler is referring to the biblical date of Creation.</note> roughly 5600 years are numbered, these divided by 800 constitute seven great periods and returns of the Fiery Trigon.

### ON THE FIERY TRIGON. 29

<margin>The utility of the doctrine of Trigons in the history of the world's ages.</margin>

It is truly remarkable that the most significant <term>Epochs</term> <note>Major turning points or eras in history.</note> fall almost exactly into the transition points of these periods. For this reason, I take great delight in this series of <term>Trigons</term> <note>In astrology, a "Trigon" or "Triplicity" is a group of three zodiac signs belonging to the same element. The Fiery Trigon consists of Aries, Leo, and Sagittarius.</note>; it serves as a sort of shorthand for time, which is most useful for aiding the memory. I also believe it will be very welcome to most people of this age, because our own era falls into one of these very transitions.

The Fiery Trigon leads the family of elements, both for the reason I mentioned regarding the Epochs, and also because it shares a common beginning with the start of Aries, which is the most important among the <term>Cardinal points</term> <note>The four points of the zodiac that mark the beginnings of the seasons; Aries marks the vernal equinox.</note>.

Observe in this table—using rounded and not strictly precise numbers—the centuries in which the Fiery Trigon began its cycle.

| Periods | Years before Christ | From the origin of things | Notable persons | Coinciding events: but you, Reader, beware of saying they were *caused* by the Trigons. |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |

| 3 | 2400 | 1600 | Noah. | The Flood. |
| 4 | 1600 | 2400 | Moses. | The Departure from Egypt. The Law. |
| 5 | 800 | 3200 | Isaiah. | The Era of the Greeks, Babylonians, and Romans. |
| 6 | After Christ. | 4000 | Christ the Lord. | The Roman Monarchy; the Reformation of the world. |
| 7 | 800 | 4800 | Charlemagne. | The Empire of the West and of the Saracens. |
| 8 | 1600 | 5600 | Rudolph II. | Our life, fates, and prayers—we who discuss these things. |
| — | — | — | | — |
| 9 | 2400 | 6400 | | Where will we be then, and where our now most-flourishing Germany? And who indeed will be our successors? Will they even remember us? If indeed the world shall last that long. |

<margin>Where the beginning of the Fiery Triplicity is to be taken.</margin>

Having progressed this far, a doubt arises concerning the genuine beginning of this Fiery Trigon. For if we look at the <term>mean motions</term> <note>The average or theoretical positions of planets, rather than their actual observed positions which vary due to elliptical orbits.</note>, the "mean" or theoretical conjunction is placed by <term>Cardano</term> <note>Gerolamo Cardano (1501–1576), a famous Italian polymath and astrologer.</note> in the year 1583, at the very point of Aries. It is indeed a beautiful thing to give the Trigon a beginning from a conjunction that is so close to a Cardinal point—one of which exists in every triplicity.

30 JOHANNES KEPLER

However, the mean motions <note>Mean motion refers to the average speed of a planet, calculating its position as if it moved in a perfect circle at a constant speed, whereas "true" motion accounts for its actual, varying speed.</note> from the **Prutenic calculation** <note>The Prutenic Tables were astronomical tables published by Erasmus Reinhold in 1551, based on the Copernican model.</note> suggest otherwise, showing a conjunction at 28 degrees and 14 minutes of Pisces; not, in fact, at the very beginning of Aries. The true conjunction, however, falls even further back, specifically at the 21st degree of Pisces.

The one that occurred in the year 1603, however, fell at 1 degree and 7 minutes of Sagittarius in respect to mean motions, but at 8 degrees of Sagittarius according to true motions; by either name, it was the first in the **fiery signs**.

Even so, not all dispute has been removed. For it is said that the time claimed for the **fiery Trigon** <term>Trigon: Also called a triplicity, this is a group of three zodiac signs associated with the same element (Fire, Earth, Air, or Water). The fiery Trigon consists of Aries, Leo, and Sagittarius.</term> is that during which no gatherings of the superior planets <note>The "superior planets" are those further from the Sun than Earth: Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn.</note> occur except in the fire signs. Yet while this holds true for the years 1603 and 1623, it varies in the year 1643. Then, the mean conjunction is in Aries, but the true one is at 26 degrees of Pisces, as retrogradation <note>The apparent backward motion of a planet in the sky caused by Earth overtaking it in orbit.</note> pulls the planets forward into the preceding signs; although even of themselves—for physical and optical reasons of motion (which the ancients expressed with the terms **Equant** and **Eccentric**) <note>Geometric models used in ancient and Renaissance astronomy to explain why planets seem to speed up or slow down.</note>—Saturn is slow in its motion from Sagittarius to Pisces, and Jupiter from Libra to Pisces, and they have subtractive equations. Therefore, all these things must be understood in a broad sense. For if the apparent truth of the motions is observed, there will not even be a triangle between the two most recent gatherings. In the year 1583, the conjunction was in 21 degrees of Pisces, and in 1603, it was in 8 degrees of Sagittarius. The interval, therefore, is not 120 degrees (which is a perfect Trigon), nor 117 degrees (which is the usual distance between mean conjunctions), but 102 degrees—closer to a **square** <note>An astrological aspect of 90 degrees, usually considered tense or discordant.</note> than a Trigon, as you can see in the diagram.

->On the natural powers of the fiery Triplicity: and first on the efficacy of Conjunctions, against Giovanni Pico della Mirandola.<-

### CHAPTER EIGHT.

Up to this point, I have argued that neither the distribution of the zodiac into twelve signs is natural, nor is this distinction into four triplicities—and the connection of three distant points—permanent or accurate, since it arises from the motions of the superior planets. It has also been said that neither do the signs possess any properties of those animals <note>Kepler refers to the belief that signs like Aries (the Ram) or Leo (the Lion) actually share characteristics with the animals they are named after.</note>, nor do the Triplicities of signs <margin>Book 6, chapter 4.</margin> possess the qualities of those elements <note>Fire, Earth, Air, and Water.</note> from which they are named. Since <margin>Book 6, chapter 15.</margin> that famous **Giovanni Pico della Mirandola** <note>An Italian Renaissance philosopher (1463–1494) who wrote a famous attack on astrology.</note> taught all these things more than a hundred years before me...

**ON THE FIERY TRIGON.** 31
<note>original: "DE TRIGONO IGNEO." The Fiery Trigon refers to the alignment of the planets in the "fire" signs of the zodiac: Aries, Leo, and Sagittarius.</note>

...[Pi]co, Count of Mirandola: I might rightly seem to yield entirely to his opinion regarding the vanity of astrology.

And not to be tedious, that author <note>Pico della Mirandola in his work *Against Divinatory Astrology*</note> rejected the effects of the "great conjunctions" <note>The rare meeting of Saturn and Jupiter in the sky</note> entirely in Book 5, Chapter 5; he rejected all "aspects" <note>Geometric angles between planets</note> in general in Book 6, Chapters 5, 6, and 7; and he denied that there was any power existing in the Trigons <term>Trigon: A group of three zodiac signs belonging to the same element—Fire, Earth, Air, or Water.</term> in Book 6, Chapter 15, to which some points from Book 6, Chapter 4 can also be referred. Since I hold a different opinion than the author on these chapters among others, I shall weigh his reasons; however, I will first explain my own opinion, as it will serve the current purpose.

Pico asks in Book 5, Chapter 5: why should one believe that Saturn and Jupiter accomplish greater things when they are together than when they are separate? <margin>The source of the effects which experience attributes to the conjunctions of the planets.</margin> I shall answer according to my own opinion, not that of the astrologers: the work that we ascribe to the superior planets <note>The outer planets, Saturn and Jupiter</note> when joined, which does not apply to them when separate, is in no way the work of the planets themselves (aside from bare illumination and heating); rather, it belongs to sublunary nature <note>Nature on Earth, literally "below the moon."</note> itself. If this work were attributed to the planets themselves, one could respond with the principle of "united virtue being stronger." But as it stands, although they do indeed move sublunary nature, they do not move it like "natural agents" that pour out some power because of the presence of a passive thing; instead, they move nature in the way that objects move the senses—as light moves the eyes, sound moves hearing, or heat moves touch. For none of these things is weaker simply because more senses are open to receive them; rather, a certain natural and immaterial outflow of the quality they possess has been granted to all of them, the form of which strikes the senses until they are exhausted, yet without exhausting its own source. At this point, some philosopher might expect me to apply what I have said in these examples regarding various qualities to the light of the stars. Thus, he will say he already knows what I am about to add: namely, that Pico's reasoning still militates against me, since the conjunction of two planets makes the light of both not a bit greater, because they contribute as much to their meeting as they possessed when separate.

But I am by no means speaking of light at present, which indeed possesses both heat and brightness according to the nature of the body, and not according to the mutual proximity of the two—of which neither, certainly, is the cause of light for the other.

I have used the analogy of light and other sensible things to explain an obscure matter. For in the conjunction of two planets, <margin>The conjunctions themselves of the planets,</margin> it is no longer the light, but this very relationship—the very positioning which we call a "conjunction"—that takes the place of the object. Therefore, as the object is, so is the "sense" in sublunary nature. The object belongs to the class of relations; therefore...

Therefore, you must admit that some kind of "impression" exists within a certain spiritual <term>formative faculty<note>The 'facultas formatrix' was a concept in early biology describing an internal power that shapes a living thing according to its natural blueprint.</note></term>, which others call the <term>seminal reason<note>original: 'rationem seminariam'; a term from Stoic philosophy referring to the rational 'seeds' or principles that govern the development of matter.</note></term>.

But how it moved from the eye to the womb, into the formative faculty, and into the fetus—how, I say, from this rod to that distant corner—this requires an explanation. It requires, I say, an "impression" of a kind that is more than ordinary.

In this same manner, I say that both the colors of the Planets—to answer <term>Pico<note>Giovanni Pico della Mirandola (1463–1494), a philosopher who wrote a famous and influential attack on astrology, which the author is here refuting.</note></term> in passing regarding his various objections—and their very conjunctions and configurations are impressed upon the natures or faculties of <term>sublunary things<note>The 'sublunary' realm refers to the Earth and everything below the moon, which was thought to be subject to change and the influence of the 'perfect' heavens.</note></term>. By these celestial objects, those faculties are deeply moved, both to the forming and the moving of the body, which for moving...

**CONCERNING THE FIERY TRIGON. 33**

(as much as can be hoped for in natural things) experience has taught us about the stirring of sublunary <term>sublunary: the region of the universe below the moon, i.e., the Earth and its atmosphere</term> natures during the conjunctions and aspects of the Wanderers <note>original: "Erronum," a classical term for the planets</note>, and it has overcome the reluctant. <margin>The opinion of the Astrologers.</margin>

But perhaps this stands in my way of gaining belief: that no astrologer has brought forward this reasoning; I alone (but what kind of man am I, where did I come from, when was I born?) am teaching Philosophy to the Astrologers?

Indeed, I appeal to philosophers and fair judges of things: whether all recent discoveries and all improvements should be excluded from Philosophy in this way, as if by a bolted door? I do not deny to Pico <note>Giovanni Pico della Mirandola (1463–1494), a famous philosopher who wrote a massive critique of "divinatory" astrology</note> that there is great vanity in the experience boasted by Astrologers, even concerning this topic; but I do not therefore concede that there has been no experience at all. <margin>How far experience is valid in Astrology: and how far it is in error.</margin>

Thus, as usually happens in obscure matters, great errors have arisen from the variety of minds thinking about causes. Let the greatest question of all be our example: Who, unless completely insane and corrupted in the school of Epicurus <note>Epicureans were often associated with atheism because they believed the gods did not interfere in human affairs</note>, will deny that God exists? Since, to use the words of St. Paul, every kind of person "feels after Him": And yet, how many falsehoods mix themselves into this experience daily, while everyone makes a God for themselves from whatever they feel relief? <margin>An example concerning God.</margin> From this come so many species of impiety. But it does not follow that because infinite myriads of gentiles were deceived, so too are the Christians, Turks <note>In the 17th century, "Turks" was the common term for Muslims</note>, and Jews today, who worship one God and "grope after Him" <note>original: ψηλαφῶντες (psēlaphōntes), a Greek term meaning to feel or grope around in the dark, referencing Acts 17:27</note>.

Therefore, in such a complex matter, it can happen that everyone is partially deceived, yet in general, they experience something true. Concerning the Sun, one person believes that the day is carried around by it like a wide-stretching cloak; another that waters are drawn from the earth by it; another that grapes are colored by its light; another that nature is made fertile by its entry into Aries <note>The start of Spring in the zodiac</note>; another that the stars are driven from the sky by its arrival. <margin>Concerning the Sun.</margin> A philosopher, weighing each point, finds that each person is in error regarding these particulars, and that the Sun is not sufficient for those things; but having weighed the matter himself, since he sees all these things happen with the Sun’s approach and diminish with its retreat, he gathers something general from it: that the Sun is the author of only light and heat through itself. From these two things, with the nature of sublunary things added, all the rest follow by natural consequence—and can be hindered if some cause beneath the Moon is lacking.

So it was formerly with the Magnet, when a certain Jacob of Florence <note>Likely a reference to an early writer on the compass</note> noticed in it a pole which would strive toward the pole of the world, and which would attract iron, while the opposite side repelled iron. <margin>Concerning the Magnet.</margin> Immediately there arose those who offered hope for marvelous inventions: of perpetual motion through iron pins on a wheel; of a Sphere moving with the heavens without rollers or weights; of an Alphabet by which one might [communicate] to another at a fixed time beyond a hundred...

<summary>Kepler argues that astrology should not be dismissed just because astrologers make errors, comparing it to how people understand God or the Sun—we recognize the general truth of their power even if our specific explanations are flawed. He uses the development of magnetism as a parallel for how new scientific understanding emerges from early, often exaggerated observations.</summary>

# 34 JOHANNES KEPLER

...a distance of a hundred miles, which he might signify as he wished: these things were excerpted from the treatise of **Master Henry of Hesse** <note>original: "Magistri Henrici de Hassia." Also known as Heinrich von Langenstein, a 14th-century theologian and mathematician who wrote on physics and astronomy.</note>, as shown in a manuscript booklet I own, written nearly 200 years ago. When these came into the hands of **Jean Taisnier** <note>original: "Taisnierij Hannonij." A 16th-century Belgian author known for his works on chiromancy and magnetism.</note>, he published them again under the title of a new invention, using for the most part the same words, and with the diagrams expressed precisely <note>original: "ad unguem," literally "to the fingernail"—a Latin idiom for perfect accuracy.</note>.

<margin>Experience proves the efficacy of conjunctions.</margin>

In the same way, I say it has been observed by the ancients, and is observed today, that the greatest power of the **conjunctions** <note>The alignment of two or more celestial bodies in the sky.</note> of the Planets lies in stirring the **faculties** <note>Natural powers or abilities inherent in things.</note> of sublunary <note>Earthly, or "under the moon"—referring to the physical world below the heavens.</note> things. However, since from this universal principle one person might try to build one thing and another something else—and everyone is striving for specific predictions—they devise a great variety of **Aphorisms** <note>Short, authoritative statements of scientific or medical principles.</note> so that their rules might match the variety of events. Afterward, each one cites experience in his own favor; they are deceived, blinded by a desire for predicting, so that they stumble into so many superstitions.

But I return to **Pico** <note>Giovanni Pico della Mirandola (1463–1494), an Italian Renaissance philosopher who wrote a famous critique of astrology.</note>. He thinks the works of the Planets are hindered by the presence of contrary Planets; thus, their effects become smaller than if each were operating alone. First, I deny that one is hindered by another, for they are mixed together just like the rays of light-emitting bodies. Next, I recall Pico to my earlier response: we are not considering here what the Planets do by themselves, but what **sublunary natures** <note>The inherent spirits or governing forces of Earthly things, including plants and human minds.</note> experience from their conjunction as if it were an object. In this matter, experience precedes reasoning. For unless I can show to the eye that the power of sublunary things is stirred by the Planets in conjunction, I do not ask that my provided reasons be believed. Conversely, if the fact is established, contrary reasoning can achieve nothing; rather, it will be correctly resolved by the introduction of this dogma concerning the sensitive impression of the **species** <note>In this context, "species" refers to the visible form or "image" of the conjunction as perceived by the Earthly faculty.</note> of the conjunction upon the faculties of sublunary things.

**Mirandola** <note>Pico della Mirandola.</note> continues: *Why should the conjunctions of the Sun and Moon not be more powerful than those of Saturn and Jupiter in administering these great changes of the world?* I respond according to my own opinion, not that of the astrologers: No change of the world is caused or pre-signified by any position of the stars. For it is not the same thing for the "University of Sublunary Natures" <note>The collective whole of all Earthly living things and their internal spirits.</note>—and even the minds of men, insofar as they are natural—to be most vehemently stirred, and for the state of this World or the human race to be transfigured from one form into another. Furthermore, the conjunctions and **eclipses** <note>original: "deliquia," referring to the "failing" or darkening of light.</note> of the Sun and Moon do indeed have power for this stirring of natures, but for a far different reason. For insofar as they are simply joined, that is a monthly occurrence; thus the conjunction does not last long, since the Moon is very fast. Therefore, this stimulus is neither sharp nor unusual, and for that reason, no great commotion follows it. Further, what reason does Pico have why—

<summary>Kepler argues that planetary conjunctions stir the Earthly "faculties" through a sensory-like impression, and he critiques Pico della Mirandola's skepticism by distinguishing between natural stirring and total world transformation.</summary>
<keywords>astrology, planetary conjunctions, Pico della Mirandola, Henry of Hesse, Jean Taisnier, sublunary nature, plagiarism, scientific method</keywords>

->ON THE FIERY TRIGON. 35<-

Why should [Pico] make the Sun and Moon more "universal" in their effects than Saturn and Jupiter? They say the Heavens are everywhere above us; therefore, this applies not only to the Sun, but to Saturn as well. Or perhaps he intended to suggest that the powers of the "luminaries" <note>The Sun and Moon are traditionally called the "luminaries" in astronomy due to their brightness compared to the stars and planets.</note> are greater than those of the other "wanderers" <note>original: "erronum," a traditional term for the planets.</note>? I admit this is true, but look: here is Pico speaking again of the "proper work" of the stars, which each one possesses according to the measure of its body and light. But we are not discussing that now. For I have already admitted long ago that this individual work of theirs is not strengthened by conjunctions. 

Since our discussion concerns the *objective commotion of nature*, we must deny that which is conceded regarding the "proper work" of the stars. Therefore: the greater the conjunction, the greater the commotion of nature. If the conjunction is long-lasting and rare, the commotion will also be great and unusual; and consequently, a greater commotion arises from the conjunction of Saturn and Jupiter than from that of the Sun and Moon.

Indeed, Pico says that the doctrine of conjunctions does not belong to all astrologers, nor to the most eminent ones, and therefore it is false. I say that regarding conjunctions in general, not a single astrologer has remained silent. As for the fact that Ptolemy, in his *Prognostics of Universal Affairs*, omitted this series—and, as it were, this *Polity*—of "Great Conjunctions," <note>The "Great Conjunctions" refers to the meeting of Saturn and Jupiter, which occurs roughly every 20 years and was thought to signal major historical shifts.</note> his Arab successors adopted it. Let the astrologers—who boast that they can predict the turnings of world events from the stars—fight amongst themselves over this. For my part, I do not seek the "turnings of events," which involve many specific details, but only the *commotion of natures*, which is universal; and even then, I do not seek every such commotion through the heavens.

Pico censures Mashallah <note>original: "Meſſahalam," an influential 8th-century Persian-Jewish astronomer and astrologer.</note> because he said that great things proceed from those planets which move more slowly. Pico himself believes that speed is a mark of a star's "nobility," and that efficacy accompanies nobility, such that those which are faster are more powerful. I say—speaking for my own view, not Mashallah’s—that Pico is "weaving straw" <note>original: "connectere paleas," a Latin idiom for making a weak or nonsensical argument.</note>. For speed has nothing in common with efficacy here, at least not inherently. As for those things which result from a kind of accidental combination, this is not the place to speak of them. In a runner, speed is noble; in a King, rest and stability are noble. What will you say to Copernicus, Pico, who teaches that the Sun stands still, even though it is the noblest of the Planets? 

Furthermore, the reasoning of those who make Saturn "faster" than Jupiter because of its larger circuit is in no way sound. For true Astronomy makes Saturn truly slower and more sluggish in proportion to how much larger and longer its path is than that of Jupiter. As for the arguments Pico draws from the 50th word of the *Centiloquium* <note>The *Centiloquium* is a collection of 100 astrological aphorisms formerly attributed to Ptolemy.</note>, they are of no concern to us, since Pico himself deduces from it only as much as we ourselves retain and defend here.

In Book 5, chapter 6, Pico objects to the astrologers who look to the "middle conjunctions," <note>In the theory of Great Conjunctions, "middle conjunctions" refer to the shifts the planets make between different astrological elements (Trigons) every 240 years.</note> because—

36 JOHANNES KEPLER

that these are fictions: for I admit that the <term>apparent positions</term> <note>The positions of planets as they actually appear to an observer on Earth, rather than their calculated average positions.</note> should have been the ones considered. I confess, and not only regarding this point, but regarding this whole art of determining the historical cycles of religions and empires from planetary conjunctions, that I also, along with <note>Giovanni Pico della Mirandola (1463–1494), a famous Italian philosopher who wrote a massive critique of astrology which Kepler is responding to here.</note> Pico, condemn it as a mix of folly and superstition. Yet, it does not follow from this that the astrologers' experience regarding <term>conjunctions</term> <note>When two celestial bodies appear in the same place in the sky.</note> is entirely worthless. For what does it matter if the true conjunction differs from the <term>mean</term> <note>The "mean" position is a mathematical average of a planet's motion, whereas the "true" position accounts for the planet's actual, variable speed.</note> conjunction? Since any true conjunction closely approaches the mean one. For although in the year 1583, the conjunction which by mean motion stood at the beginning of Aries actually occurred in the 21st degree of Pisces according to true motion, and only in the year 1703 will one first truly occur in Aries; <margin>As you see in the diagram on page 25.</margin> nevertheless, an apparent conjunction did take place in the same year that the mean motion predicted. Therefore, if something occurred in human affairs that was attributed to a conjunction in Aries, the observer might indeed have been mistaken about the specific <term>Zodiac sign</term>, but not about the occurrence of the conjunction itself, which is what we are discussing here.

I do not say this to defend the inductions of astrologers down to their specific predictions; rather, I say it to maintain that around the time of <term>Great Conjunctions</term> <note>The rare meeting of Jupiter and Saturn, which occurred every 20 years and was thought to signal major historical shifts.</note> there have been such great stirrings of Nature, and of the natural impulses within men, that astrologers were deceived by them—thinking that the events themselves, which were carried out through those stirrings, proceeded directly from this celestial principle.

->On the efficacy of the other <term>aspects</term> <note>The specific geometric angles between planets, such as 90 or 180 degrees, believed to have different influences.</note>, and likewise of the <term>Trine</term> <note>An aspect where planets are 120 degrees apart; traditionally considered harmonious.</note>, against Giovanni Pico della Mirandola.<-

### CHAPTER NINE.

Now, in Book 6, Chapter 5, Pico first attacks the idea that some aspects are inherently favorable while others are unfavorable and "evil," such as the <term>square</term> <note>A 90-degree angle.</note> or the <term>opposition</term> <note>A 180-degree angle.</note>. On this point, I agree with Pico: for I assert that aspects are distinguished only by their intensity, and that all are equally powerful in stimulating the nature of <term>sublunary</term> <note>Literally "under the moon"—referring to the Earthly realm where things are subject to change and decay, as opposed to the "unchanging" heavens.</note> things. The traditional reason given for why a square is "evil" is attributed to a difference in gender—that one of the signs at a square distance is "masculine" and the other "feminine"—notions which I have already stated I consider to be worthless. Furthermore, when he asks whether these aspects are thought to exist between the physical bodies of the planets themselves, or only in respect to the effects that follow here on Earth, I again approve of his opinion: the planets in the heavens experience nothing from one another. Nor could they have any "knowledge" of when two of them happen to meet in a mutual aspect relative to the Earth, even if you were to grant them rational souls. In short, these aspects exist between...

He dismisses the traditional astrological classification of "lucky" and "unlucky" aspects, arguing instead that they vary only in their geometric intensity.</summary>

### CONCERNING THE FIERY TRIGON. 37

...between the rays descending to the earth, but least of all between the bodies of the Planets themselves: all these things, I say, I accept along with Pico <note>Giovanni Pico della Mirandola (1463–1494), a famous Renaissance philosopher who wrote a massive critique of "divinatory" astrology.</note>. Therefore, this very thing which experience testifies—that sublunary <note>The region "below the moon," meaning the Earth and its atmosphere, which was thought to be subject to change and decay unlike the "perfect" heavens.</note> nature is moved by **aspects** <term>Aspects: specific geometric angles (like 60°, 90°, or 120°) formed between planets as seen from Earth, believed to trigger physical or weather changes.</term>, since an aspect results from the position of the earth relative to two Planets—this very thing, I say, showed me the way to that truth concerning the method [of influence]; namely, that the mastery of the cause resides in the earth, not in the Planets themselves, and that this commotion of nature happens from itself rather than from the impression of the aspects; in the same way that a sentient animal is moved to the sense of external things. 

For what Pico emphasizes from Plotinus <note>A major Neoplatonist philosopher who argued that the stars are "signs" but not "causes" of earthly events.</note>—that rays departing from heaven cannot be mixed on earth so that a new power or form arises—I indeed held that before; I also held that it seemed they could be mixed more rightly and closely if they proceeded from things joined together; but then, that rays are always being mixed, even if not always in a **Triangular** or **Hexagonal** figure <note>Referring to the Trine (120°) and Sextile (60°) aspects.</note>: finally, that in the natural body itself where the rays are mixed, as the rays converge at a point, every figure vanishes: I was diligently weighing all these things. 

<margin>How the reason for the efficacy of aspects was discovered.</margin> 

Yet these many reasons could not refute the clearest experience, which testified that then, most especially, every kind of *meteor* <note>In this period, "meteor" referred to any atmospheric phenomenon, including rain, wind, and lightning, not just shooting stars.</note> comes into existence when the Planets are configured in aspects: on the contrary, then there is a stillness of the air most especially when there are no aspects or conjunctions: and that these things happen not only if they approach [conjunction] by their bodies, but even if they approach **hexagonal** or **square** distances, and that they do so suddenly at the very moments of the aspects; whereas on the days immediately before and after, the effects cease. 

Jofrancus Offucius <note>Jofridus Offusius, a 16th-century German physician and astrologer who sought a physical, rather than mystical, explanation for planetary influence.</note>, a most sharp enemy of the aspects, experienced the same in the square aspect, besides the conjunction and opposition, which he thought he had referred back to the causes of "reverberations." But he confessed the weakness of his little reasoning, yielding to experience regarding the square aspect. Because of these things, therefore, it had to be said that neither the stars, nor their rays by themselves, nor even the configurations themselves (insofar as they are merely relations), produce these things otherwise than under the rationale of an **object**; but that there presides over passive things (such as the humors in the globe of the Earth) some **faculty**, which can both perceive and evaluate the figures of the radiations from there, and from here shake its own body with a certain impetus, either by moving it, if it were a motive faculty, or by heating it and sublimating the humors, if it had been assigned this duty: by which reasoning, once discovered, all the things that Pico brought forth from Philosophy against the aspects, and which we have commemorated, are resolved.

But the nature of this faculty, or faculties, will be more evident from what follows. For Pico, moving the controversy against the Astrologers concerning the number of aspects, also directs arguments against the matter itself. First, he refutes the reasons...

40 JOHANNES KEPLER

...akin, as from light and the shining celestial bodies, and as often as the rays of two [planets] meet in such a way that the first draft of a fit shape is represented—either for extending a plane or for enclosing a solid figure within itself—so often do [these souls] eagerly renew that image of their creation, that is, the motion of their bodies, having gathered momentum. And that which they do perpetually, they do diligently and with notable excess in those moments, stirred by a certain fervor.

<margin>Definition of Harmonic ratios.</margin>

Yet they do not indiscriminately love all body-making <note>original: *σωματοποιητικὰς* (somatopoietikas) — Kepler uses this Greek term to refer to shapes capable of forming regular solids or filling a space.</note> shapes, but they have this preference among them: that by continuously subtracting the smaller number from the larger, and the difference from the previous number, all results down to unity must be markers of body-making shapes. For example, the ratio of 5 to 8 is harmonic because 5 taken from 8 leaves 3; 3 taken from 5 leaves 2; 2 taken from 3 leaves 1. And 1, 2, 3, 5, and 8 are all names of adaptable shapes, or markers of a rational part of the circle to be designated geometrically. For 1 signifies the whole, and 2 signifies half the circle. From this boundary, eight ratios arise, to which none can be missing and none added; any of these, when translated into a circle, prescribes a limit to a single aspect <note>An "aspect" in astrology and astronomy refers to the angular distance between planets, such as a trine (120°) or square (90°).</note> of the rays. If someone cannot believe that these ratios are approved by the nature of things, let them turn to Music with these ratios so formed. They will not be testing the rays from voices, but the truly harmonic ratios, which they can perceive less clearly in the obscure example of rays, but can test in the clearer example of Music. Indeed, the voice has nothing in common with light; yet the consonance of voices and the aspects of celestial lights share a common Calculation, as well as a Geometric and World-making <note>original: *Cosmopoetica* — refers to the principles used by God to construct the universe.</note> origin.

Furthermore, one will find in Music that the proportions of strings are exactly the same <note>original: *ad unguem* — literally "to the fingernail," a Latin idiom meaning perfectly or precisely.</note> whenever the sounds harmonize well. But at that point, do not merely count for me the voices of one octave against the signs of the zodiac; for what does it matter if one counts seven or ten intervals in one system through all the notes <note>original: *διὰ πασῶν* (dia pason) — the Greek origin of the word "diapason," referring to the span of an octave.</note>? Rather, let him do this: let him divide the space over which a string is stretched—following the model of the zodiac divided by the seven aspects and stretched out in length—into signs, not deviating a hair's breadth from this Geometric section. Then, let him apply a Bridge <note>original: *Magadium* or *Ephippium* — technical terms for the movable bridge on a monochord used to change pitch by dividing the string.</note> to each of the marks of the divisions. First, let him strike the free string, then both parts of the string resting on the Bridge above that mark of division. It will then appear that just as many pairs or triads of consonant voices are established as my definition of harmonic ratios shows, and as there are celestial aspects in number, with the conjunction added. For with the voice first taken by itself, then seven other pairs, such as 1:1...

VIII. An octave harmonizes with another octave; because this last one bisects the string, the same harmonies will now be found in reverse order in the remaining half of the string to be divided, just as the same <term>aspects</term><note>In astrology, an "aspect" is the specific angular distance between planets that creates a "harmony" or "discord" in a birth chart.</note> are found in the other half of the zodiac. ...that I wonder why it is that astrologers have not followed it, especially since they have searched so anxiously for reasons from every direction. ->To what extent does some significance attach to the locations of the <term>zodiac</term><note>original: "signiferi," literally the "sign-bearer," the belt of the heavens where the planets appear to move.</note> based on the meetings of planets that occur beneath them?<-

### CHAPTER TEN.

I have spoken at length regarding what I attribute to the aspects, and among them, to the conjunctions: Now I must say a few words about the locations of the conjunctions themselves—whether that part of the sphere of the fixed stars, under which two planets meet, conceives some power from that event? Here Pico (in Book 6, Chapter 4), drawing from <note>Panaetius was a Stoic philosopher and Plotinus was the founder of Neoplatonism; both had varying degrees of skepticism regarding the causal powers of stars.</note>Panaetius and Plotinus, bursts into laughter, mocking the astrologers because they attribute properties either of the signs to the planets, or those of the planets to the signs, across such a great space of the world <unclear>spacium</unclear>...
