<meta>Continuing from the previous discussion of the Fiery Trigon and celestial influences on human life, this page moves into specific astrological observations regarding heredity and the imprinting of the heavens on the soul.</meta>

->### ON THE FIERY TRIGON. 43<-

...frequently, from which I am also persuaded of this by analogy. It is this: that for children being born, especially the firstborn, the Planets, as well as the Ascendant <note>original: "Ortus," the point of the zodiac rising on the eastern horizon at birth</note> and the Midheaven <note>original: "Meridies," the highest point the sun reaches in its daily cycle</note>, are generally positioned in those zodiacal signs, or in the squares and oppositions <note>Astrological aspects where planets are 90 or 180 degrees apart, respectively</note> of those places, which were significant in the birth chart <note>original: "genesi"</note> of the father, but especially of the mother; and the aspects <note>The geometric angles between planets</note> occur as either the same or similar, as far as the speed of the stars allows within a space of 14 days surrounding the birth. 

I will give an example, even if the exact hour is unknown, between Frederick II, Emperor of the Romans, and his grandson Conradin <note>Conradin (1252–1268) was the last legitimate male heir of the Hohenstaufen dynasty</note>: The grandson was born in the year 1252, on the 8th day before the Kalends of April <note>March 25th</note>. At that time the Sun was with Jupiter and Venus at 13 degrees of Aries; Mercury at 15 degrees of Aries; Mars at 20 degrees of Cancer; the Moon at 18 degrees of Capricorn; and Saturn at 28 degrees of Sagittarius. The grandfather, however, is recorded as being born in the year 1193, on December 26th. At that time the Sun was at 13 degrees of Capricorn; Mercury and the Moon at 17 degrees of Capricorn; Mars at 18 degrees of Libra; and Saturn at 30 degrees of Sagittarius. You see a wondrous congruence through squares, oppositions, and identical positions.

In this same way I was born, when 40 degrees remained for the Moon to reach opposition with the Sun; for my firstborn, the Moon held the same distance toward the body of the Sun <note>A conjunction, where the planets are at the same degree</note>; for the second, the Moon surpassed the Sun’s opposition by the same amount; for the fourth, the Moon was 38 degrees away from the Sun’s opposition; for the third, it was not much different, for the Moon was 40 degrees away from the Sun’s opposition, plus one further daily motion of the Moon <note>The distance the moon travels in 24 hours</note>, as she was, so to speak, planning to come forth on the day before. I will remain silent on other congruencies.

I do not say these things to suggest that I would pronounce everyone who has the same or similar constellations to be relatives; but rather that relatives generally share the same constellations. This is so true regarding the entire birth chart of a man, but it is most evident in revolutions <note>Solar returns, or the planet's return to its birth position</note> and transits. For as often as the Planets come to those places which were either rising at a man’s birth or had hosted the Sun or Moon, the man’s nature is stirred up to apply itself more eagerly to all the works which are at hand for the man, according to the condition of the places and times. And in general, a birth chart is not easy or happy unless the rays and qualities of the Planets agree aptly and indeed geometrically.

These things, however, cannot happen in any other way than through the imprinting of the "character" <note>A technical term here meaning a lasting seal or formative stamp</note> of the entire position of the sky onto the human faculty itself—the generative, nourishing, formative, sensitive, and animal faculties <note>The traditional Aristotelian divisions of the soul's functions</note>; for the body is too coarse to receive such a subtle character of a thing that is not palpable, but rational. For this reason, I believe it happens that while the rest of the sky runs on into other positions, that character of its position remains as it was at the time when the life of the man was kindled through birth, and was, as it were, recently cast. That which therefore happens...

F 2 <margin>Signature mark for the printer</margin>

<summary>The author argues that family members share striking astrological similarities in their birth charts, providing the examples of Emperor Frederick II and his own children. He proposes that the "character" of the sky at birth is imprinted onto the soul's faculties rather than the physical body.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Kepler’s philosophical investigation into how celestial events influence the Earth, transitioning from a discussion on the "soul" of the Earth to the specific astronomical timing of the "Great Conjunction."</meta>

44 JOHANNES KEPLER

Just as it occurs in individual human beings, it is credible that it also happens in that faculty with which this globe of the Earth is endowed; so that not only is the conjunction of two planets imprinted upon the heavens themselves, but the heavens are imprinted upon <term>sublunary nature</term> <note>The "sublunary" realm refers to the Earth and its atmosphere, which according to Aristotelian physics were subject to change, unlike the "unchanging" heavens above the moon.</note> itself. Along with the heavens, the very location of the <term>Great Conjunction</term> <note>The alignment of the "superior" planets, Jupiter and Saturn, which happens roughly every 20 years and was thought to signal major historical shifts.</note> is impressed there and remains for some time. Let a physician compare this to the example of memory, that faculty by which past events are guarded as if they were perpetually present. There is a great similarity between the two, and a great miracle: the only difference is that the things reflected in memory were either received from the outside through the five senses, or offered to the imagination from within by active reasoning. By contrast, these "species" <note>In 17th-century philosophy, "species" refers to the outward form or "image" of an object that transmits its influence to a perceiver.</note> of celestial things are imprinted inwardly by some hidden method of perception. 

Because of this, just as the things in memory do not stir up nature but rather the reasoning faculty, so these celestial characters, conversely, do not incite reasoning but rather nature itself. Therefore, what memory is to a character of a thing seen, heard, or thought, this faculty of the soul is to the "character of the heavens" which flowed in at the time of birth. Conversely, what the act of reasoning or a new sensation is to the process of remembering (moving us to recall something prior), the new motion of the heavens and the transit of planets through the locations of one’s birth are to this hidden impression. 

However, as this is a very difficult and "slippery" matter, I will not contend with absolute assertion; I am prepared to hear the opinions of other philosophers on this subject. 

I have now said as much as seemed necessary regarding what the <term>Fiery Triplicity</term> <note>The "Fiery Triplicity" (or Trigon) is a group of three zodiac signs—Aries, Leo, and Sagittarius—associated with the element of fire. Kepler is discussing the transition of the Jupiter-Saturn conjunctions into these signs.</note> is and what the Great Conjunction is, through both sciences, Mathematics and Physics. It remains for me to show the true day of this Great Conjunction.

***

### Concerning the time of the Great Conjunction, which initiated the return of the Fiery Triplicity.

### CHAPTER ELEVEN.

I shall do a favor for my chosen adversaries by showing the Astrologers—from a purer Astronomy than they are mostly accustomed to—that moment which is commended to us by the great authorities of the Arabs. For those Masters command us to look diligently at that degree which is on the Eastern horizon at the very moment of the meeting of the superior planets. Truly, oh wretched thing—

--- Page 61 ---
## ON THE FIERY TRIGON. 45

<note>The "Fiery Trigon" refers to the group of three zodiac signs—Aries, Leo, and Sagittarius—associated with the element of fire. A "Great Conjunction" of Jupiter and Saturn in these signs was considered a major historical and astrological omen.</note>

Regarding this wretched and desperate matter, Cyprianus Leovitius <note>A Bohemian astronomer known for his eclipses and conjunction tables.</note> records it as occurring at noon on December 18/28; Maginus, based on the *Prutenic Tables* <note>original: "ex Prutenicis"; these were planetary tables based on Copernicus's theories.</note>, places it at noon on December 14/24. Calculations based on Tycho Brahe—so far as they could be corrected in passing by that author while he was alive—point to December 7/17. In my own forecast for the year 1603, having considered both Brahe’s observations and my own, I placed it on the 6/16, 7/17, or 8/18 of December. <note>The double dates reflect the transition from the old Julian calendar to the new Gregorian calendar, which were ten days apart at this time.</note> Behold the vanity of a rule that commands us to choose a single, true degree out of the <term>degrees of the zodiac</term> <note>The 360 degrees of the celestial circle.</note> (of which 361 revolve in a single day), when in the meantime the astronomers—from whom the science of motions must be sought—cannot agree within a span of thirteen whole days. And should anyone believe this canon of the astrologers, as if it were established by experience, when astronomy has never been better than it is today? Add to this that not even the senses themselves (should one happen to witness the <term>conjunction</term> <note>When two celestial bodies appear closest together in the sky.</note> with their own eyes) can pronounce a judgment more subtle than "on such-and-such a day." For the daily motion of Saturn was 7 <term>minutes</term> <note>original: "7/"; a minute is 1/60th of a degree.</note>, and Jupiter's was 13 minutes. Daily, therefore, Jupiter overtook Saturn by no more than 6 minutes. Yet the body of Jupiter equals about 3½ minutes, and Saturn's about 2½ minutes. Therefore, for an entire day, Saturn and Jupiter will appear to cling to one another, their bodies joined.

Nevertheless, receive what I have achieved through observation, and by these results, judge the observations of that man, Crabbius. He produces them as if from **Apollo’s Tripod** <note>A metaphor for a divine or infallible oracle that requires no proof.</note>, adding no details of either his instruments or his calculations—just as if the matter were so easy that all deceptions in observing could be excluded, or as if it were enough, once you have usurped the name of "Astronomer," to establish the authority of an observation simply by asserting it boldly.

Since the Sun approached Jupiter on November 17/27, 1603, and approached Saturn on November 18/28, it happened that for many succeeding days the planets lay hidden, concealed beneath the Sun’s rays.

I first saw Jupiter with Mercury after two days of long rains on the morning of December 7/17, in a very clear dawn which overwhelmed the fainter light of Saturn so that it could not be seen. Mercury stood above Jupiter, declining slightly to the right from Jupiter’s vertical line; the distance from Jupiter to Mercury was, by ocular estimation, about 5 or 6 degrees.

From this, a <term>decuman wind</term> <note>original: "ventus decumanus"; a term used to describe a wind of extraordinary size or force, like a "tenth wave."</note> arose which lasted two whole days with great violence. At the same time, it snowed heavily. This was followed by fog, warmth, and again heavy rain until December 14/24. The public daily records of events <note>Early forms of newspapers or gazettes.</note> report that on the 7/17, 8/18, and 9/19 of December, all along the coasts of Portugal and Saxony, the ocean was so stirred up that it brought much damage and destruction to several hundred men.

Finally, on December 15/25, during the very Christmas holidays, as a morning serenity shone forth, the opportunity of the heavens was granted to me; the description of which I provide to the reader in the letter on **F 3**.

46 **JOHANNES KEPLER**

In the following letter, which I finished writing on the same day, I will offer it to be read. This, then, is the letter:

I KNOW that you are not usually interrupted by the solemnities of this day <note>December 25, 1603</note>; however, the heavens recognize no holidays. Throughout these days, as the requirements of my office suggested, I have been attentive to the sight of the **great conjunction** <note>The "Great Conjunction" is the meeting of Jupiter and Saturn, which happens every 20 years and was considered a major astrological and astronomical event.</note>. For I had written that it would appear eight days earlier than was commonly thought. Truly, I saw no clear morning until this very day.

It happened, therefore, by a beautiful chance, that on this very morning, when the holy birthday of Christ dawned, three planets—Saturn, Jupiter, and Mercury—arranged in the form of a triangle, appeared in the light of the bright dawn. And so, if any by chance looked toward the sky, this appearance undoubtedly gave them strong faith concerning the beginning of the **Fiery Trigon** <term>Fiery Trigon: A period of roughly 200 years during which the Great Conjunctions of Jupiter and Saturn occur in the "fire" zodiac signs (Aries, Leo, and Sagittarius).</term>. For the common people are otherwise persuaded that the Fiery Trigon, about which there is so much talk today, is a celestial wonder, ignited and in the shape of a triangle.

Therefore, if it pleases you to look upon this triangle with your own eyes, you will still find the planets joined tomorrow morning, even if the triangle will not retain the same beauty tomorrow due to the daily motion of Mercury, but will instead degenerate into an obtuse angle.

Furthermore, the arrangement of the planets this morning was as follows:

-> [Illustration of a morning horizon featuring a church and several houses. In the sky above, three points are connected by dotted lines to form a triangle. The top point is marked with the symbol for Saturn (♄), the bottom-right with Jupiter (♃), and the bottom-left with Mercury (☿). A diagonal line cutting through the scene is labeled "Eclipt:" <note>The Ecliptic, or the path the sun appears to follow.</note>] <-

Therefore, regarding the day of the conjunction of Saturn and Jupiter—which was attributed by other astronomers to yesterday, but by me was placed on the 16th, 17th, or 18th of December—it is clear to the eye from this observation that it far preceded this and the previous day. I will prove this by the following calculation.

At an Azimuth <note>An angle used to determine the horizontal position of a star.</note> of 45 1/2°, Saturn and Jupiter coincided on the same vertical line, and the difference in altitude between Jupiter and Saturn—that is, the true distance between Saturn and Jupiter as far as the dawn light allowed—was 1° 32', with Jupiter being lower.
<margin>Trust the assessment.</margin>
Let the location of Jupiter be assumed as 9° 40' of Sagittarius according to Magini <note>Giovanni Paolo Magini, a contemporary Italian astronomer whose tables Kepler often consulted.</note>: latitude 53' North. Therefore, the right ascension of Jupiter is 24° 8'. And from the observed azimuth, the right ascension of the **midheaven** <term>Midheaven (medium caeli): The point of the ecliptic that is at its highest on the local meridian.</term> becomes—

**CONCERNING THE FIERY TRIGON. 47**

The distance of the midheaven <note>Original Latin: *dist. caeli*. This refers to the Right Ascension of the Medium Coeli, a coordinate used to determine the position of the sky at a specific moment.</note> was 196° 46', and the Sun's was 273° 23'. The difference is 76° 37', which equals 5 hours and 6 minutes. Therefore, the time was 6:54 in the morning, with 17° 40' of Sagittarius rising on the horizon, so that the <term>Nonagesimal</term> <note>The point on the ecliptic—the Sun's path—that is exactly 90 degrees from the horizon; it is the highest point of the ecliptic at that moment.</note> was at 17° 40' of Virgo. From this, according to the 24th problem in Chapter 11 of my *Optical Part of Astronomy* <note>Kepler's *Astronomiae Pars Optica*, published in 1604, which dealt with the math of celestial observation.</note>, the distance of the Nonagesimal from the zenith <note>The point directly overhead.</note> was 51° 5 1/2', and the distance of the Nonagesimal from Jupiter was approximately 82° 20'. 

Consequently, the vertical angle (where Saturn and Jupiter were located) relative to the <term>Ecliptic</term> <note>The apparent path of the Sun and planets across the sky.</note> was 50° 46 1/2'. For this reason, if the observed distance of 1' 32" is broken down into longitude and latitude, the results are 1' 11" for latitude and 58" for longitude, by which Jupiter had moved past Saturn. But since the maximum difference in daily motion—the speed by which Jupiter overtakes Saturn—is at most 6 minutes, and often less: then, by dividing 58 by 6, the result shows a difference of at least 10 days, and likely more. Therefore, this conjunction did not actually take place on the 24th.

There remain 17 days and 0 5/6 hours in the morning, with Saturn elevated above Jupiter. The date is quite certain, which I was also able to predict in my *Prognosticon* <note>A "Prognosticon" was an astronomical calendar or almanac where astronomers predicted planetary positions and their supposed effects.</note> from the observations of Tycho <note>referring to Tycho Brahe, Kepler's predecessor and the most accurate observer of the pre-telescopic era</note> and my own, stating that it would be on the 16th, 17th, or 18th of December. But the pronouncement of the exact hour is most uncertain. For as was said above, even if these two planets touched each other with their very bodies, with no difference in latitudes—which nevertheless was 58 1/3 minutes in our case—it could scarcely be discerned within the space of a single day which one was ahead or behind the other.

<margin>The true time of the great conjunction <note>A "Great Conjunction" is the meeting of Jupiter and Saturn, occurring roughly every 20 years. This 1603 event was particularly significant to Kepler.</note> of Saturn and Jupiter.</margin>

However, this observation, such as it is, is also moderately confirmed by the calculated positions of Saturn and Jupiter, which I have completed since that time based on Tycho's data and a few of my own observations. For through these, at noon on the 17th/27th <note>original: "17/27 Decembris." This double date accounts for the difference between the Old Style (Julian) and New Style (Gregorian) calendars, which were both in use in Europe at this time.</note> of December, in the year 1603, Saturn is placed at 8° 8' 38'' of Sagittarius with a Northern latitude of 1° 41' 5''. Jupiter is at 8° 5' 32'' of Sagittarius with a Northern latitude of 0° 36' 5''. Therefore, at noon, Jupiter would have stood three minutes before Saturn. The calculation differs from the observation by 23 hours, and nothing more. The difference in latitudes was 1° 5', which after 8 days appeared to me to be 58 1/3'; the discrepancy of 6 2/3 minutes could very easily have arisen from that; if Saturn were not precisely in the same vertical line as Jupiter. For when the latitudes of planets are observed with dedicated effort, the matter is not left to such slippery methods. Therefore the reader, by this proximity, ought to be con—

...to be content. Thus far, therefore, we have only investigated the time of the Great Conjunction, neglecting its true location in the zodiac.

Come now, let us consult other circumstances by which we may be made certain of the true positions of the planets in longitude and latitude. First, let us deal with Mercury from December 22nd/23rd. For when I had brought the sights <note original: "pinnacidia">The viewing vanes or sights on an astronomical instrument, such as a sextant or quadrant, used to align the instrument with a star or planet.</note> of my instrument to a proximity of one degree and 59 minutes (for I could not squeeze them any closer), it appeared that the distance between Jupiter and Mercury was smaller. They were, therefore, less distant than 1° 59', and on a line that was nearly parallel to the horizon; however, Mercury was slightly more elevated. It formed a scalene triangle <note>A triangle where all three sides and angles are of different lengths.</note>, whose smallest angle was at Mercury, the largest at Jupiter, and the medium one at Saturn. The sides were in the proportion of these numbers: 10, 13, 14, or 10, 13, 15.

We shall first compare these to one another. For we know from what preceded that the distance between Saturn and Jupiter was 1° 15' 20''. If this distance is taken as 10 parts, then the distance between Jupiter and Mercury was about 13 of those same parts. If, therefore, 10 parts equal 1° 15' 20''—or 75 1/3 minutes—then 13 parts will equal 1° 38', which is certainly less than 1° 59', as was expected.

And since the vertical angle through Jupiter with the Ecliptic <note>The apparent path of the Sun across the sky; the primary line of reference for planetary positions.</note> and its parallel was previously found to be 50° 46', the angle of that same vertical with the horizon and its nearby parallels will be 39° 14'. But because Mercury, which was to the left, was raised slightly more than Jupiter, let the line through the planets be inclined to the horizon by an angle of 10 degrees. Therefore, between the Ecliptic and this line, the angle will be 49° 14'. This angle, when sought in the margin of our parallactic table, shows under column 49 (which is half of 1° 38', the distance between Jupiter and Mercury) half of the amount by which Mercury is further north than Jupiter, namely 37' 6''. Therefore, the difference in latitudes is 1° 14' 12''. 

Likewise, the complement of that angle, which is 40° 46', shows from the same column half of the difference in longitude, namely 32'. Thus, Mercury is further along <term>in consequentia</term> <note original: "in consequentia">Meaning "in the order of the signs," or moving eastward along the zodiac.</note> by 1° 4', which we can safely state with the risk of very few scruples <note original: "scrupulorum">In this context, small units of measurement or minor errors in calculation.</note>. And since Magini <note>Giovanni Antonio Magini (1555–1617) was a prominent Italian astronomer whose planetary tables were widely used for horoscopes and navigation.</note> makes the daily motion of Mercury from the preceding day 55 minutes, and that of Jupiter 13 minutes, the daily excess is therefore 42 minutes. Therefore, 36 1/2 hours previously passed absorbed the distance of 64 minutes. <margin>Time of the true conjunction of Jupiter and Mercury.</margin> Thus, on the evening of December 22nd/23rd at 6:30, the true conjunction of Jupiter and Mercury occurred according to longitude, with a difference in latitude of 1° 14', while the planets were below the horizon.

And since Saturn on the 25th stood 48 minutes prior to <note original: "in praecedentia">Moving westward, or appearing earlier in the sequence of zodiac signs.</note> Jupiter, by adding 1° 4', the difference in the longitudes of Saturn and Mercury was...

was 1° 52'. But Mercury's motion over three days is 2° 35': Saturn's is 21' <note>minutes of arc</note>, according to Magini <note>Giovanni Antonio Magini, an Italian astronomer whose planetary tables Kepler often consulted</note>. Therefore, the three-day gain is 2° 14'. Consequently, 2 days and 12 hours earlier—specifically on the evening of **December 12/22** <note>Kepler provides dates in both the Julian (Old Style) and Gregorian (New Style) calendars, written as a fraction</note> at 7 o'clock—there was a conjunction of Saturn and Mercury, with a very slight difference in latitude. For Saturn was also more than one degree north of Jupiter, just as Mercury was. 

<margin>The time of the true conjunction of Saturn and Mercury.</margin>

Behold the cause of the heavy rain which occurred on the 22nd, and why clarity was restored from the evening of the 23rd, when Mercury began to separate from Jupiter.

And since Mercury is otherwise very powerful in stirring up storms (the cause of which must be explained to diligent investigators of nature), the reason is partially clear why the first part of winter was so mild. For on **October 3/13**, Mercury first approached Jupiter, and on **November 5**, it first approached Saturn. Then, on **November 12/22**, with its course reversed <note>Kepler is referring to "apparent retrograde motion," where a planet appears to move backward in the sky from Earth's perspective</note> (which itself disturbs the air on its own: not because the planet truly becomes retrograde, but because the nature of <term>sublunary</term> <note>the region of the universe below the moon, i.e., the Earth and its atmosphere</note> things imagines this to be so for itself based on the position of the Earth, just as human eyes do; here I respond in passing to Pico <note>Giovanni Pico della Mirandola, who wrote a famous critique of astrology</note> based on the principles emphasized above), at the beginning of December it came to the Sun, then to Venus. Immediately on **November 25/December 5**, it came a second time to Saturn, and on **November 27/December 7**, it came a second time to Jupiter; because a few days later it was found to be 5 or 6 degrees distant from Jupiter, while the tables barely indicated 3 degrees, as mentioned above. Around **December 3/13**, with its course reversed again, on **December 6/16 and 7/17**, during which that horrible wind blew, it struck a <term>sextile</term> <note>an astrological aspect where two planets are 60 degrees apart</note> of Mars; and so finally on **December 12/22**, it reached Saturn for the third time, and on **December 13/23**, it reached Jupiter for the third time: I will say nothing for now of the very frequent meetings and configurations of the other planets.

To confirm the position of Mercury, on **December 18/28**, we began measuring its distance from **Spica** <note>the brightest star in the constellation Virgo</note>, which was 55° 53', corrected by the observation of other well-known distances. And since this distance is transverse to the Ecliptic—because Spica is in the South and Mercury is in the North—it will correspond to a longitude of approximately 55° 47'. Adding this to the position of Spica at 18° 19' of Libra produces a longitude for Mercury of 14° 6' Sagittarius, and a little beyond, because Mercury was being lifted toward Spica by <term>refraction</term> <note>the bending of light as it passes through the atmosphere, which makes celestial objects appear higher than they are</note>. Now, for the time before **December 18/28**, take the position of Jupiter as 9° 45' Sagittarius from the calculation, and add the distance of Mercury found on 그날 [that day] as 1° 4': so that the position of Mercury is 10° 49' Sagittarius. Therefore, the three-day motion is 3° 17': not much different in Magini.

On the same day, **December 18/28**, we found the distance of Jupiter from the **Heart of the Scorpion** <note>the star Antares</note> to be 7° 25': yet not without doubt, because of a "perverse sight" <note>original: "perverso pinnacidio"; likely a misalignment or error in the sighting mechanism of his instrument</note>, as the annotation says. And because the altitude was nearly the same, there is no danger from refraction. Now, however, unless we assume something already known, nothing—

...we shall build nothing from scratch. Let us take the known Northern latitude of Jupiter from calculations as 0° 36'. For it changes very little from the 7th/17th day of December. <note>The double dates reflect the difference between the Julian and Gregorian calendars, which were both in use in Europe at this time.</note> Since the latitude of the **Heart of the Scorpion** <note>original: "cordis Scorpij"; the star known today as Antares</note> is 4° 27' South, and its longitude is 4° 16' 30" of Sagittarius. Thus, since the sum of the latitudes is 5° 3', the difference in longitudes will be about 5 2/3'. Therefore, Jupiter is at 9° 44' Sagittarius: a difference from the calculation of only one minute, no more—which is surprising given the misaligned <term>pinnacidium</term> <note>a sighting vane or aiming device on an astronomical instrument</note> and such brightness of the dawn. But from this, the calculation of Jupiter's position at noon on December 7th, deduced above, is also confirmed.

Indeed, Saturn's position is obtained from Jupiter; because it was proven above that Saturn was 48' ahead of Jupiter. Thus, it was approximately at 8° 57 1/2' Sagittarius, with a latitude of 1° 40' North. However, the calculation corrected that amount of 48' somewhat. Therefore, Saturn was more truly at 9° 1' Sagittarius.

It has been shown so far on which days Saturn and Jupiter met, and on which days Mercury met with both. But because, according to the teaching of **Cyprian Leovitius** <note>Cyprian Leowitz (1524–1574), a famed Bohemian astronomer whose predictions about the Great Conjunction and the end of the world were widely read</note>, Mars approaching completes the proper measure of a **Great Conjunction**: come, let us also add the observations of this planet. Thus, at the time when Saturn and Jupiter met, Mars was lingering in Libra, having entered that sign on December 8th; so at the beginning of the year 1604, it proceeded through the **sextiles** <note>an astrological aspect where planets are 60 degrees apart</note> of both planets. Meanwhile, the Sun, having passed the triangle of Mars, showed the planet to be **stationary** <note>appearing to stop in the sky before beginning retrograde motion</note> near the beginning of March, when it had reached the final minutes of the 27th degree of Libra. Following those tracks backward from there, in the month of April, it hurried into opposition with the Sun, very near to **Spica** <note>the brightest star in the constellation Virgo</note>. In the month of May, having finished its stationary period at 8 2/3 Libra, it finally hurried on its direct path toward the superior planets <note>the planets further from the Sun than Earth: Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn</note>; and on July 15/25, it at last exited Libra, after having stayed there for more than seven months. On September 7/17, it entered Sagittarius: accompanied by much rain and cold winds due to the **aspects** <note>the geometric angles between planets</note>, which you may see in the **Ephemerides** <note>astronomical almanacs listing the daily positions of celestial bodies</note>, not neglecting the **Quintiles** <note>an aspect where planets are 72 degrees apart</note>. Especially on the days of September 18/28, 21/31, and 22/1, the sky was unstable and rainy, with cold winds; for both the Sun was in quintile and Mercury was in sextile with Mars. The day of September 2/12 dawned clear; therefore, in the evening, I kept watch on the movements of the planets. But since the Sun had entered Libra, and this along with the following sign of Scorpio are signs of **short descensions** <note>zodiac signs that rise and set at a steeper angle, meaning they remain above the horizon for a shorter time</note>, not much time could pass between the end of twilight and the setting of the planets.

Therefore, for the latitudes of the planets, I measured Jupiter’s distance from the **head of Ophiuchus** <note>the star Rasalhague</note> as 35° 38'; and Saturn's from the same once as 35° 5 1/2', and again as 35° 9', where the distance between the **Lyre** and the **Eagle** <note>the stars Vega and Altair</note> was 34° 17'. (For I use instruments far inferior to those of **Tycho** <note>Tycho Brahe, the famous Danish astronomer known for his incredibly precise instruments</note>, and those which...

In the following days, there were extremely heavy rains with winds and great cold; this was because Venus and Mercury had met in Libra, and Mars and Saturn in Sagittarius, both pairs in each other's sextiles <note>A "sextile" is an astrological aspect where planets are 60 degrees apart, thought to be a harmonious or productive influence.</note>; nor was Jupiter far away. Whosoever among the philosophers is hostile toward me because of the new doctrines by which I establish the influence of Aspects and Conjunctions <note>Kepler was a pioneer in "astrometeorology," believing the geometric angles (aspects) between planets physically affected Earth's weather.</note>; I ask him to endure the tedium of even a single year, comparing the planetary aspects with the storms: or, if he is persuaded that he is dealing with an honest man, let him diligently consider this single year, day by day, just as I have recorded it. I know that if he is eager to learn and a lover of truth, he will yield. <note>original: "manus dabit" — literally "will give his hands," an idiom meaning to concede an argument.</note>

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;On September 16/26, <note>Kepler provides dates in both the Julian (Old Style) and Gregorian (New Style) calendars.</note> the sky glowed, a sign of restored clarity, or of moisture in the cold; this frequently happens when Mars is in turmoil.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Therefore, on the following day, September 17/27, the western part of the sky being free of clouds, I returned to observing the planets: a beautiful spectacle! Four planets—Saturn, Jupiter, Mars, and the crescent Moon—were in the same area. Saturn, Jupiter, and the Moon were in a straight line; Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn formed a scalene triangle. There was a very short distance between Mars and Saturn, then between the Moon and Saturn, then Jupiter and Mars, then Jupiter and Saturn, and finally Jupiter and the Moon. Mars set first, then the Moon, then Saturn, and finally Jupiter. It appeared to the eye that the conjunction of Mars and Saturn had already passed. I believe it is evident from this record that on the day of September 17/27, the **new star** had not yet been ignited; we measured these positions with the sextant before they set.

->**ON THE FIERY TRIGON.**<- 53

Between the last star of the tail of the Bear <note>The star Alkaid in the constellation Ursa Major.</note> and the Crown <note>The constellation Corona Borealis.</note>: 30° 32'.
It should have been 30° 36¾'.
Therefore, the <term>sextant</term> <note>A tool used to measure the angular distance between two stars.</note> was in error by about 4 minutes, due to the inverted position of the <term>sight-vane</term> <note>original Latin: "pinnacidij"; the small plate or sighting mechanism on an astronomical instrument.</note>. Accordingly, I am correcting the other distances from this point.

As for the rules handed down by Tycho Brahe <note>The famous Danish astronomer (1546–1601) known for his incredibly accurate planetary observations.</note> or others for calculating the positions of the planets, you may seek those elsewhere. My goal here is not to see how elaborately I can work, but how briefly I can achieve the same results as they do—provided it is done reliably. For "life is short, and the craft is long." <note>original: "vita brevis, ars longa"; a famous aphorism suggesting that the time we have to learn and practice is limited compared to the vastness of knowledge.</note>

The Head of Ophiuchus <note>The star Rasalhague.</note> is at this time at 16° 53½' of Sagittarius. <margin>A shortcut <note>The original Greek phrase here, though partially obscured in the OCR, refers to an "easy way" or "shortcut" for trigonometric calculations.</note> for solving the two types of triangles common in observations.</margin>
The Shoulder of Sagittarius <note>Likely the star Nunki.</note> is at 6° 54½' of Capricorn.
From this, I subtract the distance of 27° 21', as if the entire distance were measured along the line of longitude; this leaves a "fictitious" position for Saturn at approximately 9° 33' of Sagittarius. Thus, the difference in longitude between the Head of Ophiuchus and Saturn is roughly 7°.

54 JOHANNES KEPLER

...appeared here, because of refraction <note>original: "ob refractionem"; the bending of light as it passes through the atmosphere, which makes celestial objects appear higher than they are</note>: in longitude, refractions disturb nothing or very little, because the fixed star was at almost the same altitude as Saturn.

### For the position of Jupiter.
The bright star of the quadrilateral of Sagittarius <note>A group of stars in the constellation Sagittarius, likely referring to the "Teapot" asterism or specific stars like Sigma Sagittarii</note> is at 6° 54 1/2' of Capricorn.
The distance of Jupiter from it: 19° 44'.
Therefore, Jupiter is as if at 17° 10 1/2' of Sagittarius.
But the Head of Ophiuchus <note>The star Ras Alhague</note> is at 16° 53 1/2'.
Difference: 0° 17'. Secant: 100001. <note>Kepler is using trigonometric tables where the radius is set to 100,000</note>

This value, used as a divisor, does not change the distance from the head of Ophiuchus, 35° 38'. Therefore, subtract this from the latitude of Ophiuchus, 35° 57'; there remain 19 minutes for the northern latitude of Jupiter. And these, added to 3° 31', constitute 3° 50'. The secant is 100224: with this, divide the secant of 19° 44', which is 106239. The result is 19° 22' 24", which you subtract from 6° 54' of Capricorn; there remains 17° 32' of Sagittarius as the true position of Jupiter; unless perhaps refraction disturbs something, which is a small amount: but certainly something, and not to be despised, will be found in the latitude.

And these observations agree moderately well with those of four days ago: For that day's distance between the Lyre <note>The star Vega</note> and the Eagle <note>The star Altair</note> indicates an instrument error of 4 minutes. Therefore, when a little earlier the distance between Saturn and the head of Ophiuchus was observed to be 35° 9', it was too much by that same amount; whereas when it was previously 35° 5 1/2', it was correct, and the sight-vane <term>pinnacidium|the small slotted plate or sighting mechanism on an astronomical instrument used for aligning the eye with the star</term> in the intervening time seems to have been moved from its place by a bump, as usually happens. Therefore, between Jupiter and the head of Ophiuchus it is truly 35° 38', which, subtracted from the latitude of Ophiuchus, leaves the latitude of Jupiter at 19', because the longitude is the same. But the latitude of Saturn (taking the difference of longitude from the head of Ophiuchus as 7° 13 1/2' and the distance as 35° 5 1/2': thus a difference in latitudes of 34° 26') is calculated to be 1° 31' North—three minutes less than four days later, because on the 22nd/27th of September <note>Kepler provides both the Julian and Gregorian calendar dates</note> it was high and more free from refractions.

### For the position of Mars on the day of September 22/27.
At noon on that day, the calculation—not yet most perfectly corrected in every respect—showed the position of Mars at 10° 38' Sagittarius, with a latitude of 1° 36' South. Since, therefore, Mars was distant from the Eagle <note>Altair</note> by 53° 8', and that fixed star is at 26° 12 1/2' of Capricorn with a latitude of 29° 21 1/2' North: assuming a latitude for Mars of 1° 30 1/2' South, its position comes out to 10° 55' Sagittarius. And since it was distant from the shoulder of Sagittarius by 26° 5', which is at 6° 54 1/2' Capricorn, with a latitude of 3° 31' South: re-calculating with the same latitude for Mars, the position of Mars comes out as 10° 53' Sagittarius: because these results do not agree, I examine another latitude for Mars, 1° 24', which gives me a position there of 10° 50'.

**CONCERNING THE FIERY TRIGON. 55**
<note>The "Fiery Trigon" refers to the grouping of the three zodiac signs associated with the element of fire: Aries, Leo, and Sagittarius. Great conjunctions occurring in these signs were thought by 17th-century astronomers to herald major world events.</note>

10. 50' Sagittarius; here 10. 54' Sagittarius. Therefore, I understand by these two observations that the latitude is established at 1° 29' South and the location at 10° 53 1/2' Sagittarius. However, the same altitude did not remain for him <note>referring to Mars</note> in both distances, and the method of wanting to define the location of Mars in longitude and latitude through two such stars—both standing in the same region of the zodiac—is very slippery. Yet I could not observe the planet in any other way as it was near its **heliacal setting** <term>Heliacal setting: The time when a planet becomes lost in the sun's glare as it approaches conjunction with the sun.</term>. For that reason, I also bypassed that very long operation which Tycho Brahe used in his *Introductory Works* <note>original: *Progymnasmata*</note>; as it would have sought precision in vain within a crude observation. Nevertheless, it agrees well enough with the calculation: for if at noon its location was 10° 38', and the daily motion 42'; then for 8 hours, 14' are owed. Thus, the location of Mars at our 8th hour is 10° 52' Sagittarius. But the fact that the latitude is 7 minutes <note>original: *scrupulis*</note> smaller, and Mars is that much higher and closer to the **Ecliptic** <term>Ecliptic: The apparent path of the Sun across the celestial sphere; the plane of Earth's orbit.</term> than in the calculation, is due to refraction; because Mars was being observed near the horizon.

From these observations, the true day of the conjunction of Saturn and Mars is also clear. <margin>True</margin> <margin>time</margin> <margin>of the</margin> <margin>conjunction</margin> <margin>of Satur-</margin> <margin>n and Mar-</margin> <margin>s.</margin> For if Saturn on this day, September 17/27, at the 8th hour was in 10° 1' Sagittarius, and Mars was in 10° 38' Sagittarius; then on the preceding day, September 16/26, in the evening after the 8th hour, they were nearest to each other in longitude at approximately 10° Sagittarius—very close to the location of the meeting of Saturn and Jupiter which occurred the previous December. They were distant from the Ecliptic by almost equal spaces in opposite directions, and separated from each other by a little more than three degrees, with Saturn being the higher.

Finally, the true day of the conjunction of Jupiter and Mars, by which the **Great Conjunction** <note>The grouping of all three superior planets: Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn.</note> is at last completed, is held from these observations. <margin>True</margin> <margin>day of the</margin> <margin>con-</margin> <margin>junction of</margin> <margin>Jupiter and</margin> <margin>Mars.</margin> For a stormy night <note>original: *nox intempesta*</note> held the day and location in a cloud; although on the days immediately before and after, the sky could be seen through gaps in the clouds, and it could easily be discerned whether the conjunction was past or imminent.

On the 29th of September or 9th of October, at noon, the location of Jupiter (from a sufficiently reliable analogy of the observation previously held, and the daily motion) was in 19° 12' Sagittarius. At the same noon, the calculation (which you have just now seen confirmed by observation) shows the location of Mars in 19° 14' Sagittarius with a South latitude of 1° 36', which during this intermediate time was, so to speak, stationary, and on this occasion at its maximum. Therefore, one hour before noon, there had been the full conjunction of Jupiter and Mars in 19° 12' Sagittarius, with Jupiter being further north than Mars by almost two degrees; since immediately on the following day, in 17° 40' Sagittarius (a degree and a half earlier), the **new star** <note>The Supernova of 1604, often called Kepler's Star.</note> first appeared.

Therefore, just as in the universe Saturn is the highest, Jupiter the middle, and Mars the lowest of the three; so in this visible Great Conjunction of the three superior planets, Saturn was also visually the highest in the north, <margin>Compari-</margin> <margin>son of the</margin> <margin>new</margin> <margin>star.</margin>

56          JOHANNES KEPLER

...[from the] north, so that Jupiter passed beneath it; likewise, Jupiter was to the north, so that Mars, being further south, passed beneath both of them. However, the New Star <note>The supernova of 1604, now known as Kepler's Supernova</note> shone more brightly than all three, both in terms of its distance from the world's center <note>original: "mundi diametrum"; Kepler is referring to the star's apparent altitude or cosmic depth</note> and its position on the Ecliptic <note>The apparent path of the Sun across the sky</note>.

<margin>Conclusion of the description of this Fiery Trigon.</margin>

          And so, this is that most celebrated Great Conjunction of the three superior planets <note>The "superior" planets are those with orbits further from the Sun than Earth's: Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn</note>. After seven great revolutions of eight hundred years have passed since the foundation of the world, this conjunction occurred for the first time again in the sign of Sagittarius, which is the third sign of the Fiery Triplicity <term>Fiery Triplicity: Also called the Fiery Trigon, this is a group of three zodiac signs—Aries, Leo, and Sagittarius—associated by astrologers with the element of fire</term>. I transmit this astronomical description, prepared with as much care as possible, to be read by those who will be born during these succeeding eight hundred years—if indeed these records are worthy and those future people are capable; and provided that the world has not perished, or some universal plague (such as those we read about in history) has not wiped out the greater part of the human race and driven the survivors back from their books to the plow from which they were taken, according to that saying: *YOU ARE EARTH* (meaning a farmer) *AND TO EARTH* (meaning to agriculture) *YOU SHALL RETURN* <note>original: "TERRA ES... ET AD TERRAM... REVERTERIS." Kepler is punning on the Latin "terra" (earth/soil) and the biblical "Dust thou art," suggesting a regression from civilization back to subsistence farming</note>. Or perhaps some wild flood of barbarian nations might inundate a Europe made effeminate by luxury and dissolved by license, utterly rooting out all arts and this prodigious abundance of books:

          *Just as when a storm cloud, under a broken star, moves toward the lands*
          *Across the middle of the sea: alas, the hearts of the wretched*
          *Farmers, sensing it from afar, shudder; that storm will bring ruin*
          *To the trees and slaughter to the crops; it will strike down everything far and wide.*
          *The winds fly ahead and carry the sound to the shores.*
               *And now, the dikes having burst, the foaming river*
          *Has rushed out and overcome the opposing mounds with its whirlpool;*
          <note>Kepler is quoting and adapting classical Latin imagery, likely from Virgil, to describe the potential collapse of civilization</note>

But let these matters be the concern of the gods above. ***

Observations of this new star.
### CHAPTER XII.

It is memorable, and should be added to what came before, that in all places from which the report of the new star reached me, the sky was rainy for several continuous days during this conjunction of Jupiter and Mars, and during the very rising of the star. On the day of October 7/17 <note>Kepler provides dates in both the old Julian and the new Gregorian calendars.</note>, with clarity restored, I observed these things with an iron <term>sextant</term> <note>An instrument used to measure the angular distance between objects in the sky.</note>, which in the following days was found to be off by several minutes.
After the sun had set, while it was still bright day, we saw it <note>the New Star</note> high enough before either Jupiter or any other star could be discerned.

-># ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT BEARER. 59<-

To test the sextant <note>An instrument used to measure the angular distance between two stars</note>; we took the distance between the **Heart of the Lion** <note>original: "cor Leonis," the star Regulus</note> and the **Ear of Corn of the Virgin** <note>original: "spicam Virginis," the star Spica</note> twice as 54° 2': it ought to have been 54° 2'.
And between the **Ear of Corn of the Virgin** and the **Southern Scale** <note>original: "lancem Austrinam," the star Zubenelgenubi in the constellation Libra</note> 21° 24': it ought to have been 21° 22'.
To be brief, in the months of March and April, I measured the meridian altitude <note>The height of a celestial body above the horizon when it is at its highest point in the sky</note> of the New Star as 18° 47', or 18° 48' indiscriminately, while that of the **left knee** <note>of Ophiuchus</note> was 30° 9' and the **right** 24° 48'. I omit the particulars to avoid causing boredom. It culminated, however, with the slightest difference before or after the small star in the heel of the right foot of Ophiuchus.

It will be beneficial to add to these observations of ours those <margin>David Fabricius, a diligent astronomer.</margin> which David Fabricius made in East Frisia: a man indeed of such standing in astronomical matters that, since the diligence of observing the heavens died out with the author Brahe <note>Tycho Brahe (1546–1601), the most famous observational astronomer of the era and Kepler's predecessor</note>, all authority in observing rests with him. I very gladly yield that palm <note>A symbol of victory or excellence</note> to him, as far as I am concerned, since I am much hindered by blurred vision. Add also a most sagacious genius in searching out the motions of the planets, and an untiring zeal in contemplation. As far as astrological matters are concerned, I confess indeed that this man sometimes succumbs to the authority of the ancients and the desire for predictions—where these two conspire—and is carried away by a certain kind of enthusiasm beyond reason; but he shares those things in common with a great crowd of learned men. On that account alone he deserves pardon. Why do you snarl, you delicate philosopher, if <margin>An excuse for Astrology.</margin> a foolish daughter—as she seems to you—Astrology, sustains and nourishes her most wise but poor mother, Astronomy, with her trifles? If she <note>Astronomy</note> found no other place among the far more foolish gatherings of men, except through the intercessions of this simplicity? For unless someone had first been so credulous as to hope that he could predict future things from the sky, you would never have become so wise as to think that Astronomy (being unknown) ought to be learned for its own sake. If we are not led to Philosophy except through wisdom, we shall never be led to it. In all admiration and in all desire, while it is raw, there is much vanity; yet this vanity, at the crossroads where one goes toward Philosophy, directs those it meets to a beautiful place. But setting these things aside for now and sending them to their proper place, we shall now extract Fabricius’s own observations from his German booklet, printed at Hamburg, and from letters sent to me. Thus he says:

### CHAPTER XIII.

Because the star was observed in relation to three planets, and because Mars changes its daily motion quickly, you can investigate its location from the observations of October 11/21 <note>Kepler provides both the Julian and Gregorian dates.</note>. This follows the method that Tycho Brahe taught in his *Progymnasmata* <note>Tycho Brahe’s foundational work, "Introductory Exercises to the New Astronomy."</note>; it consists of six operations involving sines <term>sine: a trigonometric function used to calculate angles and distances in the sky</term>, and additionally many additions and subtractions of sines and arcs. The distances examined by this method show the location of Mars at 28° 15 1/2' of Sagittarius, with a southern latitude of 1° 20 1/2'. Calculation for the previous noon shows the location at 28° 2' of Sagittarius. Therefore, since the observation was made around 7:00 and 8:00 PM, and the daily motion is 45', its location at that hour would have been 28° 17' Sagittarius—a beautiful agreement <note>original: "pulcher consensus"</note>. The latitude, however, according to calculation should have been 1° 34 1/2' South, but it appeared as 1° 23 1/2'. Atmospheric refraction <term>refraction: the bending of light by the Earth's atmosphere, which makes objects near the horizon appear higher than they actually are</term> took away the remaining 11 minutes from us, as it lifted Mars toward the North and moved it closer to the Ecliptic <note>the sun's apparent path, used as the baseline for celestial latitude</note>, since it was in the South.

Having established this, let us now proceed as we did in the previous sections. First, because the star appeared to the eye to be to the North, as was Saturn, I take the location of Saturn from the observations of the preceding days and its daily motion as 11° 39' Sagittarius. I add the corrected distance of the new star, 6° 9'; this yields a nearly true location of 17° 48' Sagittarius. The "Head of Ophiuchus" <note>the star Ras Alhague</note> is at 16° 53 1/2' Sagittarius. Therefore, the difference in longitudes is about 54 1/2', the secant <term>secant: a trigonometric ratio used here to calculate the angular distance on a sphere</term> of which is 100012 1/2. The secant of the distance from the Head of Ophiuchus, 34° 2', is 120669. At this position, the secants increase by 24 units for every minute, whereas for the previous secant they increase by only 12 1/2 above the length of the diameter. Thus, the difference in latitudes is about half a minute less than the discovered distance. Since the Head of Ophiuchus has a latitude of 35° 57':

I subtract the difference: 34° 2'.
The latitude of the New Star remains: 1° 55'. From Oct. 21.
Or

### ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT-BEARER. 61

<margin>Or 1° 56'. From 27 October.</margin>
<margin>Or 1° 57'. According to Fabricius and Bürgi <note>referring to astronomers David Fabricius and Jost Bürgi, who provided independent observations</note>.</margin>

This latitude <note>the angular distance north or south of the ecliptic</note> of the New Star is most certain, as far as the observation goes. Therefore, we shall now use this as a certain value in defining its position according to longitude <note>the angular distance along the ecliptic</note>. 

The first of all recorded distances from Jupiter was on the day 17/27 October <note>Kepler provides the date in both the Julian and Gregorian calendars: October 17 and October 27, respectively</note> at 3° 24½' <term>corrected</term> <note>meaning adjusted for atmospheric refraction or instrumental errors</note>, but observed in very bright twilight, when Jupiter (following the pattern of the preceding days) was at 20° 35' of Sagittarius, with a northern latitude of 21 minutes North; or rather, after 20 days had passed, it was a little less, about 13 minutes: for it was decreasing for two reasons. 

I subtract the latitude of Jupiter (13') from the latitude of the New Star (1° 56'). The remainder is 1° 43'. The <term>secant</term> <note>a trigonometric function used to calculate the sides of spherical triangles in astronomy</note> [of 1° 43'] is 100,045; dividing the secant of 3° 24½' (namely 100,177) by this, it establishes a secant of 100,132, whose <term>arc</term> <note>the angle corresponding to the resulting trigonometric value</note> of 2° 56⅔' is subtracted from the position...

64 **JOHANNES KEPLER**

<margin>The location of the new [star] from Fabricius's observations.</margin>

The location of the heart <note>likely referring to the star Antares, the "Heart of the Scorpion"</note> is added: 4° 16' 20'' of Sagittarius.
This yields the location of the New Star: 17° 43' 35'' of Sagittarius.
Therefore, this location differs by 3 minutes <note>original: "scrupulis," a term for minutes of arc</note> from the previous one. But that there was an error in the observation itself is clear from Fabricius's data, which makes this distance 14° 50 1/2'.

Now we will compute the star's location from all of Fabricius's observations:
First, because along with Bürgi <note>Jost Bürgi, a Swiss clockmaker and mathematician</note> he gives the distance of the star from the head of Ophiuchus <note>the star Rasalhague</note> as 34° 0': Therefore, its latitude for him is 1° 57' North <note>original: "Sept.," for Septentrionalis</note>.
II. Because the distance from the knee of Ophiuchus in Fabricius’s data is 6 minutes less than ours using the Tychonic instrument <note>astronomical instruments designed by Tycho Brahe</note>. Therefore, the star’s location is given as 17° 37' of Sagittarius; according to Brengger <note>Johann Georg Brengger, a physician and astronomer</note>, it is 17° 41', or 17° 36'.
III. Because the distance from Aquila <note>the constellation of the Eagle</note> is 2 minutes greater than mine and Bürgi’s, the location is given as 17° 39' of Sagittarius.
IV. The distance from Antares is 2 1/2 minutes less than mine. Thus, the location of the new star is 17° 41' of Sagittarius.

Therefore, Fabricius’s observations are nearly the same as ours, defining the position of the star from 17° 36 2/3' of Sagittarius up to 17° 46 2/3' of Sagittarius; refractions <note>Refraction refers to the way the Earth's atmosphere bends light, making stars appear slightly higher in the sky than they actually are.</note> introduced this variation. In the East, indeed, it seemed to be raised toward the preceding signs; but in the West, toward the following signs.

It is indeed to be noted concerning the right knee of Ophiuchus—which star advances the position of the New Star too far into the following signs for both me and Fabricius (which is undoubtedly the reason why Fabricius omitted this distance, though placed in his Latin copy, from his German one)—that the longitude and latitude in Tycho's <note>Tycho Brahe (1546–1601), Kepler's predecessor, whose star catalog was the gold standard of the era.</note> work differ from its right ascension and declination <term>Right ascension and declination are coordinates used to map objects in the sky, similar to longitude and latitude on Earth.</term> (which are obtained earlier and more certainly) due to some error in calculation. For Tycho Brahe, in the year 1600, sets the right ascension of the right knee of Ophiuchus at 251° 50' and the declination at 15° 7' South. To this, however, corresponds a position of 12° 20 1/2' Sagittarius and a latitude of 7° 17 1/3' North. Brahe sets it at 12° 24' Sagittarius with a latitude of 7° 18' for the year 1600. Thus, for Fabricius, the New Star is placed at 17° 43' Sagittarius via the right knee; for me, in the earlier observation, at 17° 45 1/2', or in the later observation, at 17° 34 1/2' or 17° 35 1/2' Sagittarius, which I have said was caused by the inconvenient placement of the instrument.

And so much for the longitude and latitude of the New Star relative to the Ecliptic <note>The Ecliptic is the apparent path of the Sun across the sky; planets and "new stars" were measured against this line.</note>: it follows that we should establish its right ascension and declination. <margin>Right ascension of the star.</margin> Assuming, therefore, the longitude to be 17° 40' Sagittarius and the latitude 1° 56' North, its Right Ascension results as 256° 47' and its declination as 21° 1 1/2' South.

Thus, it reached the meridian <note>The "meridian" is the highest point the star reaches in the sky on a given night.</note> with 17° 51' Sagittarius; it rose at Prague at 15 1/2' <margin>Declination of the star.</margin> Sagittarius and set at 19 1/2' Sagittarius, almost from the beginning together with Jupiter. And since the altitude of the pole <note>The "altitude of the pole" refers to the latitude of the city of Prague (approx. 50° North).</note> at Prague is 50° 6' and the Equator is 39° 54', subtracting the southern declination from this gives the meridian altitude of the star as 18° 52 1/2', which I found to be 18° 48' or 49' using a two-cubit quadrant <note>A quadrant is an instrument used to measure the height of stars; "two-cubit" refers to its size, about 3 feet in radius.</note>, the testing of which was done by the left knee...

<summary>Kepler compares his mathematical coordinates for the 1604 supernova with those of David Fabricius, identifying errors in Tycho Brahe's previous star catalogs. He calculates the star's specific position in the sky relative to Prague's horizon and the planet Jupiter.</summary>
<keywords>Kepler's Supernova, Ophiuchus, Tycho Brahe, David Fabricius, astronomical coordinates, right ascension, declination, Prague, astronomical instruments, refraction</keywords>

--- Page 82 ---
<meta>This page continues Kepler's technical analysis of the New Star's position (the Supernova of 1604) and begins Chapter 14, transitioning from mathematical measurements to the visual description and mythology of the constellations involved.</meta>

66          **JOHANNES KEPLER**

...the result was that, although it should have shown an elevation of 30° 14' (for it declines by 9° 40'), it actually showed 30° 9', or 30° 10', at most 30° 12', depending on how accurately or carelessly the <term>plumb line</term><note>A weight on a string used to ensure an instrument is perfectly vertical.</note> was corrected, in which a residual error remained. But you may proceed like this, beyond all error:

The altitude of the **New Star** was 18° 48', differing from the altitude of the "left knee" <note>Kepler refers to the star Eta Ophiuchi, located in the knee of the Serpent-Bearer constellation.</note> (which was 30° 9') by 11° 21'. And the knee declined 9° 40' toward the South: therefore, by adding 9° 40' to 11° 21', we produce 21° 1' for the <term>declination</term><note>The celestial equivalent of latitude; the distance of a star north or south of the celestial equator.</note> of the **New Star**, as was also proven by calculation.

It must be noted, however, that because of <term>refractions</term><note>The bending of light by Earth's atmosphere, which makes stars near the horizon appear higher than they actually are.</note>—which at an altitude of 19 degrees cannot be entirely avoided—the Northern latitude is in truth a little smaller, and the Southern declination a little larger. It was possible at this altitude to partially shed that refraction which the star experienced when it was closer to the horizon.

<margin>Where it is at the zenith</margin> Since the star held this declination, it came directly overhead only for the southernmost Africans, the Kingdom of **Mutapa** <note>original: "Monamotapæ." A powerful kingdom in Southern Africa, in the region of modern Zimbabwe and Mozambique.</note>, and in the New World for the **Brazilians** and **Peruvians** situated closest to the **Tropic of Capricorn**, who are for the most part wild peoples. As for them, I might use the saying: *The people sitting in darkness see the light.* <note>original: "Populus sedens in tenebris videt lucem." A reference to Isaiah 9:2 and Matthew 4:16, here used to suggest the star appeared over "uncivilized" lands.</note> For the rest of its path, it traced its daily circle across the vast Ocean, leaving all of Asia and nearly all the Indian Islands to the North.

<margin>To whom it touched the horizon</margin> It could be seen by the whole world, except for the Northern Frigid Zone. Indeed, for the furthest inhabitants of **Lapland**, it just grazed the horizon in the south.

***

### On the constellation of the Serpent-Bearer, the Serpent, and the Scorpion.
## CHAPTER XIV.

<margin>A description of the Serpent, useful for identifying the fixed stars.</margin> It is easily apparent in this constellation—as I said in the fifth chapter—that the names given to stars came from some slight resemblance to things familiar to us in the arrangement of neighboring stars. Furthermore, from those adopted names, new poetic fables arose, or old ones were adapted by force to fit the names chosen. In the **Serpent-Bearer** <note>The constellation Ophiuchus.</note>, which provided a home for the **New Star**, a very clear line of twelve prominent stars appears, especially when it begins to rise on clear and moonless nights. This line follows a certain circular curve around **Ariadne’s Crown** <note>The constellation Corona Borealis.</note> as if around a center; passing between the Crown and the heads of **Hercules** and **Ophiuchus**, it is drawn around by five stars and then reflects downward in pairs of stars...

<summary>Kepler calculates the New Star's declination and describes its geographic visibility, noting it passed directly over the Southern Hemisphere, before beginning a discussion on the origins of constellation names and myths.</summary>

<keywords>supernova of 1604, declination, Mutapa, Brazil, Peru, refraction, Ophiuchus, Serpentarius, star naming, constellations</keywords>

--- Page 83 ---
ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT-BEARER [Page] 67

Among the stars arranged in that same nearly straight line, there is a very notable sequence. The first of those twelve is missing from my table, excluded because of its shortness; it is referred to by Ptolemy <note>Claudius Ptolemy, the 2nd-century astronomer whose star catalog was the standard for over a millennium.</note> as being in the shoulder, the second in the forearm, and the third in the right elbow of Hercules; though this third one is smaller than the others, it is made equally manifest by the circular series of the arrangement. Finally, the fourth star is claimed by the temples of the Serpent; the fifth by the stretching out of the neck; the sixth is in the coil of the serpent; the seventh and eighth are close in location (Ptolemy’s ninth and tenth of the Serpent) and are in the serpent itself; the ninth and tenth have come closer together and are counted as the left hand of the Serpent-Bearer <note>The constellation Ophiuchus.</note>; the eleventh and twelfth are in the left and right knees of the Serpent-Bearer. After these twelve have been numbered, I have been accustomed to count the **New Star** <note>The supernova of 1604, now known as Kepler's Supernova.</note> in the thirteenth place; and thus, after it began to decrease, it fell into this view: for it was a little further south than the said line.

By this winding and self-evident line, especially when it rises steeply from the horizon, I believe the ancients were prompted to think of the likeness of a serpent <margin>The head of the Serpent-Bearer</margin> reared up on high: to which the care of those who followed conveniently added and discovered the image of a man. For at the top there is a great star, a little lower than the stars of the second honor <note>A "star of the second honor" refers to what we now call second-magnitude brightness.</note>; beneath which, on the right and left, two and two bright stars are placed below, creating the appearance of shoulders; and again, two other such <margin>Shoulders</margin> pairs created the appearance of hands: which indeed <margin>Hands</margin> Aratus <note>Aratus of Soli (c. 315–240 BC), a Greek poet famous for his poem *Phaenomena* about the constellations.</note> hints at in express words:

<note>original Greek: Τοῖοι οἱ κεφαλῇ ὑποκείμενοι ἀγλαοὶ ὦμοι...</note>
Such are the bright shoulders situated beneath the head.
They would be clearly seen even when the moon is at its midpoint;
yet the hands are not quite so equal,
for a thin radiance runs out on this side and that;
nevertheless, they too are visible, for they are not faint.

There was also a very fitting depiction of the right foot, resting on its toes: <margin>Foot</margin> an image constituted by six stars, which Ptolemy numbers as 13, 14, 15, 16, 17, 18, to which the **New Star** was added.

With a picture of a man holding a Serpent in his hands begun with so many members, it is no wonder if it was afterwards rendered more complete at the discretion of astronomers; <margin>The coil of the Serpent. The Tail.</margin> by adopting for the coil and the tail of the Serpent many more stars, which were not such obvious patterns in themselves, but which the imagination, once it had accepted the previous stars, seemed to adopt by a certain necessary sequence. Then, from the twelve numbered stars, the first three were taken away from the Serpent and handed over to the "Kneeling Man" <note>Hercules, who was often referred to in antiquity as *Engonasin* or the "Kneeler."</note>; so that no dispute might arise between him and the Serpent concerning borders, nor that he should be reduced into too narrow a space. The serpent was able to bear this loss with a calm mind, as a beautiful depiction of the serpent's jaws <margin>The Jaws or Head.</margin> fell to it in compensation.

<summary>Kepler describes the positioning of the "New Star" (Supernova 1604) relative to the constellations of Ophiuchus, Serpens, and Hercules, explaining how ancient astronomers derived these figures from the arrangement of the stars.</summary>
<keywords>New Star, Supernova 1604, Ophiuchus, Serpentarius, Serpens, Hercules, Ptolemy, Aratus, constellation borders, star magnitudes</keywords>

<meta>This page continues a discussion on the visual representation of constellations, specifically the head of the Serpent.</meta>

68 JOHANNES KEPLER

image, which consists of three stars above the stars of the temple and the base of the neck, with which the two outermost stars (the higher of which fell outside the limits of my table) form a trapezium, with the third star held in the middle. <margin>Errors in this constellation</margin> And Bayer <note>Johann Bayer (1572–1625), a German lawyer and uranographer famous for his star atlas, the *Uranometria*.</note> expresses this arrangement of stars in the head of the Serpent excellently; however, the painter, as is often the case, showed off his skill in the wrong place, shifting the eye to the location of the jaw and the forehead to the location of the nostrils. Similar things happened to the globes of both Mercator <note>Gerardus Mercator (1512–1594), the famous cartographer who also produced celestial globes.</note> and Tycho. To this was added in Tycho Brahe's *Progymnasmata* <note>Tycho's "Introduction to the New Astronomy," published posthumously.</note> the fact that he names the stars not strictly according to Ptolemy <note>Claudius Ptolemy (c. 100–170 AD), the Greco-Egyptian astronomer whose star catalog was the standard for over a millennium.</note>, but according to Mercator’s illustrations; furthermore, the scribes corrupted some of the numbers. Consequently, if you were to reconstruct the arrangement based on the reliability of his catalog, you would by no means express the arrangement of the gaping mouth that appears in the sky—just as the Tychonic globe certainly does not express it.

But even in the Greek text of Ptolemy, the designation of the direction of the five stars in the right foot is incorrect: for "South" must be substituted for "North." This ancient error also passed into the Arabic translation. Thus, Johann Bayer retained it, which is surprising given the man’s most eagle-eyed diligence. For Mercator recognized it on his globe and moved these stars to the South; from which Weidner <note>Paul Weidner, a 16th-century author.</note> correctly sings:

->*Where the Sun’s path cuts the right sole of the serpent-bearer.*<-
<note>original Latin: "Quâ dextram anguiferi plantam secat orbita Solis." The "serpent-bearer" refers to the constellation Ophiuchus.</note>

Certainly, the globes that claim the authority of Tycho Brahe will persuade the unwary of many such things; while mechanical imitators add on their own those stars which Brahe had passed over, either because of their low position or other obstacles, they leave the actual images to the whim of the painters. From this it happened that these stars are marked everywhere on Tychonic globes and in Bayer’s table on the right knee, by a most distorted <term>articulation</term> <note>original Greek: διαρθρώσει (diarthrōsei), referring to the anatomical arrangement of joints in a figure</note>; whereas the authors intended them to be in the sole of the foot, being clearly guided by the "foot" character.

Bayer’s painter also swapped (much to the author's grief) right for left; from which arises a great deal of confusion in the comparison of Ptolemy’s description with the actual sky. This matter was not unfairly criticized by Brengger <note>Johann Georg Brengger, a physician and correspondent of Kepler.</note>. I had the same struggle with my own engraver; who, when after various efforts he had finally and with difficulty reconciled the proximity of the feet of my "Idol" <note>Kepler refers to the figure or illustration representing the constellation.</note> to this width of the shoulders, demanded that he be allowed to move the right elbow, and he won by sheer persistence. For the star which Ptolemy named from the right elbow is here the fourth of the Serpent from the end. Other things are perfectly in accordance with Ptolemy’s mind.

But before I pursue everything with tedious heaps of numbers, it...

# ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT [OPHIUCHUS]. 69

<margin>The myth of the **Serpent-bearer** is uncertain and varied.</margin>

I shall conclude what I began to say regarding the myths: namely, that the origin of this celestial **figure** <term>Idolum; here meaning the visual representation of a constellation</term> arises from the arrangement of the stars. Once it was accepted into the heavens, the poets began to devise myths for it. Avienus <note>Rufus Festus Avienius, a 4th-century poet who translated astronomical works.</note> imagines this to be **Asclepius**, who, after restoring Hippolytus to life through the medical art, paid the penalty to Jupiter by being struck by a lightning bolt; yet, as a consolation, he received immortality through the translation of his image into the heavens. Doubtless, because the people of Epidaurus worshipped Asclepius in the form of a serpent, it implies the serpent was added to his likeness. Others prefer to see the boy **Hercules** expressed here, clutching snakes in his hands. These people are indeed forgetful, and perhaps have never looked at the sky to learn that Hercules is already there, kneeling, and pressing his head against the head of **Ophiuchus** <note>The Greek name for the Serpent-bearer.</note> so that he appears to butt heads with him. To others he is **Triopas**, to others **Glaucus**. For this reason, Pontanus <note>Giovanni Pontano (1426–1503), an Italian humanist and poet.</note>, remaining doubtful, assigns some myths to the Rising [East] and others to the Setting [West], in Book III of his *Urania* [The Heavens].

> *If you seek the Marsi, and tamed snakes, and the charms*
> *of Colchis, and the Dragon overcome by sleep and words;*
> *the Serpent-bearer gives these to you as the star rises:*
> *If by chance you seek Eurydice wandering through the joyful meadows,*
> *and groaning much from the wound of the snake:*
> *the Serpent-bearer will mark her with an unfavorable setting.*
<note>original: "Si marſos, domitosq́; angues, & carmina quæris / Colchidos, & victum ſomno verbisq́; Draconem; / Hæc tibi ſurgenti dat ſerpentarius aſtro: / Si quæras fortè Eurydicen per gramina læta / Errantem, atque anguis de vulnere multa gementem: / Serpentarius hanc obitu ſignabit iniquo."</note>
These lines are taken from J. Firmicus <note>Julius Firmicus Maternus, a 4th-century lawyer and astrologer.</note> almost word for word.

Such is the license of poets that they transform this **figure** from male to female every day: what wonder if he is even imagined as effeminate by some? Following Pontanus, one may therefore call him **Apollo**, or **Cadmus** (the slayers of the Boeotian Dragon), or **Jason** (who lulled the Colchian Dragon to sleep), or **Aesacus** (avenging the death of the nymph Egeria by a snake), or even **Eurydice** herself. But what if you called it that **Lesbian Dragon**, turned to stone by Phoebus—the one that gnawed the head of **Orpheus**? Do you see how, with its neck bent back toward the **Crown** [Corona Borealis], which follows the **Lyre of Orpheus** at no great distance, the head of Orpheus (who was the master of the lyre) still seems to be seeking the crown? If this pleases you less, recognize **Aristaeus** in the Serpent-bearer and **Proteus** in the Serpent, so that the reason for the struggle is clear.

But nothing is more fitting than Virgil’s **Laocoön**, whom the serpents, as he tried to save his sons from destruction,

> *Seize and bind in their massive coils; and now...*
<note>original: "Corripiunt ſpirisq́; ligant ingentibus : & jam" - from Virgil's Aeneid, Book II.</note>

...I have never lacked the will to fill these gaps in Brahe's <note>original: "in Brahæo"; referring to the star catalog of Tycho Brahe, whose work Kepler inherited</note> work, as far as the latitude of Prague would permit; but my resources were very meager. For I am working almost alone, and my eyesight is poor; moreover, the stars are small, close to the horizon, and lost in the brilliance of the Milky Way. I also lack Brahe's <term>instruments<note>Tycho Brahe possessed the most advanced astronomical instruments of the age, such as large quadrants and sextants, which Kepler did not have full access to in Prague</note></term>, and the Bohemian sky is rarely as clear as I would wish. 

Therefore, a precise observation for even one of the missing stars is hard to find. For the rest, I have noted their sequence of <term>culminations<note>The moment when a celestial body reaches its highest point above the horizon</note></term> and their altitudes at the <term>meridian<note>The imaginary line passing through the North and South poles and the observer's zenith</note></term>, as far as my vision allowed. I determined the locations of some through their conjunction with the planet Mars, for which I possess a highly accurate calculation. In most cases, I was aided by mechanical methods and the noting of straight lines; that is, whenever three stars fell into a single straight line, or very nearly so. For this reason, I present to you a diagram of this constellation.
