ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT <note>This refers to the Supernova of 1604, often called Kepler's Supernova, which appeared in the constellation Ophiuchus (the Serpent-Bearer).</note> 71

In projecting the hemisphere, it designates. As for the <term>lesser circles</term> <note>Circles on a sphere that do not pass through the center, such as lines of latitude.</note>, such as the parallels to the Equator or the Ecliptic and the like: when projected, they become <term>conic sections</term> <note>The curves obtained by intersecting a cone with a plane: the parabola, hyperbola, and ellipse.</note>. For every lesser circle of the hemisphere whose circumference is removed from the point of contact by exactly a <term>quadrant</term> <note>A quarter-circle, or 90 degrees.</note> of a circle, when projected onto a plane, it becomes a **parabola**. But if any part of the circumference falls into the other hemisphere that is not being projected—that is, if it is further than a quadrant from the point of contact—such a projected semicircle (as far as it can be projected) becomes a **hyperbola**, just as in this table of ours all circles parallel to the Ecliptic designating latitude are hyperbolas. Finally, if no part of the lesser circle's circumference is a full quadrant's distance from the point of contact, such a circle projected onto a plane becomes an **ellipse**; or, if its pole is at the point of contact, it is plainly a **circle**. The demonstration is readily available; for the center, being connected on all sides with any of the lesser circles, creates a conic surface which is cut by the plane touching the Sphere.

Although the diagrams are distorted, especially at the extremities of the table if it is wide (as here at the head of the Serpent, where it is necessary for a right angle—by which a circle of latitude is cut by a parallel of latitude on the Sphere—to degenerate here on the Plane into an obtuse one), meanwhile those same straight lines remain, the understanding of which is easier, being simple, and therefore more useful.

&emsp; Most of these were within the range of atmospheric refractions, and could not be captured with enough certainty by the <term>sights</term><note>Kepler refers to the *pinnulae*, the sighting vanes on his astronomical instruments used before the invention of the telescopic sight</note> because of their faintness <note>original Greek: ἀμαυρότητα (*amaurotēta*) — dimness or obscurity</note>. Therefore, since the <term>right ascensions</term><note>The celestial equivalent of longitude</note> could not be determined, I spared myself the labor of calculation, content with a mechanical approach; and having applied straight lines upon which three stars coincided or aligned, and other configurations—which anyone who wishes may seek from the heavens by their own industry, since these are perpetual and require no instruments—let them weigh this table against those, and where necessary, correct it, leaving aside those which Tycho Brahe’s industry has established as certain.

&emsp; As for what concerns the bright star of the right foot: because that foot was adorned with this **New Star**, it seemed worthy to me to measure it by whatever means I could. It appeared twice to be distant from the "Heart of Scorpio" <note>The star Antares</note> by 11° 53', just as the right knee was distant from the middle of the forehead by 17° 55', though they differ only by 17° 53'. Thus, the corrected distance was 11° 51'. And its altitude was 15° 26', which, when subtracted from the equator, leaves 24° 28'. Thus its location is about 15° 50' of Sagittarius, with a southern latitude of 1° 43'.

&emsp; Mars approached the stars of the left foot in March of the year 1606. For on the 10th day, New Style <note>referring to the Gregorian calendar</note>, at 5 o'clock in the morning, when Mars was placed by calculation at 1° 42' of Sagittarius with a northern latitude of 1° 5', it was seen almost in the middle place between the northernmost of the triangle...

ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT BEARER, Page 73

the angle in the forehead of the Scorpion and the Ankle, closest to the bottom of the leg, is distant by half of that which the top of the forehead is from the double star, and not yet in the line of the leg. Indeed, on the 16th day of March, when Mars was in 3° 51' of Sagittarius <note>The zodiac sign Sagittarius</note> with a latitude of 0° 59' North, it stood a Moon's diameter above the ankle of the left foot, in a line from the ankle above the middle of the leg. On the following 17th of March, when it was in 4° 11' of Sagittarius, with a latitude of 0° 58' North, it had already slightly passed the line from the Heart of the Scorpion <note>original: "cor Scorpii"; the star Antares</note> to the ankle. Since the Heart of the Scorpion is in 4° 17' of Sagittarius, the Ankle therefore falls just before 4° 11' of Sagittarius: specifically, in the year 1604, it was in 4° 07' of Sagittarius. And because it was below Mars by the diameter of the Moon—that is, by about 32 minutes—its northern latitude will therefore be 0° 26'. And because on March 17th Mars was with the Ankle, and at 4° 11' of Sagittarius, whereas on the 10th it was at 1° 42', it therefore proceeded by 2° 29'. Its prior location was distant from the ankle by 1° 32' along a transverse path. And because the most Northerly star in the triangle of the forehead of the Scorpion <note>likely the star Beta Scorpii, also known as Acrab</note> is in 29° 08' of Scorpio: from there to 1° 42' of Sagittarius is 2° 34', which you see is nearly equal to the prior distance of 2° 32', exactly as noted above. It follows from this that the bottom of the left leg is beyond 1° 42' of Sagittarius. It is therefore located at approximately 1° 48' of Sagittarius with a latitude of 1° 38' North.

From this, and from the observations of March 16th, we also derive the location of the Middle of the leg, with the addition that the line of the three stars of the leg leaves the left knee slightly to the West, etc.

Again, on the 25th day of May, at 11 o'clock in the evening: Mars, now in retrograde motion <note>The apparent backward motion of a planet in the sky</note>, had already passed the Ankle and the Heart of the Scorpion. Indeed, the calculation places it in 3° 57' of Sagittarius with a latitude of 1° 30' South. You see the agreement. The triangle formed by Mars, the Heart, and the preceding heart <note>likely referring to a nearby star used for orientation</note> was nearly isosceles, and the shortest side was between the Heart and the preceding star. Mars, however, was a little closer to the Heart than to the preceding one, which confirms its position. Furthermore, the line from the middle of the forehead, passing through the star that is under the sole of the foot, passed under Mars. And since the middle of the forehead is at 1° 54' South and Mars at 1° 30' South, it is understood from this that the latitude of the sole <note>original: "volae"</note> is greater than 1° 30' South.

On the evening of May 28th, the star under the sole of the foot could not be seen due to the proximity of Mars. And since Mars was then in 2° 56' of Sagittarius, with a latitude of 1° 51' South, this fixed star falls at about 3° 0' of Sagittarius, latitude 1° 50' South. Therefore, the locations of these fixed stars are sufficiently certain. In other matters, if my manifold weighing of figures and lines, and my observations of meridian altitudes of whatever sort, have not led me to the very smallest fractions <note>original: "scrupula"; refers to minutes or small units of measurement</note>, they certainly have not allowed me to wander too far astray.

Now, furthermore, I shall provide a catalog of the longitude and latitude of those stars which either have not yet been recorded in the catalog of fixed stars by Brahe <note>Tycho Brahe, the famous Danish astronomer and Kepler’s predecessor</note>, or are seen in these constellations in addition to those recorded by Ptolemy <note>The 2nd-century Greco-Egyptian astronomer whose star catalog was the standard for over a millennium</note>.

<meta>This page continues Kepler’s analysis of star positions, specifically correcting errors in previous catalogs regarding the constellations Serpens (the Serpent) and Scorpius. It follows his habit of comparing his own observations and calculations with those of his predecessors, Tycho Brahe and Ptolemy.</meta>

74 **JOHANNES KEPLER**

First, the naming of the first stars in the Serpent is skewed. For what is named by <term>Brahe</term><note>Tycho Brahe (1546–1601), the Danish astronomer whose precise data Kepler used.</note> is described by <term>Ptolemy</term><note>Claudius Ptolemy (c. 100–170 AD), the Greco-Roman father of mathematical astronomy.</note> as follows:

| Brahe’s naming | Ptolemy’s naming |
| :--- | :--- |
| I. The preceding one in the mouth. | Above the end of the jaw in the quadrilateral. |
| II. The one in the mouth. | Touching the nose. |
| V. The one at the left eye. | In the middle of the quadrilateral in the open mouth. |
| VI. The one at the nostrils. | Emerging from the head toward the Northern part. |

Regarding these, I consulted the observed Tychonic distances and subjected them to a new calculation. The position of the first star turned out to be the same as in Tycho's records. However, the observations that were attributed to the second star actually produced the position that the first star holds in Tycho's work. As for the second star, I have not yet found anything certain. This is because I have not had the opportunity to access clean <note>original: "munda," meaning corrected or finalized manuscript copies</note> copies of the observations. Neither this second star, nor even less so the sixth <term>unformed</term><note>Stars not included within the traditional outline of a constellation.</note> star, could be included within the narrow constraints of my table.

As for the fifth star, its distances from:
The Head of Hercules [at] 20° 50 1/2′ } prove its position to be 14° 10′
The Tail of the Eagle [at] 47° 18′ }
of Scorpio in the year 1600; not 15° 10′: and its latitude to be 37° 17′, not 37° 28′.

Regarding the right knee of Ophiuchus <note>The "Serpent-Bearer" constellation.</note>, it was mentioned above in Chapter XIII that it is in excess by 4 <term>scruples</term><note>An old term for minutes of arc (1/60th of a degree).</note>.

It was also noted in the very small stars of the triangle in the forehead of Scorpio—which is double—that there is an excess of one <term>denarius</term><note>Used here as a unit of measure, likely a tenth of a degree or a specific fractional part.</note> of scruples in longitude. For the angle at the bright star of the forehead is, to the senses, a right angle. But if you follow the numbers in the *Progymnasmata* <note>Tycho Brahe's work, *Astronomiae Instauratae Progymnasmata*.</note>, the angle becomes acute. I prove it thus: because the angle is a right angle, and the triangle, being near the <term>Ecliptic</term><note>The apparent path of the Sun across the sky.</note>, is almost rectilinear: therefore, as the difference in longitude of the two higher stars of the triangle (87 1/2′) is to the difference in latitude (37′), so is the difference in latitude between the brightest and the small star (51′) to the difference in their longitude (21 1/2′). But since the brightest star in the year 1600 is at 27° 36′ of Scorpio, then by adding 21 1/2′, this small star will be at 27° 57′ of Scorpio, not at 28° 7′ as written in the book.

# ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT. 77

->Concerning the location of this star within the diameter of the universe, or its immense distance from the center of the earth.<-

## CHAPTER XV.

The more diligently that most excellent astronomer Tycho Brahe <note>Tycho Brahe (1546–1601) was the most famous observational astronomer of the era; Kepler served as his assistant and later succeeded him.</note> investigated this location, the more briefly I will be able to dispatch the matter here. At the outset, I shall demonstrate that this star was not only placed above the orbit of the Moon, but was not even closer than the Sun itself.

<margin>This star lacked parallax.</margin>

The demonstration is easy. Since the star had no true motion of its own, if it had been closer than the Moon, it would have shifted its apparent position against the <term>zodiac|The belt of the heavens divided into twelve signs, used as a coordinate system for the positions of celestial bodies.</term> more than the Moon does. Thus, at least to our sight, it would have appeared to travel through some small space of the sky, even if it were actually standing still. For the sake of clarity, let the less-skilled reader take a simple example. Although I believe I explained the theory of <term>parallaxes|The apparent displacement of an object when viewed from two different points; astronomers use it to measure distance.</term> in Chapter 9 of my *Optics* <note>Refers to Kepler’s 1604 book, *Astronomiae Pars Optica*.</note> so clearly that nothing more could be desired, let us nonetheless use this coarse and tangible example. 

Hold your head still, and close your eyes one at a time; with one eye open, look at your nose. If you look with the right eye, the nose will seem to be toward the left part of a book or the floor. But if you look with the left eye, the nose will hide the right parts of the book. Every time you switch eyes, the nose will seem to make a jump, even though it remains still.

Afterward, do not look at your nose, but at your thumb held still and at a slightly greater distance from your face. By alternating eyes, the thumb will do the same as the nose did before, but it will not make such large jumps. From this, common sense judges that the thumb is further away than the nose, because it creates a smaller parallax, or change of its apparent place.

This example fits our subject perfectly. For what the two eyes are to us, two different locations on the world <note>Meaning different observation points on Earth.</note> are to astronomers. What the nose and thumb are in the example, the Moon and some more distant star are to them. What the book or floor is here, the sphere of the fixed stars is to them. Just as the thumb and nose are still far from the floor or book—even if, when viewed with one eye, they seem to touch and cover them—so the "wandering stars" <note>A literal translation of the Greek *planētai*, referring here to planets and the Moon.</note> are not in the highest ether among the fixed stars themselves, but far below them; yet they seem to be in the highest ether. And the Moon, when viewed from different places on the earth, appears to stand near different fixed stars. These crude points will perhaps benefit those who are a little slower in understanding the business of parallaxes.

K 3 Now <note>This is a "catchword" and "signature mark" used by early printers to help binders assemble the book in the correct order.</note>

<summary>Kepler argues that the "New Star" of 1604 must be incredibly distant—beyond even the Sun—because it shows no "parallax" or apparent shift in position when viewed from different locations.</summary>
<keywords>parallax, Tycho Brahe, New Star of 1604, celestial distance, Ophiuchus, astronomical observation</keywords>

--- Page 96 ---
78 JOHANNES KEPLER

Now it must be proven that the new star produced a smaller <term>parallax<note>Parallax is the apparent shift in the position of an object when viewed from different locations. Astronomers use it to measure how far away stars are.</note></term> than the Moon, and no greater than the Sun: and therefore, without doubt, it had no measurable parallax at all. I prove this in two ways. First, as follows: The star was observed in the month of October while setting in the West; but in the months of February and March, it was observed while rising in the East, always maintaining the same position within 6 minutes of a degree <note>A minute is 1/60th of a degree; for scale, the full moon is about 30 minutes wide.</note>; which could be attributed to atmospheric refraction or, in my case, slight defects in the instrument. For on October 11/21, 1604 <note>Kepler provides both the Julian and Gregorian calendar dates, which were 10 days apart at the time.</note>, using three stars as reference, the position of the New Star was found to be at 17° 39', 17° 40', or 17° 42' of Sagittarius while it was setting. But on February 1/11, when it was observed rising by way of <term>Aquila<note>The constellation of the Eagle.</note></term>, it was similarly found at 17° 40.5'; then by way of the "Heart of the Scorpion" <note>The star Antares.</note> at 17° 44.5' of Sagittarius; and more correctly, from <term>Fabricius's<note>David Fabricius, a contemporary astronomer and correspondent of Kepler.</note></term> data, at 17° 42' of Sagittarius.

<margin>If the star had been within the boundaries of the lunar path, how great an apparent change of position it would have produced.</margin>

But if it had been no further away than sixty Earth radii <note>Sixty Earth radii was the standard scientific estimate for the distance from the Earth to the Moon during this period.</note>, and had remained in the same place in the sphere of the fixed stars, it would have produced a large enough parallax. Let 0° Sagittarius set; let 0° Gemini rise; let the 90th degree from the rising point <note>This point is the "nonagesimal," the highest point of the Ecliptic above the horizon.</note> be 0° Pisces, which will be elevated (according to the tables of Maginus's *Primum Mobile*) by almost 23°, being 67° distant from the <term>zenith<note>The point directly overhead.</note></term>. Therefore, according to the doctrine of parallax handed down in chapter 9 of my *Optics*, and using the parallax table—under 60.5 radii and a total parallax of 57'—the parallax of latitude is shown to be 52' 28'' and the horizontal parallax of longitude 22' 16''. And since the position of the star is at 18° Sagittarius, 72° distant from 0° Pisces: the parallax of longitude under the headings of 22' and 16'' will be 21' 10'' toward the West; and the star, though in truth at 17° 40' Sagittarius, will nonetheless appear at 18° 1' 10'' Sagittarius.

Conversely, let 0° Capricorn rise, so that 0° Libra is the 90th degree, which is elevated by 33° 20', being 59° 40' from the zenith. Therefore, the parallax of latitude (using the same values we set before) is 47' 37'', which now makes a difference in latitude of almost five minutes (compared to the previous 52' 28''). But the distance of the star from the head of <term>Ophiuchus<note>The constellation of the Serpent-Bearer; the supernova appeared near the foot of this constellation.</note></term> was always the same, whether setting in the year 1604 or rising in the year 1605; thus the latitude was always the same, between 1° 55' and 1° 57' North. But let us finish the calculation for the parallax of longitude: here, through the altitude of the 90th degree being 33° 20', the heading for longitude is 31' 35'', under which, corresponding to the distance of 18° Sagittarius from the 90th degree, a parallax of 30' 53'' toward the East is shown. Thus, with the star remaining at 17° 40' Sagittarius, it would nonetheless be seen at 17° 9' 7'' Sagittarius. Thus it would have changed its position from 17° 9' 7'' Sagittarius to 18° 1' 10'' Sagittarius, a difference of 52.5 minutes.

And yet, it changed its position not at all; certainly not by anything that could be compared to this quantity.

In the attached diagram, let A be the center, B the surface of the Earth, E F the Ecliptic of the fixed stars, C D the daily path of the New Star in the vicinity of the Moon, and let there be G. H.

<summary>Kepler uses observational data and trigonometric calculations to prove that the Supernova of 1604 does not shift position (parallax) like the Moon does. This proves the new star must be located far beyond the Moon in the distant heavens, challenging the ancient idea that the heavens are unchanging.</summary>
<keywords>parallax, Supernova 1604, Sagittarius, Ophiuchus, celestial spheres, astronomical measurement, Earth radii, David Fabricius</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Johannes Kepler’s mathematical proof regarding the distance of the "New Star" (the supernova of 1604). He is using the absence of parallax to demonstrate that the star is located in the celestial realm of the fixed stars, not in the Earth's atmosphere.</meta>

**ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT-BEARER, PAGE 79**

[Diagram showing Earth at center 'A', an observer on the surface at 'B', and lines of sight extending to a celestial sphere. Points G and H represent the fixed stars, while E and F represent the apparent positions a nearby object would take.]

**G** and **H** are two markers for the 18th degree of Sagittarius. <note>The "New Star" of 1604, now known as Kepler’s Supernova, appeared in the constellation Ophiuchus (the Serpent-Bearer) near the 18th degree of Sagittarius.</note> **G** represents the Rising in the East, and **H** the Setting in the West; so that from both sides the New Star might remain on the same line, drawn from the center of the Earth **A** toward the fixed stars. This line is **AG** at the Rising in the month of February, and **AH** at the Setting in the month of October. It is manifest that if the New Star were existing so close to the Earth, it would have been seen from the observer’s position **B** along the line **BCE** when Rising, and along **BDF** when Setting. In this case, it would have appeared before marker **H** (18° Sagittarius) at point **F**, and there it would have appeared after marker **G** (18° Sagittarius) at point **E**. By no means could it have appeared in the same location among the fixed stars, at **G** or **H**. <note>Kepler is using geometry to show that if the star were "close" (sublunary), its position relative to the background stars would shift depending on where the observer is on Earth. Since it doesn't shift, it must be very far away.</note>

And at this point, what should I say—or rather, what should I *not* say—about the miserable condition of our time? In such great light of the doctrine of <term>parallaxes (the apparent change in position of an object when viewed from different locations)</term>, and with such consensus between Philosophers and Mathematicians, there yet exists a certain individual—not a common man, but a Philosopher and a physician... <note>Kepler is beginning a polemic against those who clung to the Aristotelian view that the heavens were changeless and that any "new" star must be a temporary phenomenon in the Earth's atmosphere.</note>

<summary>Kepler provides a geometric proof using parallax to demonstrate that the New Star of 1604 is located among the distant fixed stars rather than near the Earth. He then begins a frustrated critique of contemporary thinkers who ignore such mathematical evidence.</summary>

<keywords>parallax, New Star, 1604 Supernova, Sagittarius, celestial geometry, Aristotelian philosophy, astronomy vs. physics</keywords>

--- Page 98 ---
<meta>This page continues Kepler's critique of an unnamed "philosopher" (likely an Aristotelian opponent) who has published a book in Paris attacking mathematical astronomy and the findings regarding the "New Star" of 1604. Kepler is defending the validity of astronomical observations and the use of parallax to determine the distance of celestial bodies.</meta>

->80 JOHANNES KEPLER<-

...and many others? What of Christine of Savoy? <note>Christine of France (1606–1663), Duchess of Savoy; Kepler is likely addressing the patrons and nobility who supported these scholars.</note> What of the other Frenchmen, in whose country another small book by this Italian was printed, dealing with the same subject in the Latin language? Why do you tolerate such great disgrace with such patience? Or do I truly suspect that you consider these trifles unworthy of being publicly refuted?

But the reasoning of this "Philosopher" is clearly ridiculous. He perhaps heard from the Mathematicians that in the theory of <term>parallaxes|The apparent shift in an object's position when viewed from different locations; used by astronomers to measure distance.</term> the center of the Moon and of the stars comes into consideration. Understanding that "center" to be something deep inside the body of the planet, he leaps for joy, thinking he has found an escape: he denies, of course, that such a center can be seen. It would be beneath me to answer him here in front of Mathematicians; but if anyone else has been driven mad by this blind Teacher, I tell him: Astronomers speak of the visible center of a visible thing that possesses a visible size. This center does not need to be seen by itself; rather, it is estimated from the measured outer edges. This is how astronomers are accustomed to work: first they observe the altitude of the top edge of the Moon, then the bottom edge. Then they add half of the difference to the smaller value to find the altitude of the middle, or the center. For example: if the altitude of the top edge is 50° 44' and the bottom is 50° 12', the difference is 32', and the half is 16'. This, added to 50° 12', makes 50° 28'. This is what they call the altitude of the center—seen through its edges, not through itself.

But why do I linger on these things, when I see that the man is ignorant of what an astronomical observation even is, let alone having ever performed one himself? For he denies that the center of the Sun can be seen, except by those with the eyes of an eagle. And since he lacks such eyes, it follows that the Sun has never been observed by him. It was not fitting for such a man to teach the assembly at Padua <note>The University of Padua, where Galileo taught; Kepler is criticizing a rival academic from the same prestigious circle.</note> those things he dreams up about the New Star and other astronomical matters; he should instead learn from that most learned assembly what he has remained ignorant of until now.

I can hardly express with what a "turning stomach" <note>original: "quanto cum stomacho"; Kepler uses a visceral idiom for his intense annoyance.</note> I read his book written against the Mathematicians regarding celestial matters and printed in Paris. However, it had to be read for the sake of my patron. O, the distinguished occupations of the human race! That one man builds, and another destroys because he never learned how to build; all so that some work and space remains for a third person to rebuild it yet again. How gladly I exclaim here that line from Persius: *O the cares of men, O how much vanity there is in things!* <note>A famous quote from the Roman satirist Persius, *Satires* 1.1, lamenting human futility.</note> But enough on this.

For it has been said and demonstrated—despite the grumbling of this most unskilled Philosopher—that the star has not changed its position by any notable amount, not even by 52 1/2 minutes. Now let us proceed deeper.

<margin>And how / much in</margin> Since a <term>parallax|The shift in apparent position.</term> of 3 minutes is usually attributed to the Sun, because it is judged to be 1,200 <term>earth radii|A standard unit of distance in early astronomy, representing the distance from the center of the Earth to its surface.</term> away from the Earth; and since these 3 minutes are a nineteenth part...

<summary>Kepler mocks an Aristotelian philosopher for misunderstanding how astronomers calculate the center of celestial bodies using their visible edges. He defends the mathematical method of observation and expresses his frustration with critics who lack practical experience in using instruments.</summary>

<keywords>parallax, Moon's center, astronomical observation, Padua, New Star of 1604, Persius, solar distance, earth radii</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Johannes Kepler’s mathematical proof regarding the distance of the "New Star" of 1604 (SN 1604). He is comparing the observed position of the star with the expected shift (parallax) of the Sun to determine if the star is within our solar system or far beyond it.</meta>

->ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT. 81<-

...one-twentieth of 57: the already discovered <term>parallax<note>The apparent shift of an object's position when viewed from two different locations. The smaller the shift, the further away the object is.</note></term> <margin>at the boundary</margin> of longitude, 52 1/2 minutes, having now been divided by 19, yields <margin>the Sun's course.</margin> a little less than 3 minutes. If the Sun, therefore, is distant from the Earth by 1200 <term>semidiameters<note>original: "semidiametris." This refers to the radius of the Earth, which was the standard unit for astronomical distances at the time.</note></term>; observed at these two positions in which the star was observed, it would have appeared to have changed its position by 3 minutes, however much it remained at rest by itself in the same degree and minute of the <term>zodiac<note>The belt of the heavens within which the sun, moon, and planets move.</note></term>. <meta>This page continues Kepler's investigation into the Nova of 1604 (Kepler's Supernova), shifting the calculation of its distance from the traditional geocentric (Earth-centered) model to the Copernican heliocentric (Sun-centered) model.</meta>

82 JOHANNES KEPLER

<margin>The altitude of the New Star according to Copernican Hypotheses.</margin>

Truly, those distances which we have weighed for the new star until now are childish, so long as we remain within the common opinion of the Earth’s stillness. But if we open the Copernican abysses of immensity; good God, to what height will this star be raised?
      Let no one think that this labor is vain—to demonstrate something according to the opinion of Copernicus. Believe me, this opinion is not only spreading, but it is clearly gaining strength among Philosophers. Nor is it a wonder, since Mathematics and natural science fight for it emulously; nor do the <note>Kepler refers here to theology or scripture, arguing that the Bible does not contradict heliocentrism if interpreted correctly rather than by private whim.</note> more sacred disciplines contradict it, provided they are not twisted to the whim of any private individual, but are valued according to their own custom.
      In the previously provided Diagram, let **C** be the Sun, **HKO** the orbit in which the Earth goes around, and **D** the location of the new star, near the circuit of Saturn, or of Saturn itself.
      From the <term>Prutenic Tables: astronomical tables published by Erasmus Reinhold in 1551, based on the calculations of Nicolaus Copernicus.</term> it is therefore possible to see that if Saturn stood immobile at the highest point of its orbit, which is **D** (when it is ten times further away than the Sun is from the Earth); and if the Earth departed from the line **CD**, which connects the Sun to Saturn, and moved from its point **K** to the extreme side of its orbit **O**; the <term>parallax: the apparent shift in position of a celestial object when viewed from different locations, used to calculate distance.</term> of Saturn, or the angle **CDO**, would be about 6 degrees. But since the star did not change its position by even a tenth of one degree, nor occupy the angle **CDO**—that is, not even a sixtieth part of six degrees—it is necessary that this star be higher than sixty times the distance of Saturn. And because this is ten times the distance to the Sun, it is therefore higher than six hundred times the solar distance: so that the distance **CO** from the Sun to the Earth does not equal a six-hundredth part of **CD** itself. Therefore, if 600 distances of the Sun from the Earth—each having 1,200 <term>semidiameters: the radius of the Earth, used here as a standard astronomical unit of measure.</term> of the Earth—that is, if seven hundred and twenty thousand semidiameters of the Earth are extended from the Earth upward into the ether; even in that so remote a place, a parallax of six minutes could still occur. But since the most faithful observations all agreed within 2 minutes (which is a third part of six), it is therefore certain, according to the opinion of Copernicus, that this star was at least three times higher; and thus, twenty-one hundred and sixty thousand <note>2,160,000</note> semidiameters of the Earth lay between this star and the Earth. Why say more? The mind is exhausted wandering in this immensity of the world, in which it finds no rest by counting, no station, and no goal for returning. Therefore, having dismissed these overly subtle divisions of numbers, let us aspire toward a geometric contemplation of the whole.

is a hundred-year-old fable: or if something entirely new is brought forth, before it grows to such a magnitude that it strikes the eyes, most of those who are alive today will have departed this life; thus, their curiosity is useless and in vain. Therefore, let men cease (on the occasion of this beginning of the Fiery Trigon <note>The Fiery Trigon is a period in astrology lasting roughly 200 years during which the "Great Conjunctions" of Jupiter and Saturn occur only in the fire signs: Aries, Leo, and Sagittarius.</note>, considered in a solitary and natural way) to fashion magnificent dreams for themselves: the Turks dreaming of the oppression of Christians; the Christians dreaming of the subversion of the tyranny of the House of Ottoman, or of the conversion to the Christian faith of the vast kingdoms of Asia subject to the King of the Persians. They do this because, forsooth, the Fiery Trigon favors the Christian religion; meanwhile, the litigating parties across Europe dream of the extirpation or recovery of the other side—things which could hardly happen without a massive shaking of the world and unheard-of tumults. For whatever things of this kind are piously hoped for or feared, their seeds certainly do not yet sufficiently appear for an astrologer to be able to form a likely conjecture from them—at least as long as he looks only at the Great Conjunctions. This is what he does who decides that even the New Star itself <note>The "New Star" refers to the supernova of 1604, now known as Kepler's Supernova.</note> is a natural and astrological effect of the Great Conjunction: as David Fabricius <note>A German astronomer and theologian who corresponded extensively with Kepler.</note> does not obscurely suggest, as do other astrologers who wrote before the New Star had appeared. For the fact that the New Star arrived at the conjunction beyond the usual course of nature: I have not yet begun to speak of the supernatural significance of this matter; that will be done a little later. For the present, I emphasize what I said a little above: namely, that those things which are not signified by the configurations of the planets through a certain mode of nature are simply not pre-signified at all.

<margin>Which of the great conjunctions are most important?</margin>

What I have said, I have confirmed by a natural argument drawn from the Great Conjunctions themselves; there is no reason why, because of this recent conjunction, we should expect anything special or greater than in the previous two centuries by the power of Nature. For it makes no difference to the shaking of the state of human affairs whether the Trigon is named from "fire" or from "water" <note>Astrologers grouped the zodiac signs into four "Trigons" or Triplicities: Fire, Earth, Air, and Water.</note>: but it matters much here whether many or few planets meet, and whether they do so closely or loosely and at a distance.

For since that human faculty, which is capable of perceiving the harmonies between celestial radiations, also grants this to man—that he is a social animal—it is surely the foundation and principle of organized societies <note>Kepler uses the term *Politiarum*, referring to the structure of states and civil governance.</note> scattered through individuals. (For there is an instinct among men, rather than a deliberate plan, to come together into one, with God providing for the weakness of our reasonings and anticipating them with this instinct.) It follows, therefore, that a joining <note>original: ἁρμογὴ (harmogē) — a Greek term for a fitting or joining together.</note> of rays causes a joining of operations; and the minds of many more easily come together into the same work when the rays of many stars are coupled in the heavens. And the more closely the stars come together, the more closely men also embrace and help one another; and from this, more subtle and sharper (namely, harmonic) characters are born. Thus, in the years 1444, 1504, 1563, and 1623, Saturn and Jupiter are joined most closely;

because the place of the conjunctions <note>The "meeting" or alignment of two or more celestial bodies.</note> is Cancer and Leo, where the ascending nodes of Jupiter and Saturn are located—specifically the node of Saturn, which runs further out to the North, follows the other; so that around the beginning of Leo, a mutual intersection of the paths of Saturn and Jupiter occurs. Calculation indeed shows that in the years 1444 and 1504, Saturn was further south than Jupiter; but in the years 1563 and 1623, it was already further North. Therefore, within 400 years they scarcely come closer together than happened in the year 1563 (<note>original: "in Opticis meis fol. 305. sphalma unius centenarij tolle."</note> <note>In my book *Optics* on page 305, please correct the error of one century.</note>). And since only a very few minutes of a degree intervene between the latitudes of Saturn and Jupiter, I do not actually grant enough weight to the calculation that I would dare to deny that that conjunction was a "corporeal" one <term>corporeal conjunction</term><note>A conjunction so close that the bodies appear to physically touch or overlap.</note>. On that account, that conjunction was far preferable to the modern one of the year 1603 and others similar to it in the years 1544 and 1484, which show Saturn one degree further north than Jupiter; it is also preferable to the conjunctions in the sign of Pisces in the years 1464, 1524, 1583, and 1643, which depress Saturn just as much to the south below Jupiter. To those in Cancer, the "height" of the sign in this Northern hemisphere also adds power: since those which occur in Pisces and Aries are low, and much lower than in Sagittarius, like the modern one. Finally, in Sagittarius, Pisces, and the beginning of Aries, Saturn and Jupiter descend from their apsides <term>apsides</term><note>The points in an orbit farthest from or nearest to the center of motion (the Sun).</note>; in Cancer and Leo, they ascend. Therefore, as far as astronomical causes are concerned, the meetings in Cancer and Leo are much more significant than this modern one in Sagittarius.

      On the other hand, regarding the multitude of the planets, I do not know if any of the "Great Conjunctions" <note>The rare alignment of the slowest moving visible planets, Jupiter and Saturn, which happens every 20 years.</note> that have occurred since the founding of the world can be compared with the one that took place in the year 1524, in the month of February: when Saturn, Jupiter, and Mars were joined almost on the same day in the 10th degree of Pisces; Venus stood a few degrees before them; the Sun and Mercury were at the end of Aquarius, lingering just before the group; and when they were thus joined and moving forward, the Moon passed through them all within the space of thirty hours. But in the year 1603, when Saturn and Jupiter were joined, Mars was far away in Libra; after Mars reached that position in the year 1604, Jupiter had already passed Saturn. Hither, therefore, O Astrologers! Hither Astronomy commands you to turn your eyes: where there are no empty, terrifying names, but where things are made great by Nature—a conspiracy of motions and the tightest coupling of rays.

      There is indeed also this to be noted in our present conjunction: that the Sun, a few days before the conjunction, was also joined to those who were meeting; while Venus and Mercury, as frequently happens, accompanied it. But this conjunction has that in common with all the conjunctions of the recent centuries, in which the Sun is always found nearby; so that the conjunction of 1563 alone, out of all those that surround us in the nearest 250 years,

**ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT. 185**

<note>original: *Serpent.* refers to the constellation Ophiuchus, the Serpent-Bearer, where the supernova appeared.</note>

...stand out, being visible during the morning rising of the Planets; and yet even here the sun is not far away. So much has been denied to the solid conjunctions of our times, which was possible in the time of Christ; namely, that conjunctions might occur in opposition to the sun, when the planets are slow because of retrogradation <note>The apparent backward motion of planets in the sky</note>; and thus they might meet three times in a single cycle. 

Therefore, whether astrologers defend nature or their own traditions, believing that the revolutions of Republics and religions are depicted in this most beautiful governance of celestial motions: they found here an evident argument why they should expect nothing further; nothing that could be compared in magnitude with those things which had already happened in the last 150 years—at least as long as they were looking only at this great conjunction of the year 1603, before the new star had yet appeared.

What I then discussed in this manner regarding astronomy will be found to be true, if one is willing to wander a little into history and into a comparison of the past with the present. Indeed, in these 150 years, so many and such great things have happened that greater things could hardly have occurred, and they are of such a kind that, in their own respect to natural causes and the agreement of the sublunary <note>Earthly or below-the-moon</note> nature with celestial harmonies, they can be most beautifully related. For who is so drowsy that he does not perceive this from the reading of histories: that the world, from the time of the overthrow of the former Roman Empire and the invasion of barbarian nations, had slept for a thousand years as if seized by some deep lethargy <note>original: *veterno*, a medical term for a state of chronic drowsiness or senile decay</note>; <margin>A comparison of the centuries slightly preceding with the present ones.</margin> but from the year 1450, it was awakened and returned to its former alacrity? Before this, with Republics overthrown and healthy rites trampled, barbarian customs had grown common, some traces of which we laugh at and mock today: justice was routed, and force prevailed; the "exercises" of the Nobility were acts of robbery. 

With Christian doctrine uprooted, a great part of the world had consented to the most foul and absurd dogmas of Muhammad. Among Christians, there was much barbarism of speech, much ignorance, and other things which would be tedious to list: the Kings of the Saracens were created or removed from their positions by Caliphs. By the immortal God, how great was the foulness of the Kingdom of the Sultans in Egypt? Where no one could rule unless he had first been a slave, had first condemned the nation he was to rule as impious, had hated it worse than a dog or a snake <note>original: *pejus cane & angue*, a Latin idiom for extreme loathing</note>, had defected from his own beliefs, and had become a perjurer against Christians. <note>Kepler is referring to the Mamluk Sultanate of Egypt, where the ruling class was composed of former slave-soldiers.</note> I do not deny that many great deeds were performed by many; but if you compare them with ours, you will find they were achieved by sheer numbers and many hundreds of thousands rather than by wise counsel; nor were they long-lasting. 

Many arts known to the ancients had become extinct; the study of letters, reduced within the narrow confines of monasteries, lived miserably even there. The courts of princes, especially in Germany, were headed by Ecclesiastics: to whom alone it was not considered a disgrace to know let— <note>The text cuts off mid-word, likely "letters" or "literature".</note>

186 &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;JOHANNES KEPLER

...had learned to read and write. The primary languages, Greek and Latin, were publicly decaying, due solely to a scarcity of literature and books, and an influx of barbarians. How many most illustrious works of the ancients, how many histories, how many books of theologians—indeed, how many entire libraries—were lost through this neglect, so that today no hope of recovering them remains?

But look now at the wonderful transformation of affairs over the last 150 years. First, the laws of Empires were reformed, especially the German ones; a sacred and stable public peace was established, banditry was forbidden, courts of law were constituted, and most beneficial customs were solidified—among which not the least is the establishment of the postal service or swift couriers <note>original: *veredariorum* — referring to the system of relay horses used for rapid mail delivery</note>. Even the Turkish nation, having cast off barbarism, has learned civility. Europe began to weigh and recognize its own strengths, relying more on strategy than on raw impulse, especially after the loss of Constantinople and the overthrow of the Greek Empire. 

Engines of war were invented, and their use was refined by many hands to the point of greatest efficiency. The Turkish people made great strides through vigilance, industry, and bravery, while the Greeks were either weakened or—like the Europeans—divided among themselves. The Spaniards expelled the Moors; with the greatest intensity of spirit, they applied themselves to seafaring. Through many years of effort, first sending explorers through Africa and later equipping ships to circumnavigate the continent, they finally discovered the route to the East Indies. Fortune favored such industry with the discovery of the West Indies. Because of these events, European commerce reached its absolute zenith through incredible growth—driven by the burning passion of everyone for causes which, I now suggest, are celestial <note>Kepler often sought to link major historical shifts to astronomical or astrological cycles</note>.

The single art of **typography** <term>typography: the art of printing with movable type</term> alone easily proves that human industry at that time was beyond words. This is clear if one considers more carefully how much labor the art requires—how many copies can be produced by so few hands—and finally, how many stages of development lie between the simple seals (which Scaliger <note>Joseph Justus Scaliger (1540–1609), a leading scholar of the period</note> believes gave our ancestors the idea for printing) and the current magnificence of the craft. 

With the birth of printing, books became common; consequently, people everywhere across Europe turned to the study of literature. From this, so many Academies were born and so many learned men suddenly emerged that the authority of those clinging to barbarism soon collapsed. Nor did human ambition rest until there arose the founder of a new order dedicated professionally to literary studies. Since that time, almost all authority has passed from the traditional religious orders to this new class, except in cases where those older orders, busy with their own affairs, also devoted themselves to scholarship. 

From seafaring and commerce, the opportunity was offered to Europeans to spread the Christian faith far and wide among foreign and previously unknown nations. Conversely, from the Academies, the freedom of debate, the abundance of books, and the convenience of printing—arising, no doubt, from scholarship and a general public restlessness—there finally emerged that great and through all ages...

## **ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT. 187**

<margin>To what extent the modern restlessness of the human race may be referred to the great conjunctions of our time.</margin>
...the memorable separation of many European provinces from the Roman See <note>original: "secessio plurimarum Europæ provinciarum à sede Romana" — This refers to the Protestant Reformation</note>. Regarding this matter itself, with the circumstances as they have been described, I am clearly persuaded that it originated from a higher source—namely, divine providence—and is not to be confined within the narrow limits of natural causes. Nor do those who condemn it deny this. Yet, there is something universal in the matter itself, visible through all other human affairs: namely, the restlessness and fervor of men, which is especially prominent during these times. I shall speak now only of this, while I recount the examples by which this fervor is proven. For when the minds of all were stirred by celestial goads, and neighbors were easily joined together for whatever cause, there arose from this so many seditions, factions, dissensions, and wars, as much for the sake of religion as for the sake of empires.

Among the Muslims themselves <note>original: "Mahumetanos"</note>—who today, along with Christians and Jews, divide the multitude of the human race into roughly three equal parts—did not a new torch of civil wars catch fire in this century, arising from the differences in their own religion, which seems destined to bring ruin to the Turks? And because in the years 1504, 1524, and 1563 there were "capital conjunctions" <term>capitales conjunctiones: rare alignments of the planets Jupiter and Saturn that astrologers believed signaled the death of kings or the birth of new eras</term>, I think it was from these that a common cause could arise so much more easily, which joins the love and minds of many nations separated by the greatest distances. Thus, as often as battles are fought in France <note>original: "Gallijs"</note>, in Belgium, or in Britain, there are those in Hungary, in Poland, and in Germany who follow the fortune of their own side with opposing prayers, thinking their own interests are at stake; although, in the meantime, if they lacked a common enemy, they would be fighting among themselves.

You will say these are the works of the Holy Spirit, who through the diversity of languages gathers all nations into the unity of faith. I do not deny this, and I would attribute everything to this Spirit, if so many things of this kind were not also directed toward evil—if this same zeal were not present in both parties who hostiley infect one another, which is certainly not from the Holy Spirit. Or is it a contemptible thing that even those antiquities, which today are either defended most constantly or unearthed most stubbornly, shine forth today; that all their soot, contracted by the long passage of time, is being scrubbed away and cloaked in brilliant colors, so that if you trust your eyes alone, you would pronounce everything to be new? Has it ever been heard of that opposing parties, from regions not very far apart, should go to the Antipodes <note>the opposite side of the world, specifically the Americas and the East Indies</note> with equipped fleets to decide the fate of the world there, as if it were not just as easy to die at home? Did ever a Sarmatian or German fleet, sailing from the Baltic Sea during the height of the Roman Empire, circumnavigate Europe and come to Italy—outstripping the couriers who go by the shortest way through the Mediterranean—and relieve the high cost of grain by bringing in corn? What does more recent antiquity have that is similar to the modern science of military affairs? What shall I say of the modern mechanical arts, innumerable in their multitude and incomprehensible in their subtlety? Do we not today reclaim all the ancient writers, however many survive, and bring them into the light through the art of typography? <note>the printing press</note>

...do we? Or do we not see today that even Cicero himself is learning to speak Latin again from our many critics? <note>original: "An non hodie ipse Cicero à nostris tot Criticis rursum discit Latine loqui?" Kepler refers to the intense philological study of classical authors during the Renaissance.</note> Indeed, in every field of study, especially since the year 1563, a greater number of writers is published every year than were produced in total over the previous thousand years. Through these writers, a new Theology has been created today, a new Jurisprudence; the <term>Paracelsians</term> <note>Followers of Paracelsus who advocated for chemical rather than herbal medicine.</note> have renewed Medicine, and the <term>Copernicans</term> <note>Followers of Nicolaus Copernicus's sun-centered model of the universe.</note> have renewed Astronomy. <margin>No unforeseen change can be predicted from the Fiery Trigon.</margin>

Having considered these things, I have pronounced those astrologers fools if they think that we should expect more and greater events in the next 200 years than have occurred in the past 150—at least by the natural power of the <term>Fiery Trigon</term> <note>A 200-year period where planetary conjunctions occur in the "fire" signs: Aries, Leo, and Sagittarius.</note> alone, taking its new beginning from the year 1603, and considered by itself. Unless, perhaps, they think some new world will be discovered, or the art of flying will be invented so that we might go to the moon or to another globe in the universe; for by Hercules, this globe of earth is already too cramped for them!

<margin>Examples from the age of Christ.</margin>

Through a comparison of the times of Christ and Charlemagne, which Fabricius <note>David Fabricius (1564–1617), a German astronomer and correspondent of Kepler.</note> uses, the matter will become clear. In the 40th Julian year, five years before the Christian era, there was a Great Conjunction in Pisces; this could be considered the start of the Fiery Trigon just as the one in 1583 could be. Yet the Roman Empire had already devolved from the people to the family of the Caesars more than 40 years before that. Nothing new happened then; for the empire remained in this family for 70 more years, remained with Augustus for another 19 years, and stayed with other Roman families of Princes for a very long time.

<margin>Of Charlemagne.</margin>

Likewise, when the "fiery triplicity" <note>Another term for the Fiery Trigon.</note> returned around the year 809 AD: nothing was new, except that Charlemagne stabilized the Empire of the West (transferred to his father Pepin in 751) and the Roman Empire (transferred to himself in 801) with illustrious success. 

Rather, setting aside the names of constellations and "Trigons" led by fire or water, I have urged men to look at the facts themselves as they are currently situated. For some things are striving toward their peak; some are verging toward ruin; for others, a long duration can be hoped for. Therefore, I believe that this "spur" of the conjunction in Sagittarius will chiefly disturb and unsettle men in those affairs they already have in hand. As for what will arise entirely anew by the occasion of this celestial stimulation, it cannot be greater—so long as the efficacy of the Fiery Trigon alone dominates—than what happened shortly before.

And even though some greater things can now be hoped for since the <term>New Star</term> <note>The Supernova of 1604, which appeared in the constellation Ophiuchus during a significant planetary conjunction.</note> appeared; yet because that star will perhaps seem to some to have emerged by a force of nature (as we left it in suspense above), and for those people it is determined to portend nothing beyond what was said in Chapter 29: I am pleased to offer this general statement for their sake:

He compares current events to the times of Augustus and Charlemagne to show that social and political shifts often precede or outlast these astronomical markers.</summary>

**ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT BEARER. 189**

...this pronouncement on the natural effect of the <term>Fiery Trigon<note>original: "Trigonus igneus." In astrology, this refers to a roughly 200-year period during which the "great conjunctions" of Jupiter and Saturn occur in the fire signs: Aries, Leo, and Sagittarius. Kepler and his contemporaries believed these cycles signaled major shifts in history, such as the rise and fall of empires.</note></term> (and likewise of the star). Let me explain this <margin>What the Fiery Trigon naturally signifies.</margin> more broadly. Kings of Europe will undoubtedly fight for empires; here force will be used, there guile, according to the various impulses of nations. In Germany and elsewhere, tempers will become increasingly inflamed. Those who defend antiquity will grow stronger, while those who seek liberty will draw closer together. The more successful the latter are in their fortune, the more bitterly they will bite and consume one another in turn. It is possible then that new factions and new opinions will emerge. Although the zeal for contention among politicians is, as mentioned above, in decline, mediators will arise who will stir up no fewer disturbances than if they were advocating a new sect.
   Krabbus <note>Johannes Krabbe (1553–1616), a German mathematician and astronomer known for his popular almanacs and astrological prognostications.</note> said the same things regarding a "middle religion" about to arise, though <margin>Krabbe's prophecy.</margin> his eyes were turned from the heavens toward some King of Europe and certain courtly rumors. Significant changes will occur upon the deaths of current magistrates, as others of different professions succeed them, bringing their provinces along with them and un-teaching what had been learned before.
   I say all these things as one who looks at nature alone. Meanwhile, I am not unmindful of divine providence, which, if it occasionally uses nature as something it has itself provided, certainly does not take laws from Nature for dispensing its punishments or mercy among those who have pleased it.
   Those knowledgeable in affairs think the empire of the Turks <note>The Ottoman Empire.</note> <margin>Whether the Muhammadan sect will soon perish by the power of Fire.</margin> has lost much of its former strength; it is not impossible that, with the forces of the Persians on one side and the Christians on the other conspiring together, it might be overturned—if we continue to use our own strengths and temper majesty with prudence. Thus, by happenstance, I agree with those who, from the stars, threaten that empire with destruction. They look indeed to the empty name of "fire," thinking that since this dogma began under a Watery Trigon, it will perish under a Fiery one. They do not consider that it has already once endured and grown strong under a Fiery Trigon from 800 to 1000 AD. Truly, they say the doctrine of Christ began under a Fiery one, which lasted for the first 200 years, and that the same is to be spread more widely under this Fiery one from the year 1600 to 1800. Again, they forget that during these last 100 years under a Watery Trigon, it was disseminated further across the globe through the skill of navigation—in terms of land area—than ever since its first origin.
   Although I both hope for and fully believe in the coming collapse of the Ottoman family’s power, I see no natural causes from the Fiery Trigon to prove that those who embrace the Muhammadan doctrine will submit to Christian rather than Persian rule. Whether something better is to be expected now that the new star has shone forth, I defer to the following chapter. Already, many fruitful rumors have been heard concerning the Persian <note>Refers to the Shah of Persia, Abbas the Great, whom Europeans hoped would convert to Christianity or at least remain a permanent ally against the Ottomans.</note> having become a Christian...

190 JOHANNES KEPLER

...but they are written with such carelessness, with such neglect for probability, that it easily appears they are the wishes of the simple-minded rather than the reports of the experienced. For it does not matter how many of our people—whether religious or secular—are permitted to dwell in those regions; rather, it matters how many of the locals are persuaded to the faith of Christ. And would that what is desired by the prayers of all might come to pass! Then I shall have a new dispute with the astrologers, to whom I will nonetheless deny this: that such great grace of God toward wretched nations was signified by the **Fiery Trigon** <term>Fiery Trigon: A rare astrological cycle where the planets Jupiter and Saturn meet in "fire" signs (Aries, Leo, and Sagittarius) over a period of 200 years, an event occurring roughly every eight centuries.</term>; for its name is empty, while its recurrence is steady and natural, not arbitrary, nor procured by God outside the usual order. Instead, I shall relate this to other signs and to our star <note>Kepler refers to the "New Star" or Supernova of 1604, which appeared during this significant astrological alignment.</note>, as will be said shortly.

<margin>Which regions are threatened by the **Fiery Trigon**.</margin>

As far, then, as pertains to the individual regions over which the **Fiery Trigon** is said to hold significance: although there are some slight supports for subjecting certain regions to certain celestial signs—insofar as the horoscopes <note>original: "geneses"</note> of rulers and those of their consenting subjects can provide—nevertheless, I think it is apparent from what has been said (and I emphasized this three years ago) that we have no need to consult the catalogs of cities and regions believed to be under the **Fiery Trigon**, specifically under **Sagittarius**. For first, where there is profound peace, we suspect in vain an imminent and significant change from nearby. Indeed, nothing new has occurred thus far in Stuttgart, or Meissen, or in the Spains themselves, even though they are referred to Sagittarius.

On the contrary, let each person look within himself and explore his own fatherland to see if it has long been known that dissensions are smoldering, and that fraud and hidden wars are swelling. For he who has a name noted in this catalog <note>Referring to the lists compiled by astrologers claiming certain regions are "ruled" by specific zodiac signs.</note> has something to fear for himself from this conjunction, lest it bring such great stings that evils, hitherto well-concealed, burst into the open.

Therefore, the fact that we have seen **Hungary** burning with seditions and **Moravia** with plundering: the cause of this was the very thing just mentioned, situated in the men themselves. This **Sagittarius** did no harm to Hungary, Košice <note>original: "Cassovia," a city in modern-day Slovakia.</note>, or Moravia. For those same evils also reached **Austria** and **Styria**; of which the former is referred to **Libra** and the latter to **Capricorn**—neither of which is the "host" sign of the conjunction, Sagittarius.

->And by this one event alone, I consider my cause—here separated from the common cause of the astrologers—to be wonderfully vindicated.<-

I have nonetheless admitted a distinction among men: those for whom the position of this conjunction touches the principal places of their birth-horoscope—provided they are also of an active and harmonic nature—will feel the primary stings from this conjunction.

Therefore, dear reader, read what I have set down in this chapter in such a way that these things were written before the **new star** began to shine—namely, in the year 1603, in which...

<meta>This page continues Kepler's discussion of the "New Star" (the supernova of 1604). Having previously discussed physical and natural explanations, he now pivots to the "providential" or symbolic meaning of the star within the context of the "Great Conjunction" of planets.</meta>

->**ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT. 191**<-

During the time when the astrologers' prophecies regarding the Fire Trigon <note>The "Fire Trigon" refers to the alignment of Jupiter and Saturn in the "fiery" zodiac signs (Aries, Leo, and Sagittarius), an event that occurred every 800 years and was believed to herald great historical shifts.</note> were being widely discussed, we must now consider what should be concluded from this great conjunction. This is especially relevant since God Almighty has placed a new star at the very location of this conjunction, creating a massive distinction between this current alignment and those of the past. In this section, although I will allow myself a bit more freedom in making conjectures than I have so far, there is no reason for anyone to accuse me of being inconsistent with my previous statements. Up until now, I have relied on natural principles—by the admission of the astrologers themselves—but from here on, we shall adopt "more-than-natural" <note>Kepler is moving from physics and astronomy into the realm of theology and symbolic interpretation.</note> perspectives. In both cases, however, conjectures will be formed under the condition of a true underlying premise.

*Toward what end the New Star was primarily ignited.*

**CHAPTER XXX.**

The things we discussed in chapters 28 and 29 regarding the effects of the New Star treated this star as one of many natural causes; those conclusions hold true regardless of which theory from chapter 26 is accepted. <margin>The foundation of all divination through non-natural things or modes.</margin> Now, however, we shall say a few things that will carry weight only with those who are persuaded that this star was ignited not by chance, nor by the necessity of nature, but by the certain counsel and special providence of God. Once this foundation is laid, a very wide field opens up to us for <term>Universal Divination <note>original: "Manticen universam." The practice of seeking knowledge of the future or the unknown by supernatural means.</note></term>.

Most types of divination among the nations were based on this very foundation: that through dreams, through the entrails of sacrificial victims, through the meeting of animals, through monstrous births, through lightning, windstorms, floods, earthquakes, celestial portents, comets, and similar things, the Gods were believed to give answers to mankind. A very frequent expression in the writings of Dio <note>Dio Cassius, a Roman historian.</note> is "acting under divine inspiration" <note>original: "τὸ ἐπιθειάζειν" (to epitheiazein). It refers to being filled with a divine spirit or interpreting omens.</note>, which signifies this very thing. Especially memorable is that prayer of Cyrus, which testifies to the theology of either the Persians or of Xenophon himself. For Cyrus, after receiving an omen of his death, performed a sacrifice and spoke thus: *Father Jupiter, and you Sun, and all you Gods, receive these sacrificial victims, with which I—having performed many great deeds—happily place the final touch on my work; at the same time, I show myself grateful to you, because you gave me signs of your will through the entrails of victims, through celestial portents, through auguries, and through oracles, regarding what I ought to do and what I ought to leave undone. I am indeed very grateful to you, because I myself have recognized your providence toward me. Therefore, to the same Gods who gave those answers through those signs and animals...*

<summary>In this chapter, Kepler transitions from a scientific/naturalistic explanation of the 1604 supernova to a providential one, arguing that the star's appearance during a significant planetary alignment suggests a divine message.</summary>
<keywords>Supernova 1604, Fire Trigon, Great Conjunction, divination, divine providence, Cyrus the Great, Xenophon, astrology vs. theology</keywords>

192 &emsp; &emsp; &emsp; &emsp; &emsp; &emsp; &emsp; **JOHANNES KEPLER**

...they put forth, power and direction were ascribed to them. Conversely, if it became clear that the movement or appearance of an animal or a portentous thing flowed from a natural cause, so that any suspicion of supernatural governance was absent, they settled their minds and composed themselves to rest. Unless, perhaps, some natural connection was detected between the sign and the thing signified, which would not require the will of some free and rational cause.

<margin>The foundation of superstitious divination from the stars.</margin>

&emsp; &emsp; A great part of astrology was born from the neglect of this foundation. For when many celestial portents had created a belief in gods speaking with the human race through these heavenly things, the unlearned began to attribute whatever happened in the sky to the free will of the gods and to interpret it as an omen; especially if it struck the sight forcefully by its novelty or rarity, such as eclipses of the lights <note>the sun and moon</note> or the meeting of many stars <note>planetary conjunctions</note>. If they had considered from the beginning that all those things revolve by a fixed law of Nature, then they would have either sought a connection in Nature between these celestial things and those events which usually follow, or, if they could not find one, they would never have fallen into this credulity.

&emsp; &emsp; But others advanced to worse things, moving from folly into impiety: for when they saw that the motions of the stars depend on natural necessity, yet still wished to count these among portents, they again introduced a *god from the machine* <note>original Greek: "theos apo mechanes" — a theatrical device where a god appears to resolve a plot; here meaning a forced supernatural explanation</note>. This was not a god who would freely shape the motions of the heavens to signal us through them, but one who would use the pattern of celestial motions as a model for shaping and administering human affairs. From this come those many thousands of astral spirits—or more truly, legions of infernal devils—to whom that cursed Magic, falsely called "celestial," teaches men to offer certain sacrifices, pour out certain prayers, celebrate certain feasts, light candles during the "hours" of the planets, and wear clothes of the appropriate color. Indeed, it is only by this means—with demons cooperating, of course—that those most foolish and ridiculous decrees of the Astrologers concerning the "houses" and "rulerships" of the planets are validated. Now at last it is time to return to one's senses. The first inventors of these trifles were not as ignorant as we accused them of being above. Their impiety added a certain "intellect" to their craft; but you are like children, O Christian Astrologers, who, having thrown away the kernel, cling to the shell; who embrace a corpse after the soul has been removed; who practice this foolish and trifling astrology, which uses the "houses" of the planets, without the Magic that once gave it meaning. Come now, wash the leather garment, so long as it does not soak up the moisture. <note>Kepler likely refers to "washing" the practice of astrology to clean it of its pagan/magical origins, while warning not to let the superstitious "moisture" ruin the material.</note>

<margin>God acting with humans through signs.</margin>

&emsp; &emsp; I think it is clear that there is only room for conjectures when it is established that some intelligent Nature, through these signs upon which the mind of the interpreter is intent, wishes to speak with us—and that these signs are indeed subject to that Being's will and power. Thus God showed to Pharaoh what He was about to do in a dream...

He scolds "Christian Astrologers" for continuing to use empty astrological traditions like "planetary houses" while discarding the magical context that originally invented them.</summary>

<meta>This page continues a discussion on how God communicates through natural signs and dreams, concluding a reference to Pharaoh’s dream from the previous page.</meta>

**ON THE NEW STAR IN THE FOOT OF THE SERPENT** <note>Original title: *De Stella Nova in Pede Serpentarii*. Kepler is discussing the supernova of 1604.</note> [Page number] **193**

...dream, Genesis 41:25. Thus he indicated to Nebuchadnezzar in a dream those things which were to come in the final times, Daniel 2:28. Thus he still speaks with us today through the arc of the Rainbow, and makes us certain that the human race shall not perish by a flood, Genesis 9. Thus he displays signs in the Sun and Moon and stars, and moves the powers of the heavens; so that we might lift up our heads and see whether our redemption approaches.

To this category belong portents of every kind, <term>monsters</term> <note>In the 17th century, "monsters" (monstra) referred to biological anomalies or "prodigies" seen as divine omens, rather than mythical creatures.</note>, and dreams sent by divine will. Whatever of these is offered to men spontaneously, without being sought—since indeed such things cannot be procured by human effort—I would judge, not absurdly, that they can be attributed either to God himself, or to ministering angels, or to some spirit which, connected to humans and the whole world by a certain law of nature, uses instinct more than reasoning. Therefore, the meanings of these things are not to be rejected, unless it appears they occur through the usual custom of nature.

<margin>
*What is the cause*
*why dreams and*
*portents signify*
*through allegories,*
*not clearly.*
</margin>

But as to why I doubt whether the spirit presiding over these signifying matters is free, or truly bound naturally to the body of the world, I have grave reasons on both sides. For the things signified—whether through dreams or through omens and monsters—are signified through allegory, not directly, clearly, or lucidly. If Nature, playing with allegories and pictures of things, follows its own will and could, if it wished, explain the matter clearly, then the reason for this obscurity is questioned. Does it think it expedient for men to be warned in general terms, without being made certain of the specifics before the event occurs? Yet it could have warned thus in a shorter summary, such as certain <term>genii</term> <note>Guardian spirits or minor presiding divinities.</note> are said to use, signifying an imminent fall with a single pulse. Why, then, those windings of dreams and monsters, conducted through those circumstances by which one usually arrives at the specifics? Is it true what Plutarch suggested to us, while discussing the lost cause of the Oracles, by producing these verses from Sophocles:

*The fates speak to the wise through riddles:*
*To the fool, a teacher is entirely useless.* 
<note>original: "Sapientibus per ambages fata eloqui: Fatuis magistrum prorsus esse inutilem."</note>

That is, the wise man is hindered by no riddles of the Oracles; to the fool, no explanation suffices for understanding. Surely, whatever nature it is that speaks with us through the pictures of dreams and the allusions of portents, it is credible that it deeply favors human reasoning nature because of its similarity to itself. Therefore, it signifies calamities through riddles so that men may be moved toward reasoning about causes (which is the characteristic of man). Thus, once the true causes are recognized—especially if they were within man himself—they might think of genuine remedies. For men, placed in uncertainty through these labyrinths of significations, look around...

Bb <note>Printer's signature mark for the book's binding structure.</note>

<summary>Kepler argues that God and a "world-spirit" communicate through natural omens and dreams using allegories to encourage human reason. He suggests that these cryptic signs are intended to provoke us into investigating their causes and finding remedies for upcoming calamities.</summary>

194          JOHANNES KEPLER

...look at present things; they gather the past, and place the future before their eyes, until they finally arrive at the reality itself.

<margin>What are the genuine letters of God, and what is the language in which He speaks with us?</margin> 
Indeed, this is God’s most frequent custom—He who did not see fit to speak to human beings daily with explicit words, especially concerning natural things and those pertaining to the sustenance of this life. Instead, He uses another kind of speech through the course of nature and the motion of the heavenly bodies; He uses another kind of writing, which does not consist of twenty-three letters written in red or black ink <note>original: "miniata vel atrata elementa viginti tria." Kepler refers to the 23-letter Latin alphabet and the traditional use of red ink for headings (rubrics) and black for text.</note>, but rather the great bodies of the world themselves, the works of His fingers <note>A reference to Psalm 8:3: "When I consider your heavens, the work of your fingers." Kepler views the study of astronomy as reading a divine manuscript.</note>. In this way, He taught man to protect himself, to clothe himself, to raise his vulnerable offspring over a long period of time, to swim, and so forth. God did not do this as a vocal teacher sitting beside him, nor by granting him natural weapons like those of bulls and boars, or clothing like that of sheep, or the ability to walk and swim from the moment of birth as He did for almost all the beasts; instead, He gave him hands...
