**ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT. 85**

...tude. In the meantime, consider, O Philosopher, that a proportion of an <term>accident</term><note>In Aristotelian philosophy, an "accident" is a quality (like motion or color) that cannot exist without a "subject" (a physical thing) to exist within.</note> to its subject is much more lacking here than one might rightly find lacking in Copernicus regarding the proportion of a Part of the world to the whole. <margin>A comparison of the incredible swiftness in Ptolemy with the incredible mass in Copernicus.</margin> For accidents do not exist without a suitable subject. It is more credible for a large subject to exist without motion than for a large motion to exist in a small subject. Therefore, if I transfer the Ptolemaic proportion of motions to the Copernican proportion of bodies, I shall do so with the greater applause of the Philosophers. Ptolemy multiplies the motion of Aristarchus or Copernicus <margin>Ptolemaic swiftness.</margin> seven and a half thousand times. I, in turn, shall multiply the Ptolemaic body of the fixed stars—of 20,000 <term>semidiameters</term><note>A semidiameter is a radius; in this context, it usually refers to the radius of the Earth.</note>—so many times that there arise fifteen hundred thousand semidiameters taken a hundred times, that is, 150,500,000; which, when distributed into the sums of twelve hundred <margin>Copernican mass.</margin> (which is the measure of the interval between the Sun and the Earth), constitute one hundred twenty-five thousand solar distances, that is, 125,417. And yet we do not require such a great mass to defend Copernicus as the ancients required swiftness to defend Ptolemy, as is apparent from the following.

Let us restore to the World its own harmony of proportion—not the kind based on magnitude, <margin>From where the proportion of the sphere of fixed stars to the body of the Sun and its beauty is to be derived.</margin> which Brahe was accustomed to require, but one of beauty and ratio. The perfection of the World is motion, which is, as it were, its very life. For motion, three things are required: the Mover, the Moved, and the Place. The Mover is the Sun; the Moved are the planets from Mercury all the way to Saturn; the Place is the outermost sphere of the fixed stars. Now, if it is permitted to state a physical reality mathematically: the Moved objects are the proportional mean between the Mover and the Place. For indeed, they affect the rest of the Place or the surrounding body through resistance and a certain kind of weight (why do you laugh, you "Philosophrasts" <note original: "Philosophrasti">A mocking term Kepler uses for pseudo-philosophers who rely on empty rhetoric rather than mathematics.</note> inexperienced in heavenly matters, wealthy in a store of imaginary things but most needy of true ones?), from which their individual periods of time fall to them (for as far as the Mover is concerned, he is unique and moves uniformly); on the other hand, they receive their motion from the Mover, so that in the Moved objects, both Motion and rest are in some way mixed.

But if the Moved objects are physically the proportional mean between the Mover and the Place, what is more likely than that, mathematically also, the diameter of the moving planets is the proportional mean between the diameter of the Sun (the Mover) and the diameter of the Place (the fixed stars)? This being granted, it is easy to track the size of the fixed stars. For the Sun's diameter exceeds the Earth's diameter by five and a half times; let it exceed it by a full six times. In saying this, I assign to the distance of the Sun and Earth not 1,200, but 1,432 semidiameters. Yet let this be: I shall be liberal, so that the world may become truly great. In the following diagram, let CE be the semidiameter of the body of the Sun, 6 units, of which CK is...

[right] distance

He proposes a geometric relationship where the planets act as a proportional bridge between the Sun (the source of motion) and the sphere of the stars (the container of the universe).</summary>

Let the Sphere of Saturn be higher, at **G B**. Now Saturn is ten times more distant than the Sun is; truly **CG** is ten times that of **CK**. It is actually a bit less than that, but nevertheless, let the number be rounded up here. Saturn will therefore be distant by 14,320 Earth <term>radii</term><note>original: "semidiametris"; the distance from the center of a sphere to its surface.</note>, and this is the length of the line **CG**. Now, therefore, let it be that as the radius of the solar body **CO** (six Earth radii) is to the radius of Saturn's orbit **CG** or **CB** (14,320 Earth radii), so this **CB** is to **CD**, the radius of the <term>fixed stars</term><note>The "fixed stars" refers to the distant stars that appear not to move in relation to one another, as opposed to "wandering stars" or planets.</note>. This is found by extending the line **CG** and constructing a semicircle **EBD** upon this line, drawn through the points **E** and **B**, with its center at **A**. This sum is not yet even a fourth part of that previous figure which we had assumed for the example of the speed being multiplied in the <term>old opinion</term><note>The "old opinion" refers to the Ptolemaic or geocentric system, where the entire heavens must rotate around the Earth every 24 hours.</note>.

    Why then do the Philosophers <note>Kepler refers here to the traditional Aristotelian scholars who opposed the Copernican heliocentric model.</note> busy themselves with removing this "speck" of the immensity of the fixed stars from Copernicus's eye, while they meanwhile ignore the "huge beam" in their own eye—a beam more than four times larger—of the insane speed of the fixed stars? <note>original: "eximere ex oculo Copernici festucam... trabem ingentem". Kepler is cleverly quoting Jesus's "Sermon on the Mount" (Matthew 7:3-5) to mock his critics.</note> They are so much more absurd than Copernicus, in that it is much more difficult to stretch a property beyond the capacity of the subject than it is to increase the size of the subject without changing its properties. <note>Kepler argues it is logically easier to believe in a huge, slow universe (subject) than a small universe moving at a physically impossible speed (accident/property).</note> Tycho Brahe somewhat supported the opinion of the ancients here, retaining only a third part (or a little more) of Saturn's height; for he places it at seven thousand Earth radii. To the fixed stars, however, he grants double this: the former by necessary demonstration based on his assumed "proper form of the World," but the latter by probable conjecture. But whether it is two-thirds or one-third, on both sides the figure is still exceeded by a fourth or fifth part. And what is the single star of Saturn compared to the immense multitude of fixed stars? All of which, according to the accepted hypotheses, [move] with that most rapid... <margin>...most rapid [motion]</margin>

# **ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT. 87**

[They] move with the swiftest motion, but in Copernicus’s system they rest immobile; with only the seven small bodies <note>The "seven bodies" refers to the Sun, Moon, and the five planets known at the time: Mercury, Venus, Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn.</note> of the sun going [around].

But perhaps the world, adorned by this proportion demonstrated by me, will become narrow, and so close that the fixed stars would produce some <term>parallax<gloss>The apparent shift in the position of a star caused by the Earth's movement around the sun. If stars were "close," they would seem to move back and forth every year.</gloss></term>—that is, so that the proportion of the line CK or CO (the distance between the Sun and Earth) becomes sensible compared to the distance CD (the distance between the Sun and the fixed stars)? Not at all. For a little above, a distance twenty-one times [the Earth-Sun distance] (which is a little less than a sixteenth of this part, namely three hundred and forty) could produce a parallax of two minutes; therefore three hundred and forty, slightly less than the sixteenth part of this, will claim for itself a parallax of about one-eighth part of one minute. 

Or more skillfully and briefly thus: Because the Sun subtends 30 minutes <note>A "minute" is 1/60th of a degree of arc in the sky.</note> when viewed from Earth, that is, because angle CKI is 15' [half the diameter]: and since Saturn is ten times higher: the Sun viewed from Saturn will have a tenth part of 30 minutes, that is 3 minutes; indeed, this quantity is double the angle CGI. But it is proposed that as the Sun IC is to CG (the semi-diameter of Saturn's orbit), so CG or CB is to CD (the semi-diameter of the fixed stars): thus, with points B and D connected, triangles BCD and ICG will be similar; therefore, angles BCD and IGC are equal. 

Therefore even this orbit of Saturn CB, viewed from the sphere of the fixed stars or point D, would show an appearance of 1 1/2 minutes. And since the Earth's path CO is a tenth part of Saturn's diameter CB; therefore angle ODC will be almost a tenth part of angle BDC, so that the semi-diameter of the Earth's annual orbit CO would cause no more than 9", that is, a seventh part of one minute of parallax. 

But there is no astronomer, however skilled in observing, who would boast of reaching a seventh or eighth part of a minute in his observations. For example, even if the distance between two stars at point D, for an observer on Earth at O (when D is considered in <term>quadrate<gloss>A 90-degree angle between the Sun and a star as seen from Earth.</gloss></term> with the Sun or line OC), were smaller by a seventh part of a minute than when D is opposite the Sun with the Earth at K: yet the astronomer will not discern these distances, observing them both at the quadrate O and at the opposite K; since the bodies of the fixed stars themselves usually occupy and subtend one, two, three, or four minutes <note>To the naked eye, stars appear as "blobs" of light a few minutes wide; Kepler argues this visual blurriness hides the tiny 9-second shift he calculated.</note> with their bodies.

Thus it has been shown that no one is well-advised to object to the immensity of the World against Copernicus, who embraces any of these hypotheses as true, and does not wish for the whole of astronomy to be fundamentally overturned.

Now we must also see by what examples this <margin>Immense proportions [are] not [absurd]</margin> immense proportion might become more palatable to us. Here I am accustomed to object to Brahe <note>Tycho Brahe (1546–1601), the great observational astronomer who rejected the Copernican system partly because he could not find stellar parallax.</note> with the proportion of a tiny creature, which makes burrows under the skin of the hand <note>original: "animalculi, quod cuniculos ſupter cutem manus agit" - likely a reference to the itch-mite (Sarcoptes scabiei) which causes scabies.</note>, to that Serpent...

<summary>Kepler uses geometry to prove that the Copernican system's vast distances are plausible because the resulting stellar parallax would be too small for even the best astronomers to detect with the naked eye. He argues that the apparent size of stars in the sky is larger than the tiny shift his theory predicts, thus masking the Earth's motion.</summary>

88 JOHANNES KEPLER

<margin>that they are rare among creatures</margin> <note>original: *esse raras inter creaturas*</note>
...that creature mentioned by Pliny, one hundred and twenty feet long, whose remains were preserved at Rome; and that one even larger which Tycho Brahe <note>Tycho Brahe (1546–1601) was a Danish nobleman and astronomer whose accurate observations provided the data for Kepler's laws of planetary motion.</note> asserted was seen in the North. The proportion ran to a hundred thousand in length. But this is a playful example. Take another. How small, I ask you, is a human being compared to the globe of the Earth?

<margin>The lowly proportion of the human body compared to the globe of the Earth.</margin> <note>original: *Humani corpusculi contempta proportio ad globum Telluris.*</note>
Let us calculate. From the surface to the center is 860 miles <note>Kepler likely refers to "German miles," which are much longer than modern English miles—roughly 4.6 miles each.</note>; multiply this by 5,000 paces. Therefore, there are 4,300,000 paces. This is five times as many feet, namely 21,500,000. Give a man’s height a full seven feet, and divide that number by this. Thus, if you were to arrange thirty-one hundred thousand men <note>3,100,000</note> in a line, they would reach from the surface of the earth to its center. And a single diameter of the earth would equal more than six million <term>myriads</term> <note>A "myriad" traditionally refers to 10,000, but Kepler uses it here to emphasize a vast, countable unit of human height.</note> of human heights. Everyone admits this immense proportion of a man to the globe of the earth; yet now they call the proportion of the Earth to the heaven of Saturn "unbelievable," though it is only one to twelve thousand; and the proportion of the moving heavens to the immobile heaven of the fixed stars "unbelievable," which is one to three thousand—much smaller than the former. Truly, they do not look at proportion, but at sheer size, because they themselves are small. Indeed, the world is not large to God, but we are tiny to the world.

<margin>How the smallness of creatures is compensated by their nobility.</margin> <note>original: *Quomodo exilitas creaturarum compensetur nobilitate.*</note>
Yet, look also at the analogy. Where size dominates, perfection is lacking; and as mass decreases, <term>nobility</term> <note>In this context, Kepler uses "nobility" (nobilitas) to mean complexity, spiritual value, or functional superiority.</note> succeeds it. In Copernicus's system, the Sphere of the fixed stars is surely the most vast; but it is inert, with no motion. Then follows the moving World. Here, the smaller it is, the more divine it becomes, because it has received a motion so admirable and so orderly. Yet that region does not consist of a living faculty; it does not reason, nor does it discourse. What it does (while moving), it did not learn, but retains as something impressed upon it from the beginning; what it is not, it will never be; what it is, it did not make of itself; it remains the same as it was when created. 

Then follows this little pill of ours, our tiny hut, which we call Earth: the womb of plants, shaped within by a certain faculty that is the architect of wonderful works; a faculty which daily kindles from itself the little souls of so many stalks, so many fish, so many insects, that it easily despises the remaining mass of the universe in comparison to its own nobility. Finally, look at those tiny bodies we call animals; what can be imagined more meager than these in comparison to the world? But there, already, is sense and voluntary motion—an infinite architecture of bodies. Look, I say, among those, at these little specks of dust which they call Men; to whom the Creator gave this: that they are born, in a way, from themselves, they clothe themselves, arm themselves, teach themselves infinite arts, and daily progress toward the better. In them is the image of God; they are, in a way, the masters of the entire mass. And who among us is there, who would choose for himself a body...

<summary>Kepler uses mathematical calculations to show that while humans are physically tiny compared to the Earth and the cosmos, their "nobility"—intelligence, soul, and status as the image of God—far outweighs the importance of sheer physical size.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Kepler's discussion on the scale of the universe in the Copernican system and begins Chapter 17, which provides physical evidence that the Supernova of 1604 was located among the distant fixed stars rather than near the planets.</meta>

...[the world's] body, by the vastness of the World, so that it lacks a soul on account of it? Let us learn therefore the good pleasure of the Creator, who is the author of both the raw mass and the perfection of tiny things: yet He does not glory in mass, but ennobles those things which He willed to be tiny.

Finally, through these intervals from the Earth to the Sun, from the Sun to Saturn, and from Saturn to the fixed stars, let us learn to gradually ascend toward acknowledging the immensity of divine power.

And these things, regarding the vastness of the fixed stars objected against Copernicus, I have inserted all the more willingly because they pertain to estimating the incredible magnitude of the new star. <margin>The newest, huge mass.</margin> For if it occupied only four <term>minutes</term><note>Specifically "minutes of arc," a measurement of apparent size in the sky. One arcminute is 1/60th of a degree.</note> (as large as Sirius appears), then by this Copernican <term>hypothesis</term><note>The model of the universe where the Earth rotates around the Sun.</note> it was much larger than the entire machine of the moving planets; whereas we previously assigned it only three minutes if anyone were to look back at it from the fixed stars. I refrain from expressing this magnitude in numbers, fearing lest I have already too much thrust these matters upon the common crowd to be ridiculed.

->Concerning other arguments by which it is proved that this New Phenomenon stood among the fixed stars.<-
->### CHAPTER XVII.<-

**Y**ou have heard the mathematical argument; now hear two physical ones: one derived from motion, the other from light. <margin>Immobility as a physical argument for its location among the fixed stars.</margin> Although that which is derived from motion also argues mathematically. Throughout the entire time of its duration, it stood in the same place among the fixed stars; it did not change its configurations with the stars of the foot, the shin, and the right knee of <term>Serpentarius</term><note>The constellation Ophiuchus, the Serpent Bearer.</note>, nor with the Triangle in the coil of the Serpent, even in the slightest degree that can be perceived. Such is not the nature of Comets, which are found through the region of the moving planets; for none has been observed so far to be immobile for many days, being at such proximity that it would change its visible place among the fixed stars in a day's time. However many were so close as to admit a <term>parallax</term><note>The apparent displacement of an object when viewed from different positions. If the star were close to Earth, it would seem to shift against the background stars as the Earth moves.</note>, whether daily or annual: all of them, besides this optical shift which we call Parallax, were also discovered to move with a certain proper motion of their own. Therefore it is consistent that those new stars which, as Pliny says in Book 2, Chapter 25 <note>original: "lib. 11. cap. 25." Kepler likely refers to Book 2 of Pliny's Natural History, where celestial phenomena are discussed.</note>, move in the manner of "wandering stars" <note>original: "errantium," referring to the planets.</note>, are also in the region of the wanderers; but those which remain immobile stand among the fixed stars; so that the shared nature of their properties also indicates a shared location.

This immobility, which previously offered us in the Copernican hypothesis...

<meta>Continuing from the previous page where Kepler discusses the measurements and observations of the New Star of 1604, specifically addressing its lack of parallax.</meta>

90 JOHANNES KEPLER

<margin>The new star is categorized among the fixed stars even within the accepted hypotheses.</margin>

...produced the argument of zero annual <term>parallax: the apparent shift of an object's position when viewed from different locations. Kepler argues that if the star were close, its position would change as Earth moves; since it doesn't, it must be very distant.</term>, which the translation of the Earth would cause. This same fact—even in the commonly used form of Ptolemy’s hypotheses, and in the very recent one of Brahe <note>Tycho Brahe, the great Danish astronomer whose data Kepler used.</note>, in which the Earth remains motionless in its place—clearly proves by a way other than annual parallax that a place below Saturn cannot be assigned to the New Star. For as far as Ptolemy is concerned, to the extent that the ancient accepted opinion follows him, he holds that all the orbs are solid, from the lowest Moon up to the outermost Saturn. Therefore, if the star were within the complex of the Saturnian heaven (since it is most certain through all three forms of hypotheses that it is placed above the Moon), it would therefore be in one of the solid orbs. And since the "penetration of bodies" <note>An Aristotelian principle that two solid objects cannot occupy the same space.</note> is not allowed, it would clearly be swept along with the orbs; because there is none of them that stands idle and motionless.

<margin>The same argument applies to Tycho Brahe's system.</margin>

As for Tycho Brahe, he would first fight with a dilemma: For either the solid spheres touch each other, as the Aristotelians claim, and thus the new star could not have been within the orbits of the planets without being carried into those parts in which that orb was revolved which provided a home to the star, since all orbs are assumed to move; or there are no solid spheres, and no contact or sweeping-along occurs, as Tycho prefers. And then Tycho would ask: if this new body was within the region of the mobile planets, by what agitation of the mind could any corruptible and temporary body follow the fixed stars of the foot of *Serpentarius* <note>The constellation Ophiuchus, the Serpent-Bearer, where the supernova appeared.</note>—into whose region and complex it is not admitted by the opponent—so religiously in their daily motion that it did not desert them by a "broad nail's width," <note>original: "latum unguem" — a Latin idiom meaning "not even the slightest bit."</note> having already revolved around the earth so many times? For indeed the planets recede from the fixed stars in their daily motion because of their own proper motions. All comets have done the same thing when they were observed to have stood among the planets. Thus, the location of the New Star is not among the planets. Next, Tycho maintains that all planets follow the Sun in such a way that if you were to mentally remove the planet's own motion—that is, if it happened that the planet was free from its own peculiar course—nevertheless, as the Sun goes around, by some natural law of sympathy, like some **ape**, it would go around in the ethereal air in a path clearly similar to the solar path, and clearly equal in dimensions and times, each in its own place.

A planet (P) is shown outside this circle, connected by lines to the Sun (B) and the Earth, illustrating the relationship between solar and planetary motion.] <-

...connecting, becomes B P, which when extended toward the fixed stars falls on F. In 17th-century astronomy, this refers to a natural affinity or physical "pull" between celestial bodies, a precursor to the concept of gravity.</note></term>, the line B P (and the planet at its end point P) revolves with the Sun itself. While remaining parallel to its original position, it is placed at QC, XD, and IE. Thus, the planet traces this circuit PQXI through this external force—a circuit equal to BCDE itself—but it appears against the background of the fixed stars at points FKZY. 

What reason could someone who holds this view offer for why the New Star would not behave exactly as all the other planets do, if it were located in the same region as them? Is it that no planet is strong enough to resist the Sun's rotation, and so all are conquered and dragged along, while only this new star has learned a contrary path? Has it gained such power that it could retrace the very path along which it was being swept, using an opposing effort to elude the Sun and remain fixed in its place? How incredible these things are! How <term>utterly inconsistent with reason<note>original Greek: *analogōtata*. Kepler uses this to describe an argument that fails to follow the logical patterns or "analogies" observed elsewhere in nature.</note></term>! 

And yet, it is certain from the star's apparent lack of motion that either it was not moved in this way to mimic the Sun's annual motion, or the movement...

is different from the twinkling of the planets; it is certainly slow and continues at great intervals, whereas the twinkling of the fixed stars confuses the sight with an abrupt and very rapid back-and-forth motion, with one overflowing burst of rays overwhelming another. Since there is no fixed star that this new one has not surpassed, as much in the purity of its light as in the extremely rapid flashing of its sparks, there is no reason why a natural philosopher, educated in the school of Aristotle, should continue to doubt that its location is shared with the fixed stars. <note>Kepler is refuting the Aristotelian idea that the heavens are unchanging. By proving the "New Star" is among the distant fixed stars rather than in our atmosphere, he is challenging the traditional view of a perfect, immutable universe.</note>

***

->### On the purity of light, colors, and the twinkling of the New Star.<-

->### CHAPTER XVIII.<-

It is pleasing to treat this topic more diligently. For what is it that gained us knowledge of this new star, if not this very light of its own, so brilliant, so wonderful? Therefore, it is a great reward for the labor if I can extract the causes of this brightness from the inner depths of nature here and there, which is what I am attempting.

<margin>Causes of twinkling.</margin>
J. C. Scaliger, in his *Exoteric Exercises*, book 63, <note>Julius Caesar Scaliger (1484–1558) was a famous scholar; his *Exotericae exercitationes* was a standard reference for natural philosophy in Kepler's time.</note> brings forward five causes concurring to produce twinkling: Magnitude, Clarity, the Motion of the star, the Medium of the air, and the Motion of light within the star. Whoever examines these causes diligently will find the first, second, and fourth among the helping factors; he will hold the third or fifth as the primary ones. Indeed, as I previously argued with the Aristotelians regarding the connection between twinkling and altitude, I did so to convince them <note>The Aristotelians believed that only objects far away twinkled because the distance strained the eyes.</note>. As far as the matter itself is concerned, altitude can do nothing by itself unless the other causes are present or are provided through altitude. For it has been said that twinkling also suits the planets in a certain way, especially Venus and Mercury; Mars very obscurely; the others not at all. And yet, the latter are higher <term>superior planets</term><note>In the geocentric or heliocentric models of the time, the "superior" planets (Mars, Jupiter, Saturn) were further from the Sun or Earth than the "inferior" ones (Mercury, Venus).</note>, while those three are lower; and Venus is indeed lower than Mars itself. Therefore, I remain in that opinion which I handed down in chapter 6 of my *Optics* <note>Kepler's 1604 book *Ad Vitellionem Paralipomena*, which revolutionized the study of light.</note>, which Scaliger also defends: that the cause of twinkling exists in the bodies of the stars themselves, whether it be the motion of the light or of the body. And first, concerning the motion of light.

<margin>I. Motion of light.</margin>
It pleases Scaliger, and I myself strongly approve, that there is a natural power in the body of a star of spreading its light, not without motion. The explanation of this obscure matter is not very clear; I will declare it by examples, if perhaps I may grasp the author's meaning while I put forward my own. I think a sufficiently apt example is found in the motion of the heart and the arteries; so that this too is common to the heart with the Sun, and life with light, besides those...

<summary>Kepler discusses why the "New Star" of 1604 must be located among the fixed stars based on its rapid twinkling, and begins a scientific investigation into the physical causes of stellar twinkling, comparing the star's light to the pulse of a heart.</summary>
<keywords>twinkling, scintillation, New Star, 1604 Supernova, Johannes Kepler, Julius Caesar Scaliger, Aristotelian physics, optics, planetary motion, stellar light</keywords>

<meta>Continuing from the previous discussion regarding the physical nature and light of the New Star (SN 1604), Kepler now moves into a comparison between the light of stars and terrestrial fires, specifically addressing the phenomenon of twinkling.</meta>

ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT BEARER. 93

...beyond those things which I stated in chapters 1 and 6 of my *Optics* <note>Kepler refers to his 1604 work *Astronomiae Pars Optica*, where he laid the foundations for modern optics</note>, let this be common ground. You should not draw an example here as if the body of the star expresses light through a certain squeezing of itself, in the way the heart expels the spirit <note>the vital force or "pneuma"</note> by compression and pours it into the body. For the vital spirit is something corporeal; light, being the most pure quality, does not require that sort of thick, physical impulse. Nevertheless, let some analogous motion be understood in the star—not a motion from place to place, but one of "alteration," according to that quality by which the star is a luminous body. Indeed, I propose a living example of this alteration in "living coals" <note>original: *vivis carbonibus*; refers to glowing embers</note>, which have earned the name "living" from this very motion and a kind of alternating vigor. Although this example, if you imagine a greater speed for it, corresponds more closely to the twinkling of Venus.

Aha! Is this what it means to philosophize—to be reduced to such poverty that the opinions and causes of things must be begged from the common people? For by Christ, nothing is more apt for an example than that which everyone perceives, and which Francesco Patrizi <note>a 16th-century Neoplatonist philosopher</note>—who seemed to possess the deepest wisdom, whether he truly thought so or was speaking from "barbaric" philosophy—certainly imitated uniquely when he pronounced the stars to be "little flames" <note>original: *flammulas*</note>. This is undoubtedly how they appear to the common folk, and it is the thing to which they bear an express likeness; it is also why they are so named by many, especially the poets. I have used this same term above regarding our New Star.

Is this, then, the cause of twinkling in the fixed stars, just as it is the cause for our torches to belch out one ray of light after another in an interrupted flow? I do not know. And indeed, I might say this more easily of our New Star, because it disappeared—having shone forth immensely and suddenly without any gradual growth. But regarding the fixed stars, which are eternal fires, there is a reason why I cannot affirm this. <margin>Why our own flames twinkle.</margin> For if you seek the reason why our fires flash so, whether in the hearth or in candles, and especially in the furnaces of the chemists <term>furnis Chymicorum</term> <note>referring to early chemical or alchemical laboratories</note> while they burn metals, you will find something that ought not to be found in the fixed stars, lest we should eventually extinguish them just like our own fires. 

Specifically, a struggle exists between the surrounding moist air (or the spirit exhaled from the metal) and the very dry, thin fire. Always, the place occupied by the fire is thinned out by the fire’s force; it draws into itself the surrounding air, which, being heavy, presses upon the light fire by its own weight. The fire, always gaining new strength from its fuel and being carried up by its abundance, dispels the air. Therefore, it is nourished by food <note>original: *pabulo*; literally "fodder" or fuel</note> for as long as it lasts, and is attacked by the surrounding medium. But how can these things be said of the fixed stars? What is their fuel? What air is their enemy? What age do they have? When have we missed any of them in these six thousand years <note>Kepler refers to the then-standard biblical age of the universe</note>? Therefore, let those be examples of light, not of substance. Regarding the fixed stars, let the opinion of Scaliger <note>Julius Caesar Scaliger, a famous scholar and physician</note> remain: that a power of reciprocating light resides in a perennial and also transparent body, analogous to...

<summary>Kepler explores whether the twinkling of stars is caused by a physical process similar to flickering flames or chemical furnaces, but concludes that stars must be made of a "perennial" substance unlike earthly fire.</summary>

<margin>II. The *dinesis* <note>Greek: *dinesis*, meaning "whirling" or "rotation"</note> of a star.</margin>
...to the burning up of our own flames. But as for our star <note>Kepler is likely referring to the New Star or Supernova of 1604</note>, we shall attribute to it either the same type of body or the same power of light, as will be seen later.
      Let us now consider a second cause of twinkling, derived from motion. I do not mean the motion of *translation* <note>The movement of an object from one location to another</note> which Scaliger <note>Julius Caesar Scaliger, a prominent 16th-century scholar</note> cites, but rather the motion of *rotation*—which I demonstrated using a diamond—where the different parts and angles of a polished and transparent body are revealed one after another. In this case, the first cause is not dismissed (for rotation alone does not grant light to a star), but it is equipped by this second cause, as if by a tool; so that even while the star remains quietly in its place, it can display flashes and various colors. This is perhaps necessary if such a great variety of motion, within the everlasting power of an everlasting body, has not yet been accepted by the fair judgment of philosophers. And indeed, that the motion of translation—which Scaliger had in mind—can contribute nothing to twinkling,

<margin>The motion of translation is not the cause of twinkling.</margin>
is clear from both reason and experiment. For how could the daily motion interfere with the rays of light? Either this motion does not belong to the fixed stars (according to Copernicus's view), or it leaves the fixed stars in tranquility and carries them around as if enclosed in a box, according to the old, received view. For as for the idea that our air is rubbed against by the heavens through this motion, that happens below the Moon. Therefore, if this could cause twinkling, then Saturn and Jupiter would also twinkle <note>Planets generally provide a steady light because they appear as small disks rather than single points of light like stars</note>. Furthermore, what follows from the friction of air to produce twinkling? He seems to be talking about iron horseshoes when they are rubbed against stones or very hard ice. Moreover, experiments testify that not all fixed stars twinkle equally, even when they are at the same height above the horizon and of the same size. Therefore, since the daily motion is equally common to all of them, it is not a suitable cause for producing twinkling.
      But that other cause I mentioned—the rotation of individual fixed stars—is preferable for all these reasons. For it explains the manner of twinkling and of colors (which are familiar in transparent objects, as I showed in my *Optics* <note>Kepler's 1604 work *Ad Vitellionem paralipomena*</note>), and it derives that manner from the very arrangement of the stars' bodies. This is all the more probable because those perpetual colors, which differ from star to star, must reside in this very arrangement of the star's body, as I proved in the *Optics*. Therefore, I do not fear that the bodies of stars cannot be everlasting simply because they are angular, or because they are of unequal density inside—just as pearls <term>Uniones: large, high-quality pearls often noted for their unique luster</term> often are, being transparent in some parts and not in others.

<margin>The fixed stars, while remaining in their own spaces, *dineisthai* <note>Greek: "to whirl"</note>, that is, they turn.</margin>
For there is no enemy there to apply force against them; even on this Earth, we have gems that are eternal if you remove external force, and yet they are partly spotted, partly cracked, and partly cloudy.
      Furthermore, the rotation of the fixed stars itself rests on great probability and great examples. But Copernicus alone provided the example of our own Earth, which rotates every single day so that it can enjoy the sight of the Sun from every side,

<summary>Kepler argues that the twinkling of stars is caused by their individual rotation rather than their movement through space or atmospheric friction. He compares the stars to rotating diamonds or pearls to explain how their physical structure and rotation create flashes of light and color.</summary>

### ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT'S FOOT 95

...each part, and as if it roasts itself at this fire. It is therefore believable that both the planets and all the fixed stars rotate, each in its own space; so that there may be nothing in the World that is entirely deprived of the vital rays and most splendid light of the center, that most noble body <note>original: *centri, nobilissimi corporis*—referring to the Sun as the heart of the solar system</note>. 

For regarding what might occur to you concerning the Moon—that she always looks back at the Earth with the same face while she is driven around it—she actually achieves this by that very revolution. Since she embraces not the Sun but only the Earth, this very fixation of a constant face toward the Earth ensures that she enjoys the Sun on every side within the space of one month: from a distance during the full moon, on the side visible to us; and up close during the new moon, on the side turned away from us.

<margin>What might be objected to the rotation of the fixed stars.</margin> Two objections occur to me: first, that the sight of the Sun seems to have nothing to do with the fixed stars. This is because it has been said that the entire Sphere of Saturn <term>Sphere of Saturn: the vast orbital path of the planet Saturn, which was the outermost known planet in Kepler's time</term> would occupy an angle of barely three minutes <note>arcminutes; a measurement of size in the sky</note> when viewed from the fixed stars. Therefore the Sun, which occupies such a tiny portion of the diameter of Saturn's sky, would completely vanish from the perspective of the fixed stars; for will it not appear smaller than the seven-hundredth part of one minute? 

The second objection is that scintillations <note>twinkling</note> are exceedingly rapid; it is therefore a wonder if such vast bodies, some of which exceed the size of Saturn’s sky, are turning.

First, if they possess many parts of the kind we mentioned that serve for scintillations and colors—which is indeed reasonable for anyone who accepts this cause to grant, precisely because the bodies are so enormous—it is no longer necessary that every time a single spark flashes, a single complete rotation has been finished. Instead, just as a wheel has many spokes, so these bodies are believed to thrust out many angles and many flashes in a single rotation. 

And what if these rotations are indeed rapid, as a compensation for the fact that they are denied any lateral movement <term>lateral movement: *tralationis*, referring to the stars' lack of orbital motion or "traveling" across the sky compared to planets</term>? Or perhaps for this very reason: that the light of the Sun is so weak in their vicinity? For the Sun does not vanish from Nature itself even when reduced to a seven-hundredth of a minute, though it would perhaps—and indeed certainly—vanish from our eyes if they were placed there. 

For it is not fair to judge the power and sharpness of nature by the dullness of our sight; just as the contemptible smallness of our little bodies creates no prejudice against acknowledging the vastness of the heavens. If God is so powerful in mass, as we considered above, why then should He not also be so acute and subtle in dispensing the light of the Sun? 

I beg you, reader, to forgive me if I have been meticulous <note>original: *minutus*, playing on the mathematical "minutes" discussed earlier</note> in arguing about a matter where you have seen above that we humans, our globe, the sky of our globe, and even ten times that sky, are clearly the tiniest things. However, let this cause of scintillation—rotation—remain in its place. Let us now look at the remaining arguments of Scaliger <note>Julius Caesar Scaliger, a scholar whose theories on the heavens Kepler frequently analyzed</note>.

Probabl— <note>The text cuts off mid-word "Verif-" or "Probabl-"</note>

<margin>III. The clarity of light.</margin> He says most truly in the second place that a weakness of light hinders **scintillation** <note>Kepler uses the term *scintillatio* to describe the twinkling or rapid sparking of stars.</note>, which I interpret as a dullness. Therefore, the clarity and purity of light that has been purified of material impurities will greatly assist its twinkling. Certainly. For because light is the source of the sparkles, the purer the light is, the more rapid the twinkling becomes. On this account, the lights of the Planets are indeed powerful and obvious, certainly due to their proximity; but they are thick, full, corporeal, and highly colored: and for this reason they twinkle less. The light of the fixed stars, however, is more watery, thin, sharp, glassy, transparent, or diluted. And this is the reason why the twinkling in our **New Star** <note>original: "Nova stella." Kepler is referring to the supernova of 1604, which he observed extensively and which is now known as Kepler's Supernova.</note> was so rapid. For nothing purer, nothing clearer, nothing more beautiful than that extraordinary light could exist or even be imagined.

<margin>IV. Magnitude</margin> Next follows magnitude, which **Scaliger** <note>Julius Caesar Scaliger (1484–1558), a celebrated scholar who wrote on the physics of light and the nature of the heavens.</note> places in the first position. Again, however, magnitude could achieve nothing if the light were absent...

***

On the Matter of the New Star.

CHAPTER XIX.

All discussion regarding the matter of the star depends on what we have said so far. Since no one was present in the heavens in person, certainly whatever we cannot perceive here with our eyes—to which the lights of the stars descend by a certain propagation of their form—we strive in vain to investigate. It is clear, therefore, from the discussion on light and sparkling, that it was one of two things: either a flame, or a body consisting of its own matter. If it was a body, it either contained that palpitating motion within itself, from whose paroxysms <note>Kepler uses the medical term "paroxysms" (fits or sudden attacks) to describe the rhythmic bursts of light.</note> that sparkling shone forth; or the star was transparent in itself and was rotated with the swiftest revolution. In neither case is the matter achieved without the likeness of a certain life, and without the service of an informing, moving, or vegetative faculty <note>Kepler is wondering if the star is "alive" in a sense, possessing a "soul" or "faculty" that directs its growth and movement, an idea common in 17th-century natural philosophy.</note>. <margin>Whether the star was of a flaming nature.</margin> Indeed, I favor the first opinion, that it was a flame; because, like a flame, it was consumed as if its nourishment were failing. For then it would not be necessary to assume "life" to demonstrate the sparkling, except for that life which belongs to a flame or to glowing coals. But I cannot explain this: why did it scatter rays all around? Since our local flames grow upward from a certain and fixed root; nor is it likely that the same struggle between moisture and fire exists there, which produces that waving motion of flames here among us; unless someone, perhaps, would wish to carry this humidity of our air all the way up into the highest ether <note>The "ether" was thought to be the pure, unchanging substance of the heavens, distinct from the "corrupt" air of Earth.</note>, a claim that the discipline of Optics rejects. But if there were rays all around, then the nourishment was in the middle, and thus it was a body. In this uncertainty, let anyone follow what they wish. It is certain that it was either a flame, or [a result of] life

<summary>Kepler concludes that the star's twinkling must come from its own internal nature rather than just Earth's atmosphere, and he debates whether the supernova was a rotating "living" body or a cosmic flame that eventually burned out.</summary>
<keywords>Kepler's Supernova, Ophiuchus, optics, refraction, scintillation, celestial matter, ether, 17th-century astronomy</keywords>

...that it had life; unless perhaps the effective causes of these same things (light, colors, twinkling) are not the same below the Moon and among the fixed stars.
<margin>The kinship of celestial things with those below the Moon.</margin> 
Whoever objects to this casts reproach upon our entire investigation. Yet there are many things—some of which I have even brought forward in my *Optics*—which prove that those celestial things are distinct from these earthly things not so much by a difference in essence, but by their distance. I will not dwell on Moses, who separates "these waters from those waters" <note>original: "has aquas ab illis aquis"; a reference to the creation story in Genesis 1:6-7.</note> by the expansion alone (so those learned in Hebrew understand the word <term>Raquiah</term> <note>The Hebrew word *Raqia* is often translated as "firmament" or "expanse," referring to the sky or atmosphere.</note>); though they were mixed together at the beginning of the world. Nor will I dwell on Copernicus, who gave the Earth itself "citizenship" in the heavens.

<margin>That the star had its own matter.</margin> 
However it may be, this is true in general: this star had its own body and matter. For the fires of Patrizi <note>Francesco Patrizi (1529–1597) was a philosopher who argued that stars were immaterial, living fires. Kepler is cooling his "hot take" here.</note>—immaterial and living, as he describes the stars—cooled down long ago and were thus extinguished. Let an example be shown where light, whether standing by itself or depending on the illumination of another, has lacked a material, corporeal subject. Perhaps Patrizi will say that such a light is the Sun? This begs the question: the dispute is about those things in heaven which do not allow themselves to be handled or touched; I ask for an example on Earth. Will he say those celestial things are not similar to these earthly ones? Why then does he, a man of Earth, argue at all, since nothing is in his mind that did not first enter through the gates of the earthly senses? Truly, those very celestial lights descend to us on Earth. Let him show, then, in any place, a celestial light—the appearance of the Sun, Moon, or a star—where an "illuminable" subject <note>A physical thing that can be lit up.</note> is absent. He will find it nowhere; it is either brightness in the air when the air is very thick, or in a cloud (which is watery), or in a mirror.

<margin>A mirror image, hanging in the air.</margin> 
For as to the fact that in concave mirrors, images remain between the viewer and the mirror: though it appears to be "in the air" and as if without a body, this is a deception of sight, as is explained in the *Optics*. This is why it takes its name from "imagination" <note>Kepler is punning on the Latin *imago* (image) and *imaginatio* (imagination).</note>; in which a location is indeed perceived in the air, but the seen appearance itself is nowhere else but in its own subject as the source, and in the eye of the viewer (even if turned away from the object), through the reflection bounced back from the mirror, as is taught in the *Optics*. But our question here is not about the location, but about the *body* which represented that radiance we are discussing. Let Patrizi say if he pleases that there was some mirror there too, provided he admits it was a body, and does not thrust these "immaterial fires" upon us.

<margin>Planets are bodies, not immaterial lights.</margin> 
Furthermore, he cannot show any self-moving fires without matter. Certainly all the planets—by their roundness, spots, colors, their increase in size when they approach us, and by <term>parallaxes</term> <note>The apparent shift in a star's position when viewed from different locations; Kepler uses this to prove planets have a measurable distance and physical presence.</note> through which we come to know their specific heights—are proven to be bodies, not immaterial fires; and...

<summary>Kepler argues that the New Star must be a physical, material body rather than an "immaterial fire," using principles of optics and the biblical and Copernican precedents for the kinship between Earth and the heavens.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Kepler's investigation into the physical nature of the "New Star" (the supernova of 1604). He is currently debating whether the star is made of physical matter or if it is a purely spiritual or "immaterial" light, as some contemporary philosophers argued.</meta>

->ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT. 99<-

and some of these things the <term>fixed stars</term> <note>Stars that appear to stay in a permanent position relative to one another, unlike the "wandering" planets.</note> have in common with the planets. Indeed, on this earth, who has ever seen a fire that did not have a body? I am speaking of natural things. For I do not dwell here on spirits, nor on divine lights <margin>Divine lights</margin>—which are seen by those who either enjoy secret conversations <note>original: "arcanis... alloquiis"; likely referring to mystical or prophetic visions.</note> or, through a cursed curiosity, take pleasure in dealings with demons. For if this star were such a light, then some follower of **Patrizi** <note>Francesco Patrizi (1529–1597) was a philosopher who argued that the heavens were filled with a non-material "medicinal" light.</note>—who so vehemently denies it has matter—would make it out to be a mere illusion, or plainly a divine light in the midst of infinite natural circumstances. This is something I would easily allow anyone to believe if it pleases them. 

At present, however, I am attempting a path by which I may provide a natural explanation for that which appeared among natural things. Nor did **Patrizi** aim for anything else than to show that those eternal fires of the heavens, which we know to be parts of Nature, were immaterial; a point which I deny him at this time. Let someone, then, tell me of a fire or a light that exists without a subject <note>In philosophy, the "subject" is the physical substance or underlying matter that supports a quality like "light" or "heat."</note>. If there is any example, it is the <term>will-o'-the-wisps</term> <margin>The matter of will-o'-the-wisps.</margin> <note>original: "ignes fatui."</note>; yet these wander around marshy places and swamps, making it clear that they use "fatty" exhalations <note>A 17th-century scientific term for flammable gases or vapors rising from the earth.</note> as their subject. 

**Aristotle** proves that shooting stars <note>original: "Trajectiones."</note>—a phenomenon most similar to stars—consist of their own matter. Experience once taught me the same: when a huge fire broke out in this city <note>Likely referring to Prague, where Kepler served as Imperial Mathematician.</note>, I saw more shooting stars <margin>The matter of shooting stars.</margin> in a single clear night than I had seen in a whole decade before. Therefore, some kind of "fatty" or dry matter, carried upward by heat into the cold air, distributed in various places, and compressed into small spheres—and thus ignited, or if you prefer, illuminated—displayed so many types of racing fires. 

In short, none of these things exist without matter. Let us conclude, then, with great probability: <margin>The kind of matter in the new star.</margin> the matter provided for the **New Star** was either "fatty" matter that was ignited by the cold (like shooting stars), or aqueous <note>Water-like or vaporous.</note> matter in which either the Sun or an internal light shone forth. Both options have some probability. For as far as the shooting star theory is concerned, I saw very recently—on the 18th of December, 1605 <note>original: "8/18 Decembris"; Kepler provides both the Julian and Gregorian calendar dates.</note>—at the onset of a cold snap following a warm spell, a falling star very similar to the **New Star** as it first shone. It was brilliant white, perfectly clear, and it burst into flames several times like those artificial fires they call **little rockets** <note>original: "Raketulos."</note>; **Pliny** would call them **fireballs** <note>original: "Bolides."</note>. On the other hand, the colors of the rainbow and the "twinkling" <note>original: "scintillatio."</note> favor the idea of a watery globe—an opinion I also touched upon in my *Optics* <note>Referring to his 1604 book *Astronomiae Pars Optica* (The Optical Part of Astronomy).</note>.

->**Whether the matter and body of the New Star already existed long ago.**<-
#### CHAPTER 20.

The question now is, from where did the stars in the highest ether <note>In Kepler's time, "ether" was the substance believed to fill the heavens beyond the Earth's atmosphere.</note> come—such a massive amount of matter that it could suffice to kindle such a great fire? It certainly did not come from our Earth, from which smoke and "shootings" <note>Kepler refers to meteorological phenomena like shooting stars or vapors.</note> originate; for the Earth is far too small compared to that vast magnitude. If anyone should stubbornly strive to object here, either by drawing matter from the earth up into the ether, or by dragging a star down from the ether to below the Moon: I declare to him that I shall depart from the **Peripatetic school** <note>Followers of Aristotle’s philosophy.</note> with deaf ears, leaving that man shouting and raving. For such persistent defenders are only silenced when you first ignore their shouts with a firm silence. I shall deal with reasonable men. First, we must not labor over the size, if the location is certain; for this star was certainly no larger than any "shooting" in our own air.

<margin>Plutarch, *On the Opinions of the Philosophers*, Chapter 2, Book 3.</margin>
<margin>Certain **Pythagoreans** believe a Comet is a star of the same number as those which are not always seen, but arise at a fixed time in their own revolution.</margin>
<margin>The same is said by **Pliny** and **Aristotle**, who refutes them because they said that when they are not seen, they <note>original: "δύνειν"</note> **set** under the Sun. But he [Aristotle] says, many <note>original: "ἄνευ δύσεως ἠφανίσθησαν"</note> **vanished without setting**, in a place clearly visible above the horizon. Pliny ridiculously translated this <term>Pericope</term> <note>A short extract or passage from a text.</note>, claiming that Comets are not seen in the part of the sky where it sets.</margin>

There are, however, two opinions that pertain to this matter. The first is held by those who deny that anything new exists in the heavens after that first Sabbath of creation: they believe instead that bodies of this kind hide in a certain "storehouse of Nature" from the very origin of things, lacking light; except when it pleases God to signify something through them. Others, however, believe that not only the light, but the bodies themselves in the heavens are sudden and new: whether they are created by that absolute power of the Divine Majesty, by which God fashioned the whole world in the beginning; or whether they consist of some force of celestial Nature, in the same manner as those thick, or if you prefer, dry exhalations consist in this sublunary air; which, massed together by the force of cold, are converted into "running stars" <note>meteors</note>.

<margin>A skirmish with **David Fabricius**.</margin>
Theologians especially embrace that former opinion; and my friend **Fabricius** argues according to its tenor—being a theologian himself, and thus more inclined toward traditions <note>original: "ἀναιτιολογήτους"</note> **unaccounted for by physical cause**; which he demonstrates in astrological matters as much as anyone. From him—not being indignant, but rejoicing and applauding—

# ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT. 101

...as he [David Fabricius] challenges and provokes me, and I do the same in return, just as I am often accustomed to disagreeing with him through the frequent exchange of letters; so he will grant me pardon if I pursue a refutation of this opinion of his, which has burst out from our private letters into the public. First, having begun with the theologians, he himself also argues that their reasoning regarding God’s rest after the seventh day is weak; following, thus far, the thread of my own letters. For this is how I responded to him, when he proposed this theological objection based on the opinions of others: It seems an unnecessary fear that we might cast God into some "new labor," so to speak, if He were to create something new. And yet, even if those celestial bodies had existed from the beginning, this much must still be confessed as "new": that things which formerly lacked light are now illuminated. Whoever attributes this action to God establishes Him as the creator of something new through that act. For what of it? Did God not create the heavens and the earth in six days? What then of the first day? The divine Prophet says, "God said, **Let there be light**"; <note>original: "Fiat lux"</note> and with the institution of this, God finished the first day. Therefore, to produce light is to create. Unless, perhaps, He did not create a new light within the star, but merely removed an external hindrance or shadow? As if this were perhaps lighter for God than creating something new, or as if this did not pertain to creation! For what, again, did God create on the second day? What else, except that by making the firmament He separated the waters from the waters? Therefore, among the works of the second day were these: to separate what was joined, and to remove obstacles. Furthermore, God Himself promises to create a **new heaven and a new earth**. And all Orthodox believers hold this to be recorded among the principal articles of the Christian religion. Add to this that those same theologians, who have taken away from God the power of creating a new star in the heavens, nevertheless recognize His daily <term>activity</term> <note>original Greek: *ἐνέργειαν* (energeian), referring to divine operation or work</note> in creating new souls. I had raised this objection to those with whom I was dealing. But Fabricius, raising the same point, weakens it: leaving the dispute over souls in suspense, which was not necessary when dealing with a specific adversary. And so far, there is agreement between us on the matter itself.

Immediately departing from this, he takes up the opinion he had hitherto opposed, seeking to moderate it: that these stars shine "newly," but are not "new" in their physical bodies. First, he considers it an "astronomical opinion" that the bodies of these stars exist anew. I had denied in my letters that this is an astronomical dogma; for those who are first deserted by the light [when a star fades] have nothing further by which they can either establish or refute anything. That this star was suddenly kindled, and from there gradually extinguished, the astronomers confirm by the evidence of sight; but whether the bodies existed before the light was received, they leave that to be weighed by Fabricius the theologian and other physicists. I, having long ago entered upon this physical inquiry from the starting point of astronomy...

<meta>This page continues Kepler’s critique of contemporary astronomical theories regarding the "New Star" (Supernova) of 1604. He specifically addresses the arguments of David Fabricius, a Frisian astronomer and correspondent.</meta>

->102 JOHANNES KEPLER<-

investigation; I will weigh the four arguments of **Fabricius**, by which he wishes to prove that the body of a star has always existed. **First**, he says the **Moon** is often "extinguished" <note>original: *extingui*; referring to the Moon becoming invisible during an eclipse or its new phase</note> while its body remains: therefore, he believes the same is true of these unusual stars. I deny to you, **Fabricius**, that a likelihood follows from this example; only the *possibility* of the thing follows, if there are other conjectures from elsewhere that might persuade us of the thing itself. You do not bring an argument, then, but you remove a doubt <note>Kepler uses the word *scrupulum*, which literally means a small sharp stone in one's shoe, but also means a "scruple" or mental doubt</note>, provided that any arguments may follow: if not, there is not even a doubt; for "no one is pressed by a pebble <note>original: *scrupulus*</note> unless they are wearing shoes."

He draws a **second argument** from the motion of **Comets**: in which he finds something of "nobility," similar to the eternal **Planets**, such as <term>stations</term> <note>When a planet appears to stand still in the sky before changing direction</note>, <term>retrogressions</term> <note>When a planet appears to move backward against the stars</note>, and the increasing and decreasing of motion. Therefore, he thinks that the bodies of Comets, because of this "divinity," are eternal, and are not immediately annihilated when their brightness is extinguished. And if Comets are such; then it is easy to believe that the new stars are also such.

You strike us with shadows, O **Fabricius**, to force us "fearful cattle" <note>A likely reference to a Latin idiom for a crowd easily swayed by appearances rather than reason</note> into your opinion. For what does the motion of Comets have to do with the immobility of this **New fixed [star]**? If you argue for divinity based on the motion of Comets, and from this you argue for permanence: then by taking away motion from our star, you have also snatched away its divinity and its permanence, which you had wished to establish. Furthermore, those retrogressions you admire in the Planets, and those stations, and those inequalities of motion: these have rightly and deservedly tormented physicists until now as things familiar to our sublunary <note>Below the moon, i.e., earthly and imperfect</note> trajectories, but little suited to those divine and eternal bodies of the Planets.

And so there were found **Copernicus**, a new version of the ancient **Aristarchus**, and **Tycho Brahe**: who attempted to remove those turbulent motions from the heavens—which succeeded most happily indeed. Therefore, by means of the Planets, we are free to thrust your Comets down below the Moon and subject them to change by the same argument with which you strive to consecrate them to eternity: unless you also, with the same dexterity, take away from them those fantasies of stations and retrogradations, and discover that the motion which remains for the Comets themselves, when cleared of this appearance, is entirely circular.

But I will free you from the labor. I have tried the thing myself also, as you see me having professed in my *Optics* <note>Referring to his 1604 work *Astronomiae Pars Optica*</note>. And so, when that motion which was attributed to Comets because of the "annual orbit" of **Tycho** and **Copernicus** <note>The Earth's movement around the Sun</note> was removed, there remained a motion that was not circular, but for the most part entirely straight and very similar to things passing through <note>meaning linear trajectories rather than permanent orbits</note>. Except that the same can happen through several media: whence **Brahe** and **Maestlin** <note>Michael Maestlin, Kepler's teacher</note> even devised circles for the Comet of the year 1577, though by a very different reasoning. And I, unless I had other arguments by which I assign Comets to the heavens, would easily [place] them under the Moon by this argument of yours.

<summary>Kepler refutes David Fabricius’s theory that the 1604 Supernova is a permanent body, arguing that the "planetary" behaviors Fabricius sees in comets are actually optical illusions caused by the Earth's own motion.</summary>
<keywords>Johannes Kepler, David Fabricius, Supernova of 1604, Tycho Brahe, Nicolaus Copernicus, Michael Maestlin, planetary motion, retrogradation, comets, celestial permanence</keywords>

<meta>Continuing the debate against the astronomer David Fabricius regarding the physical nature of "new stars" (supernovae) and whether they are permanent bodies or temporary phenomena.</meta>

### ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT'S FOOT. 103

...I would allow it to be distinct from the Moon. Thus, this second likelihood of yours recoils against you.

Fabricius points to a third conjecture: that a gap <note>original Latin: "hiatus"</note> appeared in the location of the New Star which had shone in the year 1572 and was extinguished in the year 1574, as Tycho Brahe testifies on page 795 of his *Progymnasmata* <note>Tycho Brahe’s "Introduction to the New Astronomy," which detailed his observations of the 1572 supernova</note>. Fabricius thinks this gap is the body of the star itself, now stripped of its light. If Fabricius believed this—that the gap is the star’s body—I admit he had a just cause for establishing a new doctrine, though it is quite formless in itself. For who can argue with their own eyes? Yet I hope to strip this persuasion from Fabricius, who is an ingenious man. 

First, he himself adds the size of the spot: equal to a half-full Moon. However, the star of that year, even with the full spread of its rays (which usually seem to extend to our senses beyond the physical limits of the bodies), was smaller than Venus. Not to mention that if the star's body is visible, it must be seen by means of light; therefore, the star would not yet be extinct. Nor do I strongly press the point that God would seem not to have looked after His "treasure" very well—which He wished to hide until another occasion—since Fabricius and Brahe still possess it as if it were not extinguished. 

I bid you to consider this carefully: every gap in the Milky Way appears to be an opening <note>original: "hiatus"</note>, even if no star ever shone there. For physicists know from Aristotle's *Meteorology* <note>A classic ancient text on natural science and atmospheric phenomena</note> that if a bright or white border surrounds a dark place lacking light on all sides, a pit seems to appear in the middle, which the Greeks call a **Chasm** <note>original Greek: "Chasma"</note>. Therefore, Brahe was received with great applause when he applied this "gap" to confirm his own opinion: that the star of that year was condensed from the matter of the Milky Way that had been dispersed through this gap; and once the star's matter was consumed and, as it were, burnt out, the place was left empty of the "milk" <note>The "milk" refers to the hazy, luminous material that makes up the Milky Way</note>. This likelihood of Brahe’s opinion competes with that of Fabricius in such a way that no other arguments are needed to tear it down.

Fabricius derives a fourth conjecture from the location: that it remains among the fixed stars. Therefore, just like the fixed stars, these stars too should be seen as perennial <note>everlasting</note>. Why did he not also twist the argument toward the perenniality of their light? Thus, if it is the most characteristic property of stars **To Shine**, and this happens to certain "New" stars—that they shine when they previously did not—what wonder is it if some also begin to exist among the fixed stars which were not there before, and along with their light, the bodies themselves perish once their period is complete?

I believe it is sufficiently clear that there is no suitable reason for anyone to think that those new stars existed before they were seen, or that they are preserved as survivors after they have been extinguished, waiting for a new illumination.
