## CHAPTER XXI.
### Has this star been drawn back into the depths of the ether by its motion, and does the sphere of the fixed stars extend into infinity?

**BUT** there is another school of philosophers—those who (as Aristotle unfairly pronounced regarding the opinion of the Pythagoreans <note>The Pythagoreans were ancient Greek philosophers who suggested the Earth might move; Kepler notes that Copernicus later revived this idea.</note>, a view later repeated by Copernicus) do not derive the starting point of their reasoning from the senses, nor do they adapt the causes of things to experimental evidence. Instead, they suddenly, and as if seized by a kind of *enthusiasm* <note>original: "Enthusiasmo"—here meaning a sudden burst of divine inspiration or a feverish intellectual frenzy.</note>, conceive and depict within the walls of their own brains a certain opinion about the constitution of the World. 

Once they have embraced this idea tooth and nail, they proceed to take those things which fall under our experience, and which occur and appear to us daily, and "drag them by the hair" <note>Kepler uses the idiom "crinibus pertracta" to describe how these theorists force evidence to fit their preconceived notions.</note> to make them fit their own axioms. It pleases such men to believe that this new star <note>Kepler is referring to the supernova that appeared in the constellation Ophiuchus in 1604.</note>, and any others of this kind that have existed, have emerged from the innermost depths of Nature...

<summary>Kepler critiques philosophers who prioritize abstract theories over sensory observation, specifically those who force astronomical data to fit their preconceived notions of a boundless universe.</summary>
<keywords>Aristotle, Copernicus, Pythagoras, new star, supernova of 1604, ether, fixed stars, scientific method, a priori reasoning</keywords>

--- Page 157 ---
<meta>This page continues Kepler’s investigation into the physical nature and location of the "New Star" (the supernova of 1604). In the preceding pages, he discussed various astronomical observations regarding its brightness and position; here, he critiques the theory that the star appeared and disappeared because it was moving toward and away from the Earth in a straight line.</meta>

-> ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT. 105 <-

How then could it provide this appearance of magnitude by approaching? <note>original: "ex appropinquando." Kepler is questioning the theory that the star only looked larger because it was physically getting closer to Earth.</note> Add to this that it did not increase gradually, as it was seen to decrease gradually, but suddenly shone at its maximum.

There were those, therefore, who thirty years ago <note>Referring to the supernova of 1572, often called Tycho’s Star.</note> explained these matters more soberly: that the star of the year 1572 performed its motion in a straight line, extending from the center upward into the ether. But let them also state the cause for why it was lowered through that immense space so suddenly (since it shone forth suddenly), and then did not fly back suddenly, but only gradually—and what was the cause for its reversed course? For this is not a property of natural things. But if they say the cause was supernatural, can I not just as easily affirm that it was created from nothing by God, as they say it was lowered from on high by Him? Since both would be the work of the same power. But I break off this dispute now, as I have often done before, whenever they appeal to the absolute power of God. For it is certain that we can say nothing relevant to the matter that has any weight if we exceed the limits of nature. Let us rather say to them that they entangle themselves in that infinity of fixed stars as if in inexplicable labyrinths.

And so let us snatch away from them, if it can be done, this immensity: for then that theory of ascent will fall of its own accord. This idea is brought back from the ancient schools of the pagan <margin>Of the fixed stars</margin> philosophers, chiefly by this argument: that Aristotle demonstrated the world <margin>Sphere</margin> was not finite based on motion, while Copernicus allowed the sphere of fixed stars, once its motion was removed, to be infinite. <margin>of height, or thickness.</margin>

Thus that unfortunate <note>Kepler likely calls him "unfortunate" because Bruno was executed by the Inquisition only six years prior, in 1600.</note> Giordano Bruno defended it; and William Gilbert also asserted it not obscurely, under the guise of doubting, in his book *On the Magnet*—a work otherwise most excellent—yet he demonstrated a religious sentiment in it, because he thought God’s infinite power could be understood by nothing else more rightly than if He founded a world of infinite mass. But Bruno makes the world so infinite that he makes as many worlds as there are fixed stars, and he makes this our <term>region of moving bodies</term> <note>The solar system, containing the planets or "wandering stars."</note> one of countless worlds, distinguished by almost no mark from the others surrounding it. So much so that if someone were on the Dog Star <note>Sirius.</note> (I suppose one of the <term>Dog-headed men</term> <note>original: "Cynocephalis." A reference to the satirical "True History" by the ancient writer Lucian of Samosata, who imagined strange creatures living on other worlds.</note> of Lucian), the appearance of the world from there would seem the same as it appears to us here, looking up at the fixed stars from our world. Thus, according to them, this new star would be some new world. This thought alone carries with it a certain hidden horror; for a person finds himself wandering in this immensity, where boundaries, a center, and therefore certain locations, are all denied. And it would perhaps be better to hold up the authority of Moses, who shows us finite waters, and the Spirit of the Lord carried over them; then on the second day, the waters separated from the waters.

<summary>Kepler critiques the idea of an infinite universe proposed by Giordano Bruno and William Gilbert, arguing that such a "labyrinthine" concept creates a "hidden horror" of a world without a center or boundaries.</summary>
<keywords>Giordano Bruno, William Gilbert, infinity, 1604 Supernova, Aristotle, Copernicus, Dog Star, Biblical cosmology, infinite universe</keywords>

--- Page 158 ---
<meta>This page continues Kepler’s argument against the idea of an infinite universe, a concept championed by contemporary "infinitist" philosophers who used the Copernican system to justify their claims. Kepler seeks to prove that our solar system occupies a unique, central "cavity" within the stars.</meta>

->106 JOHANNES KEPLER<-

But because this sect <note>Kepler refers to the "infinitists," such as Giordano Bruno or the ancient Epicureans, who argued that the universe has no center and that every star is a sun like our own.</note> misuses the authority of the Copernican system—and indeed of all Astronomy—claiming that because Copernicus makes the fixed stars immobile, and because all Astronomy (especially the Copernican) shows them to be incredibly high: Come, let us seek a remedy from Astronomy itself. Just as it was by Astronomy's own indulgence that this madness of philosophizing was provoked to break its bounds and barriers and rush out into this infinity, let it also be brought back within the boundaries and prisons of the World by the same arts and by alluring whispers. <note>original: "blandientibus poppyismis" — a term for the soothing clicking sounds used to calm or call a horse.</note> Certainly, it is not well for one who wanders through that infinite void.

I say first, that it can be shown most certainly from Astronomy that the region of the fixed stars is terminated by an evident limit on its inner side. It is not true, as they propose, that this lower world with its Sun is indistinguishable in appearance from any of the fixed stars—that is, that region does not differ from region, or place from place.

For even if we granted, for the sake of argument, that the fixed stars extend outward to infinity, it is still true that in this innermost heart of things, there is a remarkable cavity. This void is distinct from the intervals between the fixed stars by a massive disproportion. Thus, if someone were able to look upon this cavity alone—even if they were ignorant of the eight small bodies <note>Kepler likely refers to the Sun, Mercury, Venus, Earth, Moon, Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn.</note> flying around the center of this space in a very narrow interval, and did not know what, of what sort, or how many they were—they would nonetheless conclude from this void alone, when compared to the surrounding spherical region packed with stars, that a certain special place exists here: the chief bay <note>original: "sinum"</note> of the world. 

For let us take, for example, the three stars of the second magnitude in the belt of Orion. Any one of these is separated from its neighbor by 81 minutes <note>81 arcminutes, or about 1.35 degrees.</note>, while the star itself has a diameter of at least two minutes. <note>Before the telescope, stars were thought to have measurable disks; we now know these "diameters" were optical illusions.</note> Therefore, if they were placed on the same spherical surface of which we are the center, an eye placed on one of those stars would see the neighbor at an angular size of nearly 2 3/4 degrees. That is a greater space than five of our Suns, placed in a row and touching each other, would occupy for us on Earth. And yet, these are not even the stars closest to one another; for innumerable smaller stars are scattered between them. 

Therefore, if someone were stationed in that belt of Orion, having our Sun and the center of the world directly above their head, they would first see a kind of perpetual sea of huge stars, appearing to touch one another. From there, the more they raised their eyes upward, the smaller the stars they would see, and the less they would appear to touch. Gradually, they would see them more and more scattered; but directly above their head, they would see the same stars we see, but twice as small, and as much closer to one another as they appear to us. Our face of the sky is not like that; we see stars on all sides, indeed of varying magnitude, but and...

<summary>Kepler argues that the solar system sits in a unique "cavity" within the universe, proving that our location is special and not just one of many identical star systems in an infinite void. He uses the proximity and size of stars in Orion's belt to show how different the sky would look from elsewhere compared to our view from Earth.</summary>

<keywords>infinity, Copernicus, fixed stars, Orion's belt, celestial geometry, cosmic cavity, astronomical observation, solar system center</keywords>

--- Page 159 ---
<meta>This page continues Kepler's discussion on the distribution of stars across the celestial sphere, specifically addressing arguments regarding the potential infinity of the universe and the varying distances of the "fixed stars."</meta>

->ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT. 107<-

And this [distribution] is for the most part equal on all sides: indeed, around Orion and the Twins <note>Gemini</note>, we behold many great and crowded stars: the Eye of the Bull <note>Aldebaran</note>, Capella, the Heads of the Twins <note>Castor and Pollux</note>, the Dogs <note>Sirius and Procyon</note>, and the Shoulders, Belt, and Foot of Orion. But in the opposite part of the sky there are equally great stars: the Lyre <note>Vega</note>, the Eagle <note>Altair</note>, the Heart and forehead of the Scorpion <note>Antares and Dschubba</note>, Ophiuchus, and the Scales of Libra; and further ahead, Arcturus and the Ear of Grain <note>Spica</note> in Virgo; likewise further back, the Last Star of Aquarius and similar ones.

When I once proposed this theme to certain people—who, to challenge me, fiercely defended the cause of infinity (as taken up from the aforementioned authors)—they thought it would be easy for them, if infinity were granted, to separate two <term>fixed stars</term><note>Stars that appear to stay in fixed positions relative to each other, as opposed to "wandering" planets.</note> from each other by as great a distance as we are certain we are distant from the fixed stars, even though we judge them to be very close from Earth. But this is impossible. For even if you could, at your whim, raise up two fixed stars at an equal distance from the center of the <term>moving world</term><note>The solar system, which Kepler viewed as the dynamic, moving part of the universe centered near the Sun.</note>, it would indeed happen, as they say, that they would move as far apart from each other as the center of the moving parts is now distant from both. But they should remember that if they raise the fixed stars, this vacuum—which is in the middle and within the circular embrace of the fixed stars—is increased along with them. They, however, thoughtlessly assumed that this vacuum would remain the same for us while the fixed stars were ascending.

"What if," they ask, "taking two stars from the Belt of Orion, one remained in that sphere where the <term>doctrine of parallaxes</term><note>The method of measuring the distance to a star by observing its apparent shift against the background from different points in Earth's orbit. Kepler argues that since stars show no measurable parallax, they must be at a certain minimum distance.</note> does not admit a lower star, and the other was said to be higher by infinite intervals? Would we not by this means cause them to appear just as small when viewed one from the other as each now appears to us? And would the intervals between the two stars be found empty of stars, equal to these intervals between us and both of them?"

Perhaps, I say, the matter could be helped in this way, if there were only two or a few stars, and if they were not cast around and scattered in a circle. Truly, you will either raise and leave behind such stars alternately, or all of them together. If alternately, the question is not removed, even if slightly diminished. For concerning those that remained nearby, the same affirmation still holds: that two stars are closer to each other—when a comparison is made of their distances to their diameters, and to their distance from the Sun. As for those that are raised higher: <margin>Whether all fixed stars are of equal body, and at unequal intervals.</margin> they will indeed be further apart, but they will nonetheless remain in a great proportion. And this I might easily concede without detriment to my cause: that all the fixed stars are of the same size, and that those which appear large to us are near to us, while those that are small are that much more remote: *Not that they are less bright, but that they recede further into the heights,* as Manilius sings <note>original: "Non quod clara minus, sed quod magis alta recedant" — A quote from the Roman poet Manilius in his 'Astronomica'.</note>.

I say I might concede it, but I would not assert it. For it can just as easily be believed that they differ in reality in their brightness and colors, as they also do in size. And perhaps...

<summary>Kepler debates the nature of the universe's scale, arguing against proponents of an infinite universe by discussing the density of stars and the relationship between their apparent size and their distance from Earth.</summary>

->108 JOHANNES KEPLER<-

<margin>...whether they are [distant], or whether, being at equal distances from us, they possess unequal diameters?</margin>

And perhaps both are true, following the example of the Planets <note>Kepler refers to the planets of our solar system to explain that both physical size and distance affect how big an object looks to us</note>, some of which are truly larger than others, while others only appear larger—even if they are physically smaller—simply because they are closer to us.

But let us proceed to the second part of the argument, describing what would happen if every single star were pulled apart from one another, each by its own specific <term>interval<note>original: diastema</note></term>: in such a way that the star closest to us maintains that proximity which Astronomy currently sets as the limit for all stars, allowing none to be closer. From that first star, let all the others be moved further away, each neighbor being placed as much further out as the distance of the closest star is from us. 

Not even by this arrangement is anything accomplished. The situation is never brought to a point where the view we see would appear the same to those who are imagined to be observing from those other stars, for example. From this it is concluded that this place where we are, the [Earth/Sun...] <note>The sentence ends abruptly at "s-", likely "solis" (Sun) or "sedes" (seat/place) on the following page.</note>

<summary>Kepler argues that the appearance of the night sky is unique to our location; even if stars were spaced out at regular intervals, an observer on another star would see a completely different universe.</summary>

--- Page 161 ---
<meta>This page continues Kepler’s argument from the previous section regarding the physical limits of the universe. He is specifically refuting the idea of an infinite universe—a radical notion in the early 17th century—by using observational data and geometric logic.</meta>

## ON THE NEW STAR IN OPHIUCHUS. 109

<margin>The world has a certain definite center.</margin>
...that this is the reasoning behind the arrangement of the fixed stars <note>original: "fixarum"; refers to the stars that do not move relative to one another, unlike the "wandering stars" or planets.</note>, as our own sight indicates: namely, that our particular location among those fixed stars possesses a unique quality, and is distinguished from the region through which they are scattered by a clear sign (which is the vast emptiness between us and the fixed stars).

It is certain, therefore, that looking inward toward the Sun and the Planets, the world is finite and, in a manner of speaking, hollowed out. As for what remains, let Metaphysics build upon it. For if such a place exists within some infinite body (as some claim), it would then be the center of the entire body; yet the surrounding fixed stars would not maintain the same proportions. Thus, there would be such worlds scattered everywhere, just like this one of ours; instead, however, those stars surround this place in a spherical shape, coming together as one. Most notably and most manifest is the Milky Way <note>original: "via lactea"</note> sown among them, <margin>The Milky Way is an indicator of the finite sphere of the fixed stars.</margin> which, in an uninterrupted circle, passes through the middle of the orb of fixed stars, holding us in its central embrace. Therefore, both this Milky Way and the fixed stars possess the character of an outer limit. For they bound this space of ours, and in turn, they are bounded at their own beginning. Is it then credible that they, being bounded on this side, should extend on the other side into infinity? Moreover, how can a center be found in the infinite, when in the infinite, the center is everywhere? For whatever point of the infinite is chosen, it is equally—that is, infinitely—distant from the extremities, which are infinitely far away. From this it would follow that the same point both is the center and is not the center, along with many other contradictions; which one may most rightly avoid if, just as one finds the heaven of fixed stars to be bounded on the inside, one also bounds it on the outside.

Take also this argument derived from visual perspective. If the altitude of the Sphere of fixed stars is infinite—that is, if some fixed stars are infinitely high up—they would themselves consist of an infinite mass of bodies. For imagine some star which is seen at a certain angle, say of four minutes; the size of such a body is always one-thousandth of its distance, which is a most certain rule of Geometry. Therefore, if the distance is infinite, the diameter of the star is a thousandth part of infinity. But all proportional parts of the infinite are necessarily infinite themselves. Therefore, such a star would be infinite. But at the same time, it would be finite, because it has a figure <note>meaning a specific geometric shape</note>: and every figure is circumscribed by certain boundaries—that is, it is either finite or defining a limit. Yet we gave it a figure when we posited it as visible at a certain angle. But what if, you might ask, it is smaller than can be seen? I say the matter returns to the same point. For it must occupy some proportional part of the circumference drawn through itself. But a circumference whose diameter is infinite is itself also infinite. Thus it follows that no star—whether it is seen or whether it vanishes due to its smallness—is at an infinite distance from us.

->O 3<-                                       Let it be <note>This is a "catchword," a common printing practice where the first word of the next page is printed at the bottom of the current one to ensure the pages are bound in the correct order.</note>

<summary>Kepler argues that the universe must be finite, using the "emptiness" of our solar system and the visible ring of the Milky Way as evidence that we occupy a central, bounded void. He employs a geometric proof to show that if stars were infinitely far away, they would logically have to be infinitely large to be visible, which he considers a physical impossibility.</summary>

<keywords>cosmology, infinity, fixed stars, Milky Way, Ophiuchus, geometry, celestial spheres, Johannes Kepler</keywords>

--- Page 162 ---
<meta>This page continues Kepler’s argument against the infinity of the universe and transitions into a discussion on the physical origins of the "New Star" (the supernova of 1604). He moves from metaphysical geometry to the observational theories of his mentor, Tycho Brahe.</meta>

110 JOHANNES KEPLER

Suppose that you extend a space without stars into infinity; certainly, wherever you place a star in it, the distance to that point is finite, and the circumference passing through that star is also finite. Therefore, anyone who speaks of an "infinite sphere" of fixed stars is uttering a contradiction in terms <note>original: "oppositum dicat in adjecto," literally "a contradiction in the added part." Kepler is arguing that a 'sphere' by definition has a center and a boundary, so it cannot be infinite.</note>. Truly, an infinite body cannot even be grasped by thought. For the mind's concepts of the infinite are either about the meaning of the word "Infinite" itself, or about something that exceeds every conceivable measure of number, touch, or sight. Such a thing is not immediately or actually infinite, because an infinite measure can never be fully conceived.

***

->### That the matter of the New Star was celestial, and concerning its origin.<-
### CHAPTER XXII.

Having rejected the opinion of those who claimed the matter and body of the star were eternal, it follows that I must also weigh the opposite view. Therefore, if either the matter of this flame or the body of this star emerged as something "new," it must either have been created by Divine power or produced by some force of nature. However, before we arrive at "creation"—which is the end of all scientific debate—I believe we must test every other possibility. I do this all the more gladly because I see that within the last thirty years, this is already the fourth, or certainly the third, "new star" to appear. Furthermore, even Pliny <note>Pliny the Elder, a Roman naturalist whose *Natural History* was a standard reference in Kepler's time.</note>, among the new stars which are usually "hairy" <note>Kepler is referring to comets; the word "comet" comes from the Greek for "wearing long hair."</note>, identifies one specific type that remains stationary. From this, one might gather that in ancient times, the region of the fixed stars also produced stars of this kind. There are also several suspicions raised by the histories of previous ages; see Tycho’s *Progymnasmata* <note>Tycho Brahe's "Introduction to the New Astronomy," published posthumously in 1602.</note>.

If the star was generated by a force of nature, we must ask both about the specific "efficient cause" or parent, and about its matter. To deal with the matter first and conclude the discussion held so far: I have already stated above that the matter did not come from the Earth. Therefore, the matter was taken from heaven—if indeed the generation of a star can be attributed to the forces of nature in any way (even if it is a "monstrous" birth, like the generation of eels in the human womb <note>Kepler uses a common medical myth of his time—that parasites like eels could spontaneously generate inside the body—to describe the "unnatural" or rare appearance of a new star in the otherwise unchanging heavens.</note>). For nature produces nothing without matter. 

And indeed, if comets are in the heavens; if, as can be demonstrated by examples, they are carried from the deepest <term>ether</term> <note>The substance believed to fill the space of the universe above the Earth's atmosphere.</note> toward the Earth, and sometimes pass very close to it in a straight motion; and if new stars arise among the fixed stars themselves: what prevents us from affirming that they consist of an <term>ethereal essence</term>?

Here, mention has already been made above of that most beautiful opinion which Brahe proposed: that stars of this kind are produced from the Milky Way. And he seemed to confirm it...

<summary>Kepler argues that an "infinite universe" is a logical impossibility and begins investigating whether the New Star of 1604 was formed from celestial "ether" or the Milky Way, rather than being a divine miracle or an Earthly vapor.</summary>

### ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT-BEARER, PAGE 111

...to confirm the matter by the evidence of the eyes; having pointed out a gap in that place where the star of the year 1572 <note>This refers to the supernova observed by Tycho Brahe in the constellation Cassiopeia.</note> shone forth, as if that portion of matter had been condensed into the body of a star, which afterwards perhaps burned out or was dissipated. To this experiment, our own new star <note>The "New Star" of 1604, now known as Kepler's Supernova.</note> is now added, whose emergence again fell within the border of the Milky Way. As for the star which shone in the Swan <note>The constellation Cygnus.</note> in the year 1600 (of which I speak in the appendix), it still shines clearly in the very midst of the "milk." One might also bring in the tradition of Aristotle and Pliny, who say that comets for the most part exist beneath the Milky Way—if it were certain that they were also speaking of new fixed stars. To give credit to our New Star regarding its proximity to the Milky Way, <margin>It is in the vicinity of the Milky Way.</margin> let us extract from Ptolemy <note>Claudius Ptolemy, the 2nd-century Greco-Egyptian astronomer whose *Almagest* was the standard astronomical text for over a millennium.</note> a description of that place, which is quite meticulous and, as Ptolemy himself says, made with "most painstaking observation" <note>original: περιεργοτάτῃ παρατηρήσει (*periergotatē paratērēsei*)</note>. This is all the more necessary because that location, in our climate, is so close to the horizon that you cannot easily discern the brightness of the Milky Way with the eyes due to the vapors of the atmosphere.

*The Milky Way, he says, appears to some extent double, beginning from the Altar <note>The constellation Ara.</note> up to the Hen <note>The constellation Cygnus.</note>. And the preceding part of this Zone is in no way connected to the other. For it makes a gap at the junctions which are near the Altar and the Hen (or Swan). Afterward, the following part, which today crosses the zodiac near the borders of the Tropic point, passes from the Altar, through the constellation of the Scorpion, through Antinous <note>An obsolete constellation now integrated into Aquila (the Eagle).</note>, the Eagle, and the Arrow, to the Hen. And having described the entire circuit of the Zone, he adds: But the Zone, which I mentioned before, having an interruption (as I said) made at the star held in the Altar, and taking its beginning from there, intercepts three nodes <note>likely referring to the segments of the constellation's shape or joints of the figure</note> from the body of the Scorpion; but of the three in the body of the Scorpion, it excludes the one that follows, being distant from it by one degree at its western margin. But the one which is in the fourth node of the Scorpion occupies the clear air in the middle between both zones, being as nearly as possible at equal distances from each, say a little more than one degree. After this, however, the preceding zone runs out with a certain bend toward the East, in the form of a circular segment, and the preceding side of the "milk" is terminated by the star which is in the right knee of the Serpent-bearer <note>The constellation Ophiuchus.</note>; while the following side is terminated by that star which is in the same ham <note>The back of the knee.</note>. The preceding star...*

<meta>Continuity note: This page continues Kepler’s investigation into the physical origins of the "new star" (the supernova of 1604), specifically whether it was formed from the visible material of the Milky Way.</meta>

112          JOHANNES KEPLER

...a portion of the "milk"; so that it is contained within that narrow boundary which distinguishes between both arcs of the Milky Way’s limit in that place, like an island between two riverbeds. Therefore, it is consistent that the matter of the new star <note>Kepler is referring to the Supernova of 1604, often called "Kepler's Star."</note> comes from the Milky Way. And natural contemplation also confirms this. For if the essence of the Milky Way is a denser part of the ether <note>The "ether" was the substance thought by ancient and Renaissance scholars to fill the heavens above the Earth's atmosphere.</note> (for it is necessary that some matter underlies such great brilliance), it will seem likely to everyone that this matter is the most suitable for new phenomena.

<margin>Whether the matter of the new star is from the Milky Way.</margin>
However likely these things may be, they are nevertheless weakened by two objections: First, that David Fabricius <note>A German astronomer and theologian (1564–1617) who corresponded with Kepler.</note>—whom I have celebrated as trustworthy in the observations mentioned above—noticed on August 13/23, 1596 (around which day the Comet of that year was last seen by Maestlin <note>Michael Maestlin, Kepler's university professor and mentor.</note>, having then become almost stationary in the 4th degree of Virgo), he found in the morning a new star of the third magnitude in 25° 45' of Aries, with a Southern latitude of 15° 54'; which disappeared after October of the same year. That location was very far away from the Milky Way. Second, if the birth of new stars is natural (as one who seeks its matter from the Milky Way suggests), it is believable, as was said above, that they are not so infrequent. Therefore, after these 1400 years which have passed since the time when Ptolemy <note>Claudius Ptolemy (c. 100–170 AD), the Greco-Egyptian astronomer whose star catalog was the standard for over a millennium.</note> described the Milky Way, it seems that something notable should have been lost from it, with so many stars having been ignited and fashioned out of it. Or, if there has not yet been such a great accumulation of them, it seems the heaven would eventually lose all this "milk" that it has; a conclusion which is not accepted by sound judgment in Philosophy.

<margin>The new star of the year 1595.</margin> <note>While the margin says 1595, the text correctly identifies Fabricius's discovery of the variable star Mira in 1596.</note>
Therefore, I am rather of the opinion that I believe the heaven is everywhere suited to provide the matter for these stars.

***

# THE MATTER OF THE HEAVENS IS ALTERABLE.
## CHAPTER XXIII.

I am not unaware of how hostile this opinion is to Aristotelian Philosophy <note>Aristotle taught that the heavens were "incorruptible" and unchanging, unlike the "corruptible" and changing Earth.</note>. But to tell the truth, it is more opposed to the sect <note>meaning the rigid followers of Aristotle</note> than to the leader himself. Give me a revived Aristotle; if only my astronomical labor might succeed so well that I might hope to persuade the man himself! It usually happens that while plaster <note>original: *Gypso*</note> is freshly poured, you may imprint whatever you like upon it; but when the same has hardened, it rejects every impression. So it is with opinions: while they flow from the mouths of Philosophers, they can be corrected very easily; once they have been received by disciples, they harden more than any stone, nor are they easily torn away by any reasons.

<summary>Kepler argues that "new stars" (supernovae and variables) form from celestial matter, challenging the Aristotelian dogma that the heavens are unchanging and incorruptible. He uses the discovery of a star in Aries by David Fabricius to suggest that this star-forming matter exists everywhere in the sky, not just in the Milky Way.</summary>

<keywords>Milky Way, ether, David Fabricius, Aristotle, stellar formation, Mira Ceti, Michael Maestlin, Ptolemy, incorruptibility of the heavens</keywords>

--- Page 165 ---
<meta>This page continues Kepler’s argument against the Aristotelian concept of the "incorruptibility" of the heavens. He is transitioning from theoretical arguments to observational evidence, specifically mentioning the "New Star" (supernova) of 1604 and historical atmospheric/celestial anomalies.</meta>

-># ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT [BEARER]. 113<-

...[continuation of a word from the previous page]. Why did Aristotle hold this view, that the heavens are unchangeable? Because (he says) for so many centuries, nothing [new] has been observed. Therefore, if someone were to teach Aristotle that in the succeeding centuries quite a few new things were observed in the heavens, he would most willingly depart from his opinion. But today, the disciples of Aristotle—looking not to reason, but to his naked opinion—dare to speak against experience based on the philosopher's dogma (which he himself sought from experience), and they object to it by seeking various diversions. <margin>Aristotle's argumentation.</margin> 

But having weighed the matter more diligently, Aristotle seems to have used induction. Concerning the motions of the stars, it is established that the laws have remained the same from the furthest memory of man: the same planets circle today that existed of old, and the same constellations of fixed stars. Nor can anything different be demonstrated regarding the intermediate space (which is not obvious to the eyes because it is transparent). Therefore, all things in the heavens are perpetual, even the very matter of the spheres. <margin>An objection.</margin> 

This induction fails. We still find the laws of motion to be the same today, which is the single and paramount <note>original: *decumana*, literally "of the tenth wave," a Latin term for something of great size or importance</note> hypothesis of astronomers. But regarding the medium—or Moses's *Rakia* <note>Hebrew: *Raqia*, the "firmament" or "expanse" described in the Book of Genesis</note>, the "Tents of the Heavens"—our own age teaches us quite clearly that the situation is different. And the previous age would have taught the same, if it had occurred to our ancestors to pay attention to these things. Indeed, I will propose here several pieces of evidence from which that which I contend can be understood in some way: so that the New fixed stars (which I produce from changeable celestial matter) might be confirmed by other examples of altered celestial matter.

<margin>1. The cause of the prodigious fog of the year 1547 was celestial.</margin> First, I present to you from my *Optics* <note>Kepler refers to his 1604 work *Astronomiae Pars Optica* (The Optical Part of Astronomy)</note> page 259, that example of the prodigious fog of the year 1547: about which the Gemmas <note>Gemma Frisius and his son Cornelius Gemma, notable 16th-century astronomers</note>, father and son, report. They say the Sun appeared for three days as if drenched in blood, so that even most stars were visible at midday. This happened not only in Belgium, but everywhere throughout France, Germany, and Britain; as Scaliger <note>Joseph Justus Scaliger, a leading scholar of the time</note> reports in *On the Emendation of Time*, book 5, and before him, Bunting. 

Indeed, histories provide many examples; among others, that most famous one during nearly the entire year of Caesar’s murder; and likewise in the year Augustus died. But let us build a demonstration upon our own example. If the cause of the darkened Sun was in the air: then since the Sun retained this pallor from sunrise to sunset—for three whole days, or as Scaliger wishes, four days—it follows that the entire air from East to West must have been dark. If this were so, how then were stars seen through the air, through which the Sun could scarcely be seen? For you cannot say that the light of the Sun was diluted by the air such that, with its brilliance removed, the lights of the stars could also introduce themselves to the eyes. For when our air becomes so thick that the light of the Sun passes through it dull and pale, then indeed by the same effort...

<summary>Kepler challenges the Aristotelian belief in the unchanging nature of the heavens by arguing that Aristotle would have changed his mind if presented with modern evidence. He cites historical atmospheric anomalies, like the "prodigious fog" of 1547, to prove that celestial matter is indeed subject to change.</summary>

<keywords>Aristotle, immutability of the heavens, Rakia, 1547 fog, Gemma Frisius, solar phenomena, celestial matter, supernova 1604</keywords>

--- Page 166 ---
<meta>This page continues Kepler’s discussion on the nature of the heavens, specifically addressing the composition of the "ether" and the location of celestial phenomena relative to the Sun and Moon.</meta>

->114          JOHANNES KEPLER<-

By its action, [the air] is accustomed to brighten immensely, receiving the Sun's rays into itself like clouds and reflecting them in every direction. Thus the air, thrusting its brightness into the eyes from all sides and occupying them entirely, leaves no room for the far weaker light of the stars. Therefore, it is most certain that in whichever part the light of the stars is seen, the air of that place is less illuminated by the Sun; it is consequently thin and not suited to dulling the Sun's light as it is brought to that place by its daily motion. Thus, there must be some other cause besides the air which dulls the Sun's light so greatly before it reaches our air, and which accompanies the Sun in its daily revolution. <note>Kepler is arguing that if stars are visible near the Sun, something in space—not just our atmosphere—must be blocking the Sun's overwhelming glare.</note>

But they will perhaps say that there was indeed some cause high up, but immediately below the Moon—and that nothing follows from this regarding the matter of the heavens?

First, it has been demonstrated in *Optics* <note>original: "in Opticis"; likely referring to Kepler’s own 1604 work, *Astronomiae Pars Optica*</note> that our air is quite low (which even Aristotle partly admits); that which follows it is already the <term>ether</term> <note>The "ether" was historically believed to be the fluid or substance filling the space of the universe beyond the Earth's atmosphere.</note>. This will be all the less rejected by anyone who remembers that it was demonstrated by Brahe <note>Tycho Brahe, the famous Danish astronomer whose data Kepler used</note> that the heavens do not consist of solid spheres. Therefore, the ether exists even immediately below the Moon; if the matter of the darkness clung to that place, it clung in the ether. Next, in what place do you think that matter was, which kept the Sun in paleness for four whole days, yet did not hide the stars at all? <note>Kepler refers to a historical atmospheric or celestial event where the sun appeared dim but the sky remained clear enough to see stars.</note> For if it were immediately below the Moon, it would follow that it must also revolve with the Moon's sphere, carried away by the swiftness of its motion, just as Aristotle claims the element of fire is carried. But because the Sun remained hidden for four whole days—during which time the Moon traveled fifty degrees away from the Sun—this matter was therefore not near the Moon, but stood entirely above it, in the very heart of the ether accepted by all. Unless, perhaps, one likes to say that a mass fifty degrees long first covered the Sun with its right edge and finally with its left; in which case it should also have covered the stars, whichever ones stood around the Sun within a proximity of 50 degrees. And yet we read that stars were seen at midday; therefore, the stars near the Sun were not covered. Thus, the matter which veiled the Sun was not so wide; and since it stayed in the Sun's location for four days (while the Moon's orbit meanwhile moved 50 degrees away), this matter could not have stood near the Moon. It must therefore be sought in the deep ether, very near the Sun, between the Sun and the Earth; indeed, offered to us from that source, it must be embraced as an argument for the <margin>II. The celestial matter of Comets.</margin> mutable nature of the ethereal matter, which is poured around the stars in liquid form.

Let the second argument for the generation of the ether be the Comets, which it is established by the most certain doctrine of <term>parallaxes</term> <note>The apparent displacement of an object when viewed from two different points; used by astronomers to calculate distance.</note> pass through the very field of the ether; they suddenly become huge, then gradually grow faint, until finally, having been dissipated...

<summary>Kepler argues that the "ether" of space is a fluid, mutable substance rather than solid spheres. He uses the behavior of light and the movement of the Moon to prove that celestial obscurations (like a dimmed sun) occur deep in space, not just in the Earth's atmosphere.</summary>
<keywords>ether, Tycho Brahe, solar phenomena, parallax, lunar motion, celestial matter, atmospheric optics</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the physical nature of celestial phenomena, specifically comparing the material of new stars (novae) to that of comets. The author, Johannes Kepler, is citing his own earlier work on optics and the observations of Tycho Brahe to explain how light interacts with celestial matter.</meta>

### **ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT BEARER. 115**

...once the matter is dissipated, they are extinguished again. See Tycho’s *Progymnasmata* <note>original: "progymn. Tychonis"; refers to Tycho Brahe’s "Introduction to the New Astronomy," volume 2, which discussed the star of 1572</note>, volume 2, and the appendix to chapter 10 in my own work on optics <note>Kepler refers here to his "Ad Vitellionem paralipomena" (1604), a foundational text in the study of light</note>.

A much more powerful argument is provided by their "hair," from which they are called comets; or their "beards," from which they are called *pogoniae* <term>pogoniae: from the Greek "pogon," meaning beard; a historical term for a comet with a tail that appears to precede it or hang down like a beard</term>. <margin>III. As also with the hair of comets.</margin> For it is established by the most certain optical reasoning that these features—whether hair, beards, or tails—are nothing other than the rays of the Sun, tinted with some color, as it were, within the body of the comets, and having undergone refraction <note>the bending of light as it passes through different materials</note> there, are projected in the direction away from the Sun.

Indeed, that same discipline <note>the science of optics</note> teaches that the Sun’s ray is not visible unless it strikes some dense matter first. For truly, the light of the Sun is nowhere seen in the air when the air is transparent and thin; but it is seen in a cloud, because the cloud is opaque and dense; and it is seen in the air when it is thickened, cloudy, and less transparent. Since, therefore, the tails of comets are nothing other than the light of the Sun, it becomes necessary—

<summary>Kepler argues that comet tails and "beards" are actually sunlight refracted through dense material within the comet's body, proving that these celestial objects consist of physical matter rather than mere illusions.</summary>
<keywords>comets, optics, refraction, Tycho Brahe, comet tails, pogoniae, celestial matter, Johannes Kepler</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Kepler's investigation into the optical phenomena observed during solar eclipses, specifically addressing the "corona" or glow seen around the Moon, which he argues must be reflecting off some form of celestial matter rather than just being an optical illusion or our own atmosphere.</meta>

110 <-> JOHANNES KEPLER <->

<margin>The material that sustained the kindling of the Moon's body was celestial matter.</margin>

The report from Naples regarding the previous year <note>referring to the total solar eclipse of October 12, 1605</note> states the following: The entire Sun was accurately covered, though this did not last for long. In the middle, where the Moon was positioned, there was an appearance like a black cloud. All around it was a reddish and flaming splendor, of equal width on all sides, which occupied a significant portion of the sky. From the region of the Sun toward the North, the sky was completely dark, just as it is in the depths of night; yet no stars were seen. To ensure you have no doubt about the reliability of this history, here is another report from Flanders; there, the Sun was not entirely covered, for the uppermost part of the solar circle remained visible and bright, about one or half a "digit" <term>Digit (digitus)</term><note>A standard astronomical measurement of the time, representing one-twelfth of the diameter of a celestial body.</note> in width (certainly because in Antwerp, a closer location, one digit remained visible). Nevertheless, the globe of the Moon was seen, tending toward blackness, dusky, or as if covered in soot; meanwhile, the upper circumference of the Moon was entirely white and as if fiery. And to confirm that the disk of the Moon was seen to be fully circumscribed <note>meaning its circular outline was clearly visible even against the dark sky</note>, the report adds that the entire area where the gaze was directed away from the Sun appeared sooty, yet with a fiery circumference. Therefore, the phenomenon itself could not have been otherwise, since its appearance in the eye was such. Something similar was seen by Jessenius <note>Johannes Jessenius, a physician and associate of Kepler</note> at Torgau during the eclipse of the year 1598. For he saw the Moon clearly encircled by splendor. See page 299 of my *Optics* <note>Kepler's 1604 work *Astronomiae Pars Optica*</note>, where you should interpret the word "air" in a broad sense to also include ethereal substance.

Here I ask: what was that fiery splendor surrounding the Moon, which to the sight appeared larger than the Sun because it had hidden the whole Sun? Indeed, what was that splendor which nevertheless embraced the Moon from the lower rim, even where the Moon exceeded the Sun by the width of one digit? You might say, "It was the splendor of the Sun." True; but that is not what is being asked. Rather, what was the matter—what was the *subject* <term>Subject (Subjectum)</term><note>In 17th-century physics, the "subject" is the physical substance or medium in which a property (like light) exists or is reflected.</note>—in which that splendor of the Sun resided? For light itself, having departed from its source body, cannot be seen unless it is in a medium; because it stays nowhere and strikes nothing unless it encounters some opaque medium.

<margin>That splendor was not from our air, not even in Naples.</margin>

If you were to say that our own air was the medium for this splendor, you would be refuted in various ways by the experiences of different locations. In the present diagram, let **αβ** be the Sun, **δζ** the Moon, and **δηζ** the cone of the shadow: let the globe of the Earth be **ε ι**. Therefore, at Naples—which we may conceive as being at point **ε**—the entire Sun was hidden. But where the Sun is hidden, that place is in the shadow...

<summary>Kepler analyzes reports of the 1605 solar eclipse from Naples and Flanders to argue that the "fiery splendor" (the corona) visible around the Moon requires a physical medium in space to reflect it, as it cannot be attributed to Earth's atmosphere alone.</summary>
<keywords>solar eclipse, corona, Naples eclipse 1605, Jessenius, celestial matter, optics, light reflection, Flanders eclipse report</keywords>

Thus, if the Moon had exceeded the Sun by two minutes: the shadow would have been two degrees wide, that is, one hundred and twenty Italian miles. But now the Sun was more than 50 degrees away from the zenith. And in that place, with the same parallactic index, a progress of three degrees is required until the Moon changes its position by two minutes. If the Moon exceeded the Sun by three minutes, the shadow ι ε was more than four degrees wide; namely 60 German miles, or 240 Italian miles. Therefore, if the shadow began at Naples at ε; the other end of the shadow would have penetrated into the middle of Sicily, which would then be at ι. Therefore that place, that matter, in

<meta>This page continues Johannes Kepler's astronomical treatises, specifically focusing on the geometric calculation of lunar eclipses and the dimensions of the Earth's shadow.</meta>

118                     JOHANNES KEPLER

[A geometric diagram illustrating a lunar eclipse. At the top, a large circle representing the Sun (S) casts rays past a smaller circle representing the Earth (ε ι τ). These rays form a conical shadow (umbra) in which the Moon (α β γ δ) is positioned. Various points are labeled with Greek letters to define the angles and distances necessary for calculating the eclipse's magnitude.]

In a **LUNAR ECLIPSE**, where the observer's eye is located on Earth, the entire calculation depends on these three factors: the difference in the motions <note>The relative speeds of the Sun and Moon across the sky.</note>, the lengths of the diameters of the **Shadow** and the **Moon**, and finally their latitude <note>The angular distance of the Moon from the ecliptic (the Sun's path).</note>.

Therefore, in this figure, let **S** be the center of the Sun and **π** its semidiameter; let the body of the Moon be **α β γ δ**, its center **α**, and its semidiameter **α β** or **α δ**. Let the Earth be **ε ι τ**, its center **ι**, and its semidiameter **ι ε** or **ι τ**. 

Through the edges of the Sun **π** and the Earth **ε**, let the line **π ε ζ** be drawn, which marks the boundary of the shadow; and from the centers **S** and **ι**, let the line **S ι ζ** be drawn, which is the axis of the shadow. Let the Moon be at **α**, and through the center **ι** of the Earth and the center **α** of the Moon, let the line **ι α κ** be drawn, representing the direction of the Moon as seen from the center of the Earth.

And because the rays of the Sun fall upon the Earth, the Earth casts a shadow **ε ζ τ**. If the Moon **α β γ δ** enters this shadow, it is eclipsed. To determine how much of the Moon is obscured, we must find the semidiameter of the shadow at the distance of the Moon, which is the arc (or angle) **α ν**.

In the triangle **ζ ι ε**, the angle **ε ζ ι** is half the angle of the cone of the shadow. This angle is equal to the difference between the Sun's apparent semidiameter and its <term>parallax<gloss>The apparent shift in position of a celestial body when viewed from different locations on Earth.</gloss></term>. Furthermore, the angle **ε ι ζ** is a right angle, or nearly so, at the Earth's surface. 

The angle **α ι ν**, which represents the semidiameter of the shadow at the Moon's distance, is found by the following rule: the semidiameter of the shadow is equal to the sum of the <term>horizontal parallaxes<gloss>The parallax of a celestial body when it is on the horizon.</gloss></term> of the Sun and the Moon, minus the apparent semidiameter of the Sun. <note>This formula is a fundamental part of eclipse prediction, allowing astronomers to calculate exactly how wide the Earth's shadow will be when the Moon passes through it.</note>

<summary>This page describes the geometric method for calculating the size of the Earth's shadow during a lunar eclipse using the distances and parallaxes of the Sun, Earth, and Moon.</summary>
<keywords>lunar eclipse, geometry, Earth's shadow, parallax, semidiameter, Johannes Kepler, astronomy, celestial mechanics</keywords>

# **ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT. 119**

<margin>Nor in Flanders</margin>

From the report from Flanders, the same point will be more clearly proved: that it was not the air near us <note>The author is arguing that the phenomenon was not a local atmospheric effect like a rainbow or halo.</note> which subjected itself to the brightness of the Sun in the form of a circle and surrounded the appearance of the Moon. Indeed, in that location, the Sun was not entirely hidden. Let $\upsilon$ <note>The author uses Greek letters to refer to points on a geometric diagram, where $\upsilon$ represents Flanders.</note> be Flanders in the diagram; with the points $\upsilon$ and $\zeta$ connected, the extended line falls on $\gamma$. Therefore, whatever was between $\gamma$ and $\beta$ still shone at $\upsilon$. Let points $\delta$, $\upsilon$, and $\zeta$ also be connected, and let the partner lines $\upsilon\omicron\sigma$ and $\upsilon\xi\rho$ be drawn out from $\upsilon\zeta$ and $\upsilon\delta$. Let the appearance of the dark disk be $\delta \upsilon \zeta$; however, let the flaming circle surrounding the dark disk be understood through $\zeta \upsilon \omicron$ and $\xi \upsilon \delta$. Therefore, by this portion of the Sun $\gamma \beta$ which remained, the entire air was illuminated equally—not only that which was around the Moon at $\zeta\omicron$, but also that which was between the Moon and the sight-line in the space $\zeta\omicron\upsilon$. Thus, in the portion of air $\upsilon \lambda$, a missing round shape could by no means be represented by the surrounding brilliance.

Add to this that the shadow of the Moon $\iota\epsilon$, which did not encompass Flanders (since part of the Sun $\gamma \beta$ was visible there), had reached much further on the southern side $\eta$, toward the Mediterranean coast of Spain, which should now be understood as being under $\iota$. Therefore, a dark circle of this kind, surrounded by brilliance, would plainly not have encompassed the Sun and Moon; instead, it would have stood entirely to the south. Indeed, the sight-line would have passed away for a small part of itself, mostly behind the mountains. For the circle $\nu\tau$, which is encompassed by the shadow and consequently appears dark, is clothed in the brilliance of the surrounding air $\lambda \mu$ that stays outside it. This circle, I say, as you see, falls entirely below the line $\upsilon\delta$, in which the lowest edge of the Moon is perceived from $\upsilon$.

For this reason, there must have been another matter, much more sublime <note>original: "sublimiorem" — In 17th-century science, "sublime" referred to things high in the heavens, above the Earth's atmosphere.</note> than the air of Flanders, in which the appearance of the flame encircling the dark disk of the Moon was represented, having been impressed by the Sun. Indeed, its illumination was so strong that even though this air $\upsilon\lambda$ was also entirely illuminated by the particle of the Sun $\gamma\beta$ in Flanders, that brilliance from $\xi\delta$ or $\rho\pi$ nevertheless overcame this brilliance from the Sun at $\gamma\beta$. Thus, being neither confused nor overwhelmed by this illumination of our air at $\upsilon\lambda$, it would descend distinctly to $\upsilon$, to the eyes of the observers.

Again, the same will be proved if both locations, Naples and Liege <note>original: "Leodium"</note>, are joined together. It is not likely that the thickness of our air is the same in all places. Aristotle attributed a thick and whitening brilliance to the air in Pontus <note>A region on the southern coast of the Black Sea.</note>, the kind of which he did not see in Athens. And what history does not emphasize this? Yet this phenomenon, which one might wish to attribute to the thickness of the air, was the same in very different places. For who would compare the Celtic <note>Referring to Northern Europe, specifically the region around Flanders and Liege.</note> sky with the Italian one? Therefore, it was not the air poured over us, but some more sublime and plainly celestial matter that cast this brilliance, received from the Sun, around the Moon.

Whither—

120 JOHANNES KEPLER

Where is this going, you ask, or what does this have to do with the topic; by which you contend that the matter of heaven is alterable? <note>Kepler is addressing a hypothetical critic who believes the heavens are made of a perfect, unchanging substance called "aether." Kepler argues that this substance can change its density.</note> For what if this splendor of the heaven around the Sun or Moon were constant whenever there is some eclipse of the Sun? Indeed, this very thing pertains to the integrity of the argument; now furthermore to show different and plainly contrary examples; so that it may appear that this splendor in eclipses—and thus also this thickness of celestial matter—is not perpetual. Although what need is there for examples, of which I have reported many in the *Optics*. <note>A reference to Kepler's 1604 book, *Astronomiae Pars Optica* (The Optical Part of Astronomy).</note> Let us remain only on the last one of the year 1560; the eclipse of which year brought an astounding darkness to Portugal. Even though the Moon was then at perigee <term>perigee: the point in the Moon's orbit where it is closest to the Earth, making it appear larger in the sky</term>, do not think that the darkness was increased only by astronomical circumstances; rather, decide this: that by the lack of celestial matter, which carries splendor far and wide, the greatest addition was made to that darkness. Receive what was recently written to me from Trier, from the mouth of a certain trustworthy old man <note>original Greek: ἀξιοπίστȣ (*axiopistou*), meaning "worthy of belief"</note>, by the labor of a most learned and not at all vain man: In that eclipse of the sun in the year 1560, around noon, there was also such a darkness of the day in Trier that men under a roof, only a little distance from each other, did not recognize one another, and many stars appeared—even though the eclipse in that place was far from total. But in the past year 1605, besides one surgeon in Brabant—a Lyncean <note>A reference to the "Lyncean" or sharp-eyed members of the Academy of the Lynx (Accademia dei Lincei), or simply someone with lynx-like vision</note>, I think—I have heard of no one who saw stars; although the Moon was near perigee, and in Naples the Sun was totally covered. What else is gathered from this, if not this: that the matter of heaven, poured around the Sun or Moon, is one way at some times and another at others—now thickening, soon again being purified and scattered?

Not only do eclipses confirm this matter, but even without an eclipse of the Sun, the Moon provides us with proof of this thing. In the year 1553, as a serious and most learned man wrote to me, the Moon was seen in Seville standing next to the Sun in Pisces, in the very middle of the sky. The whole city watched and testified. But when has anyone seen a similar new moon? Does not the doctrine of the ancients claim the first sight of the Moon two days and more after conjunction? <note>Usually, the Moon is invisible for a day or two during its "New Moon" phase because it is too close to the Sun's glare. Kepler is highlighting a rare event where it was visible much sooner.</note> Therefore, this is the fifth testimony of that which we contend: namely, that the matter of heaven, since it is different at different times, differing much from itself in its thinness, sometimes even drinks in more clarity than at other times; and so it sometimes obscures the eyes of men more, and sometimes less, in seeing stars during the day.

When these arguments have found a place in the Academy: then finally I will pray that also for this New Star <note>The Supernova of 1604, often called "Kepler's Star"</note>, and the small one in Cetus <note>Mira Ceti, discovered to be variable in 1596</note> which preceded this one in the year 1596, and the greatest in Cassiopeia in the year 1572 <note>Tycho Brahe's Supernova</note>; and finally the one in Cygnus in 1600 <note>The variable star P Cygni</note>, which is watched even today, whatever it may be,

...that this may be the case (the description of which will follow), so that for these New fixed stars, I say, a sixth place for argument may be left. For the present, however, let it suffice that the five previous proofs have made it probable that the matter of the heavens is changeable: therefore, nothing absurd follows if one says that the matter of the New star was gathered from the very Sphere of the fixed stars; and when the star was extinguished, it was in turn poured back into the same sphere.

***

-># On the efficient cause of the New star.<-
->## CHAPTER XXIV.<-

IN a doubtful matter, I will not contend much. Theologians—the opposites of those we refuted above—are quick to decide that a miracle was performed by God himself, a new star having been created. Our curiosity has been increasingly stimulated by the more and more frequent appearance of these stars. Therefore, if we had been satisfied with that opinion [of a miracle], there would be no need to speak of matter. For it is the work of God alone to create from nothing. But since we have already spoken of the matter, establishing that it is celestial, let us also add something concerning the <term>efficient cause</term><note>The "efficient cause" is a philosophical term for the primary source of change or rest; essentially, the "agent" that brings something into being.</note>, or the natural generator, if any can be found. I hope I shall be heard with fair ears, especially by Brengger <note>Johann Georg Brengger, a physician and astronomer who corresponded with Kepler.</note> and others, who have judged that this star cannot be assigned to nature unless we also devise a new Physics of the celestial bodies. I, therefore, shall be the first to voice an opinion, so that others may have all the more abundant material for discussion. And I know not what <note>original: *Virgilianum*</note> verse of Virgil some Pythagorean might chant to me here:

      *In the beginning, the spirit within nourishes the sky and the lands,*
      *The flowing plains, the shining orb of the Moon, and the Titanian stars;*
      *And Mind, infused through the limbs, moves the whole mass,*
      *And mingles itself with the great body.*
      <note>original: *Principiò cœlum ac Terras...* from Virgil's Aeneid, Book VI. This passage describes the "World Soul" (Anima Mundi) animating the universe.</note>

   Aristotle, however, says this: *Plants and small animals arise from the earth just as from moisture: because there is moisture in the earth, spirit in the moisture, and in the whole UNIVERSE, VITAL HEAT;* <note>original: *CALOR ANIMALIS*, literally "animal heat," referring to the life-giving warmth Aristotle believed permeated the cosmos.</note> by which words Scaliger <note>Julius Caesar Scaliger, a famous Renaissance scholar.</note> thought we were led into the hidden mysteries of nature.

   We have said above that our star has this similarity with <term>trajections</term><note>From the Latin *trajectio*, referring to meteors or "shooting stars."</note>: that it suddenly shone forth with great light, and from there, having gradually diminished, it finally vanished. If like effects come from like causes, then since these "trajections" near to us consist of fatty matter—which is fit for catching either fire or light—all such "fatness" must be an excretion, produced

<meta>This page continues Kepler's investigation into the physical causes behind the appearance of the New Star of 1604 (Kepler's Supernova). He is moving from the composition of the star to the "formative faculty" or soul-like power that might organize celestial matter into a star.</meta>

either through the vital faculty of the Earthly Globe, or through external and accidental heat, as seen in fires: therefore, it seems we also need a certain kind of fervor to produce this new star in the ether, which is similar to a natural faculty. For all "fatness" <note>original: *pinguedo*; in 17th-century science, this referred to oily, flammable substances believed to be the fuel for light and fire</note>, and everything flammable—everything changeable into the substance of light—seems to require the work of a certain architectonic <note>Meaning a "designing" or "master-builder" power</note> natural faculty, which acts as a propagator of native heat within its own body. It would be equipped with that heat to alter and assimilate to itself the matter upon which it settles. Furthermore, this kind of fatty exhalation is either generated within the permanent globes <note>The planets or stars</note>, or in the intermediate space—and to transfer a Virgilian phrase from the Elements to the heavens—*In the liquid fields* <note>original: *In campis liquentibus*; a quote from Virgil's *Aeneid* referring to the sea, which Kepler applies here to the fluid heavens/ether</note>. If it is generated within the globes, there is no longer any question as to who the parent is: it is well enough established regarding the Earth that it possesses such a faculty within itself; a faculty which generates different metals in different parts; which fosters heat within; which absorbs and digests seawater, causing it to evaporate into the heights to provide material for rivers and rains; and which imparts marvelous geometric figures to fossils <note>In Kepler's time, "fossils" referred to anything dug out of the ground, including crystals and minerals</note> by no other means than a certain hidden impression, which rests upon a power corresponding to the imagination of animals—as I have discussed more at length in my *Optics*. Since this Earth, which is but one of the globes, possesses such a faculty, it is consistent to believe that the other globes are also endowed with them. Thus, the author and the origin of the "breathing out" <note>The emission of the material that forms the star</note> would be clear. However, because this matter is gathered and eventually ignited not within the globes of the stars, but *in the liquid fields* of the ether: regardless of how you place natural faculties within the globes, it is now necessary to add another faculty as well—one which, *infused through all the limbs* <note>original: *totos infusa per artus*; another quote from Virgil describing the World Soul</note>, permeates the liquid ether. And to again take examples of celestial things from terrestrial ones, this faculty performs in the ether what a certain faculty—most hidden in name but most manifest in reality—performs in our own warm air. This is surely the faculty which, whenever it finds any superfluous matter, converts it into a small living creature that serves the nature of things, whether to help or to unburden: it turns the sweat of the head into lice; the sweat of the body (especially a woman's) into fleas; the sap of trees into various kinds of caterpillars, according to the different types of trees; the redundant fats of hides, abandoned by their original form, into moths; mud into eels; water into frogs, fish, leeches, and infinite and always new kinds of sea monsters; and the Earth into various species of plants and trees. In short, from this come swarms of flies, bees, wasps, hornets, and beetles; from this come vast armies of butterflies. Philosophers call this "putrefaction," which in truth is nothing other than the restoration of matter into some new form as the old form perishes and, as it were, dies—a process carried out by this most industrious... <note>The text cuts off mid-word: *operosissima*</note>

<summary>Kepler argues that just as the Earth creates minerals and life (through spontaneous generation) via a "vital faculty," the heavens possess a similar animating power that organizes "fatty" ether into new stars.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Johannes Kepler’s discussion of the "formative faculty" of the Earth and the universe, following his examination of how new stars might be "authored" or constructed by a guiding natural force. # **ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT BEARER. 123**

...most noble <note>The OCR "roſiſſimum" is likely the end of "generosissimum," meaning most noble or generous.</note> architect, whoever that may finally be. Anyone should be free to inquire into who this is: to me, it seems to be this very <term>faculty</term> <note>Kepler uses "faculty" (facultas) to describe a natural, formative power—a kind of biological intelligence inherent in nature that "knows" how to build structures.</note> which I previously said resides in the globe of the Earth itself; with this distinction, however: that what is an internal faculty of the Earth for the waters and the air (producing) butterflies and aquatic monsters <note>In the 17th century, "monsters" referred to any unusual or anomalous biological creature, rather than something scary.</note>; is for man his own natural faculty for generating lice <note>Kepler is referencing the then-common belief in "spontaneous generation," where it was thought that small pests were produced naturally by the body's own heat and vapors.</note>. The instrument of both seems to be a certain **vapor** which, generated in the body and breathed out, surrounds it on the outside, filling the more hidden recesses of bodies with its moisture, where trapped heat can persist. For once this is done, this faculty—an inhabitant of the body—immediately steps out into it (drawn out, as it were, by the heat and the outflow of its own vapor) and begins to engineer those wonders: reminded of how each part is to be ordered toward its purpose by a divine instinct received through creation and retained until now. I am uncertain how this example should be applied to the great body of the universe itself and its architectural faculty. For either it has no seat except in the empty fluid itself <note>Kepler is debating whether this creative force lives in the "ether" or space between planets.</note>: or, if it resides in the globes of the stars, it has more than one seat: or if it resides in the globe of the Sun, its seat is extremely narrow while its range is immense, similar to the infinite. I prefer to say the first...

<summary>Kepler compares the Earth's ability to create new phenomena (like the New Star) to the biological "faculty" that produces insects and aquatic life, suggesting a universal formative power driven by heat and vapor.</summary>

124    JOHANNES KEPLER
it may effect; by that instinct by which we said this terrestrial faculty, diffused among animals, causes little creatures, such as butterflies and the like, to be formed.

<margin>Is it possible for a World to be born from a World?</margin>

    I have heard of some who have gone so far that, relying on this faculty, they wish to show themselves receptive if someone wants to prove that a World can even be born from a World <note>original: "Mundum ex Mundo gigni"</note>. They are not hindered by seeing that the bodies of the World are not physically joined; for neither is the <term>crystalline humor</term> <note>The lens of the eye, which in 17th-century anatomy was thought to be nourished by "vapors" rather than blood vessels.</note> attached to the membranes of the eye; for it falls out as soon as they are opened, and there is no need for it to be cut away; nor is it nourished by veins or grown, but by vapor. One who wishes to prove a world is born from a world might claim something similar regarding the stars flying freely in the ethereal air.
    But this addition is a slander against the truth. In a human, the faculty that generates lice is far inferior to the one that generates a human. And in the Earth, no one will deny the faculty of which I have spoken; yet it would be a monstrous fabrication if anyone extended its powers—I will not say to the generation of another earth—but even to its mere enlargement. Faculties should be judged by their works through their own bodies; not, conversely, the works of all things from one or two examples of a faculty. Thus, here too, if that faculty presides over maintaining the transparency of the ether, it has its own work by which it upholds the name of a "faculty," even if it does not generate. I need not mention that its body lacks the organs for generating; a similar argument to the one Aristotle used against the motion of stars occurring without celestial spheres <note>Aristotle argued that stars, lacking limbs or organs for movement, must be carried by rotating spheres.</note>. But those fine philosophers <note>Kepler likely refers to certain Epicurean or Neo-Platonic thinkers who argued for an infinite succession of worlds.</note> struggle to ensure that God has something to act upon from eternity; to which end, they link World to World through generation. To them, it may perhaps be conveniently answered that eternity has no parts: therefore, the beginning of time is sought from the whole; which is what my reading of Saint Augustine seemed to suggest.

***

### Comparison of this New Star with the other New Star, which shone in the year 1572 in Cassiopeia, and with Comets.
### CHAPTER XXV.

I stated in the first chapter how much can be gathered from the book of Tycho Brahe <note>Tycho Brahe (1546–1601), Kepler’s mentor and the premier observer of the age.</note>: this recent star was very similar to that previous one of the year 1572 in the clarity of its light, its twinkling, its colors, its magnitude, its decrease, and its stillness.
    Nor was there any difference in duration that anyone could testify to. That one had been kindled in the month of October or November of the year 1572; this one was kindled in October of the year [1604]

<summary>Kepler rejects the idea that worlds can "reproduce" like biological organisms and begins comparing the supernova of 1604 to the famous supernova observed by Tycho Brahe in 1572.</summary>
