<meta>This page continues the comparison between the New Star of 1604 (now known as Kepler's Supernova) and the New Star of 1572 (observed by Tycho Brahe). Kepler is evaluating their duration, brightness, and appearance.</meta>

->### ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT, PAGE 125<-

...of October, in the year 1604. That earlier star [of 1572] was extinguished after 16 months, specifically in March of the year 1574; ours, however, was still visible in October of 1605, after it had already lasted 12 months. For the following four months, it could not be seen due to the presence of the Sun; by February and March of 1606, it had vanished. They were also very similar in their position, for two reasons: namely, that both were in the **Milky Way**, and both were in the highest <term>aether</term> <note>The "aether" was thought to be the pure, crystalline substance of the heavens above the moon, where the "fixed" stars resided.</note> among the rest of the fixed stars. Indeed, Roeslin <note>Helisaeus Roeslin (1545–1616), a German physician and astronomer who also wrote on the 1604 supernova.</note> also celebrates this fact: that both were joined with Jupiter in terms of <term>longitude</term>, and both, regarding the <term>equinoctial</term> <note>The celestial equator.</note>, were in the <term>fiery signs</term> <note>original: "igneis." In astrology, the fiery signs or "fiery trigon" are Aries, Leo, and Sagittarius.</note>. For that earlier star reached the meridian <note>original: "coelum mediabat"</note> with the first point of **Aries** (as Brahe <note>Tycho Brahe (1546–1601), the famous Danish astronomer whose observations Kepler used.</note> diligently emphasized), even though it was actually in **Taurus**; this star of ours, however, stood in **Sagittarius**.

And add those things which I said above in Chapter 1 concerning the position of Venus, which was the same in both cases.

With our star being equal to that earlier one in so many respects, see now also in what circumstances it is by far to be preferred. I shall say nothing of the fact that certain great men, who had seen that earlier star, pronounced this one to be much larger. For it to appear so to them, the proximity of the star to the horizon whenever it appeared, as well as the humid weather of the year, could have been the cause, as was also said above in Chapter 1.

And Brahe made his star exceedingly large—larger even than Jupiter at <term>acronychal</term> <note>original: "Acronychio." A planet is acronychal when it is opposite the Sun, making it visible all night and at its brightest.</note> and nearly equal to Venus—which surprises me. For there is a great difference in magnitude between Jupiter and Venus, especially when the latter is closer to the Earth and shines with a fuller face, as Brahe says. But he also asserts it was seen during the day. Our star was neither seen during the day (perhaps due to its low altitude, not for a lack of magnitude) nor did it equal Venus. Therefore, let this equality of magnitude remain between them.

The first difference is in the *scintillation* <note>The twinkling or rapid flashing of light.</note>: while this was especially admirable and remarkable in our star—more than all other fixed stars—and it filled the eyes and mouths of everyone, that star of 1572, by contrast, is only moderately celebrated for this twinkling. To be sure, that one closely emulated the light of Jupiter; our star represented a light not so "fat" as that of Jupiter, but more refined and pure, almost like that of Mercury or Sirius. Therefore, from the very beginning, it also reflected the colors of the **Rainbow** in a continuous vibration, whereas that earlier star, at its beginning, was merely whitish. In turn, however, that one changed its colors successively; our star was not manifest in this regard, except that with its gradual decrease, that rapid vigor of its twinkling and the projection of colors died away together.

The chief dissimilarity, and the one most worthy of consideration, was in place and time. For that earlier star shone outside the limits of the **Zodiac**, in the constellation of **Cassiopeia**—a place in the heavens infrequent and not ennobled by any visitations of the Planets.

126 &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;OF JOHANNES KEPLER

...this star chose its station very close to the Royal Road <note>The Ecliptic, the apparent path of the Sun across the sky</note> of the Sun, the Moon, and the rest of the Planets; so that it was greeted by all the Planets, and was almost bodily joined to Saturn. Although several bright stars of the second magnitude in Cassiopeia surrounded that former star <note>The Supernova of 1572</note>, those were nevertheless ordinary, and from the common number of fixed stars, distinguished by no nobility of their own motion. But this star of ours <note>The Supernova of 1604</note> thrust itself into the midst of the three superior Planets <note>Mars, Jupiter, and Saturn</note>, using Jupiter and Mars as its advance-guard attendants, and Saturn as its following escort. That earlier star occupied the twelfth part of the sky which takes its name from Taurus, an earthy sign, which was at that time illustrious by no conjunction of Planets above the others. This one, however, blazed up in the fiery sign of Sagittarius, in which that most celebrated revolution of the Fiery Trigon <note>The "Fiery Trigon" refers to a cycle where the "Great Conjunctions" of Jupiter and Saturn occur in the fire signs (Aries, Leo, Sagittarius). This cycle repeats roughly every 800 years and was considered a major omen of historical change.</note> had begun this past December; a thing which cannot happen except after intervals of eight hundred years. That earlier star broke upon a common and ignoble time, marked by no peculiar sign. This one fell precisely into that year which all Astrologers had designated with the most diligent forewarnings for the beginning of the Fiery Trigon and for the heavenly wonders about to happen; precisely in that month in which the star of Mars itself also approached the two superior planets, fulfilling the "Great Conjunction" according to the teaching of Cyprian <note>original: "Cypriani doctrina"—likely referring to the astrologer Cyprianus Leovitius</note>; on precisely 그날 <note>Note: The translator should stay in English</note> that day on which Mars, having already surpassed Saturn, had also overtaken Jupiter. In short, it appeared in precisely that place in the sky toward which the eyes of all Astrologers, expecting the meeting of Jupiter and Mars, were being directed.

Therefore, that prior star came upon a world not forewarned, and like an unexpected enemy who has occupied the city walls, it appeared in the forum before the citizens had even heard rumors of its approach. But ours, commonly expected for a long time, entered on the appointed day with much solemnity and triumphal pomp; after the manner of some most powerful Monarch who, intending to visit the Metropolis of his kingdom, sends harbingers <note>original: "Metatoribus"—officials who went ahead of a royal procession to arrange lodgings</note> far in advance to designate locations for his entourage. And now the streets are being cleaned; now the common people, excited by rumors, run about; now the children complain of the delays. Then the baggage and the silver furniture arrive. Soon, as the prince's entourage follows, the first men enter the gates, and the clatter of horses along the paved streets and the blare of trumpets resound. All the people, stirred up, occupy the windows and fill the streets—boys trembling with joy and tender young girls. Heroes and faithful ministers proceed sublime on horseback; the common folk, eager to behold the King, survey everyone with gaping mouths and anxious eyes. Finally, after a long line, there arises a troop of Drummers and Trumpeters; a venerable order of armed men follows, with an elegant company of runners on each side. In the middle, the King

# ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT BEARER. 127

...leaping on a noble horse, distinguished in purple and gold: there is no need for pointing fingers or asking questions; everyone says privately in their own mind upon seeing the retinue: "Behold, at last, the one you have longed for." Such then was the appearance of our star.

If this matters to the case, the star of the year 1572—by its height and clarity (the former did not allow it to set, and the latter made it visible even by day)—forced itself into the eyes of the common people, who are mostly outdoors, almost pulling everyone by the ear to witness it. But our star here <note>The supernova of 1604</note> experienced different circumstances. Since it did not rise very high and followed the Sun closely into the West, wrapped in the brightness of twilight like the evening star <note>original: "hesperus," the name for Venus when seen in the evening</note>, and moved among the planets as if it were one of their number, it could not become known to anyone except the learned—especially those who had studied astronomy and the positions of the planets—nor could its novelty become manifest. Thus Weidner beautifully wrote:

->*Indeed, God <note>original: "Jova," a Latinized form of the Tetragrammaton</note> gives such a rare and noble sign*<-
->*primarily to Mathematicians; it is not observable by the common crowd.*<-

It also seems appropriate here to compare our star with Comets. For there are those who call it a Comet: Günther, Moller, Bütner, Krabb, and others. And some are found who, for that very reason, deny it should even be called a star because it vanished; for they believe stars must be eternal. Johann Georg Brengger beautifully teaches them a lesson in grammar first. <margin>This is how the species described by Pliny seem to be distinguished to me. However, Achilles <note>Achilles Tatius, an ancient commentator</note> sees it differently: "Those that have their light below, with the flickering beams nodding downward, are called comets."</margin> For "Comet" or "Hairy Star" <note>original: "Crinita"</note> takes its name from "hair" <note>original: "à crine"</note>. Thus, if the "hair" stands above the star, it is a Comet; if it is below, it is a **Pogonias** <note>Bearded star</note>; if the hair is pointed, it is a **Xiphias** <note>Sword-shaped star</note>; if curved and pointed, a **Ceratias** <note>Horn-shaped star</note>; if curved and spread wide, it is called a **Hippeus** <note>Horse-mane star</note>. Thus "Comet" is the name of a species and refers to the shape, not the substance.

Indeed, all those species are referred to by writers by the name "star." Whenever they use the word "Comet," they imply "star," so that the word "Comet" serves as an adjective. They often join them, such as "cometary star" <note>original Greek: "astēr komētēs"</note>, "hairy star," or "falling star"; here you see that they have no regard for the substance being fleeting or "fading away."

But even if the name of the species, "Comet," has passed into a general category, and all forms recorded by Pliny in Book 2, Chapter 25, are called Comets: to which species then will they assign this star of ours? None of those mentioned fit. If they call it a **Lampadias** <note>Torch-like star</note>, they must be referred back to Chapter 18 regarding scintillation <note>The rapid change in brightness or "twinkling"</note>. For that twinkling of the star was by no means "hair"...

rapi-

He also debates the classification of the object, arguing against those who call it a comet based on its eventual disappearance.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Kepler’s discussion regarding the classification of the "New Star" of 1604 (Kepler's Supernova), specifically debating whether it should be called a comet or a star based on historical definitions and physical appearance.</meta>

->128 JOHANNES KEPLER<-

<margin>Achilles <note>Referring to Achilles Tatius, a 3rd-century author of an introduction to Aratus’s *Phaenomena*.</note> disagrees, and differently than Pliny: "those that have the light [streaming] upward like a beard are called *Lampades* <note>original: οἱ ἄνω τὸ φῶς γενειῶς ἔχοντες, καλοῦνται Λαμπάδες</note> [Torches]."</margin> ...very rapid, but with a reciprocal flash of light. Unless perhaps they also make the Greater Dog <note>The star Sirius.</note> and other stars into Comets, because they shared their twinkling with this star. Therefore, this celestial body can be granted the name *Lampadias* <term>Lampadias: A type of comet described by ancient writers as looking like a burning torch.</term>; but in such a way that "Torches" are not considered a species of "Long-haired stars" <note>original: *Crinitarum* — the Latin root for "comet," referring to the "hair" or tail of the star.</note>.

The final species from Pliny follow: the *disceorum* [disk-like], which have very thin hair around them; and the *hirtorum* [shaggy], which have the appearance of fleece. These the author [Pliny] calls "Manes" (*Jubas*); more recent authors call them "Roses," to which I also refer the "Night Suns" celebrated by the ancients. But our star should not be added to these species any more than the Dog Star Sirius; because it did not emit "manes" scattered all around, but only twinkled. 

Add to this that in these cases, the appearance of the "Mane" or "Disk" often changes into a "Spear," which Pliny records happening once; it happened again in the year of Christ 837, under Louis the Pious; and finally even in our own memory, observed by Tycho Brahe, in the years 1580 and 1585. For on these three occasions, the Sun was opposite the Comets: thus, because they initially turned their "hair" <note>The comet's tail.</note> away from the Sun and from our sight, they showed their "heads" in the middle of the "hair." Afterward, having left the position opposite the Sun, they gradually unfolded the "hair" from that part where they were departing. At the same time, the first two were also retrograde <note>Appearing to move backward in the sky.</note>, exactly as the Planets are when they are opposite the Sun; the cause of which Copernicus most beautifully attributes to the motion of the Earth. 

Therefore, since our star exhibited no appearance of "hair" at its beginning or its end, it should by no means be called a Comet. On the contrary, it is not absurd that the word "star" <note>original: *stella*</note> descends from the word "to glitter" <note>original: *στίλβειν* (stilbein)</note>, which means to twinkle. Since this fits our star, what prevents us from calling it a star?

But what if, they say, it actually possessed some kind of Tail? For our sight did not reach it for this reason: because the whole thing lay hidden behind the head, both when it was in "quadrature" <note>At a 90-degree angle to the Sun.</note> and when it was opposite the Sun. For the same arguments can be used to support this as those in Chapter 15, where the annual parallax <note>The apparent shift of a star's position due to Earth's orbit.</note> was taken away from the star. For this reason, look back at the diagram on page 86, where the star is drawn with a tail because of this very objection. To be sure, the elongation CO of the Earth O from the Sun C could by no means be so great that it could reveal this tail to us from the side at the star's position D, due to the excessive distance of the star. This conjecture was also tolerated by Maestlin <note>Michael Maestlin, Kepler's university professor and mentor.</note> concerning the star of the year 1572, in his booklet on the Comet of the year 1580.

I respond: sight, not reasoning, invented the names of stars. Whoever objects in this way is not reporting what he saw, but is divining what might be. Let it be a "Comet," then, when it is believed by Philosophers to project a tail upward away from us. For us other humans, let it be permitted in the meantime to speak according to our sight: for the Common People are the masters of speech for the Philosophers, and sight is the master of speech for the Common People.

Breng-

<summary>Kepler argues that the New Star of 1604 is a true star rather than a comet, as it lacked the characteristic "hair" or tail and displayed the twinkling typical of fixed stars. He uses astronomical history and the geometry of Earth's orbit to refute those who suggest a tail might have been hidden from view.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Kepler's systematic comparison between the New Star of 1604 (Kepler's Supernova) and comets, specifically addressing their appearance and movement.</meta>

->ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT-BEARER. 129<-
<note>The "Serpent-bearer" refers to the constellation Ophiuchus, where the supernova appeared.</note>

Brengger <note>Johann Georg Brengger, a physician and astronomer who corresponded with Kepler.</note> also employs another response, denying that it is common for comets to possess a light as bright as that seen in this star; not only in their tails, but not even in their heads. He notes that they are whitish, leaden, sometimes reddish, or otherwise pale—as Brahe <note>Tycho Brahe (1546–1601), the preeminent Danish astronomer whose data Kepler used.</note> reported—and they possess lights that are dull and inert on both sides, being little clouds more truly than stars. Indeed, Pliny <note>Pliny the Elder, the Roman author of *Natural History*.</note> established one species of white comets with silvery hair <note>original: "argenteo crine"</note>, the brilliance of which would turn away the gaze of the eyes; however, I know of no example of this from history.

At this point, I can also use the same argument that I used above in Chapter 15 to assign our star’s place among the fixed stars, in order to prove that it also lacked a tail. For those "hairy stars" <term>comets; from the Greek 'kometes' meaning long-haired</term> are the same ones that are mobile. Our star, however, remained immobile. Pliny seems to pull this reasoning apart, as he says some comets stick immobile in the sky. But this is easily answered from the experience of our own times.

For it can happen, as with stationary <term>planets that appear to stop moving in the sky due to the Earth's relative motion</term> planets, that although they are mobile, they nevertheless seem to stand still for a few days; this occurs if they are carried in the same direction and at the same speed as the Earth. For instance, that comet of the year 1585, when it was positioned opposite the Sun, ought to have been represented as moving backward <term>retrograde motion</term> by the motion of the Earth (even if the comet itself had stayed still). Yet, due to the speed of its own motion in the following signs <note>original: "in consequentia," meaning direct motion along the zodiac</note>, it appeared to be moving forward. If you were to take away just a little of its proper motion, you would make it appear stationary to the sight. And that other comet of the year 1580, when it appeared stationary in the month of December, was still being carried by its own motion along its trajectory toward the end of Libra and the Ecliptic, having traversed nearly equal spaces since its beginning.

Therefore, if such a comet shone in Pliny’s age or before him, which was rendered stationary when perhaps it had not been seen before or after, this could have provided Pliny the occasion for inventing a species of "immobile comets."

->place of the Great Conjunction.<-

### CHAPTER XXVI. ###

We now enter a discussion that is the most difficult and perplexed of all those concerning the whole nature of things; not only because of the obscurity of the question, but also because of the powerful divisions of the entire human race into completely contrary factions. From the previous chapters, it is evident...

->ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT BEARER 131<-

arisen from the Great Conjunction <note>A "Great Conjunction" is the rare meeting of the planets Jupiter and Saturn in the sky, which occurred in 1604.</note>: these things, therefore, although they coincide for good reason, were nonetheless joined by no necessity, yet still with great wonder; for this star did not only arise on the day of the Great Conjunction, but even in that very place where the planets met. This contains something much more sublime in its hidden depths than that first opinion of the Astrologers. For even if the star were, according to the way of nature, a genuine offspring of the Great Conjunction; what forced it to consist in that visible place of the conjunction? For there are no such examples from which the Astrologers might have collected their rule. Therefore, those who favor conjecture think it must be asked—since the Astrologers have stated no cause—what the true cause of such an admirable congruence was. For such great beauty and such great order strike the eyes of the mind: so that it seems not at all to be a mockery of chance wandering blindly, but the work of a rational mind <note>original: "rationis disponentis opus"</note>; whether that be the Creatress herself, or a spirit pervading the entire machine of the world, which both saw the appearance of the conjunction on earth, and according to this, generated this star in the highest heavens.

But a third opinion, also held by the Physicists, <margin>III. Of the Physicists</margin> not only explodes the claims of the Astrologers, but also laughs at these conjecturers, claiming everything for chance. <margin>From the sect of Epicurus.</margin> Yet they do not all argue in the same way. Some, for the sake of their own spirit, defend the philosophy of Epicurus <note>Epicurus was an ancient Greek philosopher who taught that the universe is made of atoms and governed by chance rather than divine intervention.</note> with a sought-after probability, for whom not only is that congruence accidental, but even the star itself flowed together from atoms. When asked for the cause of why the place of the star agrees so beautifully with the time, they appeal to the throwing of dice, reminding us that all things exist in infinity. I will attempt to express their sense. Let there be two dice, one of time, the other of place; the former inscribed with innumerable occasions of time, and among these even one of the Great Conjunction at the start of the Fiery Trigon <note>The "Fiery Trigon" refers to the group of zodiac signs—Aries, Leo, and Sagittarius—where the Great Conjunctions were occurring in that era.</note>; the latter marked with the characters of innumerable places in the sky, among which there is also one of the Great Conjunction. Suppose there exist infinite new stars of this kind, any one of them having obtained for itself a time and a place from a single throw of the dice. They say, therefore, it must be granted entirely that if infinite such throws were given, as if from the farthest eternity (which those arguing presuppose), it would eventually happen that among innumerable others, this throw would also fall, which joins the place of the Great Conjunction with the time of the same conjunction. And so, they claim there is no wonder, no suspicion of reason; if among innumerable new stars, succeeding one another in an eternal series (if such were given), some are even ignited under the moments of Great Conjunctions: and if among the innumerable new stars, in the times of the great

132 JOHANNES KEPLER

...arisen, some even fall into those beginnings of the <term>eight-hundred-year period<note>Kepler refers to the cycle of "Great Conjunctions" between Jupiter and Saturn, which return to the same part of the zodiac roughly every 800 years.</note></term> that return infinitely; finally, if among the innumerable new stars that coincide with the beginnings of the Zodiacal periods, some are even placed in the very location where the goal-post of the conjunction period is fixed.

<margin>From the school of Aristotle.</margin>
Others express more closely Aristotle’s doctrine concerning fortune and chance. These thinkers are pleased to argue that it is not by chance that planets meet, nor are new stars ignited by chance. They believe there are specific causes for both: for the planets, the cause lies in the course of the heavens; for the stars, the cause lies in the inner recesses of nature. These causes are distinct and not linked to one another. From both sets of causes, in their own proper time and place, there arise both the meetings of the planets and the emergence of new stars. Nevertheless, they claim it happens by "chance" if a conjunction coincides with a new star, whether in time or in place. For they argue it was not the intended goal of the <note>original: "Rectori hujus mundi"</note> Ruler of this world to provide these congruencies. Instead, they say that while He sets the celestial spheres in motion on one hand, and the force of nature on the other, these events happen spontaneously and beyond any specific intent, through material [causes] alone...

<summary>Kepler discusses the philosophical view, rooted in Aristotle, that while planetary movements and supernovae have individual natural causes, their simultaneous occurrence is a matter of mere coincidence rather than divine design.</summary>

-># ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT. 133<-

there are one hundred and twenty-one. I have said all that is relevant to the matter: for even if that condition were removed, Boethius <note>Anicius Manlius Severinus Boethius (c. 477–524 AD), a philosopher whose works bridged classical antiquity and the Middle Ages.</note> said in the aforementioned place: *A discourse that speaks of things that are not—things entirely foreign to the nature of the matter, as if one, having granted the eternity of motion, should then wish to claim that two planets could swap their orbits and orbital periods—is flawed in itself; it waits in vain for the credibility that mere chance might provide.* <note>original: "Sermo qui ea dicit, quæ non sunt... seipso vitiosus, frustra exspectat fidem, quam ei casus conciliet."</note>

The fourth opinion follows, derived from the sources of the Theologians: <margin>IV. Theologians.</margin> for those who take it as a shared premise that this entire world is sustained by divine providence, and that not only is the order of nature preserved, but also that miracles are sometimes performed in heaven and on earth beyond the course of nature (among which number they also include this star), they believe that God, in His divine goodness, is calling men to repentance. And although they despise that first opinion of the Astrologers—partly as bare, meager, and too simple, and partly holding it suspect of impiety—they do not deny that, because of these very rules of the Astrologers, this new miracle of divine omnipotence was placed by divine arrangement at both the time and the location of the Great Conjunction <note>The "Great Conjunction" refers to the rare alignment of Jupiter and Saturn, which in 1604 occurred in the "fiery" signs of the zodiac.</note>. This is not because God transforms nature based on the vain human dogmas of the Fiery Trigon <note>The "Fiery Trigon" or Triplicity is a group of three zodiac signs—Aries, Leo, and Sagittarius—associated with the element of fire in astrology.</note>, or that Sagittarius therefore receives a "fiery" quality from God simply because it pleased men to name it after fire; rather, it is because they do not think it absurd that God, by a temporary plan, uses popular human concepts to more clearly warn the multitude of men through that very reasoning.

They set these examples before our eyes: Pharaoh, King of Egypt, according to the custom of his nation, was devoted to the interpretation of dreams; God presented a dream to him when He was orchestrating the liberation of Joseph and the expansion and housing of the Church <note>Kepler uses "Church" here in the sense of the people of God/the Israelites.</note> in Egypt. And since the Egyptians worshiped the god Apis—specifically a Bull—because while other nations used oxen for plowing, they were provided for without the labor of plowing by the Nile irrigating their fields; God used this "imagination" or "knowledge" of the King. He depicted seven years of harvest to him by presenting the appearance of the same number of cattle.

Likewise, Nebuchadnezzar, King of the Chaldeans, was preoccupied with the statue of Bel <note>A Babylonian deity.</note>, who was the founder of the Assyrian kingdom and believed to be its preserver; in a dream, he saw a statue, presented to him by divine will as an interpreter of things to come, as the Prophet Daniel affirmed. To Abraham, to whom the principles of Astronomy are attributed, God showed the stars to teach him about the multiplication of his descendants. The Chaldean Magi practiced Astrology, being accustomed to predicting the future from the meeting of stars and the rising of Comets. These men, God [led] to Christ...

<meta>This page continues Kepler’s astronomical and chronological investigation into the Star of Bethlehem, comparing the Great Conjunction of Jupiter and Saturn in his own time (1603-1604) to the celestial events surrounding the birth of Christ.</meta>

134 JOHANNES KEPLER

The burning star warns us, leading us toward Christ the Lord. Almost all circumstances argue that the nature of that star was very similar to our modern one <note>Kepler is referring to the Supernova of 1604, which he observed personally.</note>, except for the immobility and altitude of this particular star: specifically, it also fell at the moment of the returning **Fiery Trigon** <term>Fiery Trigon: A rare astrological alignment where the "Great Conjunctions" of Jupiter and Saturn occur in the fire signs (Aries, Leo, and Sagittarius) once every 800 years</term>, and coincided with the time of a conjunction.

As for the mean motions <note>The theoretical average positions of planets, ignoring their slight orbital wobbles.</note>; in the 40th Julian year, Saturn and Jupiter were joined at 34 minutes before the beginning of Aries on January 22nd. Immediately on February 25th, the mean conjunction of Saturn and Mars occurred at 3 degrees of Aries; followed by the conjunction of Jupiter and Mars on March 1st at 5 degrees of Aries.

However, the apparent motions <note>The positions of the planets as actually seen from Earth.</note> were as follows. In the 39th Julian year, around June 22nd, while the Sun was positioned in 27 degrees of Gemini, there was a conjunction of Saturn and Jupiter in 23 degrees of Pisces. Shortly thereafter, both planets became stationary, so that Jupiter did not move far from Saturn. After Jupiter itself turned back into its forward course, it again encountered Saturn in retrograde motion <note>The apparent backward movement of a planet in the sky caused by Earth's own orbit.</note> the following August, around 21 degrees of Pisces. Continuing forward until November, it finally occupied the same space as Saturn for a third time in December, at 17 degrees of Pisces. Mars followed these in the year 40, during February and March, completing a conjunction with the former at the end of Pisces and with the latter at the beginning of Aries.

Therefore, that Great Conjunction was more significant than our current one because, according to the condition of their movements, there could be three conjunctions of Saturn and Jupiter in a single cycle <note>original: "unâ vice tres"</note>—just as today there can be five oppositions of those two, but only one conjunction. Conversely, although the mean conjunction reached the principal Cardinal point—the beginning of Aries—as closely as possible (where the true completion of the Great Conjunction, the true conjunction of Jupiter and Mars, also fell, and Mars in its mean motion coincided as closely as possible with the superior planets), the true conjunction of Saturn and Jupiter anticipated its place. This is similar to the conjunction of our own age in the year 1583, which is more like that one than the more recent one in the year 1603.

Therefore, in the 39th Julian year, just as that Great Conjunction was occurring, if that star rose at the same time—and if (as we believe for now) it first shone in the very location of the conjunction—it certainly warned the Chaldeans <note>Kepler refers to the Magi or Wise Men as "Chaldeans," a common term for ancient astronomers/astrologers of the East.</note> by their own rules (which still exist today) of great matters and a universal renewal of the whole world. Let that two-year period pass—the time from which Herod sought the infants of Bethlehem for slaughter—and at the end of that two-year period, the Magi come to Judea to the cradle of Christ. This would have been in the year...

He dates these celestial events to roughly 39–40 Julian years (BC) to align with the biblical account of the Magi and Herod.</summary>

**ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT-BEARER. 135**
<note>original: "DE STELLA NOVA SERPENT." refers to the constellation Ophiuchus, the Serpent-Bearer, where a supernova appeared in 1604.</note>

In the 41st Julian year. Go now and read what Laurentius Suslyga <note>A Polish priest and historian (1570–1640) who argued that Christ was born earlier than traditionally thought.</note>, a Pole, has argued regarding the calculation of time—published in Graz, Styria—using the most weighty arguments from the reliability of history. (Though, at the point where Kepler the Astronomer <note>Johannes Kepler, the famous imperial mathematician who also wrote extensively on this new star.</note> read him discussing the year of the Passion, Kepler threw down his book and pen and, fuming with indignation, stormed away from the table). Once persuaded, you will say for certain that Christ the Lord was born not in the 45th Julian year, but in the 41st. Therefore, the star, which had begun to shine two years earlier, coincided with the moment of the **great conjunction** <term>A rare alignment of the planets Saturn, Jupiter, and Mars in the same part of the sky.</term> of Saturn, Jupiter, and Mars in the 39th Julian year. For that reason, it was—as has been said—very similar to this modern star of ours. And since this star was divinely presented to the Magi, it proves that God himself accommodated himself for a time to these rules of the Magi, so that he might ignite the star at the very time when the Magi were most expecting a star; and perhaps also, as was said before, he placed it in that location toward which the eyes of the Magi were most directed due to the triple gathering of the Planets. This same thing was done in the case of our modern star.

Other points from deeper Theology also support this opinion: such as the fact that when God speaks with men through the Prophets about natural things and about arts, he does so in a way that those men can understand, as is evident in the book of Job, the Psalms, and the Prophets. Furthermore, the Holy Spirit did not propose the doctrine of salvation in some new and heavenly tongue, but in the languages of the nations, and he imbued the Apostles with the knowledge of those languages. Again, God appeared to the Patriarchs primarily in human form. Finally, the Son of God took up this very nature of ours, whole and entire with all its properties (excepting sin), along with the whole sequence of the internal faculties of the human soul—from which speech, various languages, and all human arts and disciplines flow—from the womb of the blessed Virgin into the unity of his person. It is credible, therefore, that this same Lord and our God, whose delight was so great and whose commerce with the children of men will be so great for eternity, even today does not entirely cease from the public signaling of his care for us. He has so ordered and arranged that signaling, presented in the new star through its description of time and place, that it could not escape the notice of—or fail to profoundly move—us, especially the scholars and **Astrologers** (whose daily almanacs everyone, even the lowest classes, read today).

136 JOHANNES KEPLER

->Examination of Established Opinions.<-

### CHAPTER XXVII.

I had already begun to say that the first opinion of the Astrologers is the least tolerable of all; I hold this for two principal arguments, which I will now explain.

First, it is easy for them to say: "The <term>Great Conjunction</term><note>A rare alignment of the outer planets—Saturn, Jupiter, and Mars—which occurred in 1604 and was believed to herald major world shifts.</note> of the three planets set this star ablaze." But perhaps they have never considered how difficult this is to explain. If this star had been suspended in our own air (the atmosphere), I could ask them what those eternal stars have in common with our air, which is separated from them by such a vast distance. However, this star was almost infinitely further away from the planets in the higher regions than our Earth is distant from those same planets toward the center of the world. Therefore, I ask with much better right: what do the planets have in common with that new star, which took its place among the boundaries of the fixed stars? This is to say nothing of the axiom of the Physicists, which attributes influence not from the lower realms to the higher, but rather from the higher to the lower.

And what shall I say about the mass of the new star? Is it likely to anyone that an elephant can be born from a fly? Unless, perhaps, they are going to say that just as a tiny spark suddenly ignites a massive amount of <term>gunpowder</term><note>original: *pyrij pulveris*</note>, so too here the smallness of the bodies of Saturn, Jupiter, and Mars was no obstacle to that immense mass suddenly catching light from them on all sides. But this is not "generating" a star; it is merely setting one on fire. 

I will ask, therefore: who else gathered so much matter into that place among the fixed stars? And who first made it flammable and ready to ignite? Finally, by what hands, by what staff, by what stream—or to use a German idiom—by what "running fire"<note>original: *currente igne*; likely referring to a fuse or a trail of gunpowder, the German *Lauffeuer*.</note> did the flame pass from the planets through the immense spaces to that fixed star? For without direct contact, either of flame or heat, nothing can be ignited by another object. 

Unless, perhaps, one of the three planets brought along the "Crystal" of Giovanni Battista della Porta, with which he promises to burn things at an infinite distance!<note>Della Porta (1535–1615) was a famous Neapolitan scientist who wrote about "burning mirrors" and lenses that could focus sunlight to start fires at a distance.</note> Indeed, amidst such vanity from the many who seize the title of "Philosopher," it is not incredible that some will arise who wish to compare this celestial fire to the burning of distant objects via mirrors. They are content to say something that sounds admirable and unsolvable to the common people, and which is plausible only in words. But be careful: if the new star burned and flared up, the planets themselves might finally be consumed by the same fire they communicated to the star.

But ex-

<summary>Kepler mocks the astrological theory that the 1604 supernova was "ignited" by a conjunction of planets, arguing that the distance is too great and the physics of such a "spark" makes no sense.</summary>

...rose into the highest ether? All of these things the discipline of Astronomy refutes.

They will therefore take refuge in the theory of rays, by which they say something <margin>Nor the efficacy of joined rays.</margin> happens similar to what usually happens to physical bodies; so that, once coupled and mixed together into one, they gain the power to produce something new. Already Plotinus—as cited in the works of Pico <note>original: "Plotinus apud Picum"; referring to Giovanni Pico della Mirandola, a 15th-century philosopher who famously wrote against astrology</note>—has dismissed this reasoning. Nevertheless, let us deal with them more gently and grant them their own principles for a moment. If something new is born through the binding together of rays, it must be born in the very place where they are bound. Yet the rays of any two planets are *always* bound together, and one can always point to a location among the fixed stars <note>The "fixed stars" refer to the distant stars that do not move relative to one another, unlike the "wandering stars" or planets</note> into which these coupled rays flow. It is a well-known axiom <term>axiom: a self-evident rule or starting point for reasoning</term> among Geometricians that any two points (such as the centers of the planets) are connected by a single straight line; and that any straight line in

<summary>The author uses geometric logic and the arguments of Pico della Mirandola to debunk the astrological theory that planetary rays only exert influence when "joined" in specific configurations, noting that such geometric connections exist constantly.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Kepler's investigation into the New Star of 1604, specifically refuting the idea that the star was a natural byproduct of the planetary conjunction of Jupiter and Mars.</meta>

138 JOHANNES KEPLER

The doctrine of Copernicus <note>Nicolaus Copernicus (1473–1543) proposed the heliocentric model where the Earth rotates and revolves around the Sun.</note> makes this refutation even more illustrious. For that relationship <term>conjunction|An alignment where two celestial bodies appear close together from a specific vantage point</term> is limited to the Earth (which wanders through the heavens), and not to the fixed center of the world. For someone who had been placed at the center of the world would have seen, on the day the star shone out, Mars already at the end of Capricorn, and Jupiter indeed beyond the place of conjunction but still within the borders of Sagittarius, at a very great distance from Mars. Therefore, this conjunction of Jupiter and Mars that we celebrated was seen in such a way only from the Earth. Take away from us this condition—the position of the eye upon the Earth—and you will have taken away the conjunction. Thus, what could possibly be birthed among the fixed stars by something that appears nowhere except from Earth? For if someone looked down from the fixed stars into the region of the planets, all of them would either disappear or, joined in the same globe, be seen to circle in very short spaces; certainly, junctions would be seen more frequently and closer together than they could be seen when the star shone out.

Let it therefore be held as certain that it is impossible for the conjunction of the planets alone to have combined to give birth to this new star.
As for what concerns the predictions of the Comet, the physician Johannes Weidner responds with learned verses in this manner:

*But if some lazy star-gazer,* <note>original: *Astrologaster iners*—a derogatory term for a status-seeking astrologer.</note> *with a wide-open mouth,*
*Should contend that the meeting of the two planetary stars,*
*Jupiter and Mars, gave birth to a beardless comet,* <note>original: *Cometam imberbem*. Early astronomers categorized comets by their appearance; a "beardless" comet lacked a traditional trailing tail.</note>
*And that this had been predicted by him:*
*We will oppose to him a hundred couplings of the wanderers* <note>original: *Erronum*—literally "wanderers," a common term for planets.</note>
*That produced no comet;*
*He spoke the truth by chance, having spoken a thousand lies.*

Therefore, unless the fourth opinion of the theologians <note>This refers to the "supernatural" explanation—that the star was a direct creation of God.</note> prevails, chance will claim for itself the coincidence of the star with the Great Conjunction.

<margin>
II. That the star did not fall by chance into the assembly of the planets.
</margin>

But I think we must fight no less against the opinion of Epicurus <note>Epicurus (341–270 BCE) was a Greek philosopher who taught that the universe is composed of atoms and governed by chance, rather than divine providence.</note> (for why should it not be permitted to use irony against the ironists? <note>original Greek: πρὸς τοὺς εἴρωνας ἀντειρωνεύειν</note>); an opinion which, by blind chance and the throwing of dice—perhaps more elegantly than Epicurus himself with his fortuitous wandering of atoms—nevertheless aims at the same target: to remove wonder from the minds of men. Once wonder is snatched away, every fear, every sinister omen, and finally every report of a prodigy <term>prodigy|An extraordinary event or omen believed to signal divine will or future disaster.</term> is extinguished. And this is achieved: that the brief time of life given to man may be spent in cheerfulness and sweetness, with cares excluded; and the more certainly an evil looms, the more diligently each preceding day is snatched away, as if by stealth, from the looming cruelty, and yields to a triumph in the soul that insolently despises its pressing and threatening enemy:

Living—

->*Let us live, my Lesbia, and let us love,*<-

<note>original: "Vivamus mea Lesbia atque amemus..." This is a famous poem by the Roman poet Catullus (Poem 5), often used to express the Epicurean view that life is short and death is final, so one should seek pleasure now.</note>

However, even if the reasons for this sentiment were entirely compelling to me, religion itself would have spurred me to untie these knots. First of all, such a life is poorly lived; for if it is lived cheerfully, it must be a wicked cheerfulness. Therefore, the discipline that hallows, protects, and encourages this cheerfulness with its "clucking" <note>original: "poppyfmisque" (poppysmis), a Greek-derived word for a clicking or smacking sound used to soothe animals, here used to mean flattering or coaxing words.</note> is wicked. Give me a holy life, and reason will be sober; and even if it wanders from the truth, it will at least be harmless—indeed, even beneficial. For in this way Christ, who is God, comforts His people, removes the fear of portents, and commands that the anxiety for tomorrow—the kind that eats at the heart—be kept far away. This is not because no evils threaten the nations, but because God intervenes intimately in specific affairs, stands as a protection for His own, and loves their cheerfulness—but not that foolish kind that glories in itself, but rather that which glories in God. For tomorrow will perhaps turn that former glory into smoke; but this latter glory, no one shall take from them.

Next, a respect for God also moves me. Everyone confesses that the minds of men have been struck by the sight of this new thing shining in the sky. If either chance or nature brought it forth to be born, then nature also brought it forth so that it might seize men into admiration of itself. <margin>It is not safe to downplay this omen.</margin>

Therefore, he who confesses God to be the author of the World—since he attributes the order of all nature, all its singular works, and surely this star as well, and the human admiration that follows it, to that same God—will certainly not fear that God (having been established as the author of this wonder in this way) would be angry at this human admiration. But as for the person who either denies God entirely like Diagoras <note>Diagoras of Melos, a famous atheist of ancient Greece.</note>, or makes the World itself God like the ancient Pythagoreans, or removes the care for our affairs from Him like Epicurus himself: he will have even less reason to discourage men from this admiration of novelty.

Therefore, let it be certain that God is not offended by the celebration of such a great wonder. But on the other hand, there is a huge fear that God might perhaps be offended by the downplaying of it—toward which path this opinion tries to find a way through its "tosses of the dice" <note>original: "tesserarum jactus." Kepler is criticizing those who claim the star appeared by a random "dice roll" of atoms rather than divine purpose.</note>. I have not fallen from the counsel of the Gods <note>A humble idiom meaning "I am not claiming to have secret divine knowledge."</note>, nor do I affirm anything as certain; but nevertheless, what if it was *not* the nature of things that brought this star to be born? What if God...

<meta>This page continues Kepler’s investigation into the nature of the New Star of 1604. Having previously discussed whether natural laws could explain its appearance, he now turns to the theological and philosophical implications of "Chance" versus "Divine Design."</meta>

140 JOHANNES KEPLER

...if God, by design, beyond the ordinary course of nature, and having taken special counsel, exposed this miracle to the eyes of men, and sought their astonished stupor with deliberate effort? Namely, that he might warn them? What then should I say to Him, if I were to diminish what He Himself wished to appear great—specifically, if I should attribute those things which are wonderful in this star to mere chance? For although through several previous chapters I have done this—attempting to find a way by which Nature herself might produce stars of this kind—the matter nevertheless returns to God, the author of nature. Even if the rising of the star is ascribed to the ordinary laws of nature, there remains nonetheless a place for God in the arranging of the circumstances of place and time.

<margin>Definition of chance.</margin>
But what is Chance? Truly, it is a most detestable idol, and nothing other than an insult to the highest and omnipotent God, and to the most perfect World which He established. To this idol, blind and rash motion serves as a soul, and infinite chaos serves as a body. To this "Chance," eternity, omnipotence, and the creation of the World—properties belonging solely to God—are wickedly ascribed.

Come then, let us test the reasoning of this opinion. Let us grant it <margin>What chance can do.</margin> eternity and whatever else it requires for its throws of the dice. Will it therefore have accomplished everything? I will not say that nothing animate can be made from the inanimate by chance, nor that "like" cannot possibly be born from "like" by chance; for these arguments have nothing to do with our star. I will take those things in which chance is understood to dominate most: geometric figures, innumerable numbers, and multilateral projectiles <note>Kepler is referring to dice</note>. This school of thought says that if infinite throws are made from eternity, it is necessary that all possible outcomes must eventually occur.

I, however, say that this is false. For if accidental chance dominates the throws and the way they fall, certainly in any single throw, all possibilities *could* fall—including those that had fallen before. If those same results fall again, then others will not fall—specifically those which had not yet fallen. Thus it can happen, even with infinite throws, that some things which are possible to happen do not happen. Therefore, it is not necessary that all things must eventually occur. Thus, even if we admit the existence of chance, it will never be certain whether this star fell into the place of the <term>Great Conjunction</term> <note>A rare alignment of Jupiter and Saturn, which occurred just before the New Star appeared in the same region of the sky</note> by chance. For chance could have provided this, but it also could have not provided it. We are left in uncertainty. 

Furthermore, Quintus Cicero <note>The brother of the famous Roman orator Marcus Tullius Cicero</note> in the first book *On Divination* denies that it can ever happen that chance perfectly expresses the truth. Once in my youth, when I had leisure in abundance, I delighted in that vanity of which many older men are not ashamed—a practice they attribute to the Chalcidian poet Lycophron. By rearranging the Greek letters of my name in various ways, I finally produced a definite sentence: so that instead of *Johannes Kepler* <note>original: Ιωάννης Κεπλῆρος</note>, it read *Merchant of the Sirens* <note>original: Σειρήνων κάπηλος</note>. And in Latin, *Johannes Keplerus* sounds, through the transposition of letters, like *Serpent in the sting* <note>original: "Serpens in akuleo". This is a pun on the fact that the New Star appeared in the constellation Ophiuchus, the Serpent-Bearer</note>. But since the meaning did not please me, and I desired another, yet did not succee—

<summary>Kepler argues against the idea that the New Star appeared by random chance, asserting that even with infinite time, chance does not guarantee every possible outcome. He mocks the idea of chance as a "detestable idol" and shares a personal anecdote about the vanity of finding meaning in anagrams of his own name.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Kepler's philosophical argument against the Epicurean view of the universe. **ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT. 141**

...would follow: I committed the matter to fortune. I inscribed individual letters onto cards, equal in number to the letters themselves. Then I began to shuffle the cards, looking at their sequence again and again to see if any meaning <margin> [fleuron symbol] </margin> would emerge. May all the Epicurean gods and goddesses destroy this "chance," which was never able to show me anything, even from a distance, that might allude to a meaningful sentence, while it snatched away a great deal of my time. And yet, among the possible outcomes, it was possible that the sentence I had devised might emerge: <note>original Greek: Σειρήνων κάπηλο</note> "Huckster of the Sirens." Therefore, I handed my cards over to the Epicurean Eternity to be carried off into the <note>original: Apîrum (Apeiron), the "Infinite" or "Boundless" matter in ancient Greek philosophy.</note> Infinite: and they say those cards are still flitting about there among the <term>Atoms</term> <note>Atoms: In the Epicurean view, the fundamental, indivisible building blocks of matter moving randomly in a void to form the universe.</note> in the utmost confusion, having yet to produce any certain meaning.

I shall tell my <note>original Greek: γυμναϛαῖς</note> debaters a thought that is not my own, but my wife’s; and I do so because, led by their own arguments, I have just now conceded that order *could* be produced by chance—which does not seem right to me. I do this so they may teach me how to defend the thought she provided against this troublesome and grave adversary. Yesterday, when I was called to dinner, exhausted from writing and with my mind "dusty" from considering those atoms, she served me a <term>salad</term> <note>original: acetarium. Kepler uses the preparation of a salad as a metaphor for the complexity of the cosmos.</note>. "So," I said, "if tin plates, lettuce leaves, grains of salt, drops of water, vinegar, oil, and pieces of egg were flitting about through the whole of the air, and this were to last from eternity, would it finally happen one day that a salad would come together by chance?" My dear wife responded: **But not with this beauty, nor in this order.**

Wretched me! What could I bring to the defense of my own opinion? She seemed to speak the truth. No doubt she learned it from <note>Kepler refers to Marcus Tullius Cicero, the Roman statesman whose writings often argued that design in nature implies a Creator.</note> Cicero himself. For he, too, grants to chance some "Figure" not unlike a real one, but certainly not such that you would say it was made by <note>Scopas: A famous ancient Greek sculptor known for the emotional realism and technical skill of his statues.</note> **Scopas**. The mind has great confidence whenever order appears, the cause of which must be sought from the innermost springs of **Geometry**. But even if some order were produced by chance, the mind immediately flies toward it; this is its delight, its very life. While the mind remains sluggish toward a thousand shapeless accidents, it immediately wakes up and becomes alert to a single ordered thing. Therefore, even if a star happened to fall into the assembly of the Planets by chance, it does not mean this achieves nothing. Rather, the whole nature of the world, and whatever is mental—whether supported by the movement of reason or by perennial instinct—is violently moved by it. Only these Philosophers, who prefer confusion over order and who have cast away this most beautiful world among unpleasant atoms, have earned this: that they themselves are neither moved by the beauty and harmony of this "chance," as they call it, nor do they allow others to take delight in it.

But we were not doing this to praise the beauty of chance,

142 JOHANNES KEPLER

[Decorative ornament]

...but rather that we might overturn the idea of chance itself. Therefore, what I said remains: it is not certain—even if eternity is granted—that among all things that are capable of happening, everything *must* eventually happen. This is most clearly seen when players increase the number of dice they use. With common dice, it is no wonder that all combinations appear in a short time: there are usually two dice, each with six sides, so the total forms of outcomes are no more than twenty-one. <note>Kepler is calculating the number of unique combinations possible with two six-sided dice where the order does not matter (e.g., a 2 and a 4 is the same as a 4 and a 2).</note> But when more "projectiles" are added, the possible outcomes increase immensely. This is even more true in the consideration of natural things; in these, even time itself (which one might hope to extend to provide enough space for everything to happen) actually increases the number of "projectiles" or variables. Thus, there can never be enough time for all things to happen; all eternity is too brief for the multitude of possible outcomes. As eternity grows longer, the possibilities grow many times faster: "Toil brings toil upon toil." <note>original Greek: Πόνῳ πόνῳ πόνον φέρει. This is a quote from the playwright Sophocles, expressing how one labor or difficulty leads endlessly into another.</note> See what they do, those who attribute orderly events to chance: with only a few principles, they achieve nothing—just as in my earlier example, a bowl and lettuce alone do not make a salad—but if they increase the number of principles, they defeat the very power of their "eternity" with an infinite multitude of possible outcomes that outruns eternity itself.

<margin>
Even the fall of dice is governed by its own causes.
</margin>

Finally, if that "chance," by whose power they think the most beautiful and orderly things are created, is compared to a throw of dice, then those who think these throws are purely fortuitous—that is, "without cause"—are short-sighted. <note>original Greek: ἀναιτίες (anaities)</note> If they strip their "chance" of every cause, they have not yet found an example of it in dice. Why did the "Venus" throw fall this time, and the "Dog" throw fall that time? <note>In ancient dice games, "Venus" was the name for the highest, winning throw, while the "Dog" (Canis) was the lowest, losing throw.</note> Surely it is because the player gripped the die on a different side, held it differently in his hand, shook it differently, threw it with a different impulse of mind or hand, the air blew differently, or it struck a different spot on the board. There is nothing here that lacks its own cause, if only one could follow those subtle details. In this same way, if our stars also have their own causes in nature for why they shone at this time and in this place—even if those causes are separate from the meeting of the Planets—then these thinkers have already fled from Epicurus to Aristotle. <note>Kepler is arguing that if there is a physical cause, the "random atom" theory of Epicurus is abandoned in favor of Aristotle's more organized, causal physics.</note> Therefore, they must be dealt with in a different way.

<margin>
Whether material necessity joined the star to the planets.
</margin>

So far, I have refuted that part of the third opinion which defined chance according to the view of Epicurus. As for the other part, which described chance not as a lack of all causes, but as a coincidence of events: indeed, such things were discussed in several of the preceding chapters. Thus, assuming the star is a work of nature, I would have agreed with that opinion in this case without exception, if this new star had only appeared at the same *time* as the Great Conjunction. <note>The "Great Conjunction" refers to the rare alignment of Jupiter and Saturn, which occurred in 1604, the same year this supernova appeared. Kepler is debating whether the star's appearance was a coincidence or a meaningful event.</note> But because it also appeared in the same *place* visually, I am forced to leave it in suspense, whether...

# ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT BEARER. 143

...whether the nature of the outermost heaven <note>The "outermost heaven" refers to the sphere of the fixed stars, which in the Aristotelian worldview was the most distant part of the universe.</note> itself was so arranged that it produced a new star at this most potent time and place; or whether, alongside those conjectures favoring the second opinion, a certain cause for the encounter itself must also be investigated. For the **third opinion** derived individual events separately from their own causes: the meeting of the Planets from **Astronomy**; the birth of the star from **Physics**. The **second opinion**, however, attributed the very meeting of these diverse things in this place and time to some new presiding cause. And because the third opinion fails to find a specific cause for why a new star should arise in this particular place (the 18th degree of Sagittarius) and at this particular time (namely, October 10, 1604)—even if some cause might perhaps exist, it remains hidden from us—I therefore incline more toward the second opinion. I suggest we must ask: by what reasoning did it happen that this meeting of the Planets, which took place at the same time and location, established this star for us there, since it could not have done so through the usual path of nature (as was said in the refutation of the first opinion)? And indeed, a **fourth opinion** provides a cause: *a God from the machine* <note>original Greek: Θεὸν ἀπὸ μηχανῆς (*Theon apo mechanes*). This refers to a plot device in Greek theater where a god is lowered onto the stage to resolve an impossible situation.</note>; but we should only resort to that when all natural explanations have failed us.

The conflict between these three opinions now becomes most sharp. First, it is doubted whether this meeting of the new star with the conjunction <term>copula</term> <note>Kepler uses the term *copula* to refer to the "joining" or conjunction of the planets Jupiter, Saturn, and Mars.</note> of the Planets was a mere consequence or something intentionally sought. And because it seems likely to be something intended, a further doubt arises: did **Nature** seek this, or **God** himself acting outside the normal order? Again, because it is difficult to believe that Nature alone could achieve so much, yet it seems rash to attribute these things directly to God (as we would then be dragging Him into the laws of Astrology), the mind—finding itself in a dead-end alley <note>original: *angiporto non pervio*</note>—returns to the original opinion: that this meeting was not managed by design, but followed from material necessity. 

We have indeed said much in the previous sections about the natural causes by which it is plausible this star was produced. <margin>Should this union be attributed to God, or to a deliberating Nature?</margin> Whatever those causes may be, the **Theologians** bid us take care not to attribute too much to them. And there seem to be arguments to persuade us that it is a higher task to provide that a new star should coincide with a meeting of the Planets than can be attributed to "Nature"—that is, to that spirit of the whole universe. For, first, it would be necessary for this spirit to be most powerful, sitting as it were at the helm of celestial nature, able to moderate its force so that it brings forth such a part at the intended time and place. Furthermore, a most vast knowledge would be required: namely, to foresee which meetings of the Planets would appear as such from this Earth of ours, and according to this foreknowledge, to exercise its right over the outermost heaven. Who then is...

Who then is he, whose knowledge embraces the Earth and whose power encompasses the outermost heavens? <note>In the cosmology of Kepler's time, the "outermost heaven" was the sphere of the fixed stars, the furthest reach of the physical universe.</note> Finally, the purpose toward which this providence strives proves him to be exceedingly good and **loving of mankind** <note>original Greek: *φιλάνθρωπον* (philanthrōpon)</note>. For which creature do you think this spectacle was intended? Was it for some celestial being? But as was stated in the refutation of the first opinion, nowhere in the Universe, except from the Earth alone, is a star seen associated with the Planets. <note>Kepler is referring to the fact that the Supernova of 1604 appeared in the same part of the sky as a rare "Great Conjunction" of Jupiter, Saturn, and Mars—a perspective unique to observers on Earth.</note> Was this spectacle, then, intended for any other earthly creature besides man?

By these arguments, therefore, it seems to be proven: if this harmony of place and time was sought out, it can be attributed to no one but GOD alone.

<margin>In favor of the second opinion of those who speculate about Nature.</margin>

On the other hand, those speculative physicists do not call into question that God is the highest and first cause of all things; but they contend only this: that He used an ordinary means to bring about this star. Nor do they deny that God acts through that Spirit of His, which moves through the whole World, even if [He granted] to it also this knowledge... <note>The text cuts off mid-sentence at the bottom of the page; the "Spirit" mentioned likely refers to the *Anima Mundi* or World Soul, a concept Kepler explored to explain how nature follows geometric and harmonic laws.</note>

<meta>This page continues Kepler’s philosophical investigation into whether "Nature" (an animating force or World Soul) possesses a form of intelligence or "faculty" that responds to the movements of the heavens, specifically the "New Star" of 1604.</meta>

# ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT-BEARER. 145
<note>original: "DE STELLA NOVA SERPENT." Kepler refers to the constellation Ophiuchus, often called the Serpent-Bearer.</note>

...of God. Nor do I fear that I may be rebuked for providing an opportunity to modern philosophers—by means of this "faculty," which is a part of the World—to ultimately turn it into a God. Let those men philosophize even to the point of delirium! For did their ancestors not ascribe divine honors to all sorts of things: the air, the water, the Ocean, the sky, and the visible stars? Meanwhile, some even believed these things to be inanimate. It is well-established among Christians that there are innumerable armies of blessed spirits <note>Kepler is referring to angels and celestial intelligences.</note> who are free from every worldly body and are admirable in their holiness. In dignity, they are far superior to this "spirit" which is bound by a certain link of nature to the worldly body it presides over. Among these spirits, there are those higher in the distinctions of rank: most glorious princedoms and most powerful authorities. And yet, above all these, Christians acknowledge a higher God, the creator of both themselves and all those others. Thus I read in Prudentius <note>Aurelius Prudentius Clemens, a Roman Christian poet of the 4th century.</note>, though I think it ought to be emended:

->*It was ordered by the mouth of Caesar Gallienus:*<- <note>Gallienus was a Roman Emperor who reigned from 253–268 AD.</note>
->*Whatever the prince worships, let us all worship:*<-
->**I WORSHIP THE ETERNAL PRINCE OF GODS,**<-
->*The Creator and Lord of Gallienus.*<-

He confesses, along with Augustine <note>St. Augustine of Hippo, the influential Christian theologian.</note>, that the title "Gods" is attributed in the sacred scriptures even to the holy Angels; but he acknowledges one eternal and uncreated Prince over those created beings, whom the Scripture also calls the **God of Gods** <note>original: "Deum Deorum"</note> (for there appears to be no reason why he should here specifically celebrate the "maker of days," as some readings suggest). Nor does he wish to worship what the prince imagines, but rather the supreme Prince; namely, He who was not created by Gallienus, but who created Gallienus; not one who is commanded of us by the order of Lord Gallienus, but He who is the Lord himself of Lord Gallienus.

Therefore, someone might bring these arguments forward to defend the cause of Nature, claiming that the joining of this new star with the Great Conjunction <note>The "Great Conjunction" is the rare meeting of Jupiter and Saturn, which occurred in 1604 near the appearance of the supernova.</note> is the work of that Spirit which permeates the fields of the entire World.

But so help me, Christ, even after shaking out all these reasonings, I do not satisfy myself. Regarding power and goodness, the explanation is perhaps adequate; but regarding knowledge, it is surprising how much is lacking. For let it be granted that there is in Nature a "sense" of the conjunction of the planets: shall we then also attribute to Nature a knowledge of those things which humans have written in innumerable books about the conjunctions of the planets? Shall we also accept this: that Nature, by showing this star like a displayed character <note>i.e., a written letter or omen</note>, wished to speak with us men? Or, if this is absurd, then tell me, you physical diviner: for whose good did Nature fit a star in the highest ether to those things which seemed not in the highest...
<margin>T</margin> <note>This "T" is a signature mark for the printer to keep track of the page gathering.</note>

<summary>Kepler discusses whether the "New Star" was produced by a "World Spirit" or "Nature," while carefully distinguishing this animating force from the Creator (God) to avoid charges of pantheism. He expresses skepticism about whether such a natural spirit could actually "know" human astrology or intend to communicate with humanity.</summary>

<meta>The previous page likely discussed the astronomical appearance of the New Star of 1604 and the various theories regarding its origin—whether it was a purely natural phenomenon or a divine sign. Here, Kepler continues his argument by examining the role of "Nature" versus "Divine Providence" in celestial and earthly events.</meta>

->146 JOHANNES KEPLER<-

in the highest and vastest <term>ether</term> <note>original: "aethere"; the subtle substance then believed to fill the heavens beyond the moon</note>, but in this most humble and narrow Earth? Whoever affirms that this was a destined work—the meeting of the star with the joined planets—at least in such a way that the joined planets were the object that moved the hidden sense of Nature, that person will find no other way out unless they also attribute to that same Nature an understanding of our astrological writings and a familiarity with hidden, figurative speech. But in attributing these things, one attributes the process of reasoning to it; and thus, one would make Nature a rational spirit. Yet, this line of reasoning previously protested that it was claiming for Nature an "astronomical sense," not one acquired through reasoning, but instilled by the law of creation.

Whatever, therefore, physical conjecture regarding "the machines of the world" <note>Kepler often refers to the physical universe as a "machine" or clockwork system</note> might provide from the storehouse of its own discipline: there is still a need for God the Architect, by whose counsel the star might come into the council of the planets.

Even if we grant what conjecture seeks—that Nature is sufficient for this adaptation—who will deny that the moderation of the entire matter nevertheless belongs to God himself, who uses Nature, which He created, as His servant? Do we not see in the infinite things that happen daily, each from its own natural cause, that a place is nonetheless left for divine providence? This is true in many things, but especially in the execution of vengeance for crimes; by which it is brought about that an event falls out in a way that is consistent not only with the preceding causes in nature, but also with divine justice. For what reason was the Jewish State destroyed by the Romans in a horrendous slaughter, and the city of Jerusalem buried? If you ask Josephus, the writer of the *Jewish War*, he answers you that the Jews were dragged into the war by the Romans against their will. He tells the truth, if you look at the natural order; the cause of this event arises from a twin series of circumstances. For on the part of the Romans, there was the license of domination, the insult of injuries, the pride of the Caesars, the height of the empire, and the ancient custom born with paganism itself of deifying men and dedicating statues, altars, temples, and priests. Add to this their confidence in arms, their desire for glory, and the terrible habit of provoking neighbors or even subjects so that the legions would have something to practice on and the Emperors would have material for a triumph. On the part of the Jews, there was their trust in God, the author of their laws; a numerous nation; a most fortified city; a most holy temple; the antiquity of the race; their stubbornness in defending the rites and religion of the temple; and ancient and new prophecies, including those of false prophets. Anyone who carefully considers and compares both sides will admit that, by the usual order of things, it was inevitable that Jerusalem would be overturned by the Romans. Yet behold: God is the avenger of the most innocent death of Christ the God, mingled in the midst of the causes of these singular events, fitting together and clashing the opposing customs of the nations; which previously, if not...

<summary>Kepler argues that while natural events have physical causes, they are ultimately moderated by Divine Providence, using the Roman destruction of Jerusalem as a historical parallel for how God orchestrates complex events through natural means.</summary>
<keywords>astrology, providence, Nature, Jerusalem, Josephus, Roman Empire, divine justice, Kepler, astronomy, theology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Kepler's argument regarding the origins of the "New Star" (the supernova of 1604). He is transitioning from purely physical explanations to a providential one, arguing that even if the star has a natural cause, its timing—coinciding with a rare planetary alignment—suggests divine intent.</meta>

# ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT. 147

if not at rest: they lived more mildly: who, in order to exercise this vengeance without a great disturbance of natural causes, waited for the opportunity for forty whole years: and lest it should seem fortuitous, he predicted forty years before that he would avenge the nation in this way. <note>Kepler likely refers to the destruction of Jerusalem in 70 AD, forty years after the Ministry of Christ, as an example of divine timing using natural events.</note> In the same way here, however Nature produced this star, the sum of the matter finally returns to God.

Although, what need is there for these subtleties? Or what sin is it if we say God did what Nature did? Have not the holy Prophets of GOD taught us this way of speaking? What is there in the nature of things, whether in heaven or on earth, that is not referred to God himself? A seed, a liquid and white substance, after it falls into the womb: by the same formative power, which we know to be natural and perpetual, it is curdled: and thus finally the animal is formed. But what does the divine Job say about his own birth? *Did you not milk me like milk, and curdle me like cheese?* <note>original: "Nonne sicut lac mulsisti me, & sicut caseum me coagulasti?" from Job 10:10</note> He says this not to his mother, but to God, the author of himself, of his mother, and of all of nature. Surely he does not think that nature deserves to be dwelt upon with much admiration in its contemplation: but rather he wonders at Him *who spoke and they were made, who commanded and they were created.* <note>A common biblical phrasing, such as in Psalm 33:9.</note>

So that I may finally end this anxious examination of opinions; since the Theological view has absorbed the physical conjecture and acquired it all for itself: only two opinions remain. For either it must be said that the place and time of the new star have no commerce with the <term>great conjunction: The rare alignment of Jupiter and Saturn, which occurred in 1603-1604.</term> of the Planets, but happened by chance (in a sober sense) so that the star and the coupling of Planets coincided, each arising from its own cause: Or because we cannot with any likelihood attribute the place in 18 degrees of Sagittarius, and the time in the year 1604, October 10th, to an unapparent cause, when the apparent conjunction of the Planets is at hand, which offers itself as the cause: therefore this other thing must be said, which I pronounce most safely and with full confidence: that this new celestial prodigy was associated by the almighty GOD himself with the three Planets, Saturn, Jupiter, and Mars, then joined together, by a certain plan directed toward the salvation of men. For He is that God to whom nothing in the World is either great or small, since He alone is the author of all things: who, although the human race lives in the confines of this most contemptible clod of earth, nevertheless prefers His own image <note>Referring to the biblical concept of humans being made in the "Imago Dei" or image of God.</note> to any star, even if it exceeded the magnitude of the whole world a hundred thousand times over. Who, in order to mark the place and time of the great conjunction of the three <term>superior planets: The planets further from the Sun than Earth—Saturn, Jupiter, and Mars.</term> with this monument, as it were, for a perpetual memory of the event, and to warn the human race...

148 JOHANNES KEPLER ON THE STAR IN THE SERPENT-BEARER <note>The "Serpent-Bearer" refers to the constellation Ophiuchus, where a supernova appeared in 1604, now known as Kepler's Star.</note>

...to signal human affairs concerning the greatest matters: He required no anxiety, no labor, and no weariness to create such a thing that could be seen by the inhabitants of the earth in the form of so great a star. Whether He used Nature as His servant for this purpose, or exerted this like a ray of extraordinary omnipotence; in either case, this is true: *He spoke, and they were made; He commanded, and they were created.* <note>original: "Ipse dixit, & facta sunt; ipse mandavit, & creata sunt." Therefore, with the Royal Psalmist I would feel free to exclaim: *I shall not die, but live, and declare the works of the Lord.* <note>original: "Non moriar, sed vivam, & narrabo opera Domini."
