# ON THE STAR OF CYGNUS. 151

...you prepared me for this mournful event <note>The "mournful event" refers to the death of Kepler’s mentor, the great astronomer Tycho Brahe, in 1601.</note> with very wise words; finally, you acquired at your own expense an **azimuthal Quadrant** <note>An instrument used to measure both the altitude of a celestial body and its horizontal angle (azimuth).</note> and a **Sextant**—the latter made of iron, the former of brass—constructed after the model of Brahe’s own instruments. These instruments, after Brahe had died and shortly before we heard of the star, I held for my own use by your permission, Your Excellence.

Therefore, those things which I had observed concerning this star using your instruments, and what should be said about it by an Astronomer; having been written down in that year and shared with a few others, I was thinking of publishing and dedicating to your Name—a promise you remember me making in person. Not because I hoped any splendor would be added to your Illustrious Name from this very slight writing, but because I thought it was vital to my reputation to give public evidence, at the first possible opportunity, of my gratitude toward Your Excellence. Indeed, if I were to wander in my mind through the whole series of my and my family’s fortunes, no part, no connection would occur to me that your Beneficence alone had not most strongly secured. And not to be long-winded on a short page: I could then be rightfully uplifted in a magnificent way, and exclaim with Virgil’s Aeolus:

->*You gain for me whatever this kingdom is, you win the scepter and Jove’s favor, you grant me a place at the banquets of the gods, and you make me master of the clouds and storms.* <note>original: "Tu mihi quodcunque hoc regni, tu sceptra Jovemque / Concilias, tu das epulis accumbere Divûm, / Nimborumque facis tempestatumque potentem." Kepler is quoting Virgil’s *Aeneid*, comparing his patron’s support to the divine favor shown to the king of the winds.</note><-

Since this was the case, there was nothing I would rather do than dedicate to your Name that booklet, which was the first born to me since the death of Tycho; so that those who would benefit from your Foresight in aiding the study of Astronomy might, in turn, see praises flow back upon your Illustrious Name.

What was the cause, truly, why that which had been intended was interrupted? What excuse can I offer you that is probable enough?

Naturally, I wanted to wait for the end of the new phenomenon; meanwhile, I wrote to the principal Mathematicians to collect their opinions.

However, since the star was not extinguished, nor did the mathematicians respond with that promptness which I hoped for, time meanwhile slipped away; until, after two years and more, that great new star, of which this book... <note>Kepler is transitioning from the star in Cygnus (1600) to the even more famous supernova of 1604, often called "Kepler's Star."</note>

I have treated in this little book <note>This refers to Kepler's work *De Stella Nova* (On the New Star), published in 1606.</note>, it shone out in the foot of the <term>Serpent-Bearer</term> <note>original: "Serpentarij"; this is the constellation Ophiuchus. Accept therefore, Illustrious **BARON**, <note>Kepler is addressing Wolf Sigismund von Kirchberg, to whom the book was dedicated.</note> and most skilled in all the secrets of Nature, accept, I say, from me this token of my most just goodwill toward you; and do not judge it with your eyes by the number of pages, but by that <term>skillful</term> <note>original: "dædaleâ", referring to Daedalus, the mythical craftsman; implying a complex or ingenious intellectual movement.</note> movement of secret understanding by which you carry yourself sublimely through the inscrutable treasures of divine wisdom, judge it by the weight of the matter itself of which I treat.

For it must be proven by Mathematicians, shown to Philosophers, and recorded for the memory of all posterity: whether at this time there is some star in the heavens; which, since it was not there before, has now remained there for at least six years, immobile, unchangeable, unquenchable: so that whether it is destined to perish before the consummation of the whole world, men may understand the magnitude of the miracle which lasted so many years; or if it shall endure with the rest of the fixed stars; for as long as that shall be, let those to whom the New Star will be proved by the following arguments always have before their eyes this most evident proof of the <term>world created in time</term> <note>This is a major philosophical point: Aristotelian philosophy held the heavens to be eternal and unchanging. The appearance of a "new" star proved the universe was not eternal, supporting the Christian doctrine of Creation.</note>: But those who, from the very long duration of the star, stubbornly persist in arguing against the traditions of Astronomers and denying it was recently born; let them not be ignorant of how great a weight of authority presses upon them.

And this must be performed by me all the more because Mathematicians everywhere look back to the <term>observations of Brahe</term> <note>original: "observationes Braheanas"; referring to Tycho Brahe, the great Danish astronomer whose data Kepler inherited.</note>, which they know I have the opportunity to inspect: from which, indeed, such arguments are drawn that weightier ones in this genre can be hoped for from nowhere else.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the sudden appearance of a new star in the constellation Cygnus, first observed in 1600. The author is reflecting on why such a bright star was not recorded earlier despite the increasing number of sky-watchers.</meta>

->ON THE STAR OF CYGNUS.<- 153

**A**lthough, from the start, astronomy has never before been so widely cultivated in the universities; and never have there been more numerous or more attentive observers of the sky among navigators than today: yet there is no one in all of Europe—indeed, in the whole world (which people today travel through as if strolling in their own private gardens)—who has been able, through their own experience, to refute the spreading rumor about the novelty <note>The "newness" or recent appearance of the star.</note> of this star.

And yet this star is bright, it is quite large, and it is in a prominent location; it clearly alters the appearance of the constellation's pattern <note>original: "configurationis"</note> when compared with the others, whether it is present or absent. Therefore, it is almost unbelievable that an industrious sailor could not remember it, if it were an old and permanent fixture.

As for me, I shall act with candor, as befits an astronomer. For if I were asked whether I saw this star before the year 1601 or not, I would have to answer that I do not know. For although I began to learn the heavens in the year 1591, with Maestlin <note>Michael Maestlin (1550–1631), a renowned astronomer and mathematician who was the mentor of Johannes Kepler.</note> as my guide, I do not recall being warned by my teacher that a star in the breast of the Swan <note>The constellation Cygnus.</note> had been omitted by Ptolemy <note>Claudius Ptolemy (c. 100–170 AD), the Greco-Egyptian astronomer whose star catalog in the *Almagest* was the standard reference for over 1,400 years.</note>. Even the author, trained by Michael Maestlin, does not recall it being mentioned as a star overlooked by ancient catalogs.</summary>

--- Page 206 ---
<meta>This page continues Kepler’s discussion regarding the discovery of a new star in the constellation Cygnus. The first word "ditus" is the suffix of a word from the previous page, likely "deditus" (dedicated/devoted) or "traditus" (handed over).</meta>

->154 JOHANNES KEPLER<-

...devoted <note>original: "ditus," likely the end of "deditus"</note>, I inspected the heavens quite rarely with care, and then only the parts of the **Zodiac** <term>Zodiac: the band of the sky through which the sun, moon, and planets move</term>, as I was chiefly occupied with calculation; for **Tycho Brahe** had an abundance of observations and was diligent in his observing.

Now that **Tycho** has died, this concern has indeed come upon me: that perhaps something new might arise in the heavens without my knowledge. For this reason, I have looked at the stars more frequently, including this very constellation of **Cygnus** <note>The Swan constellation</note>. Its appearance and its configuration with the other stars remained so fixed in my mind that there was no need to compare a globe with the sky. Yet, that memory was not so vivid as to prevent a single star—especially near the "breast"—from being added without my noticing, which had not been there before while **Tycho** was alive. Therefore, I did not seem to see anything new in that constellation, even though I saw this very star we are discussing at that very time—specifically in **November of the year 1601**, as my memory tells me without the slightest hesitation. 

For at that time, after the setting of the Sun, the constellation was located in the western part of the sky, stretching its beak toward the **Horizon**, its tail upward, and its wings to the right and left. Indeed, I thought to myself that if some Christian were to begin fashioning images of the stars anew, they would find in the stars of **Cygnus** a most fitting figure of a **crucifix** with a bowed head. For the star we are uncertain about was then a little to my right, representing the chest of the crucifix; whereas the star actually in the breast of the **Swan** deflected to the left, becoming the head of the crucifix. This mental image has preserved the memory of this star for me until now; so that later, when in the month of **May 1602** I was first warned by letters about a "new phenomenon" in **Cygnus**, I did not exclaim that I was seeing anything new compared to the previous months. 

I knew that I had already conceived in my mind long ago the image of the Cross (which, as **Bayer** <note>Johann Bayer, author of the famous star atlas 'Uranometria' (1603)</note> mentions, is known even to farmers). However, as for the "bowed head" <note>referring to the position of the new star</note>, I remembered it from the month of November; whether it was also there in the past, I did not remember, nor do I remember now. Thus, I am let down here by my own memory, as I said; I would not affirm on the basis of memory alone that this is a "new" star.

What I have said of myself, I can say of almost all the **Professors of Mathematics** today who are distributed throughout the Academies of Europe: they all raise the question of this new phenomenon; they either suppress their opinion or admit they do not know. **Maestlin** <note>Michael Maestlin, Kepler's former professor and mentor</note> indeed, having confessed that he is in doubt, nevertheless leans toward the idea that the longer he examines his memory, the more he thinks he saw it once before. But he immediately adds the arguments used by those who strive to make the star "ancient" through disputation: namely, that it has lasted so many years, and that **Ptolemy** <note>Claudius Ptolemy, the 2nd-century astronomer whose star catalog was the standard for over a millennium</note> omitted many fixed stars. Here, therefore, while he flees to arguments, he confesses that he has nothing to draw from his own memory against us.

What... <note>The text cuts off mid-sentence; "Quid" is the start of the next section.</note>

<summary>Kepler recounts how his focus shifted from calculation to observation after Tycho Brahe's death, leading him to notice a "new" star in Cygnus (now known as P Cygni). He describes using the mnemonic of a crucifix to remember the star's position but admits that memory alone is insufficient to prove the star's novelty.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the appearance of a "New Star" in the constellation Cygnus (the Swan), focusing on historical evidence from ancient astronomers like Hipparchus to prove its novelty.</meta>

->**ON THE STAR OF THE SWAN.** [Page number] **155**<-

What shall I say of the household <note>contubernio: literally a "tent-sharing" group; here referring to the community of assistants and students at Tycho Brahe's observatory, Uraniborg</note> of Tycho Brahe, in which many students of astronomy lived for twenty years? They were so practiced in the observation of the stars during the coldest nights that some even departed this life for that very reason. Is it possible, then, that among these survivors there is not one who could strike the report of this new phenomenon from their own memory if it were false? Thus, weighing these matters, Bayer <note>Johann Bayer (1572–1625), a celestial cartographer famous for his star atlas, Uranometria</note> says, *it has been accepted as a New Star by the silent consensus of all.*

But for those whose own memory fails them, the arguments by which faith in its novelty can be established are these:

First, this star was omitted by Hipparchus, <margin>**I.** The plan of Hipparchus. Pliny, Book 2, Chapter 26.</margin> who dared a thing that was even <note>original: "Deo improbam"</note> offensive to God: to number the stars for posterity and to map the constellations according to a rule. He devised instruments by which he could mark the locations and magnitudes of each individual star, so that from this record it could easily be discerned not only whether they perished (take note, I pray, of Hipparchus’s plan) or were born; but whether any at all moved or changed their positions; likewise whether they increased or diminished in size—leaving the heavens as an inheritance to all, if anyone should be found who could grasp that system.

Hipparchus, therefore, who described the fixed stars with this intention—so that it could be discerned whether any were being born—omitted this star near the breast of the Swan, both in his *Catalogue* and in the second book of his commentary on Aratus, and in his work *On Simultaneous Risings and Settings* <note>original: "συνανατολαῖς κ̀ συγκαταδύσεσι"</note>. In doing so, what else is he doing but calling out to us with a living voice—we to whom he left the heavens as an inheritance—warning us that now at last, after so many centuries, there appears what he himself had begun to doubt in his own age: namely, that new and long-lasting stars can indeed be born even in the highest ether.

But do not think that the constellation of the Hen <margin>Diligence.</margin> <note>The "Hen" was a common historical name for the constellation Cygnus (the Swan).</note> was surveyed and described by Hipparchus in a lazy or yawning manner. Here are his own words from the first book of his commentary on Aratus: *Again,* he says, *Aratus suffers from ignorance in what follows, when he writes of the Hen:*

<note>original Greek: "Ἀλλ’ ὁ μὲν ἠεροεὶς: τὰ δέ οἱ ἐπιτετρήχυνται Ἄστρασιν: ἔτι λίην μεγάλοις, ἀτὰρ ἐ μὲν ἀφαυροῖς."</note>
*But it is indeed misty: yet its parts are roughened*
*With stars: some quite large, and certainly not faint.*

*For the Bird has many bright stars: especially the one in the tail, which is very bright indeed, almost equal to the bright star in the Lyre.* <note>The star in the tail is Deneb; the bright star in the Lyre is Vega.</note> Thus he writes there.

Since he begins his description of the constellation from that line <margin>Order in the Swan.</margin> which extends from the beak through the neck and body of the Swan to the tail, and reviews each star in this series in order; and afterwards turns to the wings and finally the feet, as if they were joined to the sides of this principal line: how could he have passed over this star of ours, which occurs on almost that same line and in the same tract, before one arrives from the neck at the breast? How, I ask, could he have missed it, if it existed in that age? There is simply nothing that can be held up as even a likely excuse.

<summary>The author argues that the star in Cygnus is truly "new" because the ancient astronomer Hipparchus, who meticulously cataloged the stars specifically to track such changes, failed to record it.</summary>
<keywords>Tycho Brahe, Hipparchus, Cygnus, P Cygni, Nova, Johann Bayer, Pliny the Elder, Aratus, star catalogs, stellar evolution</keywords>

--- Page 208 ---
156          JOHANNES KEPLER

<margin>II. Ptolemy's revision.</margin> Next follows the Table of Ptolemy, which he himself repeated after receiving it from Hipparchus <note>Hipparchus (c. 190–120 BCE) was a Greek astronomer whose star catalog was the basis for Ptolemy’s later work.</note>, and corrected in several places, repositioning stars into more suitable members of the Figures invented by the ancients. But if Hipparchus was drowsy regarding this star, why did Ptolemy omit it; why does he himself make no mention of it?

<margin>The star's brightness.</margin> What cause could be sufficient for these most diligent craftsmen to omit a star of the third magnitude, so near to that which is in the breast? For they did not think that stars of not only the fourth, but even the fifth and sixth magnitude—and even <term>nebulous</term> <note>At this time, "nebulous" referred to clusters or patches of light that appeared fuzzy to the naked eye.</note> ones—should be omitted if they were useful for designing the outlines of the figure, as in this very neck of the Swan. 

<margin>Evidence of position.</margin> Did one star seem sufficient to them for designating the breast? Not at all. For one could also have been sufficient for designating the tail of Capricorn; yet they mark two: one bright, the other more obscure. In the tail of the Southern Fish, in the shoulder of Sagittarius, and in one horn of Aries, they place pairs. Finally, they occasionally omitted individual stars situated in isolation, even if they were large enough, if they occurred far outside the boundaries of the figures; but from pairs or trios joined together, because of their visual prominence <note>original: ἔμφασιν (emphasin)</note>, I do not know if you will find any others omitted. Therefore, for this very reason—because they are seen as a pair—if both had existed long ago, both would have been entered into the record.

Here Maestlin <note>Michael Maestlin (1550–1631) was Kepler's university professor and a significant astronomer who helped prove that comets and novae were in the heavens, not the atmosphere.</note> brings forward a little star which is seen above the middle of the three in the tail of the Great Bear; the farmers call it the Rider or the Charioteer, as if he were sitting upon the middle horse. Although this star is of no contemptible magnitude, it was nevertheless omitted by Ptolemy and Hipparchus. I respond that the cause is manifest: namely, the excessive proximity of this Rider (being so small) to its own horse (which is so large and bright). Because of this, it cannot be discerned except by those who have the most accurate vision. There are also other examples of this omission. But this cause has nothing to do with this New Star, which, although it is near the star of the breast, is yet so distant from it that vision is plainly not confused at all. Not to mention that it is of almost equal magnitude; therefore, both are discerned with equal ease.

<margin>Fitness of the image.</margin> But perhaps that which we call the New Star is unformed <note>original: ἀμόρφωτος (amorphotos), a term used for stars that sit outside the recognized shape of a constellation figure.</note>, outside the configuration of the Swan? Much less so. For hardly anyone would paint the Swan through its usual starry tracks without leaving a part of the breast—or at least the root of the neck—in that part of the heaven where that star we are speaking of is now discerned. It is so near the breast, and the neck of this Swan is so long from the star attributed to the breast to the tip of the beak. Nor are the efforts of antiquity obscure in extending and twisting the figures of animals so that they might embrace as many notable stars as possible in a single configuration.

<summary>Kepler argues that the "New Star" (Nova) in Cygnus must truly be new, as it is too bright and well-positioned to have been overlooked by ancient astronomers like Ptolemy and Hipparchus. He counters the example of the star Alcor (the Rider), noting that while Alcor is hard to see near Mizar, the Nova is clearly visible and fits perfectly within the traditional figure of the Swan.</summary>

->O N T H E S T A R O F T H E S W A N. 157<-

Who then will believe that a star almost as bright as the breast itself, placed in such proximity and so conveniently (for it is, as I said, a little outside the line from the breast to the beak), would have been overlooked and excluded from the constellation <note>Referring to the traditional figures or "asterisms" used to map the stars</note> by the ancients?

   Again, Ptolemy, in 그 chapter where he describes the path of the Milky Way through the positions of the stars, places only one in the breast: but of this one <note>the new star</note> he clearly makes no mention, even though it falls within the Milky Way—and indeed in its most memorable part, where 그 circle in the constellation of the Swan <note>original: *Gallinæ*, literally "the Hen," a common historical name for Cygnus</note> splits into a fork and extends a branch toward the shoulder of Ophiuchus <note>The Serpent Bearer</note>. The weight of this argument will only truly appear once one has industriously read that chapter and compared it with a celestial globe. <margin>III. *Location in the Milky Way.*</margin>

   "From there," he says, *"understand that after the Eagle, the Milky Way tends toward the Swan, and the margin which turns toward the North and West ends at the bend, at the star which is in the southern shoulder of the Swan, and in that which is below it, in the same wing, and in the two above the southern foot. But the margin which looks toward the East and South is terminated by the star which is at the summit of the southern expansion of the feathers, and it intercepts two stars outside the form <note>Stars not considered part of the main constellation figure</note> which are under the same wing, and distant about two degrees from this margin. And these parts of the Milky Way are a little denser around the wing."*

   The following parts do indeed adhere to this zone, but they are exceedingly dense, as if starting from a unique origin. *"But they turn toward the extremes of the other zone, yet a gap is left between these and those. And from the South they reach that part of the zone of which we are now speaking, which is very thin at the point of contact: but the density, after the said gap, begins from the bright star in the tail of the Swan, and from the cloudy wrapping which is in the northern knee: then returning somewhat, as far as that which is in the southern knee, they extend this density, vanishing more and more, as far as Cepheus, etc."* <margin>* An error of the Basel edition <note>original: *των* for *τον*</note>, let it not disturb you. The construction, the translation, and the line above it <note>a reference to manuscript marking</note> refute it.</margin>

   And in the explanation of the other part of the zone, which makes the Milky Way double: *"From there,"* he says, *"namely from the shoulder of Ophiuchus, this zone, besides being thin or sparse, is also contracted into a narrow limit, near the parts preceding the star in the beak of the Swan, so that it presents the appearance of an interruption. The rest of its part from the beak to the star in the breast of the Swan is both more widely spread and quite dense. And that which is in the neck of the Swan is in the middle of the dense part: a certain small portion, though sparse, turns toward the North and the star of the Breast, as far as the star which is in the shoulder of the right wing, and to the two contiguous ones at the extremity of the right foot. From which place, as said before, there is a gap between this and the other zone, extending from the said stars of the Swan as far as the bright star in the Tail."*

<meta>This page continues Kepler’s argument that the "New Star" (the Supernova of 1604) is indeed a new phenomenon and not a pre-existing star previously overlooked, by citing the meticulousness of ancient and modern astronomers.</meta>

158 JOHANNES KEPLER

Look at Ptolemy’s incredible diligence, as if even then he had foreseen the usefulness of this point. For Ptolemy did not think it sufficient merely to record all the famous stars that fall within the Milky Way; he even proceeded with such diligence—by "most painstaking observation," <note>original Greek: περιεργωτάτη παρατηρήσει</note> as he himself says—that he described the borders of that milky circle by using stars either falling on the margin or standing a short distance away, indicating the measurement of the distance. If, then, this star <note>Kepler refers to the New Star (Nova) of 1604, which appeared near the edge of the Milky Way</note> had appeared then just as it does today in the Milky Way, and indeed very close to the margin, do you believe it possible that Ptolemy would have omitted it? Unless, perhaps, those authors described the various spaces of the Milky Way and the stars caught within it from a globe <note>Kepler suggests the possibility of someone copying from a physical celestial globe rather than looking at the actual heavens, which he quickly dismisses.</note> rather than from the sky itself—a possibility that these very words just cited prove to be impossible.

### IV.
<margin>The authority of Brahe.</margin>
Fourth, in our own time, Tycho Brahe <note>Tycho Brahe (1546–1601) was Kepler's mentor and the most accurate observational astronomer of the pre-telescopic era.</note> resumed that ancient and most necessary labor of Hipparchus—neglected now for so many centuries—more than twenty years ago, [re-measuring] all the stars from the ancient [catalog]...

# ON THE STAR IN THE SWAN. 159

...for the sake of such certainty, or for some other reason, he would take the distances of the surrounding bright stars—the Eagle <note>The constellation Aquila.</note>, the Lyre <note>The constellation Lyra.</note>, and the tail of the Swan—from the breast of the Swan. I have never encountered a mention of two stars there, nor of a "following star in the breast," nor of a "brighter star in the breast" of the Swan; everywhere, the name is simply "the one in the breast" <note>This refers to Gamma Cygni, traditionally the only bright star in the Swan's breast.</note>. Yet, when distinguishing between two stars in the tail of Capricorn and similar cases, he is accustomed to diligently use and emphasize a note regarding whether one has greater or lesser brightness, or whether it lies in the region of the North or South, or the East or the West. But if at that time, as is the case now, there had been twin stars in the breast of almost equal magnitude, would he not have confused himself by neglecting the distinction? Would not that very hesitation—as to which was the true breast—have invited him to consult Ptolemy’s table <note>The star catalog in Ptolemy's *Almagest*, which served as the standard reference for over a thousand years.</note> to inspect it?

Seventh, how often did it happen that, because the body of the Swan does not set in Denmark <margin>VII. The advantage of the constellation not setting.</margin>, he would observe the transit of these stars through the northern meridian and measure their altitudes to account for refraction? <note>Atmospheric refraction makes stars near the horizon appear higher than they are. Because Cygnus is circumpolar in Denmark (it never drops below the horizon), Tycho could observe it even at its lowest point to study this effect.</note>
Yet no position could be more convenient for noticing a "New" or superfluous star; especially if, like this star of ours, it shines with almost the same brightness as the neighbors used as reference points for observation.
With such a great opportunity of location, and such frequent and effortless viewing—since it sits low in the sky—given that Brahe’s followers record no superfluous star being seen, it is rightly believed that none existed then.

Come then, let us provide evidence for several of the previous arguments from the <margin>From Brahe's Observations.</margin> books of observations themselves, reviewing a few examples that occurred to me while browsing the *Protocols* <note>The daily logbooks of Tycho Brahe's observatory at Uraniborg.</note>. -># **ABOUT THE STAR OF CYGNUS.** 161<-

In this constellation, the Ancients <note>referring to Ptolemy and other classical astronomers</note> count 17 stars and 2 <term>unformed stars<note>Stars located near the main figure of a constellation but not considered part of its primary outline</note></term>.

Therefore, Tycho Brahe omitted stars 7, 8, 9, 11, and 17 on this occasion, because they were reaching the <term>meridian<note>The imaginary Great Circle passing through the celestial poles and the zenith; a star "reaches the meridian" when it is at its highest point in the sky</note></term> crowded closely together.

And star 17 is indeed a nebulous cluster <note>original Greek: συσροφή (systrophe) — a gathering or massing together</note>; it was therefore omitted this time due to the great deal of work required for the more visible stars.

Regarding stars 7, 8, and 9, the reason for their omission is likewise clear: they are the first to reach the meridian, and by the time this observation was taken, they—and the beak—had already passed the meridian. This is evident from the globe, where the bright star of the wing, which was the first of those observed this time, reaches its highest point after the beak and after those three stars which are assigned to the explanation of the Northern wing. For that reason, even for the star in the beak, which had already escaped the meridian, the observers took the <term>declination<note>The angular distance of a star north or south of the celestial equator</note></term> only.

The eleventh star, however, which is in the middle of the southern wing, is smaller than the other two in the wing; and since it reaches its highest point at nearly the same time as the tenth, it is located between the tenth and the stars of the foot. It is no wonder, then, that it was omitted this time.

But what of our star in the breast? Since it reaches its highest point before the ancient star of the breast, and is of almost equal magnitude to it; why was it left out then, when it approached the meridian at such an opportune time for observation?

In that very region, while the ancient star of the breast was at its highest point, they had enough leisure <margin>Note.</margin> to record a certain small unformed star, in addition to those the ancients had cataloged.

What then, you ask, if this is the very star we are disputing here? Not at all. For that star reached its highest point after the ancient Breast star, while our star reaches its highest point before it. For the <term>culmination<note>The point at which a celestial body reaches the meridian</note></term> of the Breast star is at 302° 2'. Our star's is at 300° 46'. It also differs in altitude. Our star would have been at an altitude of 70° 57', while this one is only at 65° 8'.

But perhaps this star "outside the form" is one of the two that the Ancients also counted as unformed? Not in the least. For you see those were observed last, after the twelfth star. Therefore, you can see with your own eyes that in this very spot <margin>Note.</margin> around the breast of the Swan, the observers sought more stars than are found in Ptolemy, and they found a certain small one; but they certainly did not find that extra star of the third magnitude around the breast at that time.

Let us proceed with the observations. On September 12 and 22, the tail of the Swan was observed from certain stars in Andromeda. On September 18, the beak from certain stars in Pegasus; on September 25, the bright star of the southern wing from stars in Pegasus. On October 10, the middle star of the neck (the third in number) from Lyra and Scheat in Pegasus.

Then, between the Northern star in the right foot of the Swan and Scheat in Pegasus, the distance was 24° 40'. And it is added: *This star appears to be unformed.* You see their care and

# **O N  T H E  S T A R  O F  C Y G N U S.**          **163**

Likewise, four stars are mentioned between the mouth and the foot of Pegasus, where the "middle of the wing" of Cygnus is noted. They carelessly neglected only this one in the breast of Cygnus. <note>The author is pointing out an inconsistency: astronomers recorded minor stars in Pegasus but supposedly "missed" a bright star in the Swan's breast.</note> I will say nothing of many others added beyond the usual count, which are further removed from Cygnus.

In the year 1597, they say sixty stars were lacking to complete a full thousand in the catalog. To find them, they gathered four in a straight line between Capella and the Pole Star; four in a straight line between the side of Perseus and the Pole Star; fourteen between Capella and the Pole Star at the sides, divided into two trapeziums and two triangles; three near the end of the tail of the Great Bear <note>Ursa Major</note> at the side; nine in a winding line under the unformed stars between the tails of the Bear and the Lion; and six on either side of the Pole Star. Behold this scarcity, which did not refrain from such lowly stars! It is a wonder, then, if a diligence so "starved" for stars <note>original: "cura... famelica." Tycho Brahe was determined to reach a round number of 1,000 stars for his Great Star Catalog and was searching for any tiny star to fill the list.</note> could not catch the scent of such a large one in the breast of Cygnus.

And in this final part of the observations, our eighth argument is born.     **VIII.**
For in the year 1597, on October 27th, the times were being noted     <margin>By using vertical alignments for timekeeping.</margin>
by the stars of Cygnus, as they coincided with the Pole Star on the same vertical line. This method was prompted by the absence of instruments, for at that time Brahe had recently departed from Denmark. <note>Tycho Brahe left his island observatory of Hven in 1597 after falling out of favor with the Danish king.</note> There was, therefore, no mention of a second star in the breast. And on the 30th of October, at about 5:45, they say: *Nothing could be taken on the plumb-line because of the clouds.* You see how diligently they were intent on the opportunity. Immediately, when their clock indicated the hour of 6:21, the breast [of the Swan] aligned; and at 6:15, the tail aligned. If this star of ours had existed then, it would have been on this vertical line just a little before the breast.     <margin>Note.</margin>

Ninth, let us return from the end to the beginning of the observations.     **IX.**
For of all the arguments that can be prepared from them, this seems to me     <margin>By the tail of the Comet.</margin>
the most manifest: that in the description of the Comet of the year 1580, on November 25th or December 2nd or 9th, these things are noted:

*On Friday, around 6 o'clock in the evening, while I was in a small boat between Scania and Hven <note>Scania is the southern tip of Sweden; Hven was the island where Tycho’s observatories were located.</note>, with the stars already shining to some extent after Sunset: I saw the Comet still appearing quite brightly and shining, with a magnitude like a star of the second magnitude, though the tail was hardly visible. However, it seemed as if certain longer rays were stretched out toward the Zenith of the head, as far as the eye could see. The Comet itself was near the Head of Hercules, lower than it, toward the West and the North; and it was distant from that star in the Head of Hercules <note>The star Rasalhague.</note> by three diameters of the Sun at most, as far as the eye could see—that is, by nearly one and a half degrees. For this distance was notably smaller than the distance between the two nearer stars in the body of the Vulture <note>The "Vulture" was an old name for the constellation Lyra or sometimes Aquila; here it likely refers to Lyra.</note>.* But why did he not say that this distance was     <margin>Note.</margin>
nearly equal to the space between the two neighboring stars in the body of the Swan, if there were two then? For both constellations are equally near to Hercules.

**X 2**          <margin>He continues.</margin>

<summary>The author argues that the "new star" in Cygnus (P Cygni) could not have existed in the late 16th century, because Tycho Brahe failed to record it even while desperately searching for minor stars to complete his 1,000-star catalog and while using Cygnus for timekeeping.</summary>

He continues: It seemed that that line, passing through the Comet into the nearest point mentioned, namely into the head of Hercules, extended (where it was projected) into the one which is in the middle of the body of the Swan <note>The constellation Cygnus</note>, as far as the eye could see. If there had been two stars there at the time: he would have distinguished them, because he adds, *as far as the eye could see.* Thus far the quote. A picture is also added, where the star in the middle of the body is depicted alone: But from this it appears that he took the left shoulder of Ophiuchus <note>The Serpent-Bearer constellation</note> for the head of Hercules. For regarding the star to which he gave the name "Head of Hercules," this line from the breast of the Swan into itself separates the head of Ophiuchus to one side by a little, and the tail of the Swan to the other.

<margin>X. Witnesses of the New Star.</margin>
Tenth, then, let the authority of those who speak and live be added to these silent witnesses; and that authority is all the more powerful because, as was said from the beginning, they have no one who can contradict them from their own memory.

<margin>Jansonius</margin>
The first is Willem Janszoon <note>original: "Gulielmus Janſonius," known today as Willem Blaeu, a famous Dutch cartographer</note>, who professes that this new star was first seen by him in the year 1600, according to an inscription made on a celestial globe published in the year 1601. There is no reason for me to doubt the integrity of this man; his noble Geographic and Astronomic occupations bear witness to it. This kind of study is so constituted that it does not tolerate a lover unless he is faithful and sincere. That he has knowledge of the stars is sufficiently proved by the fact that he was a disciple of Tycho Brahe and a companion in his observations for some time. After I saw the origin of this rumor trace back to him, I did not stop—especially with the Emperor's <note>Emperor Rudolph II</note> approval—from writing to him to ask about the circumstances in which he first happened upon the sight and knowledge of this New star. However, what happened to my letters, I do not know; I have certainly received no response from him so far.

<margin>Bürgi</margin>
The other witness whom I know is Jost Bürgi <note>original: "Juſtus Byrgius"</note>, Clockmaker to His Sacred Imperial Majesty <term>Automato-pœus: a maker of self-moving machines or clocks</term>; who, although he is unskilled in languages <note>meaning Bürgi did not know Latin, the standard language of science at the time</note>, nevertheless easily surpasses many professors in the knowledge and contemplation of mathematical things. He possesses practical skill so uniquely that a later age will have a leader to celebrate in this field, no less than Dürer in painting, whose fame grows in secret, like a tree through the ages. But not to wander too far from the point: our friend Bürgi did not actually notice this star by himself at the start. However, when it was written from Holland by the architect of Prince Maurice to the Count of Lippe, and from him to Bürgi—in the same year that Blaeu’s globe came out—that a New star was seen in the Swan: although Bürgi (being a man of self-restraint and most distant from vanity) doubted the promptness of his own memory, there was a place where he could turn for confirmation. Long ago, he was in the service of the most Illustrious Landgrave of Hesse, William <note>William IV, a great patron of astronomy</note>, whose study and diligence in the science of the stars was greater than one might expect in a Prince...

<summary>Kepler corrects a previous observer's misidentification of stars in Cygnus and Hercules before introducing credible contemporary witnesses—Willem Blaeu and Jost Bürgi—who documented the New Star of 1600.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of contemporary astronomers' observations of the "New Star" in the constellation Cygnus (the Swan), focusing on the testimony of the instrument maker Joost Bürgi and the uranographer Johann Bayer.</meta>

->**ON THE STAR OF CYGNUS. 165**<-

...often you might search for, and the most brilliant discoveries, stimulated Tycho Brahe <note>Tycho Brahe (1546–1601) was the preeminent observational astronomer of the era, known for his precision and his massive star catalog.</note> to emulation, as can be seen everywhere in that man's works, especially in his book of *Letters*.) The man I am speaking of, Bürgi <note>Joost Bürgi (1552–1632), a Swiss clockmaker and mathematician who served the Landgrave of Hesse-Kassel and later Emperor Rudolf II.</note>, when preparing a celestial automaton <note>A complex mechanical device, like a planetarium or astronomical clock, that mimics the movements of the heavens.</note>, had added a celestial globe made of silver. This was at the time when Rothmann, the Landgrave’s astronomer, was devoting his greatest efforts to the fixed stars. Since many errors were being discovered in the tables <note>original: "abaco"</note> of Ptolemy, and many somewhat brighter stars had been omitted by Ptolemy outside the traditional figures of the constellations, it pleased the Prince <note>Landgrave William IV of Hesse-Kassel.</note> that whatever the mechanic was to engrave should be immediately compared with the sky itself; this was diligently performed by Bürgi. That completed globe, truly a royal gift, was sent by Landgrave William to Emperor Rudolf, and it remains to this day among His Majesty’s treasures. Another similar globe was then under Bürgi’s hands, and was given a little later by Landgrave Maurice to Archduke Maximilian while he was passing through Kassel.

Therefore Bürgi, who did not find this star in the Swan—now celebrated as a *New Star*—in his own memory, appealed most confidently to this globe of his. Indeed, it was not found in the old engraving, of whose accuracy he was so certain; he marked it in its proper place with ink. It happened shortly thereafter that, having obtained a leave of absence from the Landgrave, he went over to the Emperor's service, and here he devoted his care to the repair of many wonderful works, including that earlier globe he had made. Therefore, even this globe offered nothing to be seen in the breast of the Swan except the ancient Ptolemaic stars. Thus, Bürgi pronounces most confidently concerning the novelty of this star.

Shall I also add a third witness, Johann Bayer? <margin>**Bayer.**</margin> I shall certainly do so. For although I included him above among those who *hesitate* <note>original Greek: ἐπέχουσι (*epechousi*), meaning those who withhold judgment or remain skeptical.</note>, he himself provided the reason in his *Uranometria* <note>Bayer’s *Uranometria* (1603) was the first atlas to cover the entire celestial sphere.</note>. He was much more open in letters written to a certain great man, copies of which were made available to me without any conditions; I am not afraid that the author will be angry with me. For they contain no secrets, nothing that would damage his reputation, and nothing that is not in the public interest to know. Add to this that I can in some way claim those letters for myself, as I provided the occasion for them. At the beginning of the year 1602, the noble Master Franz Gansneb, called Tengnagel—the son-in-law of Tycho Brahe and now a Councilor of Appeals to His Imperial Majesty—had come to Bürgi in Kassel, bringing with him Johann Eriksen, who was formerly an astronomical assistant to Brahe. Having been informed about the star by Bürgi, and while they were traveling to East Friesland, they reported the matter to David Fabricius. Fabricius, by way of **X 3 Ham-** <note>The text cuts off here, likely continuing with "Hamburg."</note>

<summary>This page describes how the famous instrument maker Joost Bürgi verified the "New Star" in Cygnus by comparing his meticulously engraved celestial globes against the sky, finding no such star in older records. It also introduces the testimony of Johann Bayer and describes the network of astronomers, including Tycho Brahe’s associates, who communicated these findings across Europe.</summary>

<keywords>Joost Bürgi, Tycho Brahe, Cygnus, New Star, celestial globe, Ptolemy, Johann Bayer, Uranometria, Landgrave of Hesse-Kassel, Emperor Rudolf II, David Fabricius</keywords>

--- Page 218 ---
166 &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;JOHANNES KEPLER

Someone from Hamburg wrote about these same matters to Jæstelius, the Professor of Mathematics at Wittenberg; Jæstelius wrote to me in Prague; I wrote to Bavaria. Thus, once the rumor was shared in Munich and then Augsburg, Johann Bayer was also asked for his opinion. They knew he had his *Uranometria* <term>Uranometria: One of the first great star atlases, published by Johann Bayer in 1603</term>—that most eagle-eyed work of cataloging the stars—underway at that time; it was published immediately the following year, 1603. He responded in this manner: *Regarding the new star of the Hen or Swan <note>original: "De novo Gallinae seu Cygni astro"</note>, etc. Having been informed of it early on, I was hesitant to approach the matter, both because of the hostility that meets novelty and the absence of my own descriptions. I also recalled Pliny, who requires a man engaged in studies to be less bold and more cautious. On the seventh of June and several days following, I examined my sketch <note>original: "σχεδιογραφίαν" (skediographia)</note> of this constellation (which I had indeed prepared long ago, and as I say in the Uranographia, with long labor). Right from the start, I noticed a certain strange little star that I suspected, and I remained doubtful about it right up until the summer solstice. Therefore, with as much attention as I could muster, I perfectly traced the position of the Swan, or the Bird, and the placement of its stars according to the classical Doctors of Astronomy, adding my own (I believe, older) observations as well. I found (by making a comparison of the sky with this drawing) the usual 17 stars, along with two unformed stars <note>Stars near a constellation that were not part of the traditional figure were called "unformed"</note>. Besides these, I discovered another star at the end of the neck and the beginning of the Breast, which was no longer merely suspicious to me, but truly a star never seen before until now, of about the third magnitude, etc.* So ends the letter. And who should be believed, if not the craftsman concerning his own art, and the scholar concerning his own occupation? For it is clear from the work itself—the vast preparation for the *Uranometria* and the expensive copper plates—that this was indeed that man's occupation at the time, and for more than a single year: to transfer the celestial patterns to paper, to not pass over those omitted by Ptolemy, and thus to correct the charts of Piccolomini.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;However, as to why he became more cautious in the *Uranometria*, appealing to the silent consensus of others rather than to his own experience: I believe he was moved by the example of the rest, and especially by my own. For shortly after the rumor had spread, I had written those things mentioned at the beginning of this Narrative and shared them in Bavaria. The numbers which he transferred from my writing into the *Uranometria* prove that he read them. Having confessed at the start of that writing that my own memory had failed me, I made this conclusion:

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;*Whatever this star may be, whether old or new: it is established by geometric reasoning that it is certainly elevated at the highest level among the fixed stars themselves. Once this is understood, when one considers that so many months have now passed during which it is always seen at the same magnitude—never equaling its neighbor in the breast, never...*

<summary>Kepler describes the chain of correspondence that led to Johann Bayer confirming the discovery of a new star in the constellation Cygnus. Bayer initially hesitated but confirmed the star's existence by comparing the sky to his detailed, pre-existing star charts.</summary>
<keywords>Johann Bayer, Uranometria, Cygnus, P Cygni, new star, astronomical correspondence, star charts, Ptolemy, Piccolomini</keywords>

-># ON THE STAR IN THE SWAN. 167<-

...faded to the smallness of that star which is in the beak <note>original: "in rostro"; this refers to the star Albireo, which marks the head/beak of the Swan constellation</note>, with a color that is also steady and pale. Indeed, it is no wonder if the very duration of its appearance—even though it is very strongly confirmed through the arguments presented above—nevertheless keeps one anxious as to what will become of it. I think, therefore, that Bayer <note>Johann Bayer (1572–1625), the German lawyer and celestial cartographer famous for the star atlas *Uranometria*</note> was somewhat frightened by this hesitation of mine; having been made doubtful of his own experience (as commonly happens when many people confidently object), he stopped short of bringing this star forward as testimony. Even though I have presented her <note>In Latin, "stella" (star) is a feminine noun; the author personifies the star here</note> here "stripped bare," she is not alone, but supported by a proper band of many witnesses, so that she can easily withstand any assault <note>The author is defending the reality of the discovery against skeptics who might think the star was just an optical illusion or a temporary comet.</note>.

I believe that the **Novelty** <term>Nova: a "new" star that suddenly increases in brightness, making it appear as if it has just been born in the heavens</term> of this star has been sufficiently attested. For if these proofs are not enough for someone, it is likely that he would not believe even if entire nations affirmed it. It remains for me to review what I have observed regarding this star with the aforementioned **sextant** <term>Sextant: a high-precision instrument used to measure the angular distance between two stars</term>.

In the year 1602, on the 18/28 day <note>This notation indicates the date in both the Old Style (Julian) and New Style (Gregorian) calendars, which were both in use across Europe at this time.</note>

<meta>Continuing from the previous discussion of the celestial coordinates and observations of the new star in the constellation Cygnus, first observed in 1600.</meta>

168 JOHANNES KEPLER ON THE STAR IN CYGNUS

<margin>The appearance of the Swan after the arrival of the New Star. **N** denotes the New Star.</margin>

From these distances, the followers of Brahe <note>Tycho Brahe was a Danish nobleman and astronomer whose precise data Kepler inherited; "Braheans" refers to his assistants or those using his methods.</note>, having considered everything, established the location as 16° 18' of Aquarius, with a latitude of 55° 30' North. From this, the Right Ascension <term>Right Ascension: the celestial equivalent of longitude, measuring position along the celestial equator</term> of the New Star is found to be 300° 46', and its declination <term>Declination: the celestial equivalent of latitude, measuring how far north or south an object is from the celestial equator</term> is 36° 52' North. It therefore culminates <note>reaches its highest point in the sky</note> with 28° 37' of Capricorn. In the Andalusian part of Spain, in Sicily, the Peloponnese, Ionia <note>the western coast of modern-day Turkey</note>, Cilicia, Syria, and other places on Earth situated under this same parallel, it passes through the Zenith <note>the point directly overhead</note> daily. However, for those at a polar altitude of 53° 8', it brushes the northern horizon; such as in England, Holland, Brunswick, the March <note>the Margraviate of Brandenburg</note>, Livonia, and Muscovy <note>Russia</note>. Further toward the North, it does not set <note>it becomes circumpolar, meaning it stays above the horizon at all times</note>.

[An illustration of the constellation Cygnus depicted as a swan flying downwards. Several stars are marked on its body and wings. One star on the neck is labeled with the letter 'N'. The swan is positioned between two stylized clouds.]

*YOU HAVE HERE, Illustrious and Noble BARON, a short account written by your client, relying on the use of your instruments, promised to you three years ago, and therefore yours on many accounts. You shall read it in such a way that, with the duration of this New Star placed before your sight, you may either maintain that nothing similar has ever existed, and from that deliberate with yourself on its most grave significance and the intent of Him who created it; or, if you prefer, that many stars of this kind are born over the course of centuries. From this, you may then conjecture in what great ignorance of Nature the human race is held; for this star, now six years old, creates such astonishment and such admiration that you will find it very difficult to find anyone who believes the astronomers regarding its novelty.* <note>In Kepler’s time, most people believed the heavens were perfect and unchanging; the appearance of a "new" star was a shocking challenge to that worldview.</note> *For the rest, I reverently pray your Illustrious Nobility to accept my goodwill rather than the slightness of the work, and to keep me further recommended to you.*

->THE STAR IN CYGNUS.<-

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "New Star" (the supernova of 1604) observed by Johannes Kepler. Having established its physical location and nature in previous chapters, he now shifts to its potential astrological and physical effects on Earth.</meta>

170

[A wide decorative woodcut border with floral and scrollwork patterns]

*On the natural effects of this new star in sub-
lunary <note>In the Aristotelian worldview, the "sublunary" region is the realm below the Moon, including the Earth and its atmosphere, where things are subject to change and decay.</note> nature.*

CHAPTER XXVIII.

[Large decorative initial S] If unravelling the meanings of the star were the primary aim of this booklet, I might rightly say that everything discussed, concluded, investigated, and doubted so far is directed toward this art of divination <term>Mantice: from the Greek "mantike," meaning the art of prophecy or divination</term>. For different circumstances of a star's rising carry different meanings, and the methods of divination are drawn from the conditions of its very essence. Therefore, he who has explained nothing about those conditions must necessarily wander like a stranger when it comes to the star's meanings.

In consideration of this, if I were to refrain entirely from any explanation of its meanings, I would have plenty of excuses. For I have shown thus far that I hesitate in explaining the causes of the star and its coincidence with the "Great Conjunction" <note>A rare alignment of Jupiter and Saturn, which occurred around the same time as the supernova's appearance</note>; indeed, I have been able to pronounce nothing except with a stumbling tongue. Therefore, a fear of the same uncertainty grips me even in unfolding its meanings. This uncertainty is all the more notable because the common crowd of men craves knowledge of specific future events more greedily than they crave philosophy. 

However, because every description of this kind is thought to be imperfect if it does not also deal with meanings, the following approach can be taken: first, I shall explain those things which are connected by a natural bond to the new star, such that the star was either the cause or the sign of them. Second, I will address those things which can be said with probability through the art of conjecture—validated by other examples—beyond the appearance of a natural connection, and assuming the providence of an intelligent Nature. Meanwhile, I shall also examine certain obvious things, whether they are future events that astrologers have predicted or things that time itself has brought forth, checking them against the standard of truth to see if they were legitimately predicted and if they occurred because of the star's rising.

<margin>
*How*
*the heavens*
*influence*
*sublunary*
*things.*
</margin>

Therefore, the natural meaning of this star, if there is any, must be explained in the same manner I used above in chapters 8 and 9 to demonstrate the efficacy of "aspects" <note>Aspects are the specific angles planets make with each other, believed by astronomers of this era to exert physical or harmonic influence</note>. For every form of celestial influence is established not in the action of the heavens (except for that of light for the purpose of heating—in which respect this star, as long as it shone, was perhaps as powerful as two or three fixed stars <note>Stars that do not move relative to each other, unlike planets</note> at the beginning, and less so later; in quality, however, it was likened by its color to the "Little Dog" <note>original: "Caniculæ", referring to the star Sirius</note>, or the shoulder of Orion <note>The star Betelgeuse</note>, or Arcturus, or among the planets to Jupiter and Mars): but in the recep-

--- Page 223 ---
<meta>This page continues Johannes Kepler’s discussion on the astrological and physical influence of the "New Star" of 1604 (Supernova 1604) in the constellation Ophiuchus (the Serpent Bearer). Kepler is moving from mathematical observation to a philosophical explanation of how the heavens interact with the Earthly sphere.</meta>

**On the New Star in the Serpent Bearer.** **171**

<margin>The relationship of the heavens with sublunary things:</margin> through reception, and thus in the "passion" <note>In this context, "passion" refers to being acted upon or receiving an impression, rather than an emotion.</note> of sublunary nature—which is either sensitive itself or very similar to sensitivity. Indeed, a relationship exists between the souls and faculties of sublunary <term>sublunary: the region of the universe below the moon, specifically Earth and its atmosphere, which was thought to be subject to change unlike the "perfect" heavens.</term> things and the nature of the heavens. This relationship is threefold: of heat, of motion, and of reason. 

First, the light of the heavens is the producer of heat; and the souls and faculties of <margin>Of Heat.</margin> vegetative things each excite their own heat within the body they inhabit, and they protect that heat according to their strength. <margin>Of Motion.</margin> Likewise, the stars are endowed with faculties that move them; these are for the most part "material," or inseparable from the body—both innate and acquired (such as the magnetic force we see here on Earth)—but they are also "animal" <note>Kepler uses "animal" in the classical sense of "anima" (soul), meaning a self-moving or life-giving force.</note> in nature. Here on Earth, the souls and faculties of vegetative things are similarly present in their bodies for the sake of life and motion. Finally, <margin>Of Reason.</margin> just as those motive faculties of the stars are in a way participants in mind—so that they might "understand," imagine, and strive toward their path, as it were; not by reasoning as we humans do, but by an innate power instilled in them at the first creation—so too do the animal faculties of natural things possess a certain understanding of their own purpose (though without reasoning) toward which they direct all their actions. Since this relationship is so great, there is no further reason to wonder at that *sympathy* <note>original: "συμπάθειαν" (*sympatheian*), meaning a mutual influence or "feeling together" between the stars and Earth.</note> between the motions of the heavens and animal faculties, which I have confirmed above with manifest experiments in chapters 8 and 10. Therefore, let those things which were specifically proven there now be accepted; and let a general pronouncement finally be made through probable reason, as if collected from induction:

*That nothing exists or happens in the visible heaven whose sense does not reach the Earth by some hidden reason, extending into all the faculties of natural things; and that these animal faculties here on Earth are affected in the same way the heaven itself is affected. Thus it happens that whenever something new and unusual appears in the sky, sublunary Nature (over which some Animal faculty presides) in a certain way shudders. For I shall openly take refuge with the Poets and my own first invocation* <note>original: "προσφώνησιν" (*prosphōnesin*), an address or dedication.</note> *which I used for this star; because among those who hold power in Philosophy today, I despair of gaining a hearing for a new doctrine. Therefore, "Away, O ye profane! God, behold God!"* <note>original: "procul ô prophani, Deus, en Deus," a reference to Virgil’s Aeneid, signaling that he is speaking of something sacred or divinely inspired.</note>

*A creeping star, a messenger of the wounded Divine Mind,*
*Which burns like a glowing gemstone in a blazing fire!*
*How shall I describe you? From what origin shall I, the learned, compose*
*Your divine beginnings? For not even at that time when the mighty*
*Creator scattered the stars across the ethereal field*
*Did He give you a number; you were born of a hidden effort,*
*While the ancient senate of the stars looked on in wonder.*
*The poles stood stunned; and terrified by the light of the sudden*
*Lightning-flash, nature feared the ethereal tumult.*

**Y 2 This**

->172 JOHANNES KEPLER<-

<margin>Source of the effects of eclipses.</margin>

This same principle seems to me the basis for asserting the power of eclipses <note>literally "failings of light"</note> as well. For it is **unharmonious** <note>original Greek: *ἀνάρμοστον* — referring to a lack of musical or mathematical proportion</note> (qualitatively, not geometrically or quantitatively) and in some way contrary to sublunary nature <note>The "sublunary" realm refers to the Earthly sphere below the moon, subject to change and decay, unlike the eternal heavens.</note> for the moon to be suddenly extinguished, or for the sun's light to be turned away from the measured half of the terrestrial globe, which is ordinarily and perpetually in the sun's light. Such a sight strikes the animal nature <note>Kepler posits that the Earth and living things have a "soul" or faculty that reacts to celestial light.</note>, which is kin to light, causing it to conceive a long-lasting and destructive affliction. Therefore, let this same principle be the basis for asserting the power of these new phenomena as well.

<margin>How the effects of three natural and supernatural heavenly bodies are mixed together.</margin>

Therefore, through a doubling; since even the great conjunction <note>The "Great Conjunction" refers to the rare alignment of the outer planets, Jupiter and Saturn; here, Mars was also involved in the same area of the sky.</note> of the three superior planets added massive <note>Kepler uses "decumanos," literally "of the tenth wave," a Roman term for something exceptionally large.</note> stimuli to the sublunary natures to move themselves: and since this new star <note>The Supernova of 1604, often called Kepler's Star.</note> fell into the place and time of the great conjunction: it follows that both by its conjunction with each of the joined planets, and by its very novelty considered in itself, it added immense forces to the ordinary disturbance, and caused a most memorable **astonishment** <note>original Greek: *ἔκπληξιν*</note>. Although a doubt is necessary: whether we should argue **by addition** <note>original Greek: *κατὰ πρόσθεσιν*</note> or **by subtraction** <note>original Greek: *κατ’ ἀφαίρεσιν*</note>: that is, whether nature, having turned toward the new star, turned itself away somewhat from the great conjunction; so that the imagination of a more unusual thing overwhelmed and obliterated the prior, more accustomed thing: or whether it felt the joined forces of both. Nor could one doubt this any less: whether the disturbance the star caused was more like joy or sadness; whether it hindered Nature in its work, or incited and helped it. Therefore, which of these seems more likely to me, I shall explain by a review of sublunary Natures.

<margin>The star's effect on weather.</margin>

The first and most conspicuous is that **animal faculty** <note>Kepler believed the Earth was a living being with a soul-like faculty that responded to planetary alignments by creating weather.</note> which, presiding over the globe of the Earth, produces all kinds of **meteors** <note>In the 17th century, "meteors" referred to any atmospheric phenomena, including rain, wind, and snow.</note>. And since the entire winter that occurred between the years 1604 and 1605 was exceedingly humid, especially on those days when the planets had met with the new fixed star or were in configuration with it: and indeed it was a warm winter on this side of the Alps in Upper and Lower Germany, but the coldest of all within human memory across the Alps in Italy and in the Spains: I therefore argue from the effect that the forces of each celestial cause were applied in turn.

<margin>Source of the winter's diversity in different regions.</margin>

I attribute the already mentioned disparity of the winter to the Alps: so that if through this winter the Alps were in a state of perpetual exhalation, the matter of the winds, struck back by the cold of the air, was spread in a circle—part toward the north, part toward the south. That which went south over Italy, coming from a northern region, was naturally cold; but that which was carried north over Germany, coming from the South, retained the warm nature of that region, by no great miracle. But what if someone [considers] this whole...

<summary>Kepler discusses how the 1604 New Star and the Great Conjunction influenced the Earth's "animal faculty," leading to extreme and varied weather patterns across Europe. He compares these effects to the disharmony caused by eclipses, which disturb the natural balance of light.</summary>

### ON THE NEW STAR IN THE SERPENT. 173

...place this tract or climate where the warmth prevailed within the exhalation <note>Kepler uses the term "expiratione," viewing the Earth as a living body breathing out vapors</note> itself: while Italy and that other climate remained outside the exhalation, so that the winds were broad there, which among us breathed their last. For whoever says this will rely on this likelihood: that every exhalation is warm when it first emerges, but when it is carried afar, stirred by its own momentum, it grows colder and colder.

Something similar must be imagined regarding this very summer, which was excessively humid through Germany and the Gallic lands <note>modern-day France</note>, and therefore plague-ridden; yet across the Pyrenees in the Spanish lands, it was dry and sterile. Why is it a wonder if nations differ in their pursuits, when such contrary things befall them from the heavens? Nor was the winter notable only for its moisture, but much more for the nocturnal fires of the sky, very frequent in their blood-red appearance, and for the "chasms" or openings <note>likely referring to the Aurora Borealis or similar atmospheric light displays</note>; and, what is equally prodigious for winter, rainbows appearing in the regions of both the Sun and the Moon: add to this the somewhat more frequent sun-dogs, moon-dogs <note>original: "parelia, paraselenas" — optical phenomena caused by ice crystals in the atmosphere</note>, and halos.

Therefore, it is likely that this winter humidity and the frequency of celestial wonders were the effect of the new star: which we shall manage in such a way as to divide them with good reason between the new star and the Great Conjunction <note>The rare alignment of Jupiter and Saturn that occurred in 1604, which Kepler believed had significant astrological and physical effects</note>.

In the globe of the Earth, there is a twin faculty (insofar as it concerns <term>meteors</term> <note>In the 17th century, "meteorology" referred to all atmospheric phenomena, including rain, wind, and light, not just falling rocks from space</note>): one for <margin>Foundations of meteorology, and a description of the faculties of the Earth's "viscera" <note>internal organs</note>.</margin> attracting sea waters into hidden seats of concoction <note>A process of heating or ripening within the Earth</note>, the other for expelling concocted vapors through a kind of sweat, as it were; or, if you prefer, compare this expulsion to the seminal excretion of living creatures. That one of these faculties—the expulsive one—is stimulated by the harmonic aspects of the planets is most certain: by this stimulation it is brought about that the expulsion is indeed continuous (in the generation of rivers) but not perpetually uniform; rather, its vehemence is interrupted by many intervals, according as the aspects happen in their astronomical order. And by Hercules, one might not absurdly <margin>The expulsive faculty.</margin> even add a certain pleasure of its own to this excretion: so many things about the Earth correspond with living animals. For <margin>Whether with pleasure?</margin> Physicians teach that whenever the veins swell with genital fluid, an excretion easily occurs even during sleep when a sweet image is presented, even with no contact occurring. What indeed of this matter...

<summary>Kepler discusses the unusual weather and atmospheric phenomena of 1604-1605, attributing them to the influence of the New Star and the Great Conjunction. He proposes an organic theory of the Earth, suggesting it "exhales" vapors in response to planetary alignments much like a living creature.</summary>

<keywords>Johannes Kepler, Supernova 1604, Great Conjunction, meteorology, Aurora Borealis, sun dogs, planetary aspects, Earth as a living organism</keywords>

174 [tab] JOHANNES KEPLER

[centered] *Into the lap of his joyful spouse he descends, and great,*
[centered] *Mixed with her great body, he nourishes all offspring.*
<note>Kepler is quoting Virgil’s *Georgics* (Book 2, 325–327), a classic poetic description of the sky fertilizing the earth with rain.</note>

Indeed, that poet, luxuriating in fiction, attributes rains to the Sky as seed which it pours into the earth. I, however, attribute nothing to the sky except that "Venereal appearance" <note>By "Venereal," Kepler refers to the astrological "aspects" or angles between planets that stimulate the Earth's natural functions, much like a biological urge.</note> (I mean the astrological aspects) which provokes the Earth to pour forth this seed from herself—provided her veins are swollen with a bounty of humors. Such is the expulsive faculty of the Earth.

<margin>The attractive faculty</margin>
It is indeed obscure at what intervals the Earth’s **attractive faculty** operates. In living creatures, there are various intervals for that faculty which digests the genital humor within and inserts it into the veins: youth, spring, a stomach full of food, and a full-blooded body promote it; while old age, winter, hunger, and disease hinder it. Yet these intervals cannot be compared with those of excretion. For the latter are sudden, headlong, and brief; while the former are slow, more sluggish, and in a way more continuous. Let philosophers investigate what similar thing may be found in the globe of the Earth. This much is certain: sometimes the Earth lacks what it should excrete, even if it is most fervently stirred by the astrological aspects. For then, as experience teaches, instead of rains, there usually arise certain burning, unpleasant, sulfurous, and biting vapors; whence come droughts, parched conditions, and <term>auchmoi</term><note>original: αὐχμοί. A Greek term for parched, dry heat or severe drought conditions.</note>. Compare this to the sterile, fruitless, and labored couplings of animals; so it is, by Hercules, for sterility follows these affections of the Earth. And Aristotle celebrates the effects of Comets in this regard, saying they signify sterilities and parches, a view supported by experience.

<margin>From where it is stimulated.</margin>
But if Comets signify as causes, since they are for the most part above the moon; they will not be causes in any other way than what has been said and practiced until now: namely, as objects that move the senses. Therefore, it will be common to Comets and Eclipses of the Sun to terrify and dismay the Earth’s vegetative faculty, and thus to hinder its work, which is—among other things—to attract nourishment and material for rains and rivers, and, if you please, even to digest it.

Is it then the case that, except when there are Comets and Eclipses, the work of attraction and digestion of humors in the Earth boils continuously? Or are there also stimulating causes here, by which this fervor is sometimes rendered sharper? I do not know; except that it is already well enough celebrated among astrologers that fertile years follow <term>Great Conjunctions</term><note>The rare alignment of the outer planets, Jupiter and Saturn, which occurred in 1603.</note>. If this is true, then the conjunction of the higher planets will stimulate the Earth to attract sea waters and will aid its digestion.

<margin>The natural effect of a Great Conjunction.</margin>
Two pieces of evidence from current events support this teaching: first, that fertile years, by the grace of God, followed the Great Conjunction of December 1603; and indeed the low price of wine near the Neckar River was almost the same as in the years 1584 and 1585 after that conjunction in Pisces—except for how much money itself becomes cheaper every day. Second, because both the previous winter after the autumn of 1603, and this very one of which we have said so much, shows the Earth with humors...

<summary>In this passage, Kepler argues that the Earth is a living soul that reacts to planetary alignments (aspects) much like an animal. He explains that while the sky provides the stimulus, the Earth itself produces rain and weather through its own "digestive" and "expulsive" faculties, and notes that major events like the Great Conjunction of 1603 directly correlate with earthly fertility and harvests.</summary>

<note>original: "bus fuisse turgentiffimam"</note> ...[the Earth] had been most swollen; therefore, it had previously drawn in so much moisture. Indeed, there was also a **Great Conjunction** <note>The meeting of Jupiter and Saturn in the same zodiacal sign, which occurred in 1603, just before the supernova appeared.</note> shortly before. Thus, it can be believed that this conjunction served as a stimulus for the Earth to draw in that moisture. Is it the same stimulus for drawing in as it is for excreting? I do not know, except that even in a living creature, the mere sight of a related creature is believed both to generate genital humor and occasionally to discharge it. If this comparison is allowed, we shall also apply this distinction: that just as in living beings, the "cooking" <note>Kepler uses the term 'coctio' (concoction), an old medical term for the body processing fluids.</note> of humor takes a span of time, while the excretion is momentary; so too in the Earth, the cause stimulating it to attract, draw in, and "cook" is more prolonged than the cause stimulating it to excrete. And since the definition of an *aspect* <note>In astrology/astronomy, an 'aspect' is a specific angular relationship between planets.</note> depends on extreme precision, which is momentary, the **conjunction** or proximity of Saturn and Jupiter will be called a stimulus for the Earth to attract—not merely as an aspect, but in a more general sense due to the very proximity of their lights—so that while the cause lasts a long time, the effect can also be more long-lasting. Since the **New Star** arose at that very time when the hidden depths of the earth were beginning to overflow in this way, it added to this stimulus for attraction once it joined the planets; it continued that attraction so that the Earth’s moisture could not be quickly exhausted. Meanwhile, whenever it formed a configuration with the planets, it gave a stimulus to the "expulsive faculty" <note>The natural power of an organism to rid itself of waste or processed fluids.</note>; and thus, it can be believed to have worked in both ways.

And so much for that general faculty of the globe of the Earth and its agitation by the new star. Let us speak of the others; and having set aside plants and other living creatures whose workings are more obscure, let us come to the prince of living things, **Man**.

<margin>
On the natural effects of the new star on Man.
</margin>

Now, there is a twofold consideration of Man: the first is as a **rational creature**; the second is as one endowed with that **natural faculty** which he shares with the globe of the Earth itself—the ability to perceive celestial things without the need for logical discourse, without formal schooling, without progress, and even without self-awareness. For it was said above in Chapter 10 that this is common to the **Stag**, to **Actaeon** and **Melthaeus**, to **Ptolemy**, and to **Aristoxenus**: that the listener is sweetly affected by that **Geometrical ratio** which exists in sounds (and in this subject is called **harmonics**), even though the person may be unaware that what affects them is a Geometrical matter, and may never have learned Geometry nor be able to learn it. It was also said that the same ratio exists in the rays of the planets as in sounds; and by these, the emotions are similarly moved, as is whatever in the human soul is "natural," which **Aristotle** pronounced to be "brute and mortal." <note>Aristotle distinguished between the rational, immortal part of the soul and the irrational (brute), mortal part that reacts to physical stimuli.</note>

<margin>
The star's effect on human reason is accidental to it, but essential to this.
</margin>

Regarding these different aspects of Man, I also speak distinctly. And first, regarding **Reason** itself, which the Best Creator shared with us in His own image: the matter requires no lengthy explanation; the minds of mortals were vehemently struck with consternation at the first sight of this star. **Natural History** bears witness to this.
